The Seed of Ruin

The Seed of Ruin

Riarhin X

Part I — Work left unfinished

Chapter 1 — The work on the table

Brede Ashby wanted the shoulder strap lower.

“It rides there,” she said, pressing two fingers into the frame where the ash rail met the cross-piece. “Not up by the neck. You’ve set it for somebody your height.”

“I’ve set it for somebody who has to walk eleven miles under it.”

“Ottley’s boy is shorter than you.”

“Then he’ll walk eleven miles with it rubbing his ear.”

Brede took the frame off the table and put it on her own back to prove the point. She was a broad woman with grey braids pinned flat and a scar along one forearm from a mule who had disliked a gate. The empty frame sat awkwardly on her; she bent forward under nothing, as though the load were already there, and walked twice round the yard.

The mules watched from the rail. One of them, a dun with white eyelashes, had bitten Riarhin on her first morning and had since decided she was furniture.

“Too high,” Brede said, coming back.

“Load it.”

“With what?”

Riarhin nodded at the sacks of salt by the store door. Brede looked at them, then at the sky, which had the hard brightness of late summer before a change, and at the yard boy, who was pretending to sweep. She sighed. The boy brought two sacks. Riarhin lashed them to the frame with the old cord while Brede held it upright, and then Brede put it on again.

She walked round the yard once. On the second round she stopped by the trough and straightened, and the frame came with her instead of dragging at her shoulders.

“Hm,” she said.

“Lower is where it rides when it’s empty.”

“I heard you.”

“I didn’t say anything.”

“You were going to.” Brede let Riarhin lift the frame from her back. “Leave it where it is. If Ottley’s boy complains, I’m sending him to you.”

“Send him.”

Riarhin set the frame back on the table and found the strap end, which had to be stitched where the leather doubled. Brede’s yard was a narrow place behind the salt store, walled on two sides, with the mules’ lean-to at the back and the long table under a slanting roof where the harness was mended. Beyond the wall, Hessel went on with its afternoon: gulls, a cart with a squealing wheel, somebody calling the price of mackerel, then calling it lower.

She had come down the coast with Brede’s string in early summer, guarding salt and dyed wool. Brede had lost a man to a fever and needed someone who could walk and lift and look at the hedges properly. Riarhin had stayed. The mules did not ask her where she had been.

Brede sat on the end of the table with her feet on a crate.

“That letter came,” she said.

“Which?”

“The one you said would come. Fellow brought it this morning while you were out with the dun. I put it in the jar.”

Riarhin looked up. “You didn’t say.”

“You were arguing about the strap.”

The jar stood on the shelf above the table, where Brede kept nails, a broken spur, the yard’s small coins, and anything with writing on it she had not yet decided to read. Riarhin wiped her hands and took the letter out. It had been folded twice and sealed with green wax that bore no device, only a thumbprint. Her name was on the outside in Coryd’s square letters.

She broke the seal.

A man called Vesket has written to me through Eshai. You remember Eshai. He read her copies of the papers. He is a Sumwright from the hawthorn country and he says he has something about our grandmother you should see. I have not met him. Eshai says he is careful and talks too much. Both of those seem to be true from his letters.

I gave him the yard at Hessel and told him to come on the twenty-second. I did not give him anything else. He knows I am your sister because Eshai told him.

I am still expecting you on the first. Sivelle has written about the stair again. I will tell you about it whether you like it or not.

It falls.

Riarhin read the last line twice. Then she counted. It was the twenty-second.

“Bad?” Brede asked.

“I don’t know yet.”

“That’s what you said about the mule.”

“I was right about the mule.”

Brede did not dispute it. Riarhin folded the letter and put it inside her shirt, where it lay against her skin, a little damp at once in the heat. She picked up the awl and the waxed thread. The strap end had to be finished before Ottley’s boy came for the frame at dawn.

She had made four stitches when the gate opened.

The man who came in was small, even for one of the hawthorn people, and carried more than seemed possible for someone his size. A long box hung from one shoulder on a wide strap. A covered basket rode on the other hip. On his back was a pack with a tall, lidded copper case lashed to the top of it, and from somewhere under all of this a thin line of smoke rose, as though he were quietly on fire.

He stopped inside the gate and looked at the yard with bright, deliberate attention: the mules, the trough, the boy with the broom, the long table under its roof. His eyes rested on the table and stayed there.

“Good afternoon,” he said. “I’m looking for Riarhin. I was told the salt yard behind Ashby’s. I hope this is Ashby’s. I’ve been to two salt yards.”

“This is Ashby’s,” said Brede. “Who’s asking?”

“Vesket. I’m expected. I hope I’m expected.” He looked at Riarhin. He had recognised her before Brede spoke; she had seen him do it, the small check of his gaze at her hair and her left hand, which held the awl. “You’re Riarhin.”

“Yes.”

“Your sister wrote that you’d be here.” He took one step further in. The smoke rose straight up from the copper case and then bent over in the breeze from the sea. “Forgive me. Before anything else. Is there somewhere out of the wind where I could put this down? It has to stay warm, and it has been an exceptionally long walk from the boat.”

His eyes went to the table again.

There were things on it: the frame, the awl, a coil of cord, three spare buckles and the pot of wax, a heel of bread Brede had been eating and forgotten. It was the only flat surface under a roof.

“That’s a work table,” Brede said.

“I can see that.” He did not seem to think the observation settled anything. “It would only be for a little while. If I could clear one end—”

“You can’t.”

He looked at her properly then. Riarhin watched him take in the braids, the scarred arm, the feet on the crate. He looked at the store door behind her, at the mule rail, at the trough with its green slime, and his eyes came back to Brede.

“No,” he said. “I don’t suppose I can. I apologise. Is there anywhere at all? The ground would do if it were dry. The difficulty is the wind more than the surface.”

Brede studied him a moment longer. Then she got off the table, went into the store and came back with an empty salt crate, which she put down in the corner of the lean-to where the wall met the roof and the mules’ warmth made the air close.

“There.”

“Thank you.” He went to it at once. He set down the basket first, very carefully, then unslung the box, then crouched and loosened the straps of his pack without taking it off, so that the copper case came down onto the crate in one movement while he stayed bent beneath it. When it was settled he stood, put both hands in the small of his back, and breathed out.

The dun mule leaned its head over the rail and smelled the smoke.

“No,” Vesket told it.

It went on smelling. He lifted the case’s lid an inch, looked in, and lowered it. Riarhin caught a glimpse of a dull red glow and of something bright laid in a cradle of wool.

“Is it alight?” Brede asked.

“A little. It’s a heater. It is meant to be alight. I can show you that it won’t set fire to anything.”

“Show me.”

He showed her. There was a small iron grate in the base of the case, no bigger than a hand, holding a few lumps of charcoal behind a copper screen, and above it a space packed with wool and felt in which several things lay in their own hollows: two slender rods, a row of stoppered pots, a flat leather wallet. He showed her how the screen latched, how the air came in through holes too small for a coal to fall through. Brede listened as if he were selling her a mule. At the end she said he could stay in the corner as long as he kept the dun’s nose out of it, and went back to the table.

Riarhin had not stopped stitching. She made the last three stitches, drew the thread through, and tied it off.

Vesket came across the yard and stood at a polite distance from the table, watching her hands.

“Your sister said you were working,” he said. “I didn’t think she meant this.”

“What did you think she meant?”

“I don’t know. Guarding something.” He gestured at the yard. “I suppose this is guarding something.”

“Salt.”

“Salt,” he agreed. He watched her cut the thread end and press it flat. He had copper-coloured stains on the first two fingers of his right hand and a burn, old and shiny, across the back of the left. His coat had a great many pockets and most of them were full. “I would like to explain why I’ve come,” he said. “But I can wait until you’ve finished. I’ve been told it’s rude to ask for attention in a workroom before the work is done. In my country we would say it’s an error of order. I was told the second time very firmly by a woman who mended nets.”

“Did she let you finish?”

“She let me carry the net.” He seemed to remember this with pleasure. “It was very wet.”

Brede snorted.

Riarhin took up the frame and checked the stitching where the strap met the rail. There was one more thing to do: the buckle at the waist band had to be moved two holes, and the punch was under the bread. She moved the bread. Vesket watched her do this as well.

“You can talk,” she said.

“Thank you.” He drew a breath. “There’s a woman of the Lethai-thora named Farieth.”

Riarhin put the frame down.

It made a soft knock against the boards. The sacks of salt were still lashed to it from Brede’s test, and it sat there leaning slightly to one side, weighted and awkward.

Brede looked from her to the stranger.

Vesket had stopped. He was watching her with the same deliberate attention he had given the yard, and Riarhin saw him decide not to say the next sentence yet. A cart went by beyond the wall with its squealing wheel. A gull landed on the store roof and walked along the ridge.

“I know who she is,” Riarhin said.

“Yes. Your sister said so. I wasn’t certain how much she meant.” He glanced at Brede. “Would you rather I said this elsewhere?”

“No,” Brede said. “I’d rather hear it.”

“It’s her yard,” said Riarhin.

“Then I’ll be brief, and I’ll be clear about what I know and what I only think.” He folded his hands in front of him like a pupil. “Farieth has been paying people to carry things into the hawthorn country for most of this year. Carriers, a ship’s master, a man with a cart. The things went to the tide-sheds below the Green Gate. Since late spring, a set of measurements I keep, measurements of crossings, which I can show you, have been changing in a way they have not changed in the eleven years I’ve kept them. One crossing near the coast has closed. I think the two facts are connected. I think she means to do something to what lies under the Valewood, and I don’t think she understands what it will do. Or she understands and has decided it doesn’t matter, which may be worse. I came to you because you know her and I don’t. I’ve read what she wrote forty years ago. That isn’t the same.”

He stopped. He looked, for the first time, uncertain whether he had said it well.

Riarhin looked at the frame.

The buckle needed moving two holes. She could see where, the faint mark of a thumbnail she had made in the leather that morning. She picked up the punch. She set the frame upright again, held the waist band flat against the table with the heel of her right hand, and made the first hole, then the second. She threaded the strap through. She drew the buckle tongue into its new place and pulled until the band sat true.

Brede watched without saying anything.

When it was done, Riarhin unlashed the salt sacks and set them on the ground. She put the frame on the end of the table where Ottley’s boy would find it in the morning, and hung the awl on its nail, and put the punch back under where the bread had been.

Then she wiped her hands on her trousers and looked at Vesket.

“Show me,” she said.

He let out a breath. “Thank you. It will take a little while to set up. It will take less while if somebody holds the lamp.”

“It’s daylight.”

“Not for this.” He was already moving back towards the crate. “I need the light to be steady and close. Daylight moves about. There are clouds.”

Brede got up from the table.

“I’ve a lamp,” she said. “You’re not burning my lean-to.”

“I’m not burning anything.”

“The mule thinks you are.”

The dun had put its whole head over the rail now and was breathing the warm air from the copper case with half-closed eyes. Vesket looked at it, then took a strip of felt from one pocket and draped it over the vent.

“You can’t have it,” he told the mule. “It isn’t for you.”

The mule withdrew its head with dignity. Brede went into the store for the lamp. Riarhin stood by the table a moment longer, feeling Coryd’s letter against her ribs where it had stuck to her skin.

It falls.

The line was in her sister’s hand. Coryd had remembered the ending Bea used to sing, and Riarhin was gladder of it than she wanted him to see.

She crossed the yard to the lean-to and crouched beside the crate, where the air smelled of mule and warm copper and very faintly of something sharp, like vinegar on a cut, that she could not name.

“Where do you want the lamp?” she asked.

“Here.” He touched the edge of the crate, exactly, with one stained finger. “And then here, when I say. And if you could keep it from swinging.”

“I can keep it from swinging.”

“Your sister said you would say that.”

Riarhin looked at him. He was lifting the first rod from its hollow with a cloth wrapped round his fingers, his whole attention on not letting it touch anything else, and he did not see her expression. She found she wanted to know what else Coryd had written. She did not ask.

Brede came back with the lamp lit. Riarhin took it from her and held it where she was told.

Chapter 2 — The second measurement

The rods were a little longer than Riarhin’s forearm and no thicker than a reed. They had been drawn from copper, Vesket said, from the same length of wire, on the same morning, by the same hand.

“Mine,” he added. “Badly. I was seventeen. One of them has a flaw near the end where I let the die cool. You can see it.”

She could. A slight dullness, a thickening the width of a thread.

“Does it matter?”

“It mattered to my teacher. He made me keep them so I’d remember. It doesn’t matter for this. What matters is that they were made together.” He laid them side by side on a strip of felt across the crate, not touching. “When they’re warm and still, if I strike them together, they don’t quite sing the same note. They’re close. There’s a beating between them, like two people walking almost in step. You can count it. When they were new it was a little more than three beats in a breath. It has stayed so for twenty years. I’ve carried one through crossings and brought it back and struck them again, and as long as the crossing was sound, the beat came back the same.”

“And now?”

“Listen.”

He took a small copper hammer from his coat, no bigger than a spoon, and struck each rod once at the same point, quickly, one after the other.

The sound filled the lean-to: two thin, bright notes wound round each other, and through them, a slow throb, like a pulse felt in the ear. Riarhin counted without meaning to. The throb came and went, came and went. The mules shifted at the rail.

The notes faded. Vesket held up a hand to keep them quiet until the last of it had gone.

“Fewer,” Riarhin said.

“Fewer. A little over two in a breath. It has been falling since spring. Not evenly. A little, then more, then nothing for a week, then more.” He picked up the flawed rod with his cloth and turned it. “This one went through the Green Gate in the spring with a carrier I trust. It came back sounding like this. I thought I’d damaged it. I made two more pairs from new wire and sent one rod of each through. They came back changed as well. Differently changed, but changed.”

Brede, who had sat down on an upturned bucket by the lean-to post, said, “What does a copper stick have to do with anything?”

Vesket turned to her with an expression Riarhin was to learn: pleasure at being asked, and dismay at being asked in the middle.

“Nothing by itself. It’s a—” He searched for a word. “A witness. It has been somewhere and come back, and the place it went has left a difference in it. If the place were the same as last year, the difference would be the same as last year. It isn’t.”

“So the place has changed.”

“Something about the way through it has changed. The rod can’t tell me what. It can only tell me the beat is different and when it became different.”

Brede considered this. “Like a dog that comes home smelling of somebody else’s farm.”

“Yes,” said Vesket, delighted. “Exactly like that.”

Riarhin kept the lamp still.

From the covered basket came a small dry rustle.

Vesket glanced at it. “Ah. Forgive me. She’ll want to come out. She doesn’t like being shut up while I work. Could I—” He looked at Brede. “She’s a spider. She’s very small. She won’t leave the crate.”

Brede’s face did something complicated. “How small?”

“Smaller than your thumbnail.”

“Fine.”

He lifted the basket’s lid. Inside, on a frame of fine copper wire, a web had been built in perfect six-sided cells, layer on layer, catching the lamplight so that it looked like a little window of lead and glass. In the middle of it sat a spider the colour of tarnished brass, with a pale cross on its back. As the light reached it, it raised its front legs.

“This is Tessel,” Vesket said.

The spider walked to the edge of the wire frame and down onto the felt, where it stopped beside the flawed rod. Vesket took a twist of paper from his pocket, unrolled it, and shook out two tiny dead flies, which he set on the felt a hand’s breadth away. Tessel ignored them. It walked to the end of the rod, along its length, to the other end, and back, and then it began, with great deliberation, to draw.

It drew with a thread so fine that Riarhin could see it only when the lamp caught it: a line, an angle, another line, anchored to the felt at points no larger than a grain of sand. It worked back and forth between the two rods, and the figure grew between them, a lattice of triangles that leaned, very slightly, to one side.

“She does this every day,” Vesket said quietly. “If she doesn’t, she frets, and her work goes wrong, and then mine does. Today she can do it on the rods. That’s useful.”

“What is she drawing?”

“The relation between them. As she understands it. I don’t always understand what she understands.”

He took a bone case from his pocket, uncapped it, and drew out a roll of vellum. It was dark at the edges from handling and covered in close patterns drawn in brown ink: interlaced figures, tessellations, here and there a line of small writing in letters Riarhin did not know. He unrolled a hand’s width of it on his knee, found a place, and looked from the vellum to the spider’s lattice and back.

“May I tell you what I’m about to do before I do it? I don’t like people being surprised.”

“Tell me.”

“There’s a working. A small one. It lets me see how a pattern is held together a little more clearly than I can by looking. It doesn’t tell me anything that isn’t already in front of me. It helps me see which parts of what’s in front of me belong to each other. It will cost me something afterwards. Not much. It will tire me and I’ll owe it.”

“Owe what?”

He paused. “Attention. Care. Regular work, in service to the Sacred Geometry. It’s hard to say in your language. It’s hard to say in mine.”

Brede said, “Is this sorcery in my lean-to?”

“A little,” said Vesket.

“Will the mules mind?”

“I don’t think so.”

She looked at the mules. They did not appear to mind anything except the felt over the heater.

“Go on, then.”

He did not do anything Riarhin could see. He sat on his heels with the vellum on his knee and his left hand open above Tessel’s lattice, not touching it, and he looked. His lips moved once. The lamp flame stood straight. The sound of the town beyond the wall seemed to recede by a step, as though somebody had closed a door in another room. Then it came back.

Vesket closed his eyes for a breath and opened them.

“Yes,” he said. “That’s what I thought. That’s good.”

“What is?”

“That I wasn’t only seeing my own fear.” He rolled the vellum and put it away. He looked tired already, a faint greyness under the brown of his face. “The change isn’t weather. It isn’t a slow wearing. It began at a point. It comes in steps. Each step comes after something. Something is being done.”

“By someone.”

“I didn’t want to say that until I could. Now I can.” He reached into his coat again and brought out a cord.

It was an ordinary-looking cord, the thickness of a bootlace, woven from three strands, one green, one undyed, one the dull red of brick. Knots had been tied in it at intervals. He laid it across his palm.

“You know what this is?”

“A way-cord.”

“Yes. The tide-reeves at the Gate give one to every party that goes through and is meant to come back. It’s tied to show the way you mean to go and how many days. It comes back with you, and they untie it, and that closes your crossing. It’s a courtesy. It’s also a record.” He held the cord up to the lamp. “Eight knots. One for each day he’d planned. This one was given to a carrier in early summer. He went in with a load, came out, gave it back. The reeve who took it from him put it in a box. She took it out again a month later because her hands remembered something wrong about it, and she sent it to me.”

He laid it on the felt beside the rods. Tessel stopped drawing and turned towards it.

“Look at the knots,” he said.

Riarhin counted the knots and found nothing wrong until she looked at the spaces between them. The first gaps were even. Then two knots sat closer than the rest, and the last gap was long.

“Somebody retied it.”

“Somebody retied two knots. Carefully, in the same knot, with the same twist. The reeve thinks it was done inside. The carrier swears he never untied anything. I believe him. He didn’t know what the knots were for.” Vesket touched the long gap with a fingertip. “If this were a road, I would say somebody shortened one day and added the difference to the last. If it were a boat, I’d say somebody moved the cargo.”

Brede leaned forward on her bucket. “Why would anyone do that?”

“So that the cord would come back the right length,” Riarhin said. “And the reeve would put it away without looking.”

Vesket nodded slowly. “Yes. That’s what I think. The reeve doesn’t untie a cord that comes back right. She coils it. The days go into the box with it and nobody counts them again.”

“Then why did she?”

“Because her hands remembered.” He shrugged. “She has been doing it for thirty years. She coiled it and her hands stopped. That’s all.”

Riarhin looked at the cord and thought of a woman at a kiln asking how long it would be before the potter could come inside.

“What did the carrier take in?”

“Bundles. Long, heavy. Wrapped in waxed linen with copper bands, so they could go before the Gate courteously. He didn’t ask what was inside. He was paid to carry them as far as the tide-sheds below the arch and leave them with a woman who met him. He was paid well. She thanked him and asked whether his mules had been watered.”

“Did he see her?”

“He says she was old and tall and courteous. She had a stiff leg. She asked about his children.” Vesket looked at Riarhin. “I haven’t met her. I’ve read her papers. They’re very good papers. I didn’t know about the leg.”

“I gave her the leg.”

He was silent a moment. Brede looked at Riarhin and did not ask.

“The crossing that closed,” Riarhin said.

“Coombe Barrow. On the coast, a day north of the Gate. It’s a stile in a hedge, and in the right season, if you count it properly, it lets you down into a lane that isn’t there the rest of the year, and the lane comes out above the tide-sheds and saves you a day round the headland. Shepherds use it. Children use it, which they shouldn’t. Since the spring, you climb the stile and come down in the same field you started in. I counted it four ways myself.” He spread his hands. “It isn’t important. Nobody died. Some people walk round the headland now. But it closed at the same time as the first step in the rods.”

Riarhin set the lamp down on the crate’s edge, careful of the spider, and sat back on her heels.

“You think she’s carrying something into the forest.”

“I know she’s carrying something to the edge of it. I think the cord means she doesn’t want the reeves counting how long she stays in. I don’t know what the bundles are. I don’t know what she means to do with them. I have an idea, from the papers, of where she might want to go. I don’t know that she’s going there.”

He looked at the rods. “What I saw just now says the change is being made, not suffered. That’s all it says.”

“That’s a good deal.”

“It isn’t enough. I’ve been to two halls with it already. One thanked me. One asked whether I’d considered that my rods were old.”

Brede said, “Are they?”

“Very,” he said. “They’re older than half the people in that hall. That’s why they’re useful.”

Riarhin looked at the lattice the spider had drawn between the rods. It still leaned. Tessel had gone back to the flies and was eating one with small, precise movements.

“The carrier,” she said. “Who was he?”

“A man with a cart, from this coast. That’s why I came here first, partly. The reeve had his name. Rudge. He carries from Hessel up the cliff road to the tide-sheds.” Vesket hesitated. “I went to his yard this morning. He wasn’t there. His sister said he’d be back tonight, and she didn’t want to talk to me about it. I don’t think she liked the look of me.”

“What did she say?”

“That they’d been paid fair and done nothing wrong and she didn’t care for people from the hills asking about other people’s carriage. I said I didn’t think they’d done anything wrong. I said it twice. I think that was a mistake.”

“It usually is.”

He looked at her with surprise, and then a reluctant laugh came out of him. It was a good laugh, short and dry, and it took some of the greyness out of his face.

“Your sister said you would be practical.”

“What else did my sister say?”

He considered whether to tell her. “That you’d want to talk to the carrier yourself. That you wouldn’t take my word for how she asks people to do things. She said you’d know it if you heard it.”

Riarhin did not answer at once. The dun mule had put its head over the rail again, at a safe distance from the felt, and was watching them with its white-lashed eyes. The lamp hissed a little where the wick wanted trimming.

She had heard it herself, across a broken walkway, in a voice that was interested in where the water went.

“Yes,” she said. “I want to speak to him.”

“Tonight?”

“When he’s back.”

Vesket nodded. He began to put the rods away, each into its own hollow in the warm wool, lifting them with the cloth so that his fingers never touched them. He set the hammer in its place, closed the copper screen over the coals, and drew the lid down. Then he looked at the spider, who had finished the second fly and was sitting in the middle of her lattice with her front legs folded.

“She’ll want to stay out a little longer,” he said apologetically. “She’s been in the basket since dawn.”

Brede got up from the bucket.

“She can stay out,” she said. “You can both have supper. Not on my table.”

“Thank you.” He hesitated. “Where, then?”

“On your knees, like everybody else.”

He looked at his knees as though they had been unexpectedly assigned a duty. Brede went to see about the pot. Riarhin stayed crouched by the crate, watching the spider sit in her slanting web, and when Vesket made to lift the basket to put her back, she said, “Leave her.”

He left her.

After a while Tessel climbed down from the felt and began, unhurriedly, to draw again: a new line, from the end of the flawed rod to the edge of the crate, and then another, and another, until the figure was wider than before and leaned the other way.

Vesket watched it with his chin on his hand.

“I don’t know what that means,” he said. “I wish I did.”

“Does she?”

“I think so. I think she’s been right more often than I have.” He looked at Riarhin sideways. “I don’t say that in the halls.”

Riarhin found that she liked him. It was inconvenient, and she did not do anything about it.

Chapter 3 — What the carrier agreed

Rudge’s yard lay at the top of the town, where the cliff road began. It was bigger than Brede’s and less tidy: two carts under a thatched shelter, a third with a wheel off, a stable of four stalls and a house built against the rock with a door painted a blue that had faded to the colour of milk.

Tam Rudge was unharnessing when they arrived. He was a square, quiet man with a sunburned neck and the tired, deliberate movements of someone who had walked beside a cart since before dawn. He looked at Riarhin, then at Vesket behind her, and went on unbuckling the breeching.

“I told your friend,” said the woman in the doorway, “we’ve nothing to say.”

She was older than Tam and thinner, with ink on her fingers and a pen still behind one ear. She had the docket book under her arm, pressed against her ribs, as though she had picked it up when she saw them coming.

“I heard,” Riarhin said. “I’m not here for him. I’m here for my grandmother.”

The woman frowned. Tam stopped with the strap in his hand.

“Your grandmother.”

“The tall woman. Courteous. Stiff in the leg. She asked about your children.”

Tam looked at his sister. She looked back at him.

“She’s your grandmother?” Tam said.

“Yes.”

“She didn’t say she had family hereabouts.”

“She wouldn’t.”

He hung the strap on the cart shaft and wiped his hands down his front. “She paid fair,” he said. “More than fair. I want that understood. She paid half before and half after and the second half came when she said it would, and there was a bit over for the mules. Nobody’s cheated anybody.”

“I know.”

“Then what do you want?”

Riarhin had thought about it on the walk up. People who had been kind to Farieth, who had been treated with consideration, did not want to discover what they had been considerate to.

“I want to know what you carried,” she said. “And where. And how she asked you.”

“Why?”

“Because she’s killed people I’m related to. Not near here. Not with anything you carried, as far as I know. But she was courteous to them first.”

The sister made a small sound. Tam stood very still.

“That’s a thing to say in a man’s yard,” he said at last.

“Yes.”

“Have you proof of it?”

“Not with me.”

“Then why should I—” He stopped. His sister had put her hand on his arm. She was looking at Riarhin’s face, not her sword, and whatever she saw there made her grip tighten.

“Tam,” she said. “Get the mules in.”

“Jenet.”

“Get them in. I’ll talk to her. You’ll want to hear, so be quick about it.”

He looked at her a moment longer, then took the near mule’s headstall and led it towards the stable. Jenet watched him go. When he was inside, she turned back.

“I’m not saying anything,” she said, “until I’ve the copy.”

“What copy?”

“Of the docket. I wrote it out for her. Every load, the day, the weight, where it went. She asked for it. Very particular about it. She took the fair copy and I kept the rough, and the rough’s not here, it’s at Petty’s, because Petty took the book to reckon the cliff toll and hasn’t brought it back.” She pressed the book under her arm harder. “This is this month’s. Hers is in the old one. I’m not telling you anything I can’t show you. Not if you’re going to go about saying we carried for a murderer.”

“I won’t say that.”

“You said it to Tam.”

“I said she’d been courteous first.”

Jenet’s mouth tightened. “That’s the same thing, the way you said it.”

Riarhin had no answer. Behind her, Vesket shifted his weight and said nothing.

“Where’s Petty?”

“The toll house at the turn. It’s not far. I’ll go myself. You’ll wait here, and you won’t touch anything, and when I’ve the book I’ll show you what there is. Then you’ll tell me what she did, and I’ll decide whether I believe you.”

“All right.”

Jenet went. She took the docket book with her.

Tam came out of the stable with an empty bucket. He filled it at the pump and went back in without looking at them. When he came out a second time, he set an upturned crate by the house wall and another beside it and went back to his mules. Riarhin sat on one. After a moment Vesket sat on the other, carefully, with the copper case on the ground between his feet.

“You said that badly,” he murmured.

“I know.”

“I only mention it because I said it badly this morning, and I thought I was the only one.”

The evening came on. Swifts went screaming over the yard. From the stable came the sounds of mules being rubbed down, a bucket set down, Tam talking to an animal in a low, steady voice that did not change when he knew he could be overheard. She had come into their yard and put a dead woman in it.

Jenet came back with the book before the light went. It was older than the first, the cover patched with sailcloth. She did not sit. She opened it on the cart tail and turned the pages until she found the place, then stood back so that Riarhin could see without coming closer than she liked.

The entries were neat and brief. Seven bdls, long, wrapped, copper-bound. From the Lark (Marten master). Hessel quay to the tide-sheds below Green Arch. Two carts. Paid half. A date in early summer. Then, ten days later, the same: Five bdls. Then two. Then Paid in full, and a half-mark over for the mules (her word).

“Fourteen,” Riarhin said.

“Fourteen carried. There was meant to be sixteen.” Jenet put a finger on the last line. “Eight to a ship. Two ships. The first brought eight and the second only six. Tam took them up as he had room on the carts. The last two came in a week after on the coaster from the south, and by then she’d said she was done with the carts, she’d send someone for them. They’re in Petty’s store. She paid storage for a month. The month’s out in four days.”

Vesket made a small involuntary movement.

Riarhin looked at the page. Beside the second entry, in the margin, there was a small mark: a fold, drawn rather than made, a little shape like a letter folded in three and then its corner turned down. It was drawn twice, once on each delivery.

She knew it.

She had seen Zemia’s hands make it without thinking, on messages left at the drop in the Valewood, the corner tucked under so it would hold without a seal.

“What’s this?” she asked.

Jenet looked. “That’s how the bundles were marked. On the linen. In chalk. I drew it so I’d know which were hers if there were others on the quay. There weren’t.”

“Who chalked them?”

“They came chalked. From the ship.”

Riarhin sat back. Vesket leaned to see.

“Is it something?” he said.

“It’s hers.”

Jenet looked from one to the other. “What does it mean?”

“That she’s particular about how things are wrapped.” Riarhin heard the edge in her own voice and made it smaller. “How did she find you?”

“She came to the yard. Like you.” Jenet’s hand was flat on the page. “In the spring, before the first ship. She asked who carried up the cliff road, and somebody sent her here, and she stood about where you’re sitting and asked whether we had room for long loads, and Tam said yes, and she asked how long it took to the sheds, and he told her. She asked whether the mules could do it twice in a week without hurting, and he said not if you wanted them sound, and she said then she’d wait. She’d sooner wait than hurt them.” Jenet stopped. “She asked about my boy. He’d had the cough all winter. She said there was a woman in the hawthorn hills who sold a syrup for it, and the next time she came she brought a bottle. It worked.”

Tam had come to the stable door and was standing there with a cloth over his shoulder.

“She waited,” he said. “On the second load. Sat on that crate while I mended a trace. Two hours. She could have had the bundles carried by hand from the quay to the sheds by a dozen men in a day if she’d wanted to pay for it. She waited for my mules.”

Riarhin looked at the crate she was sitting on.

“She does that,” she said.

“Does what?”

“Waits.”

Neither of them understood. She did not know how to make them. She thought of saying she waited seven years for the woman I loved to die, and did not, because it would sound like something said to win, and because it was not theirs.

“What’s in the bundles?” she asked instead.

“Iron.” It was Tam. “Rods. I didn’t open them. You can tell by the weight and the way they shift. Long rods, bent at one end. Like for a gate, or a fence. A lot of iron to take up to the gnomes, I said to her, and she said yes, she knew it wasn’t polite, that was why it was wrapped.”

Vesket said very quietly, “Iron.”

“They don’t like it up there,” said Tam. “Everybody knows that.”

“Who’s she sending for the last two?”

“She didn’t say. Someone with her token, she said. She left a token with Petty. A bit of linen with that mark on.” Jenet nodded at the drawn fold. “Whoever comes with the twin of it takes the bundles.”

“Can you stop that?”

Jenet’s face closed.

“They’re hers. She paid for them. She paid for the store. You’re asking me to keep a woman’s goods from her because you say—”

“Not keep them from her. Hold them. Longer than the month. I’ll pay the storage. I’ll pay Petty to hold them until I say, and if she comes herself and asks, he can tell her who’s paying and where to find me.”

“And if she sends somebody with the token?”

“He tells them the same.”

Jenet stared at her. “That’s a thief’s trick.”

“It’s a carrier’s trick,” Riarhin said. “Brede’s held a load for a dispute before. So have you. I’m paying for the dispute.”

Tam laughed, once, unwillingly. Jenet did not.

“And when she comes to my yard and asks why my store won’t give her what she paid for?”

Riarhin had no answer to that which would satisfy anybody. She was asking these people to put themselves between Farieth and something Farieth wanted. She had told herself, walking up, that she would not ask anyone to do that again. Here she was asking it of a woman whose boy had been given cough syrup.

“Tell her the truth,” she said. “Tell her I asked. Tell her where I’m going. I’ll tell you where I’m going. Then she’ll come after me and not you.”

“You don’t know that.”

“No.”

Jenet looked at her brother. Tam had come out of the doorway and was standing by the cart tail, looking down at his sister’s neat entries.

“She waited for the mules,” he said.

“I know she did.”

“I liked her.”

“So did I.” Jenet closed the book. She held it against her chest a long moment. “Petty won’t do it for nothing.”

“I’ll pay.”

“He’ll want it in writing. With your name.”

“He can have my name.”

Vesket opened his mouth and shut it again. A name written on a store docket in Hessel was a name that could be found.

She had known that before she offered it.

“I’ll come to Petty with you,” Jenet said. “Tomorrow. Not tonight. Tam needs his supper and so does my boy.” She looked at Riarhin, and her face was hard and very tired. “And then you’ll tell me what she did. All of it. Before I decide whether I’ve been made a fool of.”

“All right.”

They walked back down through the town in the dark. The lamps were lit along the quay. Vesket carried the copper case in front of him with both arms, as though it were a child, and did not speak until they had passed the fish market.

“Iron,” he said again.

“You didn’t know.”

“I knew bundles. I knew heavy. I didn’t know iron.” He walked a few steps. “Iron in the hawthorn country is rude. Iron at the Gate is very rude. Iron past the Gate—” He stopped. “I don’t know. I can think of reasons. I don’t like any of them. I’ll have to look at the papers again.”

“She asked them to wrap it.”

“Yes. She was courteous about the iron.” He laughed without humour. “That’s the most frightening thing anybody’s told me this year.”

At Brede’s gate Riarhin stopped.

“You’ll want a bed.”

“I’d thought the inn.”

“Brede will give you the loft over the store. It’s warm. The spider can stay out.”

He looked at her in the light from the kitchen window, and she saw him understand what she was keeping back: him, and the copper case and the rods and the vellum, where she could see them.

“Thank you,” he said, and went in.

Riarhin stood in the yard. Coryd’s letter was still inside her shirt. She took it out and read it again by the window light. I am still expecting you on the first.

Nine days. She had meant to walk inland with Brede’s string as far as the dales market and go on from there. She would have to tell Brede tomorrow that she was leaving the string early. Brede would say something about the strap.

She folded the letter and went in to supper.

Chapter 4 — The locked cupboard

Drevan stood where the river road crossed the Wenn by a bridge of eleven arches, each with a little house built on its pier. Coryd had written about the bridge houses all spring. Riarhin had read the letters aloud to Brede on the road, because Brede liked to hear about other people’s building troubles, and by the time they reached Hessel Brede had firm opinions about the fourth pier’s footings.

Coryd was on the roof of the fourth house when Riarhin arrived.

She was sitting astride the ridge with a bundle of split oak shingles beside her and a mallet hanging from a cord on her wrist. She saw Riarhin on the bridge, lifted the mallet in greeting, and went on fitting a shingle. When she had driven the peg, she slid down the roof on her backside, dropped from the eaves onto the parapet, and from the parapet to the roadway, landing without a sound.

A woman passing with a basket of eels said, “I’ve told you about that,” and walked on.

“Everybody tells me about that,” Coryd said. She looked at Riarhin, and then past her at Vesket, who had stopped a little way back with his pack and his case and his basket and was looking up at the arches with frank pleasure. “That’s him.”

“That’s him.”

“He’s smaller than I expected.”

“He hears you.”

“Good.” Coryd raised her voice. “You’re smaller than I expected.”

“So are most people,” Vesket said, coming forward. “I’m sorry to arrive with her. I know you had a visit arranged. It wasn’t my intention to be part of it.”

“You’re part of it now.” Coryd looked at the case. “What’s smoking?”

“A heater. It’s meant to.”

“Not in my house, it isn’t. The roof’s half off.” She considered him. “There’s a shed. Come on.”

She had taken two rooms on the north bank, at the back of a cooper’s, with a yard full of staves seasoning in stacks and a lean-to where she kept her tools. It smelled of oak and pitch. She cleared a shelf in the lean-to for the copper case without being asked and stood watching while Vesket settled it and checked the coals, and when he was done she showed him the water butt and the privy and where he could sleep if he didn’t mind the cooper’s dog, who would want to sleep with him.

Then she took Riarhin into the house and shut the door.

They looked at each other.

Coryd’s hair was shorter. She had a new scar, small and white, on the back of one knuckle, and the brown of her face had gone darker from a summer on roofs. She looked well. She looked like someone who had been somewhere for a while and meant to be somewhere else soon.

“You came early,” she said.

“Two days.”

“You were going to the dales with the mule woman.”

“Brede.”

“Brede.” Coryd sat on the edge of the table. “Tell me.”

Riarhin told her all of it: the rods, the cord, the iron, the fold in the docket margin, the two bundles in Petty’s store at Hessel, held now for a month on Riarhin’s silver and her name, and the long hour she had spent with Jenet in the yard afterwards, telling her about the potter and the landlady and the room with the screen, while Jenet listened with the book in her lap and her boy coughing in the house behind her.

Coryd listened without interrupting. When Riarhin came to the fold, she looked up.

“You’re sure.”

“Yes.”

“Zemia folds like that.”

“I know.”

Coryd looked down at her own hands. After a moment she said, “She’s here.”

“In Drevan?”

“At the Swan. Singing in the evenings, writing letters in the day for people who can’t. Since midsummer. She wrote and asked if she could come where I was, and I said she could come to the town, not to the house. She’s kept to that.” Coryd turned the scarred knuckle against her palm. “I asked her to come tomorrow. When I knew you were coming. I thought you’d want to see her.”

“Did you want to see her?”

“I wanted you to see her. That’s different.”

Riarhin did not answer that. Coryd got up and went to a cupboard in the corner of the room.

It was a plain cupboard of dark wood, tall and narrow, that had been made for somebody’s best linen and had a lock in its door. The lock was new. Riarhin could see the pale wood where the old keyplate had been taken off and a heavier one fitted. Coryd took a key from a cord round her neck, opened the door, and stood back.

The sabre lay on the shelf inside, wrapped in the same spare coat they had wrapped it in beneath the shutter. Someone had tied the coat with tape, neatly, at three places.

Riarhin did not touch it.

“Still here,” Coryd said.

“Yes.”

“I check it on the first of the month. Mother Ennis checks it on the fifteenth. She has the other key. She’s the bridge-warden’s wife. She doesn’t know what it is. She thinks it’s my husband’s.” Coryd’s mouth moved. “I let her think it.”

“Harissa?”

“Harissa had a key for the first winter. She sent it back. She said she didn’t like having it in the house. She said it made her listen to the door.” Coryd closed the cupboard and locked it again. She stood with her hand on the wood. “That’s the first thing I wanted to talk to you about. Before any of this.”

“Sivelle.”

“Sivelle.” Coryd turned. “She’s built the stair. It took her the whole of the spring and she sacked two men and the third one did it. She’s written that it’s finished and it doesn’t creak and she wants me to come and walk up it before the autumn rains. I said I’d come after the bridge. The bridge is done in three weeks.” She looked at the cupboard. “I’m not taking that to Valora.”

“No.”

“And I’m not leaving it with Mother Ennis and two keys and no idea what she’s guarding.”

“No.”

“Don’t keep saying no. Say what, then.”

Riarhin sat on the bench by the window. Outside, in the cooper’s yard, the dog had found Vesket and was leaning its whole weight against his legs while he tried to fill a cup at the water butt.

“Who’s told if it moves?” she said.

Coryd sat down opposite her.

They worked at it for an hour. It was dull, careful work, and Riarhin was grateful for it. Mother Ennis could keep the second key, but she would be told, now, that what she kept was dangerous, and that if anybody came asking about it she was to say it had gone to Valora with Coryd. The first key would not go to Valora. It would go to Harissa after all, with a letter, and Harissa could hang it somewhere she did not have to look at it. If either key was lost, the cupboard was to be opened by Mother Ennis and the sabre carried to the bridge-warden, who could lock it in the toll chest where the bridge money was kept, and who did not have to know what it was either.

“And if she comes here?” Coryd said.

“She won’t come for the sabre.”

“You don’t know that.”

“She’s managed without it for a year. She’ll have something else.”

Coryd looked at the cupboard.

“And messages,” she said. “When you’re gone. How do I know it’s you?”

“The same way I knew it was you.”

Coryd frowned, then understood. “It falls.”

“It falls.”

“Harissa knows it. Linias knows it. Zemia knows it.” Coryd said the last name flatly. “She was there when you sang it. She heard Linias change the ending.”

“Did she tell Farieth?”

“She says not.” Coryd rubbed her eyes. “I can’t keep asking her what she didn’t tell. There’s no end to it.”

Riarhin thought of the workroom, and Zemia sitting by the bolts of cloth while Linias played.

“Then something else with it,” she said. “Something she wasn’t there for.”

“Like what?”

Riarhin looked out of the window. The cooper’s dog had given up on Vesket and lain down across his feet. He was standing very still with the cup in his hand, apparently resigned to being stood upon for the rest of the evening.

“What you told me in the yard at the baths. About the boy and the dry berth.”

Coryd was quiet a moment. Then she said, “He checked after rain.”

“Nobody else was there.”

“No.” Coryd almost smiled. “All right. It falls, and he checked after rain. That’s a stupid thing to have to put at the bottom of a letter.”

“Yes.”

“Harissa will want to know why.”

“Tell her. And if a letter goes missing, we change it.”

Coryd nodded. She took a scrap of paper from the table and wrote both lines on it, in her square hand, and looked at them. Then she held the scrap in the candle until it caught, and dropped it on the hearthstone, and watched it go.

“Now nobody has it written down,” she said.

“Now nobody has it written down.”

There was a knock at the door. Coryd went to open it. Vesket stood on the step with the cup in one hand and the dog leaning against his knees.

“I’m sorry,” he said. “I don’t want to interrupt. I wanted to ask whether the dog is allowed in the shed. It’s very warm in there with the case and I think he knows it.”

“He’s allowed anywhere he likes,” Coryd said. “He’s the cooper’s.”

“I see.” Vesket looked down at the dog. “That explains his manner.”

Coryd laughed. It was the first time Riarhin had heard her laugh since she arrived, and it went on a little longer than the remark deserved.

“Come in,” Coryd said. “Leave the dog. Have you eaten?”

“Not since the road.”

“Then you’ll eat. I want to see the rods.”

He looked surprised. “You do?”

“She says they sing.” Coryd jerked her head at Riarhin. “I want to hear it.”

He came in. The dog came in after him, ignoring the instruction, and lay down across the threshold where the draught came under the door. Coryd put bread and cheese and cold mutton on the table and a jug of the cooper’s small beer, and Vesket went out to the shed and came back with the copper case in his arms, and they sat round the table while he unpacked the rods and struck them, and the thin wound sound filled the low room.

Coryd listened with her head on one side.

“That’s the slow part,” she said. “The throb.”

“Yes.”

“It used to be faster.”

“Yes.”

“It sounds like a man walking beside you who keeps falling a step behind.” She listened again as the notes faded. “Do it again.”

He did it again. Coryd closed her eyes. When the sound was gone she opened them and looked at Riarhin.

“You’re going,” she said.

“I haven’t said that.”

“You put the frame down.”

Riarhin looked at her. Vesket was carefully looking at the rods.

“He wrote ahead from Hessel,” Coryd said. “Told me how you finished the frame before you listened. He thought I would want to know.”

Vesket said to the rods, “I thought it was the sort of thing a sister would want to know.”

Riarhin did not know what to do with her face.

“Eat,” Coryd said. “Zemia’s coming tomorrow. She’ll want to see the mark you found. I’d rather you were fed first.”

She pushed the bread across the table. Riarhin took it.

In the morning Zemia came up the cooper’s yard at the hour Coryd had named, neither early nor late, in a plain grey dress with a satchel of paper and ink over her shoulder. She stopped at the door of the house and waited there, not knocking, until Coryd saw her through the window and went to let her in.

Riarhin was sitting at the table. Zemia looked at her from the doorway. She had grown thinner. Her face still had its broad pleasantness, but something had gone out of it, a readiness to begin talking before anyone else had, and what remained was attentive and careful and slightly too still.

“Riarhin.”

“Zemia.”

Zemia came in. She did not sit. She looked at Vesket, who had risen when she entered and was standing by the hearth, and at Coryd, who had gone back to the window. Then she looked at the table, where Riarhin had laid out a sheet of paper on which she had drawn the fold from Jenet’s docket book: the letter in three, the corner tucked under.

Zemia looked at it for a long time.

“May I see the wrapping?” she said. “The real one. Not a drawing.”

“I haven’t got the wrapping.”

“Then where is it?”

“In a store at Hessel. On two bundles of iron.”

Zemia’s hands closed on the strap of her satchel. She stood looking at the drawn fold.

“I’d like to see it,” Zemia said again. “Before I tell you what I think it is.”

Chapter 5 — A direction remembered

“It isn’t a mark,” Zemia said. “Not the way you mean. Not a sign she puts on things.”

She had sat down at last, at the end of the table farthest from the window, with her satchel on her knees. Coryd had not moved from the window. Vesket had sat down again by the hearth with his hands folded and his face arranged into an expression of great politeness, as though he were attending a dispute between strangers at an inn.

“What is it, then?” Riarhin asked.

“It’s how she taught me to fold a message so it would hold without wax.” Zemia touched the drawing, very lightly, at the tucked corner. “Three, and the corner under. She showed me at a table in Assel the first winter. She said wax was for people who wanted to be seen sealing things. She said a fold anyone can open, and that everyone can see has been opened, was more honest.” Her finger stayed on the corner. “I did it for years. I still do it. I did it this morning, to a letter for a man at the Swan whose daughter married in the hills.”

Coryd said, from the window, “She chalked it on iron.”

“I know. That’s why I wanted to see.” Zemia did not look at her. “If it was drawn on the linen, the way you’d draw a sign, the corner would be a line. If it was folded into the linen and then chalked round, the way you’d mark how a thing was wrapped, the corner would be a fold.”

“Does it matter?”

“It matters to me.” Zemia’s voice was quiet. “If she put it there for somebody to see, she was telling them something. If it’s only how the wrapping was done, it’s only her hands.”

Riarhin thought of Jenet’s drawing in the margin, quick and certain. Jenet would have drawn a fold as a line.

“I can’t tell you.”

“No.” Zemia took her hand away from the paper. “Then I’ll tell you the other thing, and you’ll have to decide what it’s worth.”

She opened the satchel. Inside were sheets of paper, cut pens, a little horn of ink stoppered with cork, and a bundle of letters tied with tape. She did not take out the letters. She took out a single sheet, much folded, soft at the creases, and laid it on the table beside the drawing.

“I wrote this out after the baths,” she said. “When the binder was asking me for everything. I wrote down every instruction she ever gave me about where to leave word and where to go if I needed her. I gave the binder a copy. I kept this one.”

Riarhin drew the sheet towards her.

It was a list, in Zemia’s hand, which was so like Farieth’s in places that Riarhin’s skin crept to see it. Places, each with a few words beside it. The stair at Assel, the loose stone, left side. The posting house at Rew, ask for the room over the pump. The ferry steps at the Slow Water, under the bench. The spring-house above the ferry, with the keeper, if the stair at Assel is watched.

There were nine places. The last had been written and crossed through.

“What’s the last?”

Zemia hesitated. “I don’t know. She told it to me once and then told me to forget it. I didn’t forget it. I couldn’t. But I crossed it out because she told me to.” She looked at the sheet. “It’s in the forest. A place with a name I didn’t understand. She said I would never need it.”

Riarhin could read the name through the line. She did not know it either. It was in the old language, and it was built like the names on Eshai’s papers, two parts joined, the second part meaning something like held or kept.

“The order,” she said. “Is that the order she gave them?”

“Yes.”

“Always?”

“Always.” Zemia’s fingers went to the edge of the paper and stopped. “I thought the order was the way she travelled. Coast to the hills, hills to the river, river to the forest. I thought she was telling me where she’d be in turn, so I could find her.”

“And now?”

“Now I think—” She stopped. “I don’t know what I think. I never walked it. I left a letter at Assel and one at Rew. That’s all. I never went to the ferry, or the spring-house. I assumed that was where she was going. I assumed it was towards the forest because she was from the forest.” Zemia looked up, and her face was very tired. “I assumed a great deal.”

Riarhin heard her own next question forming before she spoke it, and heard how it would sound: Why didn’t you tell us this at the baths? She had written it all down for the binder. The order had not mattered then because nobody had been asking about a road.

She asked something else.

“The spring-house. With the keeper. Did she say the keeper’s name?”

“No. Only that the keeper would take a letter and not read it.”

Riarhin turned the sheet towards Vesket.

He came to the table and bent over it with his hands behind his back, careful not to touch, and read it through twice. When he reached the spring-house his eyebrows rose. He straightened, went out to the shed without a word, and came back with the leather wallet from the copper case. He opened it on the table. Inside were papers of his own, many of them, in a small sloping hand, with drawings of stiles and arches and wells.

He found one and laid it beside Zemia’s list.

“The spring-house above the Slow Water,” he said. “It’s a crossing. A small one. The keeper is a woman called Briar. Her family has kept it for six generations. If you go down into the cellar on the right night you come up, if you’ve been polite, in a dell above the tide-sheds, two days’ walking saved. It’s on my list because the beat through it changed in the spring, a little before Coombe Barrow closed. Not much. But it changed.”

Coryd turned from the window.

“So it’s true.”

“It’s consistent,” said Vesket. “Which isn’t the same. But it isn’t nothing.”

“The ferry steps,” Riarhin said.

“Are the ferry to the spring-house. The same family runs it. Briar’s daughter.” He looked at the list again. “Rew I don’t know. Assel I know. Neither is a crossing.”

“She used them for messages,” said Zemia. “Not for going through.”

“Then the spring-house is the only one of these that’s a crossing,” Vesket said slowly, “and it’s the one she told you to use if the others were watched.” He looked at Zemia. “When did she give you this?”

“The second year.”

“Before the spring-house changed.”

“Long before.”

Vesket was silent. He put a finger on his own drawing of the spring-house, a little square house above a river with a dark mouth in its cellar.

“Then it isn’t evidence of what she’s doing now,” he said. “It’s evidence that she’s known about that crossing for years. That she thought of it as hers to use.” He turned to Riarhin. “It isn’t a road to the Seed. It goes north along the border and gives up at the coast. If it’s a road at all, it’s a road round something. A way of keeping out of sight of the Gate.”

“She hates being counted,” said Riarhin.

Zemia looked at her quickly.

“The cord,” Riarhin said. “She retied the cord so the reeve wouldn’t count her days. She sends her messages by places that aren’t crossings and keeps the crossing for when she has to.” She heard herself and stopped. She was building again, out of a woman’s habits, the way she had built a father out of a crooked finger and a turned cup. She made herself say the next thing plainly. “That’s my guess. It isn’t anything else.”

“It’s a good guess,” Vesket said.

“It’s still a guess.”

Zemia sat with her hands in her lap. Coryd looked from Zemia to the list and back.

“You knew about the spring-house all this time,” Coryd said.

“I wrote it down at the baths.”

“And you didn’t say it mattered.”

“I didn’t know it mattered.”

“You never know what matters.” Coryd’s voice was flat and quiet. “That’s what you always say. You didn’t know about Telvan. You didn’t know about Dovra. You didn’t know about the loft.”

Zemia flinched. She did not answer.

Coryd was standing very straight by the window with her hands at her sides.

Riarhin did not tell her to stop. She did not tell Zemia it was all right. She sat with the list in front of her and let the silence stay in the room, and after a while Vesket, very carefully, began to put his papers back in the wallet.

“I’ll copy it,” Riarhin said at last. “The list. If you’ll let me.”

“It’s yours,” said Zemia. “I made it for you. All of you.”

“I’ll copy it. You keep yours.”

Zemia looked at her. Then she nodded.

Riarhin copied it. She left the crossed-out name crossed out on her copy, a line through the same letters. When she had finished, she gave the sheet back. Zemia folded it in three and tucked the corner under, and then looked at what her hands had done, and put it in the satchel quickly.

“I have to go,” she said. “I promised a woman at the Swan I’d write to her son before noon.”

“Go, then,” said Coryd.

Zemia stood. At the door she stopped.

“Riarhin.”

“Yes.”

“If you see the wrapping. The real one. Will you look at the corner?”

“Yes.”

Zemia nodded and went out. Riarhin watched her walk down the cooper’s yard between the stacks of staves, not hurrying. The cooper’s dog lifted its head as she passed and put it down again.

Coryd let out a long breath.

“I’m not sorry,” she said.

“I didn’t ask you to be.”

“You looked as though you might.”

“I didn’t.” Riarhin folded her copy of the list. “You’ve watched her for a year. I haven’t.”

Coryd looked at her for a moment. Then she came and sat down at the table, heavily, as though she had been standing a long time.

Vesket said, to nobody in particular, “I’ll go and see to the case,” and went out.

Neither of them watched him go.

“There’s a letter from Harissa,” Coryd said after a while. “It came last week. I kept it for you.”

She fetched it from the shelf. It was long, three sheets written close on both sides. Harissa had taken work teaching dance to the daughters of a wool merchant who believed his daughters should be able to move as though they did not care whether anybody was watching, and who paid well for it, and who watched every lesson. Harissa did not like him. She liked the daughters. One of them had fallen over her own feet in the first lesson and laughed so hard that Harissa had to sit down. The neighbour with the washing line had moved it again. Linias had written from the west that the instrument repairer was a genius and a thief and possibly mad, and that the instrument sounded better than it ever had, and that he had played it for three nights in a tavern and been paid in fish.

At the bottom, in a different ink, Harissa had written: If you are with Riarhin, tell her I am still angry with her about the walkway and that she is to eat something.

Riarhin read it twice.

“She doesn’t know,” she said.

“About this? No. How could she?”

“She’ll have to.”

“She’ll be furious.”

“Yes.”

Coryd took the letter back and folded it. “I’ll write tonight. I’ll send her the key with it. I’ll put the stupid lines at the bottom so she knows it’s me.” She hesitated. “What do I tell her you’re doing?”

Riarhin did not know how to answer that. She looked at the cupboard in the corner, locked, with the sabre lying in its wrapping inside.

“That a man came to Hessel with a box of hot copper,” she said, “and I’m going with him to look at a field.”

“A field.”

“His friend has a field. In Amedell. Something’s wrong with it.” She had read it in Vesket’s papers on the road. “I don’t know yet whether it’s anything.”

“And if it is?”

“Then I’ll write.”

“With the stupid lines.”

“With the stupid lines.”

Coryd nodded. She got up and went to the hearth and began, without asking, to build up the fire, though it was not cold. Riarhin watched her hands set the sticks.

“You said you’d walk to the weir with me,” Coryd said with her back turned. “When you came. Before any of this. You said you wanted to see the eel traps.”

“I did say that.”

“Well.” Coryd struck the flint. “Will you?”

They walked to the weir in the afternoon. It was a mile downstream, past the bridge and the tanneries and a long reach of willows where children were swimming in the brown water and shrieking at the cold. The weir was an old one, a slanting wall of timber and stone with a gap at one end where the eel traps were set: long baskets of split willow, narrowing to a throat, so that what went in could not turn round. The eel-man was there, a skinny old man with no teeth, who showed Coryd how the throats were made and told Riarhin that her sister had mended his punt for nothing in the spring and so she could have an eel for her supper if she liked.

She liked. He chose one from the keep-net and killed it with a blow on the gunwale and wrapped it in dock leaves.

“She was good with the punt,” he said to Riarhin, nodding at Coryd. “Didn’t talk the whole time she was doing it.”

“She does that.”

“I like that in a woman,” said the eel-man, and grinned, and Coryd laughed and told him he liked anyone who did him a favour.

They walked back with the eel. The light lay long across the willows. Coryd told Riarhin about the stair at Sivelle’s house in more detail than anybody could possibly want: the risers, the treads, the third workman’s opinion of the second workman, a newel post carved with acorns that Sivelle had insisted on and that Coryd thought was hideous and had not said so.

“You’ll say so when you see it,” Riarhin said.

“I will not.”

“You will. The first night.”

“I won’t,” Coryd said, and then, after a few steps, “The second night.”

Riarhin laughed. Coryd looked pleased.

At the bridge they stopped and leaned on the parapet of the fourth house, where the new shingles shone pale above the old. The river went under the arch below them, brown and slow and full of sky.

“I don’t want you to go,” Coryd said.

Riarhin watched the water.

“I know you’re going. I want you to know I don’t want you to.”

“I know.”

“I’m going to Valora anyway. I’m going to walk up that stair. I’m not sitting here waiting for a letter.”

“Good.”

Coryd turned her head and looked at her. Then she leaned sideways, briefly, so that their shoulders touched, and stayed there long enough for Riarhin to feel the warmth of her through the shirt. Below them a moorhen came out from under the arch, looked about, and went back in.

In the evening Riarhin went to the shed. Vesket had the rods out again and was sitting on an upturned bucket with the vellum on his knees, comparing Tessel’s morning lattice with a drawing on the roll. The cooper’s dog lay across the doorway. It did not get up for her. She stepped over it.

“The spring-house,” she said. “Is it on the way to your friend’s field?”

He looked up. “It’s on the way past it. A few days north. Why?”

“Because she told my sister to use it.”

He set the vellum down.

“I meant to go to Osselin anyway,” he said. “Her field is the first thing I want to show you. It’s the thing I don’t understand at all. But it’s on the way.”

“Then we go on the third.”

He looked at her for a moment.

“We,” he said.

“You came to Hessel for something.”

“I came to ask you to look.” He hesitated. “I didn’t come to ask you to come.”

“I know,” said Riarhin. “I’m coming anyway.”

Chapter 6 — The lane after dusk

The downs of Amedell began a day out of Drevan, where the river road climbed away from the Wenn and the hedges grew higher and closer and the lanes sank between them, worn by generations of carts until the fields lay at shoulder height on either side. The hedges were cut back from the way, and at every gap, stile and branching of the road there was a post with a lamp-hook on it, and on most of the hooks a small iron lantern, cold in the daylight.

Vesket walked beside her with his pack and his case and his basket. He had slept badly in the cooper’s shed because the dog had kept him company all night, and he said so twice. Then he began to tell her about the lantern-law, because she asked what the posts were.

“The lanes belong to the lamps after dusk,” he said. “Every post has a warden who keeps it. You can walk the lanes in the dark as long as you walk from light to light. Between them you count. Eight steps and a pause, eight steps and a pause. Not the ninth. If you count the ninth aloud, the Aelaerem will tell you very politely that you’ve made a mistake, and then they’ll find a reason for you to sleep somewhere else.”

“What happens if you don’t count?”

“Nothing, usually.” He walked a few steps. “Usually.”

“And unusually?”

“The lane goes somewhere else.” He shrugged. “I’ve never had it happen. I’ve always counted. I know people it’s happened to. They didn’t like it.”

Riarhin looked at the hedges. They were very high and very green and full of small birds.

“Osselin keeps these?”

“Osselin keeps the lanes round Wendle. Fourteen posts, three stiles, a ford and the turning by the barrow. And the houses along them. It isn’t only the lamps. It’s whoever lives on the lane and needs a light carried, or a message, or a doctor fetched at night.” He looked at her sideways. “You’ll like her. Or you won’t. People usually decide quickly.”

“How long have you known her?”

“Since we were young. Twenty years. More.” He smiled at something. “We travelled together for a while. Before she took the lanes. She’d say we argued the whole time. I’d say we discussed.”

They came over a rise and saw Wendle below them: a village of low houses under thatch, all with their doors facing the green, and every door painted a dark, warm red. Smoke rose straight from the chimneys. There was a church with a squat tower, an orchard behind it, and beyond the orchard the downs went up, smooth and pale gold with summer, to a ridge where a line of dark trees stood against the sky.

“The Valewood,” Vesket said. “The edge of it. Further than it looks.”

Riarhin looked at the trees for a long moment. Then she looked away.

The sun was low when they reached the village. Vesket went straight to a house at the end of the green, larger than most, with a lantern hung beside its red door and a bench under the window. He knocked. He stood waiting with an expression of great expectancy, and when the door opened his face had already begun to arrange itself into a greeting.

It was not Osselin.

The woman who opened the door was perhaps thirty, broad-shouldered and fair, with a child on her hip and flour to the elbows. She looked at Vesket, at his pack, at the smoking case, and at Riarhin behind him, tall in the doorway with a sword on her back.

“Oh,” she said. “You’re the Sumwright.”

“Vesket. Yes. I wrote.”

“She said you might come. She didn’t say today.” The woman shifted the child. “She’s up at the Coombe end. The lane’s closed. She’ll be back before dark. I’m Posy. Fielding. I’m doing her round tonight.” She looked past him at Riarhin. “And you are?”

“Riarhin.”

Posy did not know the name, and did not seem to mind.

“You’d better come in,” Posy said. “Before the lamps.”

They came in. The house was one long room with a loft above, a great hearth at one end and a table down the middle, already laid for more people than were present. There were benches along the walls, and on them, in a row, sat four children with bowls in their laps, eating something that smelled of onions and apples. They stopped eating to look at the visitors.

“Eat,” said Posy. They went on eating, watching over their spoons.

Posy put the child down. It went at once to the bench and climbed up between two others, who moved to make room without being asked. She wiped her arms on a cloth and set two more bowls on the table.

“Sit. There’s plenty. Osselin always says make for twelve. I made for twelve.” She looked at the case. “Will that set the thatch going?”

“No,” said Vesket.

“Put it by the hearth, then, where I can see it.”

He put it by the hearth. Riarhin sat down and found a bowl in front of her and a spoon in her hand. The food was a stew of mutton and onions and sour apples, thick and hot, and it was very good. She ate the whole bowl before she noticed she was eating it. Posy filled it again without asking.

The children were whispering. One of them, a boy of about seven with a scabbed chin, was looking at Remorse where it leaned against the bench beside Riarhin.

“Is that a real one?” he asked.

“Yes.”

“Have you killed anybody with it?”

“Wat,” said Posy.

“I only asked.”

“You only ask everything.” Posy looked at Riarhin apologetically. “He’s mine. The others are my sister’s and the Brookside girl. I have them while their mothers are at the hay.”

“It’s all right,” said Riarhin.

Wat looked at her hopefully. She did not answer his question. After a moment he went back to his stew.

The light outside the window was turning gold, then grey. Posy got up, took a long pole with a hook at one end from beside the door, and a basket of small things, oil and wicks and a little pot of something, and went out. Riarhin watched through the window as she crossed the green to the first post, unhooked the lantern, trimmed it, filled it, lit it from a covered taper, and hung it back. Then she walked to the next.

The children had finished eating. The eldest, a girl of ten or eleven with her hair in two plaits, gathered the bowls without being told and took them to the tub by the door. The others sat in a row on the bench, swinging their legs.

One of them began to sing.

It was the Brookside girl, a small, freckled child with a gap in her front teeth, and she sang in a high, careless voice, not to anyone, as children sing when they have eaten and are warm and waiting for something.

Under the hill and under the stone, the door was open and nobody home. Eight to the stile and one to the gate, and one to the field where the hollow folk wait.

The tune was nothing. A rising line and a falling one, the sort of tune children invent in every country. Riarhin knew it.

She did not know where from. The knowing came into her body before it came into her head: the lift on stone, the drop on home, a pattern in it like a step that comes down a little lower than you expect. Not here. Not in these words. Somewhere with water, and the smell of wet leaves, and her own feet cold.

She reached for it and found nothing but the shape of the tune and the cold feet, and the certainty that she had not been attending, at the time, to whoever sang it.

Under the hill and under the—

“Ivy,” said the eldest girl sharply.

The Brookside girl stopped. “What?”

“You know what.”

“It’s only the field song.”

“You’re not to sing it in the house.”

“Why?”

The eldest girl did not answer. She looked at the visitors and her face went pink. She picked up the last bowl and took it to the tub and began to scrub it very hard.

Ivy looked at the others on the bench. Wat shrugged. The little one Posy had carried was asleep against his shoulder.

Vesket had put down his spoon. He was looking at Ivy with his whole attention. Riarhin put a hand on his arm, lightly, and he looked at her and then back at his bowl.

“Where did you learn that?” Riarhin asked Ivy.

The child looked at her with suspicion. “Everybody knows it.”

“I don’t.”

“You’re not from here.”

“No.”

Ivy considered this. “It’s the field song,” she said again, as though that explained it. “From up the Coombe. We sing it when we play counting.”

“Who taught it to you?”

“Nobody.” Ivy frowned. “It was just there. Wat knew it first.”

“I did not,” said Wat. “Hodge’s Tam did.”

“Hodge’s Tam is a liar.”

The door opened. Posy came in with her pole and her basket and the smell of lamp oil, and a moment later somebody came in behind her.

She was very muddy. Mud to the knees, mud on her hands, mud in a long smear across one cheek and in her hair, which was dark and cut short and stood up in a crest where she had pushed her fingers through it. She was small and solid, with a round, weathered face and quick eyes, and she carried a lantern on a pole over one shoulder, not lit, and a coil of red thread round her wrist like a bracelet.

She stopped in the doorway and looked at Vesket.

“You’re early,” she said.

“I wrote that I’d come in the autumn.”

“It isn’t autumn.”

“It nearly is.”

“It isn’t.” She looked at the case by the hearth. “And you’ve brought the oven.”

“It isn’t an oven.”

“It cooks things.” She looked at Riarhin. Her eyes went over her quickly, the hair, the hands, the sword against the bench, and settled on her face, and stayed there. Riarhin had the sense of being weighed very exactly and found, if not wanting, then at least heavier than expected.

“Osselin,” said Vesket, “this is Riarhin. I wrote about—”

“I know who it is.” Osselin set the lantern pole by the door. “You wrote eleven pages.” She looked at the children on the bench, and her face changed. “Who was singing?”

Nobody answered.

“Ivy,” said the eldest girl at the tub, not turning round.

Osselin looked at Ivy. Ivy looked at her feet.

“I told you,” Osselin said. Her voice was not loud. It was very even. “I told all of you. Not that song. Not in the lanes and not in the houses and not up at the Coombe. Who went up the Coombe today?”

There was a silence. Then Wat said, in a small voice, “Hodge’s Tam.”

“Who else?”

Nobody answered. Osselin looked along the row of them. Then she looked at Posy.

“The Brookside boy was over the boundary again,” she said. “On the field side. Past the hurdles. I had to go in after him.”

Posy’s hand went to her mouth.

“He’s all right. He’s at home. His mother’s got him.” Osselin wiped her face with the back of her wrist and made the mud worse. “He was singing it too. He said he followed the singing.”

Ivy began to cry.

Osselin crossed the room and crouched in front of the bench, muddy as she was, so that her face was level with the child’s. She did not touch her.

“I’m not angry with you,” she said. “I’m frightened. That’s different. Do you know the difference?”

Ivy nodded, crying.

“I’m sorry I was sharp. But I need you not to sing it. Not because it’s a bad song. Because the field is listening for it. Do you understand?”

Ivy shook her head.

“No,” said Osselin. “Nor do I. But it’s so. Will you not sing it?”

Ivy nodded.

Osselin straightened. She looked at Posy.

“The hurdles want moving. The whole line. It’s come over again since this morning. I need help with them before it’s full dark or the Brookside cows will be in it by morning.” She looked at Vesket, and at Riarhin. “Can you lift?”

“Yes,” said Riarhin.

“Then come and lift.” Osselin took the lantern pole from beside the door. She lit it from the hearth with a spill, carefully, and closed the little door of the lantern over the flame. “Posy, put them to bed. The field’s mine. The rest of the round’s yours.”

“The Coombe lane?”

“Closed. I’ve put the thread across it. Nobody up there tonight.”

Posy nodded. Osselin went out. Riarhin picked up Remorse and followed.

Vesket stood up, looked at the case by the hearth, looked at Posy.

“I’ll watch it,” Posy said. “Go on.”
