Outside, the green was lit with a ring of little lanterns, each a warm point against the dusk. Osselin was already walking up the lane towards the Coombe end with her own lantern swinging from its pole. Riarhin caught her up. Behind them, Vesket came hurrying, his coat flapping.

Osselin did not look round.

“Count,” she said.

Riarhin counted. Eight steps. A pause. Eight steps. Osselin’s lips moved beside her, silently, keeping the same measure. At the first post she lifted her lantern and touched its flame very briefly to the flame of the post lantern, as one might touch a hand in passing, and walked on.

Behind them, in the long red-doored house, Riarhin could hear the children being put to bed, and one small voice protesting, and Posy’s voice, firm and kind, beginning a different song.

Chapter 7 — The field left alone

In daylight the Hollow Field looked like nothing at all.

It lay at the head of the Coombe, a shallow bowl of grass under the ridge, ringed by a hedge that had been let grow wild. Below it ran the Coombe lane, sunk between banks, with a stile into the field at the top end and a gate at the bottom. Beyond the field, the land rose to the down and the line of the forest, still a long way off, blue in the morning.

The grass inside the hedge was taller than the grass outside it and a slightly different colour, greyer, as though it had been dusted with ash. Nobody had cut it. Nobody grazed it. Riarhin could see where the Brookside cows had stopped on the far side of the hurdles they had moved the night before, the grass bitten short right up to the line and not a hand’s breadth past.

“It used to be smaller,” Osselin said.

She stood on the bank above the lane with her arms folded. She had washed and changed and her hair still stood up in its crest. She had slept perhaps three hours; so had Riarhin. They had moved forty hurdles by lantern light, Osselin calling the line, Riarhin and Vesket and two of the Brookside men lifting and setting, and the whole time the field had lain silent and grey on their left, and nobody had looked at it longer than they had to.

“How much smaller?”

“There’s a stone.” Osselin pointed. Inside the hedge, three paces in, a squat grey boundary stone stood half-hidden in the long grass. “That was the edge, when I took the lanes. Eleven years. It didn’t move in eleven years. My mother said it hadn’t moved in her time either. Then in the spring Hodge Brookside put his plough to the field below and the line came up crooked.” She pointed again, down the slope, to the field below the hedge where the furrows of last spring’s ploughing still showed under the stubble. Riarhin looked, and saw it: the furrows ran straight and even down the field until they came within a few yards of the hedge, and there they bent, all of them together, curving away from the Hollow Field as though something had pushed them aside.

“He thought it was his ox,” Osselin said. “Ploughed it again. Same thing. The ground won’t take a straight line near it now. He’s lost a strip the width of a cart, all along. And the path.” She nodded at the lane. “The path used to go round the field on the high side. That’s the way the children go to the down, and the shepherds. You can’t now. It’s inside.”

Vesket had gone down into the lane and was crouched by the stile with his case open on the ground beside him and the rods out on their felt. He had not struck them yet. He was looking at the stile.

“When did the singing start?” Riarhin asked.

Osselin did not answer at once.

“Midsummer,” she said. “Or that’s when I heard it. Hodge’s Tam had it first, they say. He says he heard it from the field. He says there was somebody singing in there one evening when he was bringing the cows down, and he stood by the hedge and listened, and then he had it.” She unfolded her arms and folded them again the other way. “He’s nine. He says a lot of things. But the song’s old. My grandmother knew it. She sang it to me once when I was very small and then my mother found out and there was a row. I’d forgotten it. Then I heard Tam singing it on the green and I knew every word.”

“What is it?”

“A song.” Osselin looked at her. “There’s a barrow in it. A door that was open. People going through who shouldn’t have. That’s all I know. Nobody sings it. Nobody’s sung it in my lifetime, until this summer.”

“The children say nobody taught them.”

“The children are right.” Osselin’s face was hard. “Nobody did. It came up out of that field like the grass.”

Below them, Vesket struck the rods.

The thin wound sound came up out of the lane. Riarhin counted the throb. Two beats in a breath. Then, as the notes faded, something else: a third note, very faint, lower than the others, that was not coming from the rods. It came from the field. It was so faint that she was not certain she had heard it until she saw Osselin’s head turn.

The note faded with the others.

Vesket stayed crouched, very still.

“Did you hear that?” he called up, not loudly.

“Yes,” said Osselin.

He stood up and looked at the field over the stile, and Riarhin saw on his face an interest so strong it was almost hunger, and under it something that might have been fear.

“Don’t do it again,” said Osselin.

“I need to—”

“You don’t need anything. Don’t do it again.”

He looked up at her. They looked at each other for a moment.

He put the hammer back in his coat.

There was a shout from further up the lane.

A man was coming down from the top end, from the down side, running, which in the sunken lane meant stumbling, one hand on the bank. He was large and red-faced and in his shirtsleeves, and he was shouting a name.

“Hodge,” said Osselin, and was off the bank and into the lane before Riarhin had seen her move.

Riarhin followed. Hodge Brookside reached them at the stile, gasping.

“Tam,” he said. “Tam’s in it.”

“Where?”

He pointed. Riarhin looked over the stile and saw nothing but grey grass. Then she saw a movement, perhaps sixty paces in, towards the middle of the bowl: a small dark head, and the grass swaying round it.

“He went up for the cows,” Hodge said. “I told him the top way. I told him. He went over the hurdles.”

“Is he singing?” said Osselin.

Hodge stared at her. Then they all heard it, high and thin and careless across the grass:

Eight to the stile and one to the gate—

Osselin’s face went white.

“Don’t,” she said to Hodge, who had put a foot on the stile. “Don’t. You’ll go in after him and you’ll go wherever he’s going.”

“He’s my boy.”

“I know he is.” She had him by the shirt. She was half his size. “Listen to me. I went in last night after the Brookside boy and I got him out. I know the way. There’s one way. It’s narrow. If you go in heavy and fast you’ll miss it.”

“Then go.”

Osselin let go of his shirt. She looked over the stile at the small head in the grass, and then at her own feet.

“I went in last night,” Osselin said, “and I can’t eat this morning. I can’t eat. If I go in twice—” She stopped.

“I’ll go,” said Riarhin.

Osselin turned on her.

“You don’t know the way.”

“Show me.”

“I can’t show you. It isn’t a path. It’s a count.” Osselin’s hands were shaking. “It’s eight and a turn. Eight and a turn. Left foot first, always, and the turn is towards the stone. Never away. If you lose the count you stop, you don’t guess. You stand still and you say the last number aloud and you start again from it. Never the ninth. Never say the ninth. If you hear yourself about to, stop.”

“Towards the stone.”

“Towards the stone. That stone.” Osselin pointed at the boundary marker in the grass. “Every turn, you turn so that you’re facing more towards it than you were. It doesn’t look like it makes sense. Don’t make it make sense.”

“And coming out?”

“The same. Back. With him.” Osselin looked at her. “He won’t want to come. He’ll want to finish the song.”

Riarhin unbuckled Remorse and handed it, sheathed, to Vesket. He took it in both hands as though it were the copper case. Then she put her hands on the stile.

“Left foot,” said Osselin.

Riarhin climbed the stile and came down on her left foot.

The grass closed round her at once, up to her thighs. It was cooler than the air. It smelled of nothing, which was strange: grass in summer smells of something, of earth and sap and heat, and this smelled of nothing at all, like a room that has been shut up.

She counted. One. Two. Three. She made each step deliberate, heel first, the way Ghere had made her walk on a bad floor, feeling for the ground before she gave it her weight. The ground was there. It was ordinary ground, a little soft. Five. Six. Seven. Eight.

She turned towards the stone.

It was a quarter turn, no more. She had expected the field to change, and it did not. The same grey grass, the same smell of nothing. But the boy’s head was further away than it had been.

She counted again. On the fourth step she heard the song more clearly.

—and one to the field where the hollow folk wait.

Her body knew the tune again, the step that came down a little lower than you expected, the cold feet. She almost missed the count. She stood still.

“Four,” she said aloud.

Behind her, Osselin’s voice: “Four. Go on.”

Five. Six. Seven. Eight. Turn. The stone was nearer. The boy was nearer. The grass whispered against her legs. She was aware that she did not know how many turns there would be, and that the not knowing was a kind of number too, sitting at the edge of her count, waiting for her to say it.

She did not say it.

On the sixth turn she came round and the boy was there, three paces away, standing in a little clearing where the grass had been trodden flat. He was nine, thin and brown, with his father’s red face, and he was walking in a slow circle round the edge of the clearing, singing.

Under the hill and under the stone—

He was walking towards the middle. Each circle was a little smaller than the last.

“Tam,” said Riarhin.

He did not look round.

—the door was open and nobody—

She stepped into the clearing on her left foot and took him by the shoulder.

He stopped singing. He looked up at her with a face of complete, incurious calm, the face of someone woken in the middle of a dream who has not yet understood that he is awake.

“I’m not finished,” he said.

“You are.”

“There’s more.”

“Not today.” She picked him up. He was light, all bones, and he did not struggle; he lay against her as though he had forgotten how to hold himself. Over his head she looked at the middle of the clearing. There was nothing there. Trodden grass. A little bare earth. In the bare earth, a dip, no bigger than a bowl, perfectly round, with sides so smooth they looked polished.

She did not look at it longer than that.

She turned round. Facing back the way she had come, the stone was behind her left shoulder. She could not turn towards it without turning away from the stile. She stood still with the boy in her arms and felt her heart begin to go fast and loud.

“Osselin,” she called.

“Here.”

“Which way is towards the stone, coming out?”

A pause.

“The same way,” Osselin called. “Turn towards it. Don’t think about where the stile is. Turn towards it.”

Riarhin turned towards the stone. It put the stile behind her. She counted eight steps in the direction that her body told her, quite clearly, was away from everything she wanted, and turned towards the stone again, and counted, and turned, and the boy hummed against her neck, very softly, the tune without the words, and she said, “Stop that,” and he stopped.

On the fifth turn she saw the stile in front of her.

On the eighth step after it she was at the hedge, and Hodge’s hands were reaching over for his son, and she gave him the boy and climbed the stile on her left foot and came down in the lane, and her knees nearly went.

Osselin caught her elbow.

“Sit,” she said.

Riarhin sat on the bank. She found that she was shaking and that she was very cold. Hodge was holding his son against his chest and saying his name over and over, and the boy had begun to cry, at last, in a thin bewildered wail, as though he had only now discovered where he had been.

Vesket was crouched by the stile with his rods out and his hammer in his hand.

He struck them.

Riarhin heard the two notes, and the slow throb between them, and the third note from the field, lower and fainter than before. Vesket listened with his eyes shut. Then he took out the vellum and unrolled it on his knee, and Tessel, whom he had let out of her basket onto the felt, began to draw.

Osselin looked at him with her mouth set hard. She did not tell him to stop.

“Now,” she said to Riarhin, “you won’t want to eat for a day or two. It goes off.”

“Does it?”

“It went off for me last time. My mother said it went off for her.” She looked at the field. “Hodge.”

Hodge looked up.

“The path’s gone. You know that. The top way round’s inside now, and it’ll stay inside. I’m putting thread across the stile and the gate, and you’ll take your cows down the long way by the barrow.”

“That’s two miles.”

“I know what it is.”

“And my strip? The field below? I’ve lost a cart’s width already.”

“You’ll lose more.”

Hodge stared at her over his son’s head. His face had gone from white to red again.

“You’re the warden,” he said. “You keep the lanes. That’s what you’re for. And you’re telling me—”

“I’m telling you I can’t fix it. I don’t know how. I don’t think anybody here knows how.” Osselin’s voice cracked and steadied. “I’m telling you that if Tam goes in there again I may not be able to go after him, and she may not be here.” She jerked her head at Riarhin. “So you’ll take the long way, and you’ll keep him off the Coombe, and you’ll thank the woman who carried him out.”

Hodge looked at Riarhin.

“Thank you,” he said thickly.

Then he turned and went down the lane with the boy in his arms, and did not look back.

Osselin watched him go. When he had turned the corner by the gate, she sat down on the bank beside Riarhin, suddenly, as though her legs had stopped holding her.

“He’ll be angry for a year,” she said.

“He has reason.”

“He has. He’s right.” She put her face in her hands. “He’s right. I keep the lanes. That’s what I’m for.”

Vesket rolled up the vellum. He sat back on his heels and looked at them both.

“It’s the same direction,” he said quietly.

Osselin lifted her head.

“The change. The beat from the field. It leans the same way as the way-cord, and the rods from Coombe Barrow. Tessel drew it.” He held up a hand, flat, and tilted it. “North and east. Towards the Gate, and past it. Towards the forest.” He looked at the field. “This isn’t yours, Ossie. It isn’t something you didn’t do. It’s something somebody else is doing, a long way from here, and this is where it comes out.”

Osselin looked at him for a long moment.

“Don’t call me that,” she said.

“I’m sorry.”

“You always do it when you want me to believe you.”

“I know. It doesn’t mean I’m wrong.”

She looked away, up at the ridge and the line of blue trees.

“Then I have to go,” she said.

Chapter 8 — Another person’s round

Osselin did her last round the next day, and Riarhin went with her.

It took from dawn until well after noon, and the posts were the least of it. At every house along the lanes somebody wanted something. The old man at the ford wanted his salve, which smelled of mint and goose fat. The weaver at the second stile had a letter that needed reading aloud to her, because her eyes were going, from a son who had gone to work in the dales; Osselin read it standing in the doorway while the weaver listened with her hands over her mouth. A family at the barrow turning had a child with a fever, and Osselin went in and sat by the bed for a quarter of an hour, and came out and told the mother to send to Posy, not to her, if it got worse in the night, and the mother looked at her as if she had been told the sun would rise somewhere else.

“Posy?”

“Posy’s doing the round from tomorrow.”

“For how long?”

“I don’t know.”

The mother did not ask where she was going. Riarhin watched her not ask. In the next house it was the same, and the next. They knew something was happening. They had heard about the Brookside boy and the field. Nobody asked Osselin to explain it. They asked about the lamps and the salve and who would carry the doctor’s message to the dales if the bridge was down, and Osselin told them, carefully, house by house, who would do what now, and the whole time she did not once say why.

Riarhin carried the basket. She did not ask either.

At the barrow there was a stile, old and grey, with eight steps cut into the bank beside it and a ninth that had been filled in with stones. Osselin climbed it counting. Riarhin climbed after her. At the top, Osselin stopped and looked down the lane they had come by, where the post lanterns stood cold in the sun.

“My mother did these for thirty years,” she said. “She did them till she couldn’t walk, and then she sat at the window and told me which ones I’d done wrong.”

“Had you?”

“Mostly.” Osselin almost smiled. “She was right more than I liked. She was right about Vesket.”

“What did she say?”

Osselin looked at her sideways.

“That he’d always want one more measurement,” she said, “and somebody else would always be carrying it.”

They went on.

He had stayed in the house that morning with the copper case and the papers, writing. When they came back for the noon meal he was still writing, at the end of the long table, with Tessel on the felt beside his elbow drawing a careful hexagon in the sunlight. He had used the whole morning to make three copies of a letter. One was to his hall in the hawthorn country. One was to a woman named Vesper at Thornholt. The third he did not explain.

“You’ve been feeding that spider at my table,” Osselin said.

“She eats very little.”

“She eats at my table.”

“She eats on her own felt. On your table.” He looked up. “I’m sorry. I’ll move her.”

“Leave her.” Osselin put the basket down. “She was here first today.”

Posy came in with her children and her sister’s children and the Brookside girl, who had been crying all morning and was now cheerful, and the house filled up. They ate bread and cheese and the last of the onion stew, cold. Posy asked about the fever at the barrow turning and the ford man’s salve, and Osselin told her. Then Posy put her bread down.

“I’ll do the lamps,” she said. “And the stiles and the ford, and I’ll go to the barrow if the child’s worse. I’ll carry for the doctor. I won’t do the field.”

Osselin looked at her.

“I can’t do the field,” Posy said. “I’ve got Wat. I can’t go in there. If somebody goes in, I’ll put thread on them so they can be found, and I’ll send to Hodge and the Brookside men, and I’ll send for you. But I won’t go in.”

“I didn’t ask you to.”

“You were going to.”

There was a pause. The children went on eating. Vesket looked at his papers.

“Yes,” said Osselin. “I was going to.”

“Well, I won’t.”

“All right.”

“And I won’t do Ivy’s gran on a Thursday. She talks for two hours and I can’t.”

Osselin laughed, suddenly and helplessly, and put her hand over her face. Posy watched her with her chin up.

“All right,” said Osselin through her fingers. “Not Thursdays.”

“And I want the good pole. The ash one. Not the one with the crack.”

“Take it.”

“Thank you.” Posy picked up her bread again. “Then that’s all right.”

In the afternoon there was a meeting on the green.

It was not called a meeting. Nobody summoned anybody. But by the middle of the afternoon most of the village was on the green, in ones and twos, doing things that could be done on a green: the carved stones that stood round its edge were being turned, slowly, one after another, until all of them faced inward towards the maypole; somebody was mending the stocks; a man was sharpening a scythe on the bench by the church wall. Osselin walked from group to group, and Riarhin, who had been told she could come or not as she liked and had come, listened for who would carry what.

The weaver’s son-in-law would take the dales letters. Hodge Brookside, red-faced and unforgiving, said he would keep the Coombe hurdles because they were his cows; he said it to the man with the scythe, not to Osselin. The miller, who had a flock of geese that followed him everywhere and hissed at Riarhin whenever she came near, said he would keep a key.

“What key?” said Riarhin.

“The church key,” Osselin said. “And the key to the lamp store. Somebody keeps them while the warden’s away. It’s usually the priest. The priest’s at the hay.”

“I’ll keep it,” said the miller.

“You lost it last time,” said a woman by the stones.

“I did not lose it. It was in my other coat.”

“For a month.”

“It was in my other coat. I knew where it was.”

“You didn’t know where your other coat was.”

They began to argue. The miller grinned before the first answer came. The geese hissed. The man with the scythe went on sharpening.

The church bell began to ring.

It was the hour. Riarhin counted without meaning to. Everyone on the green had stopped, she saw, to count too, or had not stopped but was counting all the same; she saw the miller’s lips moving. The bell was old and cracked and its voice came flat and heavy across the green. Eleven. Twelve.

Thirteen.

The miller stopped counting.

Nobody moved. The bell’s last note hung over the green and faded. The geese had gone quiet. The man with the scythe held the whetstone against the blade without drawing it.

Osselin turned and walked to the church.

Riarhin went after her. The church door was open. Inside it was cool and dim and smelled of old stone and beeswax. The bell rope hung down through a hole in the ceiling of the tower and was looped over a peg on the wall, as it should be. Osselin went to it and looked at it. She did not touch it. She looked at the loop on the peg, at the end of the rope, at the floor beneath it.

Then she went to the tower stair and climbed it.

Riarhin waited at the bottom. She heard Osselin’s feet on the wooden steps, slow, then a pause at the top, then the boards of the bell chamber creaking. A long silence. Then the feet coming down.

Osselin came out of the stair. Her face was very calm.

“The rope’s on its peg,” she said. “Nobody’s up there. The clapper’s still.”

“It rang thirteen.”

“I know.”

Riarhin waited.

“Sometimes it does that,” Osselin said. “My mother heard it twice. It’s an old bell.” She looked at the rope on its peg, and then at Riarhin. “There might be a reason. A boy in the tower who got down before I got up. The frame shifting in the heat. I don’t know. I looked. That’s what you do.”

“And then?”

“Then you go back and finish what you were doing.” Osselin walked past her to the door. “That’s what you do too.”

On the green, the argument had begun again. It was quieter. The woman by the stones said the miller could keep the key if he wore it round his neck. The miller said he would not be told how to keep a key by a woman who had once locked herself in her own privy. Somebody laughed. Osselin went to the miller and took the key from her belt, the church key and the lamp store key on a ring, and hung them round his neck herself, on a length of red thread, and tied it with a knot, and said, “There.”

The miller looked down at the thread. He touched it.

“There,” said the woman by the stones, satisfied.

The man with the scythe drew the whetstone along the blade.

That evening, after the lamps, a boy came from the coast road with a letter for Riarhin. It had been sent on from Brede’s yard at Hessel, Brede’s mark on the outside, and inside it, folded small, a note in Jenet Rudge’s neat docket hand.

A woman came to Petty’s for the two bundles. She had the twin of the token. Tall, old, a stiff leg. Petty told her as you said, that you had paid him to hold them and where you had gone. She asked how much you had paid. He told her. She thanked him and said that was fair, and asked after my boy. Then she went away. She did not come to our yard.

J. Rudge

Riarhin read it standing by the window in Osselin’s house, where the light of the green lanterns came through the glass and lay on the paper in small warm circles.

Osselin was packing. She had a bag on the table and was putting things into it with great precision: a spare lantern, wrapped in felt; a pot of the mint salve; a coil of red thread; a little loaf, round and dark, with a cross cut in the top. Vesket sat by the hearth with the copper case open, checking his coals.

“What is it?” Osselin said, without looking up.

Riarhin gave her the letter. Osselin read it and gave it to Vesket. He read it twice.

“She asked how much you paid,” he said.

“Yes.”

“Why would she ask that?”

“So she’d know.”

He looked at her.

“She likes to know what people are willing to give,” Riarhin said. She took the letter back and folded it. “She knows I’ve stopped her having them, and where I’ve gone, and that I paid Petty a month and two marks.” She heard her own voice, very level. “She’ll be thinking about what that means.”

“What does it mean?”

Riarhin did not answer at once. Outside, the green lanterns burned in their ring. Somebody went past the window counting, eight steps and a pause, eight steps and a pause.

“That I’m coming,” she said.

Osselin put the little loaf into the bag and tied it shut.

“Good,” she said. “Then we needn’t wonder.”

Part II — The neighbouring roads

Chapter 9 — Across the slow water

The Slow Water was a mile wide where the ferry crossed it, brown and flat and sliding, with reed-beds on the Amedell side and on the far bank a long low cliff of red stone with hawthorn growing out of its cracks. It took three days to reach it from Wendle. For most of those three days Osselin talked about Vesket.

She did it as though he were not there. He walked beside them with his pack and his case and his basket and listened with an expression of mild suffering.

“He’ll tell you he travels light,” Osselin said on the first morning. “He doesn’t. He travels with everything he owns that might be useful and three things that won’t, and then he buys a fourth at the first market because it’s very interesting.”

“That isn’t true.”

“The bell from Thornholt.”

“The bell was a gift.”

“You carried it for six weeks and you’ve never rung it.”

“It isn’t for ringing.”

“And he doesn’t eat,” Osselin went on. “He forgets. He sits down to do a measurement at noon and looks up at dusk and asks why it’s dark. If you travel with him you carry his food and you put it in front of him and you stand there until it’s gone.”

“I eat.”

“You ate a candle once.”

“It was a cheese. It looked like a candle.” He turned to Riarhin with great dignity. “It was dark and it was a very pale cheese.”

On the second day Riarhin asked about provisions. Osselin had it all in her head: what they had, what the copper case needed every day in charcoal and how much charcoal weighed. Riarhin would carry the charcoal, because she was tallest and could take a frame; Vesket would carry the case, because nobody else was allowed to; and Osselin would carry the food, because otherwise it would be eaten in the wrong order.

“What’s the wrong order?”

“His order. Bread first, then whatever’s nicest, then nothing for three days.”

Vesket said, “Once.”

“Twice. Once at Coombe and once on the Kelling road, and both times I ended up carrying you.”

“You didn’t carry me on the Kelling road.”

“I carried your pack and your case and held you up by the coat.” Osselin looked at him sideways. “And you talked about tessellations the whole way and told me I was walking wrong.”

“You were walking wrong. You put your heel down first on a slope.”

“I still do.”

“I know. I’ve stopped telling you.” He walked on a few steps. “It’s the only thing I’ve ever stopped telling you.”

Osselin laughed. It was a short laugh, unwilling, and it went on a little longer than she seemed to want it to. Riarhin walked between them and carried the charcoal, and felt, briefly and absurdly, that she was the one who had been brought along to watch.

They came down to the ferry on the third afternoon, later than Vesket had hoped. A tree had come down across the lane above the reed-beds and they had had to go round by the dyke. The ferry was on the far side. There was a bell on a post at the landing, a big iron bell with a rope, and a bench beside it, and on the bench sat a woman with three children and a basket of eggs, and an old man with a goat on a string.

“It’s gone over with the school,” said the woman. “It’ll be back.”

“The school?”

“The little ones go over to the hall at Briar’s for their letters, three mornings a week. And back in the afternoon. This is the afternoon.” She looked at Riarhin, then at Vesket. “You’re for the spring-house?”

“Yes.”

“So am I. So’s he.” She nodded at the old man with the goat. “He’s going to his daughter’s. I’m going to my mother’s. There’s room for eight and the goat counts as one, so if there’s any more of you somebody’ll wait.”

“There’s three,” said Osselin.

“Then you’ll go.” The woman settled her basket. “Unless the school brings anybody back.”

They sat down to wait. The bench was not long enough, so Riarhin sat on the ground with her back to the bell post. Osselin sat beside the woman with the eggs and in a few minutes they had discovered they had a cousin in common, a woman at Kelling who kept bees and had once been bitten in the face by a horse, and were deep in her history. Vesket set the copper case on the ground at his feet, opened its lid an inch, and looked at the coals with a frown.

“They’re low,” he said.

“We’ll be across in an hour,” Riarhin said.

“It’s been low since the dyke. I didn’t want to stop and feed it in the wind.” He glanced at the water. “It wants to be level on the crossing, too. The rods don’t like being tipped when they’re warm.”

The old man with the goat said, “Boat’s level enough. It’s a flat one.”

“Thank you.”

“Unless there’s a wind.”

Vesket looked at the sky. There was no wind. The river slid past, smooth and brown, and on the far side a dark shape was putting out from the foot of the red cliff, slow and square, with a figure standing at one end of it working a long sweep.

“That’s Fennel,” said the woman with the eggs. “Briar’s girl.”

It was a broad flat-bottomed barge with low sides and a rail, and it came over crabwise, the current carrying it down and the sweep bringing it back. As it came closer Riarhin could see the woman at the sweep: tall for one of the hawthorn people, thin and brown with her sleeves rolled up and her hair tied back in a cloth, leaning her whole weight on the oar and then walking it back along the stern, again and again, in a rhythm that never broke.

She brought the barge in to the landing with a scrape and a bump and jumped onto the bank with the rope and made it fast to a post in one movement. There was nobody else aboard.

“Afternoon,” she said, out of breath. “That’s eight? And the goat?” She counted them with her eyes. “Seven and the goat. Good. Get on. Not you, goat, you go in the middle.”

The goat went in the middle. The woman with the eggs got on, and her children, and the old man, and Osselin, who took the egg basket so that the woman could lift the youngest child over the rail. Vesket picked up the copper case and stepped down into the barge with great care, holding the case in front of him level as a tray.

Fennel watched him.

“What’s that?”

“A heater.”

“Is it alight?”

“A little.”

She looked at it with deep suspicion. “Not near the goat.”

“I’ll stand at the end.”

“Stand in the middle, where it doesn’t tip.” She jerked her head. “And if it sets fire to my boat I’ll put you in the river with it.”

He stood in the middle. Riarhin got on last with the charcoal frame and her pack and Remorse on her back, and Fennel cast off and pushed the barge out from the bank with a pole, and then walked to the stern and took up the sweep.

“You could help,” she said to Riarhin, “if you’ve the arms for it.”

Riarhin went to the stern. There was a second sweep lying along the rail. Fennel showed her how to take it: hands apart on the loom, lean, walk it back, lift, return. “Not too deep. If you dig it in she’ll swing.” Riarhin dug it in and the barge swung. The goat bleated. Fennel laughed at her, not unkindly, and showed her again, and the second time it went better.

They worked the sweeps together. Riarhin found the rhythm and let her body have it, and the barge crabbed out into the brown water, and the reed-beds fell away behind them. It was hard work and pleasant. The sun was warm on her back. She could feel Fennel beside her adjusting to her stroke without seeming to think about it.

“Who’s she?” Fennel asked, nodding at Osselin in the bow.

“A lantern-warden. From Wendle.”

“I know Wendle. I meant why’s she coming to my mother’s.”

“She’s travelling with him.”

“And who’s he?”

“A Sumwright. From the hills.”

Fennel looked at Vesket standing in the middle of the barge with the case held level and his eyes on the coals.

“He looks like one,” she said. She leaned on the sweep. “And you?”

“I’m carrying the charcoal.”

Fennel laughed again. She did not ask anything else.

In the bow, Osselin and the woman with the eggs had moved on from the cousin at Kelling to the matter of guest-right, and were disagreeing about it. The woman with the eggs had a daughter married on the far bank, in the hawthorn country, and the daughter’s husband’s family did guest-right the new way, the way that had come down from the halls in the hills in the last few years: you were offered salt on the threshold and you had to refuse it twice before you took it.

“Twice,” said Osselin. “Why twice?”

“To show you’re not greedy.”

“Nobody thinks you’re greedy for taking salt.”

“Well, they do up there now. My daughter says if you take it the first time they look at you.”

“Then they’re looking at the wrong thing.” Osselin settled the egg basket on her knee. “You take bread and you take light. If they offer you salt as well, you take it because they offered. You don’t make them offer three times. Making somebody offer three times isn’t courtesy. It’s a test. You don’t test people at your own door.”

“That’s what I told her,” said the woman with the eggs, triumphant. “That’s exactly what I told her.”

Fennel said under her breath to Riarhin, “They’ve had that argument every week since midsummer. Different people. Same argument.”

“Who wins?”

“Nobody. Then they get off.”

Halfway across, Riarhin could see a path going up the face of the red cliff in zigzags to the top, where there was a long low house with a turf roof and a column of smoke. Further along the cliff, upriver, there were boats moored at a stone quay, and among them, larger than the rest, a high-prowed ship with a mast and a furled brown sail.

“Linn,” said the old man with the goat, following her look. “Northern boat. Come down for salt and wool and going back before the gales. There’s been three this month.”

“Going back where?”

“North.” He shrugged. “Up the coast and round. They go up the Thin Shore past the forest and on up to wherever they come from. My daughter sold them eggs. They paid in amber.” He seemed to find this both admirable and suspicious. “They don’t like to be late. They say after the equinox the coast up there gets bad and they don’t stop for anything.”

“They stop for water,” said Fennel. “They have to. There’s places they know.”

“They stop for water,” the old man agreed. “And nothing else. My daughter tried to sell them a pig.”

Riarhin looked at the ship. It sat low and heavy at the quay. Men were carrying sacks aboard along a plank. One of them was singing something; she could hear it faintly across the water, a rough, cheerful line repeated and repeated.

Then the barge lurched.

A swirl had come up under them out of the smooth brown water, and the bow swung hard round to the left and the deck tipped. The goat lost its feet and fell against the old man. One of the children screamed. Riarhin and Fennel both leaned on their sweeps at the same moment without a word and brought the stern round, and the barge steadied, rocking.

In the middle, Vesket had gone down on one knee to keep the case level. He had managed it. But the lid had jolted open, and as the deck tipped, a little stream of red coals had spilled out of the grate through the copper screen and onto the boards.

They hissed on the wet planks.

“Fire,” said the old man.

“It’s not fire,” said Vesket, and swept the coals with his bare hand off the edge of the barge into the river.

He did it very fast, three sweeps. The coals went into the water with a sound like a breath. Then he sat back on his heels and looked at his hand.

Osselin was already beside him. She took his wrist and turned the hand over. The palm was red, and on the heel of it there was a white mark where a coal had stuck.

“Fool,” she said.

“They were on the boat.”

“There’s a bucket.”

“I didn’t see a bucket.”

“There’s always a bucket.” She looked at Fennel. “Is there a bucket?”

“By the rail,” said Fennel, “for the goat.”

Osselin dipped the bucket over the side and put his hand in it. He hissed, and then let it stay. The children had stopped screaming and were watching with great interest. The goat had got up and was eating the woman’s egg basket.

“The coals,” Vesket said.

Riarhin looked into the case. The grate was empty except for a single coal, smouldering, wedged against the screen. The wool round the rods was scorched at one edge. The rods themselves lay in their hollows untouched.

“There’s one left.”

“Feed it. Please. The charcoal, in the side pocket of your frame, the small pieces, not the large. Not too many at once.”

She knelt on the wet boards and laid small pieces of charcoal one by one against the last coal, and blew, very gently, and watched them catch. It took a long time. Fennel brought the barge on alone. Nobody spoke except the old man, who said several times that he had never seen the river do that before in sixty years, and then corrected it to fifty, and then to forty.

“It does it,” said Fennel shortly. “Lately. Not before.”

“Since when?”

“Spring.”

Riarhin looked up. Vesket, with his hand in the bucket, had turned his head.

“Where?” he said.

“Just there. Over the old channel.” Fennel did not look at him. She was watching the landing come towards them. “Mother says it’s the spring. Says the spring’s been different since the spring.” She almost smiled at her own words. “That’s how she says it.”

The coals had caught. Riarhin closed the screen and lowered the lid. Vesket took his hand out of the bucket and held it against his chest.

“One of the inks,” he said. “At the back. The little blue pot. Is it all right?”

She looked. The little blue pot had cracked, and something dark had soaked into the wool around it.

“No.”

He shut his eyes briefly.

“It doesn’t matter,” he said. “It matters. I’ll make it again.”

They came in to the landing below the red cliff in the last of the afternoon light. Fennel made fast and the passengers got off: the woman with the eggs, scolding the goat; the old man, still telling everybody about the swirl; the children, running up the path. Fennel stood on the landing with the rope in her hand and watched Vesket climb out of the barge with the case held level and his burned hand tucked against his coat.

“You’ll want my mother,” she said. “She’s at the top. Tell her I said you burned yourself on my boat and she’ll give you something for it.”

“Thank you.”

“And tell her the old channel swirled again.”

Vesket nodded. Fennel looked at Riarhin.

“You row well enough,” she said. “For a big one. If you want work, I need somebody at the sweep in the mornings. The school’s heavy.”

“I’m not staying.”

“Nobody is.” Fennel turned back to the barge. “That’s what I keep telling her.”

They climbed the zigzag path up the face of the cliff. It was steep and turned eight times, and at every turn somebody had set a flat stone into the path like a step. Osselin counted the turns aloud. At the top, in front of the house, was a garden full of beans and marigolds, and beyond it the hawthorn country went away north in ridges, grey-green and stony, towards a line of hills.

From the top of the path Riarhin could see the Linn ship again, upriver at the quay. The men had stopped loading. The brown sail was still furled. Somebody on the deck was lighting a lamp.

Chapter 10 — The familiar room

Briar Tidespeak was a small, square woman with grey hair cut short like Osselin’s and an apron so stiff with flour it stood away from her body. She came out of the house as they reached the gate, saw Osselin, and stopped.

“Well,” she said. “Look what the river brought.”

“Briar.”

They embraced. Briar was even shorter than Osselin and had to reach up. Then she held her at arm’s length and looked at her.

“You look terrible.”

“I’ve been walking for three days.”

“You looked terrible when you got here last time and you’d come in a cart.” Briar turned to Vesket. “And you. I wondered when you’d come. Your letters have been very dull.”

“They’re not meant to be interesting.”

“They aren’t.” She saw his hand. “What have you done?”

“Your daughter said to tell you I burned myself on her boat.”

“Did you set fire to it?”

“No.”

“Then come in. I’ll see to it.” She looked at Riarhin. “And you’re the one with the grandmother.”

Riarhin stopped.

“He wrote,” said Briar. “He writes everything. Come in.”

The spring-house was one long building of red stone with a turf roof and a door at each end. Inside it was dim and warm and smelled of bread and woodsmoke and, faintly, under everything, of wet stone. There was a hearth in the middle of the long room with a hole in the roof above it, and benches and a great scrubbed table, and at the far end, in a chair by the window where the light fell, an old man with a rug over his knees.

“That’s Linden,” said Briar. “My husband’s father. He doesn’t get up.”

“I get up,” said the old man.

“He gets up when he wants to and not when I want him to.” Briar went to the hearth. “There’s a room at the back for you, and the loft, and the bench. Put that box down where it won’t burn anything.”

Vesket put the box down. Riarhin put down the charcoal frame, which had grown heavier with every turn of the cliff path. Osselin put the food bag on the table and began at once to take things out of it, as though she lived there.

Briar dressed Vesket’s hand with something from a crock that smelled of honey and comfrey and bound it with a strip of linen. While she did it she asked him questions about the swirl, and about his rods, and about why he had not written in the spring when the beat through her cellar first changed, and he answered all of them except the last.

“I did write,” he said.

“You wrote that there was a variation and you’d keep an eye on it.”

“That’s what there was.”

“There was more than that. The spring knew.” She tied the linen. “Linden knew.”

“I knew,” said Linden from the window.

Briar looked at Riarhin. “He says the water’s talking different. He’s been saying it since Lady Day.”

“What does it say?” Riarhin asked.

The old man turned his head and looked at her. He had very pale eyes, filmed a little at the edges, and his face was all lines.

“It doesn’t say anything,” he said. “It’s water. It used to sound like it was going somewhere. Now it sounds like it’s waiting to.”

There was a silence. Briar patted Vesket’s hand, hard, so that he winced.

“There,” she said. “Don’t knock it.”

Fennel came in at dusk, after the last crossing, smelling of river and sweat, and the household sat down to supper. There were more of them than Riarhin had counted. Briar and Linden and Fennel; a young man who was Fennel’s cousin and helped with the ferry; a girl of twelve who was somebody’s child and seemed to belong to everybody; and a travelling trader who had come over on the morning ferry with a pack of needles and ribbons and was staying the night. Osselin sat between Briar and Linden. Vesket sat by the hearth where he could see the case.

The food was a pie of river fish and leeks, and new bread, and a bowl of the little sour yellow plums that grew along the cliff. Riarhin had not been hungry since the field. She found, to her surprise, that she was now.

Fennel and her mother argued about the ferry.

“I need two at the sweep in the mornings,” Fennel said. “The school’s eleven now. Eleven and their bags. Kit can’t do it every day.”

“Kit can do it.”

“Kit’s got his own work.” Fennel’s cousin, Kit, said nothing and went on eating. “And the market boats on Thursdays. And the Linn want taking over to the quay twice a day while they’re loading because they won’t walk round by the bridge.”

“They pay.”

“They pay well. That’s not the point. The point is I need two at the sweep.”

“Then find somebody.”

“I’ve asked everybody. Everybody says they’ll come after the harvest.” Fennel looked down the table at Riarhin. “I asked her.”

The trader cleared his throat. He was a cheerful, round man with a little pointed beard and a habit of tapping the table when he spoke.

“I’ll settle my bed for tonight, mistress,” he said to Briar, “if you’ll take it in something besides silver. I’ve a route. Over the hills to Thornholt by the old drove, saves a day and a half, nobody uses it. I’ll give you every turn of it.”

Briar looked at him.

“What do I want with a route to Thornholt?”

“It’s a good route.”

“I don’t go to Thornholt. I go to the ferry and back. You’ll pay me in silver.”

“Mistress—”

“Silver,” said Briar. “So I can give it to her.” She nodded at Fennel. “She can pay Kit with it. Kit can’t pay his mother in a route to Thornholt.”

The trader looked round the table for sympathy and found none. He paid in silver. Briar put the coins at Fennel’s elbow without looking at them. Fennel put them in her pocket.

“I’ll have the route anyway,” said Vesket. “If you’re giving it.”

The trader brightened. “For a penny.”

“For a penny.”

They did it across the table, the trader tracing the drove road on the board with a wet finger, and Vesket watching with his chin on his good hand, and Osselin rolling her eyes. Briar said, to nobody, “There he goes,” and Linden laughed by the window, a dry rattle.

Afterwards Kit told a tale.

He had been drinking from the spring, Fennel said. He did it before supper every night, though his mother had told him not to, because it made him good at telling things, and he liked being good at something. The tale was an old one, everybody at the table seemed to know it: a woman who kept a crossing and was asked by a stranger to let him through without counting, and how she tricked him into counting anyway by asking him to help her with her stockings. It was not a very good tale, Riarhin thought, until Kit told it. He told it quietly, without any flourishes, and every part of it went exactly where it should, one thing after another, so that when the stranger finally bent and counted the stocking stitches aloud and the crossing closed behind him, the whole table laughed at once, and Riarhin laughed with them and did not know why it had been so satisfying.

“That’s the spring,” said Fennel, half proud and half disgusted. “He can’t tell a tale like that when he hasn’t had it.”

“I can,” said Kit.

“You can’t. You tell it all wrong. You forget the stockings till the end.”

Osselin said, “The stranger didn’t have a stick.”

Kit looked at her. “What?”

“In the tale. You gave him a stick with a silver head. He didn’t have a stick. My grandmother told it. He had a hat.”

“He had a stick.”

“He had a hat with a feather. You only gave him a stick because it sounds better.”

Kit looked at her a moment. Then he grinned, slow and pleased, and did not argue. Briar said, “She’s right, he had a hat,” and Linden said from the window, “It was a stick,” and they began to argue about it, and the evening went on.

The game came out after the plates were cleared.

It was a board of dark wood with an eight-pointed figure inlaid in pale lines, and pieces of carved bone, some white, some stained brown, kept in a leather bag. Briar set it on the table without asking anybody and looked at Vesket.

“You owe me a game,” she said.

“I don’t.”

“You left in the middle of one. Two years ago. You said you’d come back and finish it and I’ve kept the pieces where they were.”

Vesket stared at her. “You haven’t.”

Briar opened a box under the window. Inside it was a second board, set out with pieces in the middle of play, a thin skin of dust over the whole.

“Linden wouldn’t let anybody touch it,” she said. “I was winning.”

“You weren’t winning.”

“I was going to.”

Vesket got up and went to the box and looked down at the board. His face changed. Riarhin had seen the look before, at the stile above the Hollow Field.

“Oh,” he said softly. “Oh, I’d forgotten. You put the white Apex there.”

“I did.”

“That was a terrible move.”

“It was a very good move. You went away and thought about it for two years and you know it was.”

He sat down on the floor beside the box with his burned hand in his lap. He did not touch the pieces. He looked at them for a long time while the household went on round him, Fennel washing pots and Kit mending a net and the girl of twelve falling asleep on the bench. Osselin sat at the table and watched him.

“He’ll be there till midnight,” she said to Riarhin.

“Does he win?”

“Sometimes. Against her? Never.” Osselin smiled very slightly. “He lost a wager to me on that board once. At Kelling. He said a piece couldn’t be taken from the Cross by a piece coming from the Sanctum side, and I said it could, and he bet me a month of carrying the food.”

“And?”

“And it could.” Osselin turned her cup in her hands. “He carried the food for a month. He ate it in the wrong order the whole time.”

At last Vesket reached out and moved a piece. A small brown one, from the edge of the board to a point near the middle. Briar, who had been pretending not to watch, came across the room and looked down.

She was silent a long moment.

“That’s—” she said.

“Yes.”

“You thought of that in two years?”

“I thought of it just now.” He looked up at her with a face of pure delight. “Look. It closes the Cross from both sides. You can take it, but if you take it—”

“I lose the Apex.”

“You lose the Apex.”

Briar looked at the board. She looked at him. She sat down on the floor beside him, slowly, in her floury apron, and put her chin in her hands.

“Well,” she said. “I’ll think about it.”

“You’ll think for two years.”

“I’ll think till morning.” She did not move. “Go to bed. All of you. I’m thinking.”

Riarhin slept in the loft above the long room, on a pallet of straw under the turf, with the warmth of the hearth coming up through the boards and the sound of the household settling below. Osselin had the room at the back. Vesket had the bench by the hearth, with the copper case beside him, and by the time Riarhin lay down he was asleep on it with his burned hand on his chest and Briar still sitting on the floor by the board in the firelight, looking at it.

She woke in the dark.

She lay still. The fire had sunk. Below her, somebody was breathing slowly on the bench. The house ticked and creaked as old houses do.

Then a sound came: a knock, low and soft, as of a knuckle on wood. Three knocks. A pause. Three more.

It came from the far end of the long room, from the wall beyond Linden’s chair. It came from outside.

Riarhin did not move. She did not know this house, but she knew where its doors were, and the sound had not come from a door.

Below her, somebody got up.

She rolled to the edge of the loft and looked down. In the last red light of the embers she saw Osselin, barefoot, in her shirt, crossing the long room with a lantern unlit in her hand. Osselin went to the hearth, knelt, and lit the lantern from the embers with a spill, carefully, shielding the flame. Then she walked to the far wall and stood there.

She waited.

The knock came again. Three, and three.

Osselin lifted the lantern so that its light fell on the wall. She said something under her breath, a phrase Riarhin did not catch, and then she knocked on the wall herself, three times, with her knuckle, and waited.

Nothing answered.

Osselin stood with the lantern raised. The light shook a little. She knocked again, three times.

Nothing.

From the bench by the hearth, Vesket said in a low voice, “Ossie.”

Osselin did not turn round.

“It should answer,” she said. “It always answers. My mother did this here when I was a child. Briar’s done it every year since. It knocks, and you knock back, and it answers, and it goes away.” Her voice was very steady. “It’s on the wrong side. It should come from the cellar. It came from outside.”

The knock came again. This time it came from the cellar, from under the floor at the far end of the room, three knocks, slow.

Osselin turned towards it. Her lantern swung. The light ran over the floor and up the side of Linden’s chair and over his face, and Riarhin saw that the old man was awake. He was sitting very still under his rug with his pale eyes open, looking at the floor.

“It doesn’t answer,” he said. “It’s asking.”

Chapter 11 — The guest below

Briar would not have the cellar closed.

“It’s been open since my husband’s great-grandmother,” she said. “It’s been open through two floods and the year the cliff came down. People come up that river to use it. They come from the dales and they come from the coast. If I close it they go round by the headland and that’s two days, and half of them won’t go at all.”

It was the grey hour before dawn. Nobody had gone back to sleep. Briar had come out at the first knock with a shawl over her nightdress and had knocked back herself, as she had done every autumn for thirty years, and nothing had answered her either.

Now the knocking had stopped. The household sat round the hearth, which Fennel had built up again, and drank hot water with honey in it because nobody wanted to make anything stronger.

“I don’t want it closed,” Briar said again. “I want it quiet.”

“So do I,” said Osselin.

“Then make it quiet.”

“I don’t know how.”

“You knocked.”

“I knocked the way we knock. It didn’t answer.” Osselin held her cup in both hands. Her face in the firelight was drawn. “I’m not going to knock again. Not the whole of it. There’s a part of it you don’t do twice in a night.”

Briar looked at her. She did not ask which part. Riarhin watched her not ask.

“Then what?”

Vesket had been sitting very still on the bench with his bandaged hand in his lap. He stirred.

“Let me look first,” he said. “Before anybody does anything. Let me see whether something’s changed down there that I can see.”

“Like what?”

“The water. The draught. Whether the stone’s wet where it used to be dry. Something small.” He looked at Briar. “If something small has changed, then perhaps something small will mend it.”

Briar put down her cup.

“The light’s nearly up,” she said. “It doesn’t knock in daylight. Go on.”

The cellar was reached by a trapdoor at the far end of the long room, under a rag rug, beside Linden’s chair. Fennel and Kit lifted it. Below, a stair went down, stone steps worn into hollows, eight of them, and a ninth, a sill, a single long slab set across the bottom like a threshold.

Vesket went down first with a lantern. Riarhin went after him. Osselin came last and stopped on the eighth step and stayed there.

The cellar was larger than the house above it. It had been dug, or found, in the red rock of the cliff, and its walls sweated a little in the lantern light. The air was cool and smelled of water and earth and something older and drier, like the inside of a stone jar. Along one wall stood the household’s barrels and crocks and sacks, ordinary as any cellar. At the far end the floor dropped away into a channel where water ran, fast and clear, out of a crack in the rock and away into darkness under a low arch, and the water sparkled as it went, as though there were sand in it that caught the light.

“The spring,” Vesket said.

In the middle of the floor stood a stone table: two great uprights and a capstone laid across them, older than the cellar, older than the house, grey and lichened even here where no light came. On the capstone, in a row along its front edge, eight small studs of green-crusted copper had been set into the stone.

Beyond the stone table, in the back wall, was a low dark opening, just wide enough for one person bent double. The air came out of it cold.

“That’s the crossing,” said Osselin from the stair. “On the right night, you go in there polite and you come out above the tide-sheds. The rest of the year it’s a hole that goes in twenty feet and stops.”

Vesket set his lantern on the floor and crouched by the spring channel. He put his good hand in the water. He scooped a little sand from the bottom of the channel into his palm and held it to the light. The grains glittered, clear as glass. He tasted the water.

“It’s lower,” he said. “The flow. Not much. A finger’s breadth down the wall.” He pointed to a line on the rock above the water, a stain where the water had stood for years. “It used to run there.”

“Since when?” said Riarhin.

“Since Lady Day,” Osselin said. “Linden said.”

Vesket went to the stone table. He looked at the copper studs without touching them, then went round behind it and held his hand in front of the dark opening.

“It’s colder than it should be,” he said. “And it shouldn’t be blowing out. It should be still, this time of year. It’s an in-breath, the rest of the year.”

“It’s breathing out,” said Riarhin.

“Yes.”

He came back to the stone table and stood in front of it. Then he took out the rods.

Osselin said sharply from the stair, “Vesket.”

“Only once. Only to hear.”

She did not answer. He took the copper hammer from his coat and struck the rods.

The sound filled the cellar. The two thin notes, the throb between them, slower than at the Hollow Field, slower than at Hessel. And under it, from the stone table, from the eight copper studs along its edge, a faint ringing answer: not a note, a scatter of notes, all slightly wrong, like a handful of small bells shaken in a bag.

Then the knocking began.

It came from inside the stone table. Three knocks. A pause. Three more. Then it came from the opening behind it. Then from the wall above the spring. Then, very loud, from directly under Riarhin’s feet, so that she felt it through her soles.

Vesket stood with the rods in his hand and did not move.

The knocking stopped.

In the silence there was another sound. It came out of the dark opening on the cold air, very faint, so that Riarhin had to hold her breath to hear it: a murmuring, like many voices in a far room, all speaking at once, none of them raised. It was not words. It had the rhythm of words, the rise and fall of people making a case to someone who is not listening.

It went on until the rods had stopped ringing. Then it stopped too.

“What was that?” said Riarhin.

Osselin had come down off the eighth step. She was standing on the sill at the bottom of the stair with her lantern held high.

“That,” she said, “is what you get if you wake them. Put those away.”

Vesket put the rods away.

“They didn’t like it,” he said.

“They don’t like anything. That’s the point of them. You keep them asleep by not doing anything they don’t like.” Osselin’s voice was shaking very slightly. “Which is everything.”

“Who are they?”

“The ones under the table,” said Osselin. “The ones who were here first. Briar calls them the old guests. My mother called them the jury.” She looked at the stone table. “You knock, and they knock, and you knock again, and they’re satisfied, and they go back to sleep for a year. That’s all it ever was.”

“And now they’re not satisfied.”

“Now they’re asking. And I don’t know what.”

Riarhin left Vesket looking at the copper studs and went round the stone table to the opening in the back wall.

She crouched in front of it with the lantern. The passage was low and narrow, cut in the red rock and worn smooth by feet, and it turned a little to the left about ten feet in. The cold air came steadily out of it against her face.

On the floor of the passage, just inside the mouth, there was a hollow.

It was shallow and round, worn as smooth as the inside of a cup by something set down in the same place for a very long time. It was empty. There was a ring of slightly paler stone round its rim where something had covered it.

She took the lantern and went into the passage on her knees.

Round the turning the passage went on a little way and then stopped, as Osselin had said, at a blank wall of red rock. On the floor at the foot of the wall, in a crack where the floor met it, something caught the light.

She reached in and drew it out. It was a stone. A smooth grey river stone, the size of a hen’s egg, flattened on one side. On the flat side somebody had cut a mark: two lines crossed, and a circle round them.

She took it back.

“What’s this?” she said, holding it up.

Osselin stared at it. Vesket turned.

“Where was that?” Osselin said.

“At the back. In a crack. It belongs there.” Riarhin pointed to the empty hollow at the passage mouth. “It fits.”

Osselin came round the stone table. She looked at the stone in Riarhin’s hand and did not take it.

“That’s the threshold stone,” she said. “It sits in the mouth. It’s always sat in the mouth. Briar dusts round it.” She looked at the hollow. “It’s been moved.”

“Or it moved.”

“Things don’t move in there.”

“The air’s blowing out,” said Riarhin. “Hard. If it had been blowing out for a long time, and the stone was on a smooth floor—” She stopped. A river stone the size of an egg would not be blown along a stone floor by any draught she had ever felt.

Osselin looked at her.

“Put it back,” she said.

“Should I?”

“It goes there. Put it back.”

Riarhin knelt and set the stone in the hollow, flat side down. It settled into its place with a small sound, like a cup set on a saucer.

The cold air stopped.

It stopped all at once, between one breath and the next. The lantern flame, which had been leaning, stood up straight. In the silence Riarhin heard the spring running in its channel, and then, very faintly, nothing else at all.

“Oh,” said Vesket.

They waited. Nothing knocked.

Osselin let out her breath. “Good,” she said. “That’s good. That’s—”

The water in the channel began to rise.

It came up quickly, a hand’s breadth, two, brimming over the old stain line on the rock and spilling across the cellar floor in a sheet. It was icy. It ran round Riarhin’s knees where she knelt and round the feet of the stone table and up to the bottom of the stair, and as it rose it began to sing, very faintly, a high thin sound, the same note again and again, like a finger drawn round the rim of a glass.

And beyond the stone table, through the opening, where the passage had ended in a blank wall ten feet in, Riarhin saw light.

It was grey light, morning light, the light of somewhere outside. It came round the turning of the passage and lay on its floor. She could smell, faintly, on the air that was no longer blowing, salt and grass.

“That’s the lane,” said Osselin. Her voice had gone very small. “That’s the way through. It’s open.”

“Is it the right night?”

“It isn’t a night at all.” Osselin was backing towards the stair. “It’s not the season. It’s not the hour. It should never be open now. Get out of the water.”

Riarhin got up. The cold had gone into her legs to the bone.

“Take it out again?” she said.

“No.” It was Vesket. He had not moved from in front of the stone table. He was looking at the copper studs, which were wet now with spray from the brimming channel, and his face was white. “No. Not without knowing what it does. Taking it out might be worse than putting it in. Leave it. Get upstairs. Get them all upstairs.”

“Them?”

He looked at her.

“Everybody,” he said. “Out of the house. Not upstairs. Out.”

They came up out of the cellar with water running off them. Fennel and Kit, who had been waiting at the trapdoor, looked at their faces and did not ask. Briar was standing by the hearth with the shawl round her shoulders.

“It stopped knocking,” she said.

“Yes,” said Osselin.

“Then—”

“The spring’s coming up. The crossing’s open. It’s open now, Briar. In the morning.”

Briar looked at her. Then she went to the trapdoor and looked down. Riarhin saw her shoulders go stiff.

“It’s on the bottom step,” Briar said.

“It’ll be on the second step by the time you’ve dressed.” Osselin went to the hearth and began, quite calmly, to put out the fire with the ash shovel. “We need everybody out of the house. Linden too.”

“We’re not leaving.”

“For the morning. Till it goes down.”

“We’re not leaving the house.”

“Briar.”

Briar turned round.

“I am not,” she said, “evacuating my house because the spring came up. It’s come up before. It came up in the flood year and my husband’s mother sat on the stair with her feet in it and sang to it till it went down.”

“Was the crossing open?”

Briar did not answer.

“Then we’re not leaving,” said Osselin. “We’re going outside for the morning. Into the garden. Where it’s dry. And we’ll wait to see what it does.”

Briar looked at Linden, in his chair by the window, with the rug over his knees and his eyes on the open trapdoor.

“Outside for the morning,” Briar said.

“Outside for the morning.”

“Then I’m bringing the bread. I’m not leaving the bread to the spring.”

“Bring the bread.”

“And the bedding off the loft. In case it’s the afternoon as well.”

“Bring it.”

“And the box,” said Linden.

Briar turned. “What box?”

“The seed box. Sorrel’s box.” Sorrel had been his wife, Briar’s husband’s mother, nine years dead. “I’m not going anywhere without it. If it’s only the morning, I’ll bring it back. If it’s longer I want it with me.”

Briar opened her mouth and closed it again.

“The seed box,” she said. “Kit.”

Kit went to fetch it. It was a flat wooden box with a sliding lid, the size of a book, painted with flowers that had worn almost away. Linden took it in his lap and held it with both hands.

“Now,” he said. “Who’s carrying me?”

Chapter 12 — The longer way home

It was the whole day.

By the time the sun was up the water had reached the fourth step of the cellar stair and stopped there, brimming and singing, and the grey light in the passage beyond the stone table had not faded. Fennel went down once at noon, on a rope, and came back up white-faced and said she could see the lane at the end of the passage, the high-banked lane above the tide-sheds, with dew on the grass, as though it were dawn there still. She had not gone in. Nobody went in.

The household sat in the garden among the beans and the marigolds with their bedding and their bread and Linden in his chair, which Kit and Riarhin had carried out between them, and the day went on round them as though nothing had happened.

The ferry bell rang from the far bank in the middle of the morning.

“The school,” said Fennel.

She looked at her mother. Briar was sitting on a sack of flour with her arms folded, staring at the house. She did not look round.

“They can’t come up,” said Osselin.

“They come up every Tuesday. The hall’s at the back. They do their letters in the hall.”

“Not today.”

Fennel stood. “Then somebody has to go down and tell them. And the people on the bench. And the Thursday boats, if this goes on.” She looked at Riarhin. “You’d better come. I’ll want somebody at the sweep.”

Riarhin went.

They went down the eight turns of the cliff path in silence. At the bottom, Fennel cast off and they took the sweeps together and crabbed out across the brown water. Over the old channel, in the middle of the river, there was no swirl. The water slid past smooth and flat and Riarhin found that she was waiting for it all the same.

On the far bank the bell post had a crowd round it: the school, eleven children with satchels and a young man in charge of them who looked harassed, and behind them a family with a handcart loaded to the height of a man, two adults and an old woman and three children, and a dog.

Fennel brought the barge in. She did not make fast.

“No school today,” she called. “Go home.”

The children cheered. The young man in charge of them did not.

“Why not?”

“The spring’s up. The house is shut.”

“Shut?” It was the father of the family with the handcart. He was a thin man with a long jaw and a tired face, and he came down to the edge of the landing. “We’re for the crossing. We’ve come from the dales. Four days. We’re going through to my wife’s people above the tide-sheds.”

“Not through the house,” Fennel said. “Not today.”

“When, then?”

Fennel looked at Riarhin. Riarhin did not know.

“I don’t know,” Fennel said. “It might be tomorrow. It might be longer.”

“Then we’ll wait at the top.”

“You can’t wait at the top. Nobody’s in the house.”

The man stared at her. Behind him his wife had put down the handle of the cart and was rubbing the small of her back with both hands.

“We can’t go round,” she said. “Not with her.” She nodded at the old woman on the cart, who sat very upright among the bundles with a shawl over her head and looked at nobody. “It’s two days round the headland and there’s no road for a cart past the Hollins.”

“There’s the coast road,” said Fennel.

“There’s three days of coast road and an inn that wants a mark a night.”

“I know.”

“We paid.” The man’s jaw had set. “We paid for the crossing in advance, at the dale end.”

“She’ll give it back.”

“I don’t want it back. I want the crossing.”

Fennel stood on the landing with the rope in her hand and said nothing.

“You can’t have it,” Riarhin said.

The man looked at her for the first time. He saw the sword on her back and his face changed.

“Who are you?”

“Somebody who was in the cellar this morning.”

“And?”

Riarhin looked at the old woman on the cart.

“And it isn’t safe,” she said. “I don’t know why. Neither does she. Neither does her mother. If you take your family through it today you won’t come out where you meant to.”

“How do you know?”

“I don’t. That’s why you can’t.”

The man looked at her a moment longer. Then he turned away without a word and went back to the cart and picked up the handle. His wife looked at Fennel with dislike and said, quite loudly, that she would be writing to the Hall at Thornholt about it, and that if the spring-house could not keep its crossing open it should not take the fee at the dale end. Then she picked up her side of the cart, and they turned it, slowly, the old woman swaying among the bundles, and went back up the track along the river towards the coast road. The dog went after them. It looked back once.

Fennel watched them go.

“She’ll write,” she said.

“Yes.”

“They always write. Nobody reads it.” She coiled the rope. “The school, then. Go on, all of you, home. Tell your mothers the house is shut. Tell them I said.”

The children went, shrieking. The young man went after them. The bench was empty except for the old man with the goat, who had come down from his daughter’s to go home and wanted to know what all the fuss was.

“Spring’s up,” Fennel told him.

“It does that,” he said, and got in the barge with his goat.

They crossed back. Halfway over Riarhin asked, “How many come up for the crossing in a season?”

Fennel thought about it.

“A hundred,” she said. “More, in a good year. Shepherds going up to the high grazing. Families going to their people. Traders like the one last night, if they’ve got goods for the coast and don’t want to pay the headland tolls. The children for the school. My mother’s cousins at the tide-sheds come over for the market.” She leaned on the sweep. “It’s why there’s a ferry. If there’s no crossing at the top, there’s no reason to come over at the bottom except to go to the hall. And there’s a hall at Kelling.”

In the afternoon they moved Linden.

The water in the cellar had not gone down. It sat at the fourth step, singing, and Vesket, who had been down twice more on the rope, came up the second time and sat on the edge of the bean bed with his head in his hands.

Briar sat on her flour sack and looked at him.

“Well?”

“It’s not here,” he said into his hands. “It’s not the cellar. It’s not the stone. I thought the stone had moved because something here had changed. But the knocking was only the arrangement complaining.” He lifted his head. “The arrangement’s been pulled. From somewhere else. Like a cloth on a table when somebody tugs the other end. Putting the stone back put one corner right and pulled the rest further off.”

“Pulled from where?”

“From wherever the rods have been pointing since spring.” He looked at Riarhin. “North and east. Past the Gate.”

Briar looked at the house. Smoke no longer rose from its chimney. The door at their end stood open on the dark long room.

“Can you mend it?” she said.

“No.”

“Can anybody?”

He was silent a long time.

“I don’t know,” he said. “Not from here. Not by anything we can do in the cellar. If it can be mended, it’ll be mended where it’s being pulled. And I don’t know yet what’s pulling it, or why, or whether mending it would mean—” He stopped.

“Would mean what?”

“I’m sorry,” he said. “I don’t know.”

Briar got up.

“Then we’re closing it,” she said. “For now. Not for good. For now. We’re shutting the trap and putting the thread on it and we’re going to my sister’s along the cliff until it goes down. Linden too.”

Nobody argued.

Linden would not be carried facing backwards.

They tried it first on the cliff path, which ran along the top towards Briar’s sister’s house half a mile away. Kit and Riarhin made a chair of their arms, the way you carry someone out of a fight, and Linden sat in it with the seed box in his lap and his arms round their necks, and they set off along the path with Riarhin walking backwards, so that Linden faced the house they were leaving.

After twenty steps he said, “Put me down.”

They put him down on a stone by the path. His face was grey.

“I can’t,” he said. “Going backwards. It turns my stomach. I couldn’t sit at the back of the ferry when I was a boy.” He looked at Riarhin with his pale filmed eyes. “I need to see where I’m going.”

“Then we’ll turn you round.”

“Then I can’t see the house.”

They looked at each other.

“I’ll see it when I come back,” Linden said at last. “Turn me round.”

They turned him round, and Linden faced the way they were going, along the cliff top with the river below on one side and the hawthorn ridges on the other. It was better. They went a quarter mile. Then the path came to the head of a gully where a stream came down to the river, and dropped down one side of it in a run of rough steps and climbed the other, and there was no way to take the steps three abreast.

“I’ll take him,” said Riarhin.

“On your back?” said Kit.

“On my back.”

Linden looked at her.

“I’m heavier than I look.”

“So am I.”

He laughed, his dry rattle. They got him onto her back, his arms round her neck and his knees under her arms and the seed box pressed between his chest and her shoulders, and she went down the steps into the gully one at a time, with Kit behind her holding Linden’s coat, over the plank across the stream and up the other side. Her legs were shaking by the top, and she set him down on the grass and stood with her hands on her knees, breathing.

Linden sat on the grass with the seed box in his lap.

“Thank you,” he said.

“It’s nothing.”

“It’s a good deal. I haven’t been down that gully in four years.” He looked back the way they had come, across the gully and along the cliff path to where the spring-house stood with its turf roof and its cold chimney.

“Sorrel planted those hawthorns,” he said. “Along the top there. The year we were married.”

They carried him the rest of the way between them. Briar’s sister’s house was small and full of cats, and her sister was a large loud woman who embraced Briar and shouted at Linden and put him in her best chair by the fire with the seed box on his knees, and he sat there looking pleased and exhausted and asked for tea.

Osselin had stayed behind at the spring-house.

When Riarhin went back in the dusk she found her at the gate with her lantern lit and a coil of red thread in her hands. She had shut the trapdoor in the long room and put the rag rug back over it and laid a line of red thread across the rug from wall to wall, knotted at both ends to nails. Now she was doing the same at the gate, a single strand of red thread across the gap, knotted to the gatepost on one side and the hawthorn on the other, at the height of a child’s chest.

Then she took a small iron lantern from her bag, the spare one wrapped in felt, and hung it on the gatepost beside the thread. She did not light it.

“What does that mean?” Riarhin asked.

“That the house is shut and the warden knows.” Osselin tied the last knot. “Any warden from here to Kelling will know it. If they see a dark lamp and a red thread, they don’t go in, and they tell people not to go in, and they send to the nearest warden to find out why.” She stepped back and looked at it. “It’s the oldest thing we do. It’s older than counting.”

“Will they come?”

“Somebody will.” She looked at the house. “And they’ll find it shut, and they won’t know why either.”

They stood at the gate in the dusk. Below them, on the river, the ferry was coming back from its last crossing, Fennel alone at the sweep. Upriver at the stone quay the Linn ship had lit its lamps, and there was singing on its deck again, the same rough cheerful line, and the sound of somebody laughing.

“They’re going in two days,” Fennel said when she came up the path. She had brought the empty bread basket from the barge and stood with it on her hip, looking at the red thread and the dark lantern. “The northern boat. Kit asked them. Before the equinox, they said. They don’t stay for anybody.”

She looked at the thread a moment longer.

“Mother says it’s only for a day or two,” she said.

Nobody answered. Fennel went past them along the cliff path towards her aunt’s house with the basket on her hip, and did not look back at the house.

Vesket came out of the dark of the garden with the copper case in his arms. He had packed it. He stood by the gate beside Osselin, not looking at her.

“We should go to Thornholt,” he said. “In the morning. I need to talk to Vesper. I need to tell the hall what I saw. I need—” He stopped. “I need to be wrong about something, and I need somebody to tell me what.”

Osselin looked at the dark lantern on the gatepost.

“You’re not wrong about this,” she said.

“I know.” He shifted the case in his arms. “That’s what I’m afraid of.”

Chapter 13 — A bench for the apparatus

Thornholt was built into a hill.

From the drove road it looked like nothing but a long slope of grey rock and hawthorn, with smoke coming out of it in a dozen places. Closer, Riarhin saw doors in the rock, round doors and square ones, painted green and blue and yellow, with steps cut up to them and little gardens in front on terraces walled with stone. Bridges of wood and rope ran between the terraces. Somewhere inside the hill a small clear bell was ringing, struck at regular intervals, and from somewhere else came the steady chime of hammers.

“The workshops,” Vesket said. “The hall’s at the top. The benches are inside.”

They had come by the trader’s drove road, which had saved them a day and a half, as promised, and had also taken them through a bog in which Osselin lost a boot. She walked the last mile with one boot and one foot wrapped in a piece of sacking and had not spoken since the bog.

Vesket went up the steps first. At the third terrace a door opened and a woman came out with a basket of washing, saw him, put the basket down and said, “Vesket! They said you’d gone to the coast,” and embraced him.
