At the fifth terrace two young men carrying a long copper pipe between them stopped and asked whether he had heard about the vote on the new bell. At the seventh a very old man sitting in the sun outside a yellow door said, without opening his eyes, “You owe me a pot of ink,” and Vesket said, “I know, Fennick, I know,” and kept walking.

“Everybody knows you,” Riarhin said.

“Everybody knows everybody. It’s a small hill.”

The workshops were inside, in a long gallery cut into the rock, lit by shafts that came down from the hillside above and by lamps hung on chains. Along both sides of the gallery stood benches, stone benches built out from the wall, each with its own hearth and its own racks of tools and its own little brass plate set into the stone at one end with a name cut in it. At most of them somebody was working. A woman was grinding a lens. Two children were sorting copper wire by thickness under the eye of an old man. Somebody was casting something in a sand mould, and the gallery was full of the smell of hot metal and the sound of a dozen different kinds of work.

Vesket went to an empty bench near the end. He stopped in front of it and looked at the brass plate.

The name on it was not his.

“Oh,” he said.

A man at the next bench looked up from a crucible. He was a broad, bald Aelinnel with burn scars on both forearms.

“That’s Hollin’s now,” he said. “Since midsummer. You were gone.”

“I was gone four months.”

“You were gone. Benches don’t sit empty.” The man went back to his crucible. “Hollin’s got a bell to cast for the Coombe parish. He’s booked it through the autumn.”

Vesket stood looking at the plate.

“I need a bench,” he said. “For three days. Two.”

“Ask the steward.”

“The steward will say—”

“The steward will say there’s a list.” The man did not look up. “There is.”

Vesket went to find the steward. Riarhin and Osselin waited in the gallery. Osselin sat on the end of somebody’s bench and unwrapped the sacking from her foot and looked at it with disgust. A girl of about fifteen, passing with an armful of charcoal, looked at the foot and said, “There’s a cobbler on the ninth terrace,” and walked on.

Vesket came back after half an hour.

“There’s a list,” he said.

“How long?”

“A bench comes free on the fourth day from now. A woman named Thrift is finishing a set of scales for the Kelling market. The market’s on the sixth.” He sat down on the end of the bench beside Osselin. “I could ask Hollin to share.”

“Will he?”

“Hollin hates me.”

“Why?”

“I told him his last bell was out of true. It was.” Vesket rubbed his eyes with his good hand. “The whole hill heard it ring. It was a fifth sharp on the hum. Everybody knew. Nobody said. I said.”

Osselin, without looking up from her foot, said, “Go and ask Hollin.”

He went and asked Hollin. Hollin turned out to be a young woman, sharp-faced and heavily pregnant, with her sleeves rolled to the shoulder and a sand mould for the Coombe bell half-built on her bench. She listened to Vesket with her arms folded on top of her belly. When he had finished she said she would let him have the far end of the bench for two days, the hearth end, provided he did not touch her mould, did not offer any opinion about her mould, and did not, under any circumstances, strike anything in her hearing.

“I need to strike the rods,” said Vesket. “That’s the whole point.”

“Then strike them somewhere else.”

“There isn’t anywhere else with a hearth.”

“There’s the steward’s kitchen.” Hollin turned back to her mould. “Two days. The hearth end. You heard me.”

They took the hearth end. Vesket unpacked the copper case onto it, and his inks, and his wallet of papers, and the vellum in its bone case, and let Tessel out onto her felt at the very end of the stone, as far from Hollin’s mould as it was possible to be. Hollin watched the spider with her mouth pressed tight and said nothing.

Then he began to work.

Riarhin had seen him strike the rods and sit with the vellum on his knee. She had not seen him work, and she sat on a stool at the end of the bench for most of two days and watched him.

He made ink. The little blue pot had cracked on the ferry and he had no more of it, and he said the work could not be done without it. He took copper filings and vinegar and something from a twist of paper that smelled of rotten eggs, and set them in a small glazed dish on the hearth, and tended it for hours, stirring it with a glass rod, adding a drop of water, holding it to the light. It turned green, then grey, then a dirty brown. He scraped it into the waste bucket with a horn spatula, scrubbed the dark crust off the glaze, and began again.

The second batch went brown too.

“What’s wrong with it?” Riarhin asked.

“The vinegar.” He held up the bottle. “It’s Thornholt vinegar. It’s made from the hawthorn berries. Mine was made at home from apple. It’s different. It shouldn’t matter. It does.”

“Can you get apple vinegar?”

“On the ninth terrace. From the cobbler’s wife.” He looked at Osselin, who had gone up to the cobbler that morning and come back with a pair of borrowed boots and a sour expression. “I should have asked when you were there.”

“I’m not going back up.”

“I’ll go.”

“You’ll go and somebody will stop you on the fifth terrace and you’ll come back at dusk without it.” Osselin got up. “I’ll go.”

She went. Vesket watched her go.

“She hates this,” he said.

“The hill?”

“The halls. The benches. The lists.” He scraped the second batch into the waste bucket. “She says everybody here talks about the right way to do something until there isn’t time to do it.”

“Is she right?”

He was silent for a moment.

“Often,” he said.

Osselin came back with the apple vinegar before noon. The third batch of ink went from green to grey to a deep, clear blue, the colour of a summer evening just after the sun has gone, and Vesket held the dish up to the shaft of light from the hillside and looked at it, and Riarhin watched his face change.

“There,” he said softly.

Hollin, at the other end of the bench, looked at the ink without wanting to.

“That’s a good blue,” she said grudgingly.

“Thank you.”

“I’d want to know how you got it.”

“Apple vinegar.”

Hollin looked at him. Then she laughed, a short surprised bark, and turned back to her mould.

He annealed copper. He drew a new pair of rods from a coil of wire the cobbler’s neighbour sold him, heating the wire in the hearth until it glowed and then drawing it through a steel plate pierced with holes, each smaller than the last, his face red in the light, his burned hand wrapped in a wet cloth. He cut the wire into two lengths. He did not heat them again. He laid them in a box of plain wood, not the copper case, and closed it.

“Those are for Vesper,” he said.

At night, when Hollin had gone home and the gallery was dark except for the banked hearths, he worked on something else. Riarhin woke on the second night on the bench where she had been sleeping and saw him at the hearth end with a lamp, bent over a frame of thin copper wire laid flat on the stone: a net of fine squares, each crossing knotted with a twist of wire and touched with a drop of the blue ink, the whole of it folding on itself in hinged panels like a screen. He was working out from the middle, square by square, with the brass compasses.

“What is it?” she asked.

“A court.” He did not look up. “A place laid out true. For the working, if there has to be one. Her papers say where the old binding is weakest. If I can lay this over it and hold it, I think it will keep its shape while somebody does what has to be done.” He tightened a twist of wire. “I’ve been drawing it since the spring. I didn’t know what for.”

On the afternoon of the second day, Vesper came.

She was older than Riarhin had expected from the letters, a thin grey woman with a face like a hatchet and a long black coat buttoned to the throat. She carried a staff with a brass head shaped like a pair of compasses. Everyone in the gallery looked up when she came in and then looked carefully back at their work.

She came to the hearth end of Hollin’s bench and stood looking at Vesket’s apparatus.

“Vesket,” she said.

“Vesper.”

“You wrote to me from Wendle. And from the river. And from the coast. Three letters in nine days.” She did not smile. “You’ve never written me three letters in a year.”

“It’s important.”

“So you said. Three times.” She looked at the copper case. “Show me.”

He showed her. He struck the old rods, the flawed pair, and let her hear the slow throb between their notes. He showed her the way-cord with its two moved knots. He told her about the Hollow Field and the stile at Coombe Barrow and the spring-house cellar. He laid out his papers, eleven years of measurements in his small sloping hand, crossings all along the hawthorn coast, and the steps in the beat since the spring, one after another, each with its date.

Vesper listened without interrupting. When he had finished she sat down on Riarhin’s stool, which Riarhin had given up to her without being asked, and looked at the papers.

Then she said, “Your rods are old.”

Vesket closed his eyes.

“I know they’re old.”

“They’re old, and they’ve been warmed and cooled and warmed again in that box of yours every day for twenty years.” Vesper put a finger on the first page of measurements. “Copper changes when you heat it. You know that. It softens. Its note falls. Slowly, but it falls. You heat these every day to keep them steady. You’ve been annealing them for twenty years without meaning to.”

“The beat was steady for twenty years.”

“Was it? Or was it falling so slowly that you corrected for it without noticing?” Vesper turned a page. “Here. And here. Eleven years ago and eight years ago. The beat you record at the same crossing differs by a hair. You put it down to the season.”

Vesket looked at the page. Riarhin saw his face go still.

“It was the season,” he said.

“Perhaps. And perhaps it was the rods.” Vesper turned another page. “I’m not saying nothing’s happening. I heard you about the spring-house. I know Briar. If Briar has shut her trap, something’s wrong. I’m saying the measurement you’re bringing to the hall is a measurement made with an instrument that’s been changing all its life, and the first thing Ashlar and the others will say is what I’ve just said. And they’ll say it in front of everybody.”

Vesket was silent.

“You know I’m right,” said Vesper.

“You’re right about the rods. You’re not right about what’s happening.”

“I didn’t say what’s happening. I said you can’t show it with those.” She looked at the plain wooden box at the end of the bench. “Are those new?”

“Drawn yesterday. Never heated since.”

“Good.” She stood up. “Then we do it properly. We take a new pair and an old pair through the same crossing on the same day. If the new ones come back changed the same way the old ones do, it’s the crossing. If they come back steady, it’s the rods.”

“Which crossing?”

“The Steward’s Gate. Up the hill. It’s small. It’s a door in the back of the steward’s garden that goes through to the old orchard on the far slope, on Tuesdays and Fridays. The steward uses it to fetch apples.” She almost smiled. “It’s Friday tomorrow.”

“That’s a day.”

“It’s a day. You’ve waited since spring. Wait a day.”

“And then I’ll have to wait for the hall.”

“Then you’ll have to wait for the hall.” Vesper looked at him. “Unless you’re planning to go to the Gate without them.”

Vesket did not answer.

Vesper looked at Riarhin for the first time. It was a long, considering look.

“You’re the granddaughter,” said Vesper.

“Yes.”

“He wrote that you’ve met her.”

“Yes.”

“Then you’ll speak at the hall. They’ll want to hear what she does. Not what she wrote. They’ve all read what she wrote. Ashlar has a copy on his shelf with notes in the margins.” Vesper picked up her staff. “I want to hear it too.”

She turned to go, then turned back.

“The tide-sheds,” she said to Vesket. “You wrote that she hired a shed below the Gate.”

“Yes. To store the bundles.”

“Under a salt licence.”

“Yes.”

Vesper was quiet a moment.

“The salt licences are issued through brokers,” she said. “Have been since the flood year. The hall gave up issuing them directly because nobody would keep the register. A broker can buy a licence and sell it on to anybody. The shed doesn’t know whose name is on the paper. The reeves don’t count what goes into a licensed shed, because salt’s salt.” She looked at the way-cord on the bench. “You told the hall eight years ago the licence arrangement was sound.”

“It was sound,” he said. “When I looked at it.”

“It was sound when you looked at it eight years ago,” said Vesper, “and you never looked at it again.”

She went away down the gallery with her staff tapping.

Vesket sat on the end of the bench. He picked up the dish with the blue ink in it and looked at it, and put it down again.

Hollin, at the other end of the bench, said without turning round, “She’s like that with everybody.”

“I know.”

“She was like that with my mother. My mother cried for a week.” Hollin pressed a thumb into the sand of her mould. “My mother was wrong, though.”

Vesket laughed, very quietly, into his hand.

Chapter 14 — A place in the calculation

The Steward’s Gate was a door of grey weathered oak set into the back wall of a vegetable garden at the very top of the hill. On Friday morning, Vesper opened it with a key on a chain round her neck, and through it, where the hillside beyond the wall should have been, Riarhin saw a slope of old apple trees on the far side of the valley, laden with small red fruit, with the sun on them.

Vesket went through with the old rods and the new ones in their two boxes. Vesper went with him. Riarhin and Osselin waited in the garden among the cabbages with the steward, who was a fat cheerful man with an apple in each hand and no interest in any of it.

“They’ll be an hour,” he said. “They always are. I’m usually ten minutes. I know where the good trees are.”

They were gone an hour and a half. When they came back through the door, Vesket was carrying both boxes and Vesper was carrying her staff and neither of them was speaking. They went straight down the hill to Hollin’s bench without stopping, and Riarhin and Osselin followed.

At the bench, Vesket struck the new rods.

The two notes. The throb between them. Riarhin counted it.

Then he struck the old ones.

He did it twice more, each pair, alternating, and the gallery went quiet round them, and Hollin stopped working on her mould and turned round to listen, and somewhere down the gallery the woman grinding the lens put down her wheel.

The new rods, never heated, drawn two days ago, beat a little faster than the old ones had at Hessel. They had come back from the steward’s orchard changed. The change was not as large as the old rods’. It leaned the same way.

Vesper stood with her staff and listened to all six strikings without moving. When the last notes had faded she said, “Again. The new pair. Once.”

He struck them once.

“Yes,” said Vesper. She looked at the floor. Then she looked at Vesket. “It isn’t only the rods.”

“No.”

“The rods have been changing. You’ll have to correct for it. Your eleven years are worth less than you thought.” She said it quite flatly. “But it isn’t only the rods. The steward’s door has moved. Not much. It’s moved the same way the others have moved.”

Vesket let out his breath.

“Thank you,” he said.

“Don’t thank me. I’ve just made myself responsible for telling Ashlar.” Vesper turned. “The hall sits at noon. You’ll want to clean your hands.”

The hall was at the very top of the hill, a round room cut into the rock with a domed ceiling and a hole at the top of the dome through which a single shaft of sunlight came down and moved slowly across the floor through the day. The floor was paved in an eight-pointed figure of pale and dark stone. Round the edge of the room, on a raised step, were benches, and on the benches sat perhaps twenty Aelinnel in coats of many colours, and in the middle of the figure on the floor, on a low stool, sat a very old man with a white beard down to his belt and a copy of a book open on his knees.

“Ashlar,” Vesket murmured. “Warden of the Tide Court.”

“What’s the Tide Court?”

“The people who decide who goes through the Gate. And when. And what they carry.”

“Does he decide?”

“He speaks last.” Vesket straightened his coat. “Which is the same thing.”

There was an order to it. Riarhin learned the order by watching. Vesper spoke first, because she had brought the matter, standing at the point of the figure nearest the door. She said what had been observed and by whom, without saying what she thought it meant. Then Vesket spoke, standing at the next point of the figure, the one to Vesper’s left, and said what he thought it meant, until a woman on the benches asked who had checked his correction.

“Vesper,” he said. “This morning. The change remains.”

“On a crossing you chose,” the woman said. “With an examiner you brought.”

“Vesper isn’t mine. Nobody’s ever accused Vesper of being anybody’s.”

There was a ripple of laughter round the benches. Vesper did not laugh.

Then it was Fennel.

Riarhin had not known she was there. She came in from the door, dusty from the drove road, in a borrowed coat too large for her, and stood at the third point of the figure with her chin up and her hands clasped behind her back. Somebody on the benches asked who she was. She told them. She said that she had come from the spring-house with a letter from her mother, and that the trap was still shut and the water still at the fourth step, and that a family from the dales had gone round by the coast road and had written to the hall to complain.

“We’ve had the letter,” said Ashlar. It was the first thing he had said. His voice was thin and dry, and everybody in the room leaned forward a little to hear it. “It was very angry.”

“They’d paid,” said Fennel.

“I don’t doubt it.” Ashlar turned a page of the book on his knees. “Is your mother asking the hall to open the crossing?”

“No. She’s asking the hall to tell her when it’ll open.”

“We can’t tell her that.”

“Then she’s asking you to tell her why it’s shut.”

Ashlar looked at Vesket.

“The Sumwright,” he said, “has been telling us why for most of an hour. I am still waiting to hear it.”

Vesper looked at Riarhin. Riarhin went to the fourth point of the figure, the one nobody had stood at yet.

She did not know how to stand. She did not know whether she was meant to speak to Ashlar or to the benches. She could feel twenty pairs of bright Aelinnel eyes on her, on her hair and her height and the sword on her back, which a man at the door had asked her to leave outside and which she had refused to leave until Vesper told him to let her keep it. She stood in the shaft of light and felt it warm on the side of her face.

“I don’t know why it’s shut,” she said. “I know who.”

There was a murmur on the benches. Ashlar did not look up from his book.

“Go on.”

She told them. She did not tell them about the potter or the landlady or the room with the screen, because they had read Eshai’s copies. She told them what she had seen herself, this summer, in order: the way-cord with two knots moved and retied; sixteen bundles of black iron in waxed linen and copper bands, carried up the cliff road and stored below the arch under a salt licence; the fold chalked on each bundle, the same fold Farieth had taught a granddaughter to use on letters; two bundles held at Hessel on Riarhin’s silver, and a tall old woman with a stiff leg who had come for them with the twin of a token, said the price was fair, and gone away.

“She didn’t argue,” said Riarhin. “She doesn’t argue. She finds another way.”

The woman on the benches who had spoken before said, “You’re telling us she’s carrying iron into the forest.”

“I’m telling you she’s carried fourteen bundles of iron to the foot of your Gate in a shed your reeves don’t count, and she means to take them further.”

“How do you know she means to take them further?”

“Because she doesn’t want them counted.” Riarhin looked at the woman. “You don’t hide what you’re carrying unless you’re taking it somewhere somebody would stop you.”

“Or unless you’re discreet.”

“She’s discreet. She killed my sister by a kiln while the potter waited for his firing.” Riarhin heard her own voice go flat and hard and did not try to soften it. “She was discreet about that as well.”

There was a silence in the hall. The shaft of light had moved a hand’s breadth across the floor.

Ashlar closed his book.

“Sumwright,” he said.

Vesket stepped forward.

“You told us an hour ago that the woman intends to do something to the enclosure beneath the forest. You used the old word. You told us the change in your measurements began at a point. What point?”

Vesket hesitated. Riarhin saw him do it, and saw Vesper see him do it.

“I said the change began at a point in time,” he said. “I can say more exactly than I did. The first step falls within six days of the first delivery to the tide-sheds. The second within four days of the second. The third—”

“And the enclosure?”

“I can’t say she means the enclosure.” He said it slowly. “I said that an hour ago because I believe it. I can’t show it. I can show that the pull is towards the forest, past the Gate, and that it began when the iron arrived. I can’t show where in the forest. I have an idea from her papers. It’s an idea.”

“Thank you,” said Ashlar. “That is more useful than what you said an hour ago.”

Vesket bowed his head.

“The examiner,” said Ashlar.

Vesper stepped forward.

“I told the Sumwright yesterday that his instrument was unreliable,” she said. “It is. I told him I thought his case rested on it. I was wrong about that. It rests on the instrument and the iron and the cord and the spring-house together, and none of them alone would move me, and together they do.” She paused. “I withdraw what I said about the season. The correction for age doesn’t account for the steps. I’ve checked his dates against the delivery dates from the carrier’s book. They fall where he says.”

A murmur went round the benches.

“And the interval?” said Ashlar.

Vesper looked at Vesket. Vesket looked at the floor.

“We worked it this morning,” he said. “After the orchard. With the new rods and the old, corrected.” He took a sheet from his coat and held it up, though nobody could have read it from the benches. “The steps aren’t random. Each step comes a little sooner after the one before. If they go on doing that, they’ll meet.”

“Meet?”

“Fall on the same day. Coincide.” He searched for the word. “Align. The old papers call it an alignment. The enclosure has a rhythm. If the steps are being made to fall in time with it, then there’s a day coming when they’ll fall together with the rhythm, and on that day—”

“On that day?”

“I don’t know,” Vesket said. “I know the day is coming. I can’t name the day exactly. Between twenty-six and thirty-four days from now. Closer to thirty. I could be wrong by a week either way if the steps change their spacing.”

“And what happens on that day if nobody interferes?”

“I don’t know that either.” Vesket lifted his head and looked at Ashlar. “I know what her papers say happened the last time somebody did something at the enclosure. Two boundaries failed. She went into a vat. I know that she believes there is a danger in the enclosure, and I think she believes she can end it. I don’t think she cares what ends with it.”

Ashlar looked at Fennel.

“And the spring-house,” he said. “What does your interference do to the spring-house, Sumwright?”

Vesket was silent.

“I don’t know,” he said at last. “I would like to say it can be mended. I can’t say that.”

“Thank you,” said Ashlar again.

He opened his book and turned a page and read for a while, as though the hall were empty. The shaft of light crept across the eight-pointed figure on the floor. Nobody on the benches moved.

At last Ashlar closed the book.

“The Sumwright, the warden of Wendle, and the granddaughter may use the Gate,” he said. “By the tide-path only, at the neap. They will take a cord and they will return it, all of them, together, within forty days, or the reeves will close the arch to them by name. They will carry no iron unwrapped. The granddaughter’s sword will be named at the arch, and wrapped, and carried wrapped while the arch can see it. They will not go by any crossing that has been shut, or offer to open one. They will not offer the reeves any promise on the hall’s behalf.” He looked at Vesket. “And the Sumwright will not represent the Tide Court in anything he does past the Gate. He goes as himself.”

“I understand.”

“I doubt it,” said Ashlar. “But you’ll go.”

He stood. Everyone on the benches stood.

Outside the round hall, in the sun on the top of the hill, Vesper stopped Vesket with a hand on his arm.

“You asked for the true-lens,” she said.

“I asked whether I might borrow it.”

“You may not.” Vesper looked at him with something that was almost kindness. “It’s four hundred years old and there isn’t another. If it goes past the Gate and doesn’t come back, nobody on this hill will be able to read the old drawings properly again. I won’t lend it to anybody for that. Not even you.”

He nodded.

“You can have the small set,” Vesper said. “From my own bench. The compasses and the scale and the little resonance plate. And four pots of the old inks, if you can carry them warm. Wren will bring them down to you tonight.” She looked at a girl standing behind her, the one who had told Osselin about the cobbler. “Wren is responsible for them. If they don’t come back, it’s Wren’s name in the steward’s book.”

The girl went red to the ears.

Vesket looked at her. Then he looked at Vesper.

“That’s cruel,” he said.

“That’s responsible,” said Vesper. “Now you’ll bring them back.”

She went away across the hilltop with her staff tapping. Wren followed her, then stopped and looked back at Vesket, and then at Riarhin, and ran after Vesper.

Fennel had come out of the hall behind them. She stood with her borrowed coat hanging off her shoulders and looked down the hill towards the drove road and the long way home.

“I’ll tell her,” she said. “Mother. That they don’t know.”

“I’m sorry,” said Vesket.

“You said that in there.” Fennel did not look at him. “Ashlar asked you a question and you said you didn’t know, and that was the first true thing anybody said all afternoon.” She pulled the coat round her. “I’m going home by the coast road. The drove road’s got a bog.”

“We know,” said Osselin.

Fennel looked at Osselin’s borrowed boots and almost smiled. Then she went off down the steps, between the coloured doors, and Riarhin watched her go until she turned the corner of the seventh terrace and was gone.

“Thirty days,” said Osselin.

“Twenty-six to thirty-four.”

“Thirty.” Osselin looked at the line of the hills to the north and east, where the hawthorn country ran down to the sea. “Then we’d better buy charcoal.”

Chapter 15 — What must stay warm

The reeves’ village at the foot of the Green Gate was called Underarch, and it was mostly sheds.

It lay in a cove on the hawthorn coast, three days north of Thornholt, where the red cliffs came down to the sea in a long curve and stopped at a headland of black rock. The sheds stood along the shingle above the tide line in two crooked rows, low buildings of tarred timber with turf on their roofs and their doors facing the water. Behind them, up a short steep valley, stood the houses of the reeves, white-walled, with their gardens terraced into the slope. And at the end of the cove, where the headland met the sea, was the arch.

Riarhin saw it from the cliff road on the evening they arrived and stopped.

It was a sea-arch, a great span of black rock standing out into the water, with the waves going through it. The top was grown with grass and thrift. The inside of the span was smooth and dark and wet. Through it, as through a window, she could see the sea beyond, grey and flat, and the line of the horizon, and nothing else.

“That’s it?” she said.

“That’s it,” said Vesket.

“It looks like a rock.”

“It is a rock.” He shifted the case in his arms. “At the neap, at the right hour, if you walk through it the way the reeves tell you, you come out somewhere else.”

“Where?”

“That depends on the cord.”

The neap was four days off. They spent the four days in Underarch.

They took a room above a net loft, two beds and a floor, with a window that looked across the shingle to the arch. The woman who rented it to them was a reeve’s widow named Pellet, small and brisk and deaf in one ear, who charged them a half-mark a night and said that the last people who had taken the room had been a party of pilgrims who wanted to see the forest and had come back in two days, white as milk, and had not wanted their deposit back.

“Why not?”

“They didn’t say.” Pellet looked at the sword on Riarhin’s back. “They didn’t have one of those.”

On the first morning, Riarhin went to look at the sheds.

Vesket had the name of the broker from Vesper and the number of the shed from the broker. It was at the end of the second row, nearest the arch, a long low building with a sagging door and a little brass plate on the jamb that said SALT and nothing else. The door was padlocked. Riarhin did not try the lock. She walked round the shed slowly, looking at the ground.

The shingle in front of the door had been churned some weeks ago; rain had flattened it, and a storm tide had smoothed the lower part. But higher up, where the shingle gave way to sand and coarse grass at the shed’s back corner, she found what she was looking for: two shallow parallel grooves, a little wider apart than her shoulders, running from the corner of the shed up the beach towards the path to the headland, and between them, at intervals, the round deep marks of a single foot set down hard.

Somebody had dragged a sledge alone, walking backwards. The other foot had left fainter marks, as though it took less weight.

She crouched over them.

She followed the grooves up the path towards the headland until they turned off onto the turf, and she lost them in a patch of bracken below the arch.

Fourteen bundles. Long iron rods, bent at one end, the weight of a man in every two. Dragged on a sledge up a beach by a woman with a stiff leg, walking backwards, one foot set and pull, one foot set and pull, alone.

Riarhin asked Pellet that evening and Pellet said nobody ever saw anything at the salt sheds, that was what the salt sheds were for.

Osselin went for charcoal on the second day.

The case needed it. Vesket had worked out exactly how much: enough to keep the coals alive for forty days, with a third more in case, which came to more than Riarhin wanted to carry and less than Vesket wanted to take. There was a burner who sold it on the hill above the village, a day’s walk there and back, and Osselin said she would go because Vesket would get talking and Riarhin would frighten him.

“Frighten a charcoal-burner?”

“You frighten everybody. You don’t mean to. You stand like you’re about to hit something.”

Riarhin looked at herself. “I don’t.”

“You’re doing it now,” said Osselin, and went.

She did not come back that night.

Riarhin sat at the window of the room above the net loft until the light went, watching the path down from the hill. Vesket sat on the floor with the vellum unrolled across his knees and did not look at the window at all. He had told her, an hour before, that Osselin had walked a great deal further than to a charcoal-burner’s in much worse country, and that if she was late it was because the burner wanted to talk, or because she had decided to sleep on the hill rather than walk down in the dark.

“I know,” Riarhin said.

“You keep looking.”

“I know.”

He went back to the vellum. After a while he said, without looking up, “She’ll be angry if she comes down and finds you sitting there.”

“Then she’ll be angry.”

He was redrawing a figure on the vellum, a great interlaced thing of circles and lines that he had told her he must draw again, all of it, once every eight days, or it would stop being true. Riarhin had asked what he meant by true and he had tried to tell her and then given up and said, “Like a song you haven’t sung for a long time. You think you remember it, and then you sing it and the middle’s gone.”

He drew with the good blue from Thornholt, with a fine pen and a pair of brass compasses, the vellum spread across a board on his knees. Sometimes he stopped and looked at what he had drawn before he drew the next line. Once he made a sound of disgust and took a knife and scraped a line off the vellum and drew it again a hair to one side.

Tessel sat on her felt beside the board and drew too. She drew her own figure, small and exact, a lattice of six-sided cells, and when Vesket stopped to look at what he had done, she stopped. When he began again, she began again.

“Does she always do that?”

“Always. Since the first week I had her.” He did not look up. “I don’t know whether she’s copying me or I’m copying her.”

“Which do you think?”

He was quiet a moment, the pen above the vellum.

“Her,” he said.

Osselin came down in the morning with the charcoal on a borrowed donkey and a face like thunder.

“The path moved,” she said, before anyone could ask. She unloaded the donkey without help, sack after sack onto the shingle in front of the net loft. “The burner’s path. I walked it with Vesket six years ago. It goes up the valley and over the shoulder and down to the burner’s clearing by the beech. I went up the valley and over the shoulder, and the beech was on the wrong side of the stream.”

“The stream moved?”

“The path moved. The hill moved under it. There’d been a slip. The whole side of the shoulder had come down in the wet, months ago, and the trees were lying with their roots in the air, and the path went across where the slip had been and then there wasn’t a path.” She threw down the last sack. “I spent the afternoon finding the burner. I found him at dusk. He wouldn’t let me walk down in the dark because he says the slip’s still moving. He was right. I heard it in the night.”

“Did he charge extra?”

Osselin looked at Vesket.

“Twice,” she said. “Twice the price. Because the slip took his kiln road and he’s carrying everything round by the top now, an extra day every time, and he’s got one donkey and a bad back and a daughter who’s gone to the coast.” She folded her arms. “I paid it. I’d have paid three times. You can tell me I was wrong.”

“You weren’t wrong.”

“I know I wasn’t.” She sat down on a sack. “It cost us a day. I know that too.”

Vesket was already counting the sacks. Riarhin watched him do the reckoning in his head, the forty days, the third more, the day lost, and saw him arrive at a number and not like it.

“It’s enough,” he said.

“Is it?”

“It’s enough if we don’t lose any more on the ferry.” He looked at Osselin. “Or on boats generally.”

Osselin laughed despite herself. It was a short, tired laugh, and she put her face in her hands at the end of it.

That afternoon they redistributed the weight.

They did it on the floor of the room above the net loft with everything they had spread out round them: the charcoal in its sacks, the food, the bedding, Vesket’s copper case and his wallet and the vellum in its bone case, Vesper’s small set in its wooden box, the four warm pots of the old inks wrapped in felt, the folded copper court in its oilcloth, which weighed more than all the inks together, Osselin’s lanterns and her thread and her salve and her little round loaf. Riarhin’s pack, which was small. Remorse, which lay along the wall by the door in its scabbard, apart from everything.

It took the whole afternoon and most of a quarrel.

The quarrel began about weight. Vesket wanted to carry the copper case and Vesper’s set and the inks, because nobody else knew how to keep them level and warm. Osselin said he could not carry all three and walk for thirty days in the forest. He said he had walked further with more.

“Yes,” she said, “and I carried you.”

Riarhin was cutting felt. She had found a roll of it in the net loft below, old and greasy, and Pellet had sold it to her for a penny, and she was cutting it into pads with Toothpick, to fit round Vesper’s instruments in their box so that they would not knock together on the road. The felt was thick and full of grit, and each pad had to be cut to the exact shape of its hollow, and tested, and trimmed. She kept her head down and her hands moving and heard them anyway.

“Every time,” Osselin was saying, low and furious. “Every time it’s a small thing. ‘Carry this, Ossie, it isn’t heavy.’ ‘Count that stile for me, it won’t take a minute.’ ‘Go into the field, you know the way.’ And then it’s me with the cold in my bones for a week and you writing it down.”

“I never asked you to go into the field.”

“You didn’t have to ask. You stood at the stile and looked.”

“I was measuring.”

“You were measuring.” Osselin’s voice cracked. “You’re always measuring. And somebody else is always carrying it.”

Vesket said something Riarhin did not catch. Osselin said something back, quieter. Then neither of them said anything for a while, and Riarhin cut another pad of felt and fitted it into the box round Vesper’s little brass compasses and found it was too large and took it out and trimmed it.

When she looked up, Osselin was sitting on the bed with her arms round her knees and Vesket was sitting on the floor with his back against the wall and his eyes closed. Neither of them was looking at the other. Neither of them had left.

“Here,” said Riarhin, and held out the box.

Vesket opened his eyes. He took it. He looked at the felt pads fitted round the compasses and the scale and the little resonance plate, each in its own hollow, snug and exact.

“You did these,” he said.

“They were knocking.”

He turned the box in his hands and listened. Nothing knocked. He looked at her.

“Thank you,” he said.

“I’ll carry it.”

He opened his mouth.

“I’ll carry it,” Riarhin said again. “You carry the case and the inks. She carries the food and the lanterns. I carry the charcoal and Vesper’s box and the court. They fit on the frame. I’ve measured it.”

Osselin, from the bed, said, “She’s measured it.”

“I can measure things.”

“Everybody can measure things,” said Osselin. “He’s the only one who can’t stop.”

Vesket laughed. Osselin did not look at him, but her shoulders came down a little, and after a moment she got off the bed and came and sat on the floor beside Riarhin and began, without being asked, to sort the food into what would be eaten first.

They ate supper on the floor among the sacks. Pellet sent up fish stew in a pot and a loaf and a jug of beer, and they ate the whole of it, and Riarhin ate three bowls and was still hungry and ate Osselin’s bread. Vesket told a tale about a man at Thornholt who had tried to build a bell that rang only for honest people and had cast it so badly that it rang for nobody, and had then claimed that this proved there were no honest people on the hill. Osselin said that was not how it went, he had claimed it proved the hill was full of liars, and they argued about it, and Riarhin laughed until she had to put down her bowl.

Tessel walked out from under the edge of the vellum and onto the floorboards and stood among the crumbs.

“She wants her figure,” said Vesket.

“She’s done one today.”

“She wants another. She does when there’s been shouting.” He moved the lamp so that the light fell on the floor beside the spider, and Tessel began to draw, very small, in the lamplight among the crumbs and the fish bones, a perfect lattice of six-sided cells that leaned, just slightly, north and east.

Pellet’s boy came up the stair after dark with a letter.

It had come down the coast road from the south, he said, from a carrier who had it from a carrier who had it from somebody at Thornholt, who had been told to send it on to wherever the Sumwright was. It was addressed to Riarhin in Coryd’s square letters. The seal was green wax and a thumbprint.

Riarhin opened it at the window by the light of the lamp.

A woman came to Drevan on the fifth day after you left. Mother Ennis says old, tall, a stick. She asked at the bridge house for the woman who mended the roofs. Mother Ennis told her I had gone to Valora, as we agreed. The woman asked whether I had taken my things. Mother Ennis said yes. The woman asked whether my sister had been to visit. Mother Ennis said she did not know my sister. The woman thanked her and asked about the eel traps and bought two eels from the old man at the weir and went away towards the river road.

She did not go to the Swan. Z. was singing there that night and did not see her. Z. says she would have known.

I am going to Valora on the twelfth. The bridge is finished. Mother Ennis has the key and the warden has the toll chest. H. has the other key and says she hates it.

It falls. He checked after rain.

Riarhin read it three times. Then she folded it and stood at the window with it in her hand, looking out across the dark shingle to where the arch stood black against the faintly lighter sea.

“What is it?” said Osselin.

“My sister.” She did not turn round. “Farieth went to Drevan and asked about her, and went away.”

“Then it worked.”

“It worked.”

“Then why do you look like that?”

Riarhin did not answer at once. She thought of Coryd on the roof of the fourth house, sliding down on her backside and dropping to the parapet without a sound. She thought of Mother Ennis, whom she had never met, a bridge-warden’s wife who had been told that the thing in the cupboard was dangerous and had stood in her own doorway and lied about it to a tall old woman with a stick.

“Because she was in the town where my sister lives,” she said. “Five days after I left.”

“And because you’re not there,” said Osselin.

Riarhin turned round.

Osselin was sitting on the floor among the sacks with her knees drawn up. Vesket had stopped putting away the vellum.

“That’s it, isn’t it,” Osselin said. “You’d like to be there. You’d like them all to stay exactly where they are until you get back, so you know where they are. And they won’t.”

Riarhin looked at her.

“They won’t,” said Osselin, more gently. “Mine won’t either. Posy’s doing my round. The miller’s got my keys round his neck on a bit of thread. Hodge Brookside’s taking his cows the long way and cursing me every step of it. Briar’s at her sister’s with a house full of cats.” She rubbed her face. “I don’t know where any of them will be by the time I get back. I have to let them be wherever they are.”

Riarhin looked down at the letter in her hand. I am going to Valora on the twelfth.

“Yes,” she said.

She put the letter inside her shirt.

The neap was two days off. On the next morning, Riarhin went down to the shingle with her knife and the roll of greasy felt and sat in the sun outside the net loft and cut pads for the copper case too, though Vesket had not asked her to, because the inks knocked a little against the rods when he carried it up the stair, and she had heard them.

Chapter 16 — The gate’s permission

The reeve who met them at the arch was the reeve whose hands had remembered.

Her name was Tern. She was very old and very small, smaller even than Vesket, with white hair braided round her head and a face as brown and creased as a walnut, and she stood on the black rock at the landward foot of the arch in the grey hour before dawn with a lantern in one hand and a wooden box in the other, waiting for them.

The tide was out. It had gone out further than Riarhin had ever seen a tide go, so that the floor of the cove lay bare for a quarter of a mile, a waste of wet sand and black weed and pools that held the grey sky. The arch stood out over the sand with nothing going through it now, its great dark span open to the morning, and beyond it, where the sea should have been, a long path of pale flat stones ran out across the sand, wet and shining, towards the edge of the water far out.

“That’s the tide-path,” Vesket murmured. “It’s only there at the neap.”

“Where does it go?”

“Out. Then through.”

Tern watched them come down the beach. When they reached the foot of the arch she lifted her lantern and looked at each of them in turn, Vesket, Osselin, Riarhin, a long look for each, and then she looked at what they carried.

“Sumwright,” she said.

“Reeve.”

“You have something of mine.”

Vesket took the old way-cord from his coat, the one with the two knots moved. He held it out on his open palm. Tern took it and ran it through her fingers, knot by knot, her eyes half closed, and Riarhin saw her fingers stop where the knots were close together and then go on.

“You’ve brought it back,” said Tern.

“You lent it to me.”

“I did. It’s returned. That’s finished.” She put it in the wooden box and closed the lid. “What else?”

Vesket opened the copper case and showed her the rods and the coals and the inks, and Vesper’s instruments in their felt hollows, and unfolded a corner of the copper court from its oilcloth. Copper and brass. Osselin showed her the lanterns, which were iron, and Tern looked at them a long time.

“Iron,” she said.

“Lantern iron,” said Osselin. “From my hearth. They’ve carried my light for eleven years.”

“Named?”

Osselin hesitated. “They have names. I don’t say them.”

Tern considered this. Then she nodded. “Wrap them, then. And keep them lit when you can.”

Osselin wrapped them in felt.

Riarhin showed her the two knives. Tern touched each sheath, then pointed to the sailcloth. Riarhin wrapped them and tied them shut.

Then Tern looked at Riarhin.

“The sword,” she said.

Riarhin unslung Remorse from her back. She held it in its scabbard in both hands, across her body, the way she had once seen a woman hold a child out for a blessing.

“Steel,” said Tern.

“Yes.”

“The Tide Court said it would be named at the arch.”

“Yes.”

“Then name it.”

Riarhin looked at the old woman, and at the black span of rock above them, and at the long path of wet stones running out to the sea. People knew the sword’s name. Na’ar had known it. She disliked being made to say it here, under the waiting arch.

“Remorse,” she said.

The word went out into the grey air under the arch and did not echo. Tern stood very still with her lantern. Somewhere out on the sand a bird called, once, and was silent.

“Remorse,” Tern repeated. She looked at the scabbard a long time. “That’s a hard name to carry.”

“I didn’t give it.”

“No,” said Tern. “Nobody gives the hard ones. Wrap it.”

Riarhin wrapped it. She had brought a length of sailcloth for the purpose and she wound it round the scabbard from hilt to tip and tied it at three places with cord. Tern watched her do it and nodded when she had finished.

“While the arch can see it, it stays wrapped. When you’re past where the arch can see, you’ll know. It won’t mind then.” She turned to Vesket. “And the iron she carried in. You know about that.”

“Yes.”

“Fourteen bundles. Wrapped and banded and named for salt.” Tern’s mouth thinned. “I watched them go by on the cart in the summer and I thought, that’s a great deal of salt. I didn’t think any more than that. I should have.” She opened the wooden box again and took out a new cord: three strands, green and undyed and brick-red, the same as the old. “How many days?”

“Forty.”

“The Court said forty.” She began to knot the cord. She did it without looking, her old fingers moving fast and sure, and Riarhin watched the knots go in: eight, and a space, eight, and a space. Five groups. “You’ll bring it back with forty knots,” said Tern. “Or fewer, if you come back sooner. Never more. You’ll bring it back together. All three of you. If one of you comes back without the others, the cord’s not closed.”

“And then?”

“And then I keep it.” Tern finished the last knot. “I have a box of cords that aren’t closed. It isn’t a large box. I’d rather not add to it.”

She held out the cord. Vesket reached for it. Tern did not let go.

“There’s a quicker way back,” she said.

Vesket looked at her.

“The cord can be tied for a return by another door. The spring-house above the Slow Water. The cellar comes out in the dell above my sheds, up the valley there.” She nodded towards the cliff behind Underarch. “You’d come out right here, two days’ walking saved. I can tie it that way. It’s a kindness. People ask for it.”

“The spring-house is shut,” said Osselin.

“I know it’s shut. I had the warden’s thread from the Kelling road two days ago.” Tern’s face did not change. “It might be open by the time you come back. I can tie the cord for it now and if it’s open you come home that way. If it’s still shut, you come back by the tide-path, same as you went.”

Riarhin looked at the cord in the old woman’s fingers, held out between her and Vesket.

“If you tie it that way,” Riarhin said, “what does it mean?”

Tern looked at her.

“It means you’ve promised to come home by the spring-house,” she said. “If you can.”

“And if we can’t?”

“Then the promise wasn’t kept.”

“Even if it was shut?”

“The cord doesn’t know it was shut.” Tern’s eyes were very bright in the lantern light. “The cord only knows what you told it.”

Nobody spoke. Out on the sand, the bird called again.

“No,” said Vesket.

Tern looked at him.

“No,” he said. “Thank you. Tie it for the tide-path. We can’t promise the spring-house. I don’t know that it’ll ever open. I don’t know that what we’re going to do won’t—” He stopped. “I don’t know. Tie it for the tide-path.”

Tern let go of the cord.

“The narrow way, then,” she said. “It’s longer. You’ll come out further north. The ground’s rougher.” She did not seem displeased. “Hardly anybody refuses it.”

“What happens to people who take it?” said Riarhin.

Tern looked at her a moment longer than was comfortable.

“They come home,” she said. “Usually. One way or another.”

She turned and led them out under the arch.

Riarhin went first, with the wrapped sword on her back. Osselin went second, with her lanterns wrapped and one of them lit and shuttered, carrying her own small flame. Vesket went last, with the case, because he had to keep the interval.

“What interval?” Riarhin had asked him on the beach.

“Between the stones. You walk the tide-path on a beat. Not too fast, not too slow. Eight stones, then a stop. If you get ahead of the beat, the path goes out from under you. If you get behind it, the tide comes back.” He had looked at her. “I’ll count it. You walk it. Don’t count it yourself. If we’re both counting we’ll get out of step.”

So she walked it, and he counted.

The tide-path was a line of flat pale stones, each a long stride from the next, wet and gleaming and slightly ridged, as though they had been laid by somebody with a very long leg. Riarhin stepped onto the first one. It was cold through her boot and very slightly soft, like stone that has been under water a long time.

“One,” said Vesket behind her, quietly.

She stepped onto the second.

“Two.”

They walked out under the arch. The span of black rock passed over them, wet and dripping, its underside hung with weed and small white shells, and the air under it was colder and smelled of salt and iron. Riarhin felt the arch’s attention on her back like a hand laid flat between her shoulders, on the wrapped sword, and did not look up.

“Eight,” said Vesket. “Stop.”

She stopped. They stood on the stones under the arch, the three of them in a line, and the dripping went on round them.

“Go.”

They went. Eight stones. Stop. Eight stones. Stop. They came out from under the arch on the seaward side, and the light was different. It was brighter, and greener, and it came from the wrong direction, from in front of them, as though the sun were rising out of the sea.

Somebody called her name.

It came from behind her, from the beach, from Underarch: a voice she knew, calling her, the way somebody calls across a yard to tell you supper is ready. Riarhin. It was a woman’s voice. It was warm and slightly impatient. She knew it so well that her foot lifted to turn before she had decided anything.

“Don’t,” said Osselin.

Riarhin stopped with her foot raised over the next stone.

“It’s not anybody,” Osselin said. Her voice was low and steady, the voice she had used with Ivy on the bench. “It’s the ninth call. There’s always one. You don’t answer it. You don’t look round. You keep walking on his beat.”

The voice called again. Riarhin. Nearer this time, and puzzled, as though the woman calling could not understand why she did not come.

She had known the voice since the first syllable. It was Ailsa’s.

She put her foot down on the next stone.

“Five,” said Vesket behind her, very quietly. His voice was not steady. “Six.”

“Seven,” said Osselin.

“Eight. Stop.”

She stopped. She stood on the wet stone with the green light in her face and her hands shaking at her sides. Behind her, very faintly, the voice called once more, and then did not call again.

“Go,” said Vesket.

They went. Eight stones. Stop. The path ran on across the sand, and the sand was no longer sand; it was moss, wet and bright, and the stones were no longer pale but grey and furred with lichen, and the green light came down from above now, through a canopy so high and so thick that the sky had gone.

Riarhin stepped off the last stone onto the forest floor.

She stood still. Behind her, Osselin stepped off, and then Vesket, breathing hard, with the copper case held level in front of him. Nobody spoke.

There was no arch behind them. There were trees: great grey-barked trees with roots like walls, rising out of the moss into the green dimness, and between them, ferns, and the soft constant sound of water somewhere, and birds she did not know. The air was warm and damp and smelled of leaf-mould and something sweet underneath, like bruised fruit.

Riarhin knew where she was.

Ahead of them, thirty paces away between the trees, stood a split rock: a great grey boulder the height of a house, cracked from top to bottom so that light came through the crack. She knew it. At the beginning of her second year, Ghere had sent her up it. She had refused to come down the far side. He had waited at the bottom and sent her up again.

She had always seen it from the south, with the crack running from upper left to lower right.

Now the crack ran from upper right to lower left. She was seeing it from the north. From the side she had never stood on, the side she had refused to climb down.

She stood and looked at it a long time.

Behind her, Osselin said, quite gently, “You know it.”

“Yes.”

“Is that good?”

Riarhin unwrapped the sword. She untied the three cords and folded the sailcloth into her pack, and slung Remorse on her back again, and felt its weight settle.

“I don’t know yet,” she said.

Part III — The forest she knows

Chapter 17 — A bed above the roots

The first night, Riarhin would not let them sleep on the ground.

They had walked from the split rock all afternoon, north and east, by Vesket’s reckoning and Tessel’s lattice, through forest that went on and on without changing: grey trunks, moss, ferns, the green light coming down from very high up. There were no paths. There were places where it looked as though there might be paths, long clear stretches between the trees where the moss was flat and the ferns stood back, and each time they came to one Riarhin went round it.

Osselin noticed on the third time.

“Why are we going round?”

“Because it’s clear.”

“That’s a reason to go through.”

“Not here.”

Osselin looked at the long green avenue with the light lying in it. Then she looked at Riarhin and did not ask again.

When the light began to fail, they came to a place where the ground rose in a long hump between two streams, and the trees on the hump were very old and very large, with roots that came out of the earth higher than a man’s head and went back into it again in arches and loops. Under the roots there were hollows, dry and floored with old leaves, large enough to sleep three. Vesket looked at them with frank longing.

“There,” he said.

“No.”

“It’s dry. It’s out of the wind.”

“Anyone on the hump can come down into it from three sides and you can’t see any of them.” Riarhin walked past the hollows to the biggest of the trees and put her hand flat on its bark. It was cool and smooth, faintly ridged. She looked up. Twenty feet above her, a great limb came out of the trunk almost level and went out over the stream, and at the place where it left the trunk there was a crotch as broad as a cart, filled with moss. “Up there.”

Osselin followed her gaze.

“You’re not serious.”

“I’m serious.”

“I can’t sleep in a tree.”

“You can climb.”

“I can climb. I can climb stiles and ladders and the tower at Wendle when the bell rope jams. That isn’t sleeping.” Osselin looked at the limb. “What if I roll?”

“You won’t roll. I’ll show you.”

Vesket said, “The case.”

Riarhin had been thinking about the case since the split rock. It had to stay level. It had to stay warm. It weighed as much as a small child.

“I’ll take it up.”

“It can’t tip.”

“It won’t tip.”

He looked at her with real distress. “It has coals in it. If it tips, the coals come out. If the coals come out on moss twenty feet up a tree—”

“Then we’ll be awake.” She unslung her pack. “Give me the rope.”

It took an hour and a good deal of Osselin swearing. Riarhin went up first, without her pack, finding her hands and feet on the bark and the knobs and old scars of fallen limbs, and in the crotch she found what she had hoped: the moss was deep and dry and the crotch was hollowed, a basin, with the great limb going out on one side and two lesser limbs on the other, so that it was walled almost all round. She tied the rope to the limb above. She came back down. She did not explain.

She sent the packs up first, hauling them on the rope. Then the charcoal sacks. Then Vesper’s box, wrapped in her own coat. Then she went up with the rope over her shoulder and hauled while Vesket stood below holding the copper case, and she brought the case up on a sling made of the sailcloth from the Gate, slowly, hand over hand, so that it rose without swinging, and Vesket stood at the bottom with his head tipped back and his face white and said nothing at all until she had set it down in the moss and unwrapped it and lifted the lid, and the coals were still glowing in their grate.

“There,” she called down.

He let out his breath.

Osselin came up next. She came up badly, with her heel first on every hold and her whole body pressed to the bark, and halfway she stopped and said, quite calmly, “I’m not going any further.”

“Yes, you are.”

“I’m going to stay exactly here.”

“There’s a hold above your right hand. A knob. Feel for it.”

Osselin felt for it. She found it. She went on. At the top Riarhin took her wrist and pulled her over the lip of the crotch, and she lay on her back in the moss and looked up at the leaves and said, “I hate you.”

“I know.”

“I’ll hate you in the morning too.”

“I know.”

Vesket came up last. He came up better than Osselin, quick and light, using his good hand and his knees, and at the top he went straight to the case and looked into it again, and then lay down beside it in the moss with his arm over his face.

Riarhin went down one more time.

She did not tell them why. She went down the trunk to the ground in the dusk and walked round the base of the tree, and round the hollows under the roots, and down to the stream on the north side and up again, and as she went she did small things. She pushed two fallen branches across the easy approach up the hump from the south, where the clear avenue came in. She broke off a frond of fern and dropped it, bent, at the mouth of the largest hollow, as though someone had been there and gone. She scraped the moss with her boot at the foot of the tree where they had climbed, and then went to a different tree twenty paces off and scraped the moss at its foot too, and pulled down a handful of bark from the lowest limb as though someone had tried it and given up.

Then she went back up the rope, and drew it up after her, and coiled it in the crotch.

Osselin had been watching her over the lip of the moss.

“What were you doing?”

“Making it look wrong.”

“Wrong how?”

Riarhin sat down with her back against the trunk. Now that she had stopped moving she could feel the day in her legs.

“If anyone comes up the hump tonight,” she said, “they’ll come by the clear way, because it’s clear. They’ll find the fern and think we were there and left. They’ll look at the foot of this tree and the foot of that one and not know which. And they’ll look up into both.” She paused. “From the ground you can’t see into this crotch. I looked.”

Osselin was silent a moment.

“You could have said that before you made me climb.”

“You’d have argued.”

“I’d have argued anyway.” Osselin turned on her side in the moss. “I’d have argued less.”

Vesket, from under his arm, said, “Who taught you that?”

Riarhin looked at the leaves above them, going dark.

“My brother,” she said.

Neither of them asked anything else.

They ate in the dark: bread, cheese, some of Pellet’s dried fish. Riarhin had told them there would be no fire, and Osselin had looked at her for a long time and then said nothing, and opened her lantern only once, briefly, shuttered to a slit, to see what she was eating. The copper case glowed faintly under its lid. Vesket fed Tessel on the edge of the felt. Riarhin could hear his nail tapping beside her.

The forest changed with the night.

It happened slowly, so that Riarhin did not notice at first. The green dimness went to grey and then to black, and the black was not empty. Lights came. They came out of the moss on the forest floor below, pale and greenish, small as candle flames, hundreds of them, then thousands, spread out between the trees as far as she could see, glowing very softly, so that the whole floor of the forest seemed to be a dark lake with the stars reflected in it. Fungus, she knew. Ghere had told her the name and she had forgotten it. She had never forgotten the sight.

Osselin sat up.

“Oh,” she said.

The lights pulsed very slowly, brighter and dimmer, as though they were breathing. Above them, in the canopy, other lights moved: tiny points of gold drifting between the leaves, rising and falling, insects of some kind, flying. And sounds came, very faint, from a long way off, a long falling note like a bird or like somebody singing a single word over and over.

Vesket had taken his arm off his face. He was lying on his back looking up at the drifting golden lights with his mouth a little open.

“I’ve read about this,” he said. “I’ve read the accounts. I never—” He stopped. “They don’t say how many.”

“No.”

“They say the floor glows. They don’t say—” He waved a hand at it. “They don’t say it’s like this.”

She had sat in a tree like this, on a night like this, at fifteen, with Ghere asleep on the limb beside her, and looked at the lights, and thought that she would live here always, that this was what she had been born for, that every hard thing he made her do was worth it to be allowed to see this. She had been wrong about almost everything she believed at fifteen. She had not been wrong about the lights.

“It’s beautiful,” said Osselin quietly.

“Yes.”

“Is it safe?”

Riarhin looked at the lights. They breathed.

“They’re lights,” she said. “They’re only lights.” She considered. “Don’t go down and walk in them.”

“Why not?”

“Because you won’t want to stop.”

Osselin lay back in the moss. After a while she reached out in the dark and found Riarhin’s ankle and held it, not tightly, the way a child holds a sleeve in a crowd. Riarhin let her.

Vesket fell asleep first. Then Osselin, with her hand still round Riarhin’s ankle. Riarhin sat awake with her back to the trunk and Remorse across her knees and watched the lights breathe, and listened.

Some time in the night, she heard something below.

It was very quiet. It was the sound of a foot set down on moss and lifted again, slowly, and set down again. It came up the hump from the south, along the clear avenue, and stopped. A long silence. Then it came on, round, from a different direction, and stopped again at the foot of the hump near the hollows under the roots.

Riarhin did not move. She did not reach for the sword, which was already under her hands. She breathed very slowly and listened.

The lights on the forest floor below breathed on as before. Nothing crossed them that she could see.

The sound did not come again. After a long time, an hour perhaps, a bird began to call somewhere in the canopy, one of the long falling notes, and then another answered, and the forest went on.

In the morning Riarhin went down first, alone, while the others slept.

The fern frond she had dropped at the mouth of the hollow had been moved. Not much. It had been bent towards the north, and now it was bent towards the south. The moss at the foot of the other tree, the one she had scraped, had been scraped again, very lightly, beside her mark, a single long line, as though somebody had drawn a finger through it.

And at the foot of the clear avenue, where she had laid the two fallen branches across the way, there was a place in the moss where somebody had stood still for a long time. The moss was pressed down in the shape of one foot, deep, and beside it the shape of another foot, much lighter, the weight held off it.

Riarhin crouched over the prints.

Then she went back up the rope.

Osselin was awake. She looked at Riarhin’s face as she came over the lip of the crotch, and sat up.

“Someone was there,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Her?”

Riarhin did not answer at once. She looked at Vesket, still asleep beside the case, with his burned hand on his chest. She looked at the moss where Osselin had slept with her hand round Riarhin’s ankle.

“She waited,” said Riarhin. “She looked at both trees. She didn’t climb.”

“Why not?”

“Because she didn’t know which.” Riarhin sat down. “And because if she climbed the wrong one, she’d be on a rope twenty feet up with her back to the other.”

Osselin looked at her for a long moment.

Then she lay down again in the moss and pulled her coat over her head.

“I still hate you,” she said from under it. “I want that clear.”

Chapter 18 — The path that offers itself

On the third day they found a path that wanted to be walked.

It came out of the forest on their left without any warning: a long, clean, straight way between the trees, paved. The paving was old, great slabs of pale stone fitted so close that the moss grew only in a thin green line along the joins, and it ran away ahead of them through the forest in a perfectly straight line, level as a floor, with the trees standing back from it on either side in two even ranks. At the far end of it, a long way off, where the light came down more brightly, Riarhin could see something pale and tall: a broken column, or the corner of a building, white against the green.

“Oh,” said Vesket.

“No,” said Riarhin.

He looked at her. “I haven’t said anything.”

“You were going to.”

“I was going to say that it goes the right way.” He held up Tessel’s morning lattice, which he had drawn onto a scrap of paper with the blue ink so he could carry it. It leaned north and east. The path ran north and east. “It goes exactly the right way. It goes to a ruin. Her papers mention ruins on the approach. There might be—”

“It goes exactly the right way,” said Riarhin, “and it’s clear, and it’s level, and it’s paved, and it’s been waiting for us since we came out of the trees.” She looked down the long straight avenue. “Look at the edges.”

He looked.

“What about them?”

“The trees stand back. Even. Both sides. Like a road with walls.” She pointed. “Anybody walking down the middle of that is in the open from both sides for half a mile, and there’s cover all along both edges. Every tree.”

Osselin said quietly, “Like the clear way on the hump.”

“Like the clear way on the hump. But longer.” Riarhin stood at the edge of the forest, looking. “Ghere used to set me to walk down a place like this and then he’d be in the trees. He’d let you get halfway.”

“And then?”

“And then you were dead.” She turned to them. “We go along it. Not on it. In the trees beside it, on this side. Where we can see the path and what’s on it, and nothing on the path can see us.”

“It’s slower.”

“It’s slower.”

Vesket looked at the long paved way, shining pale and level, and at the tangled forest beside it, all roots and ferns and fallen trunks.

“Very well,” he said, and sighed.

It was slower. They went parallel to the path, twenty paces in among the trees, and the going was hard: up over roots, down into hollows, round the fallen trunks of trees so old that their bark had gone to moss and their wood to something soft and red that gave under the foot. Riarhin went first and chose the way, and the others followed, and every so often she stopped and went forward alone to the edge of the trees and looked at the path.

On the fourth time, she saw the marks.

They were on the path itself, in the middle, and at first she took them for a crack in the paving. Then she saw that they were two cracks, parallel, a little wider apart than a cart’s wheels, running straight down the middle of the path towards the ruin. She went out onto the edge of the paving, crouching, and looked.

They were not cracks. They were gouges. Something very heavy had been dragged down the path, and it had been dragged on something with two narrow iron runners, and the runners had bitten into the old pale stone so deeply that in places the slabs had cracked right across, and in one place a slab had broken in two and tipped, so that one half stood up on its edge out of the paving like a broken tooth.

She put her hand in one of the gouges. It came to her second knuckle.

Behind her Vesket said softly, “That isn’t the bundles.”

“No.”

“The bundles were dragged on a sledge in Underarch. On the beach. You said the marks were shallow.”

“They were.”

“This is—” He crouched beside her and put his own hand in the gouge, and drew it out, and looked at the stone dust on his fingers. “This is something much heavier. On iron runners. On stone.”

“Yes.”

He looked down the path towards the ruin. Then he looked back the way they had come, along the gouges, to where the path came out of the forest.

“Those don’t come from the Gate,” he said slowly.

Riarhin had seen it too. The gouges did not begin where the path came out of the forest on the south, where they had come. They came from the east. They came out of the trees on the far side of the path a hundred paces back, through a place where the forest had been broken down, saplings snapped and ferns crushed flat in a wide swathe, and turned onto the path and ran down it towards the ruin.

“From the coast,” said Riarhin. “The other coast. The Thin Shore. There’s a portage there. They drag boats across it.” She stood up. “With iron weights.”

Vesket looked at her.

“She couldn’t get her last two bundles,” Riarhin said. “So she went and got something else. Something heavier.” She looked at the broken slab standing up on its edge. “She went up the coast and took a counterweight from the portage and dragged it in from the east because she couldn’t get it through the Gate. It’s three times the weight of a bundle. Four. She’s had to drag it on runners on stone because nothing else will carry it.”

“Alone?”

Riarhin looked at the gouges. Beside them, in the stone dust that lay in the bottom of the cracks where the runners had passed, there were footprints. One deep. One light. One deep. One light.

“Alone,” she said.

Osselin had come out of the trees behind them. She stood at the edge of the paving and looked at the broken slab.

“She’s broken the road,” she said.

“Yes.”

“That road’s been here—” Osselin looked down the long straight way. “I don’t know how long. Longer than Wendle. Longer than anything.” She looked at the gouges. “And she dragged a lump of iron down it and broke it, and didn’t care.”

“She cared,” said Riarhin. “You can see where she tried.” She pointed. Further down, the gouges swerved off the paving into the moss beside it, and came back onto the stone where the trees closed in. “It was too heavy for the moss. It sank.”

“And break it.”

“And break it.”

Vesket was still crouched by the gouge. He had taken out his rods.

“Don’t,” said Osselin.

“I need to know—”

“You need to know a lot of things. You don’t need to strike those in the middle of an open road in a forest where somebody stood under our tree all night.”

He looked at her. Then he put the rods away.

They went on through the trees towards the ruin.

The ruin was a building, or had been. It stood at the end of the paved way in a wide clearing where the trees fell back, and it had been tall and white and full of columns, and most of it had fallen down. What remained was a great half-circle of white pillars, broken off at different heights, standing round a floor of the same pale stone as the path, and at the back of the half-circle, a wall, with an arched doorway in it that went into darkness.

The gouges ran straight across the floor between the pillars and into the doorway.

Riarhin stopped at the edge of the trees and would not go further.

“She went in there,” said Vesket.

“Yes.”

“With the counterweight.”

“Yes.”

“Then that’s where—”

“That’s where she went in. Days ago. Weeks.” Riarhin looked at the dark arch. “It isn’t where she is.”

“How do you know?”

“Because she wants me to go in there.”

She had seen it a moment before she said it. On the nearest pillar, the one at the very end of the half-circle, closest to where they stood among the trees, something was tied. It was a strip of cloth, pale, waxed, the kind of linen the iron had been wrapped in. It had been tied round the pillar at the height of a tall woman’s eyes, neatly, with a flat knot. And the end of it had been folded. In three. With the corner tucked under.

Riarhin stood and looked at it.

Osselin saw it too. She did not say anything. Vesket looked from Riarhin’s face to the pillar and back.

“Is that—”

“Yes.”

“For you?”

“For me.” Riarhin heard her own voice come out quite level. “She knows I’m following. She knew at the tree.” She looked at the cloth. It hung very still in the windless clearing. “It’s a greeting.”

“A greeting.”

“She’s courteous.”

Osselin made a sound under her breath.

Riarhin did not go to the pillar or the doorway. She stood among the trees for a long time and looked at the dark arch.

Inside the arch, just visible from where she stood, something stood on the floor. It was a shape, low and angular, catching what light came in through the door. Iron. She could see rods, black, set upright in a ring, bent over at their tops, their ends meeting in the middle like the ribs of a cage.

“That’s one of the bundles,” said Vesket softly. He had seen it too. “Set up. She’s set it up. In a ring. That’s—” He stopped. He was leaning forward, his whole body straining towards it. “I need to see that. I need to know what it’s for. If I knew what that was for—”

“If you go in there,” said Riarhin, “you go in through the one door, down a dark passage, past a thing she left for us to find, on a floor she’s walked a dozen times and we haven’t walked once.”

He was still leaning towards the doorway.

“We could break it,” said Osselin. “Couldn’t we? If it’s hers. If it’s part of whatever she’s doing. We could go in and pull the rods out and break it.”

Riarhin looked at her.

“Do you know what it does?”

“No.”

“Does he?”

Vesket said, without turning round, “No.”

“Then we don’t know what breaking it does.” Riarhin looked at the iron ribs in the dark arch. She thought of the threshold stone in the spring-house cellar, and the water rising round her knees, singing. “It might be nothing. It might be something she left so that we’d break it. It might be the thing that’s holding something else in place, and we pull it out and it lets go.” She shook her head. “I don’t know. Neither do you. We leave it.”

“And go where?”

Riarhin looked down at the gouges.

But the counterweight was not inside. The iron ribs stood in the doorway in their ring, and nothing else did. And at the side of the doorway, half-hidden behind a fallen column, there was another, narrower opening, low in the wall, and from it the gouges came out again. Shallower, now. Further apart. As though the load had been shifted onto a different sledge, wider, so that the weight spread. They came out of the low opening and went away across the far side of the clearing, north and east, into the trees.

“She brought it here,” said Riarhin. “She set up one of the bundles in there. And she took the counterweight on.” She looked north and east, where the gouges went into the forest. “That’s where she’s going.”

“And the thing in the doorway?”

“Is in the doorway.”

Vesket turned at last.

“I want to see it,” he said. “I want to see it so much I can feel it in my teeth.”

“I know.”

“If I knew what it did—”

“You’d know what she meant to do.” Riarhin looked at him. “And we’d lose a day, and she’d know we’d stopped, and she’d know exactly where we were while we stood in that doorway with our backs to the trees.”

Vesket looked at her a long moment. Then he looked at Osselin, who was standing beside him with her arms folded and her mouth set.

“Ossie,” he said.

“Don’t.”

“Am I being a fool?”

Osselin was silent. Then she said, “Yes. But you’ve been a fool about the right things before.”

He laughed, a small cracked sound. He looked at the iron ribs one last time.

“All right,” he said. “We follow the counterweight.”

They went round the edge of the clearing through the trees, keeping well back from the ruin, and picked up the shallower gouges where they came out on the far side and followed them into the forest. Riarhin went first. She did not look back at the pillar where the strip of linen hung, folded, very still.

An hour later they came to a stone.

It stood at the side of the way, beside the gouges, a tall grey standing stone as high as Riarhin’s shoulder, and it was covered in lichen. The lichen was of many colours, grey and green and a pale bright yellow and a rusty orange, and it grew on the stone in patches and rings and spreading crusts, as lichen does. Then Riarhin looked again and saw that it did not grow as lichen does. It grew in lines. In marks. In something very like writing.

Osselin stopped in front of it.

“That’s a word,” she said.

The marks were in the yellow lichen, at the height of her face. They were in letters Riarhin knew, the old letters, the ones on Eshai’s papers. She read it without meaning to.

Well met, travellers. Who goes?

It was the ordinary greeting a person might give on a road, the kind she had heard a hundred times in the forest when she was young, from people coming the other way. And under it, in the orange lichen, smaller, there was more, and the more was fresh. The orange was brighter than the rest, the edges soft and new.

I go, it said. I am—and then there was a name.

It was half a name. The first part of it. Riarhin read it and felt her skin go cold. She knew the first part of that name. Zemia had said the whole of it at Ailsa’s cutting table, with a dead woman’s waistcoat spread between them.

“What does it say?” said Osselin.

“It’s a greeting.” Riarhin did not take her eyes off the stone. “Somebody came this way and it greeted them. And they answered.”

“With what?”

“With their name.”

Osselin was looking at the yellow marks. Her lips moved. “Well met,” she said slowly, sounding out the shapes she could not read. “Well met, travellers.”

“Don’t,” said Riarhin.

“I’m only—”
