“Don’t read it aloud. Don’t answer it.” Riarhin put out a hand, not touching her. “It asked who goes. If you say anything back to it, it’ll take what you say and grow it. Look.” She pointed at the orange lichen, the half-name, soft and bright. “It’s still growing hers. It’ll have her whole name on this stone in a month.”

“And then?”

“I don’t know.” Riarhin had heard of stones like this, once, from Ghere, in a tale told to frighten her. She had not believed it. “And then it knows her.”

Osselin took a step back from the stone.

Vesket had not spoken. He was looking at the half-name in the orange lichen with an expression Riarhin could not read.

“She answered it,” he said. “She gave it her name.”

“Half of it.”

“She must have known better.”

“She was dragging a counterweight,” said Riarhin. “Alone. On a bad leg. She was tired.” She looked at the soft orange marks. “She was polite.”

“You’re very polite yourselves,” said a voice, “for people standing in front of a scribe’s stone with your mouths shut.”

Riarhin turned with her hand on her sword.

There was someone sitting on a fallen log behind the stone, in the ferns, where a moment before there had been nobody. A woman, or a person shaped very like one, small and brown, wrapped in a cloak that seemed to be made of moss and was, perhaps, moss. She had a tray across her knees, a flat board, and on the board were little pots and dishes and something that might have been a brush made of a single long feather. Her face was lined like old bark. Her eyes were very green.

“Don’t draw that,” the woman said mildly, looking at Riarhin’s hand. “I’ve had a long morning.”

Riarhin did not draw it. She did not take her hand away.

“Who are you?”

“I tend the stone.” The woman nodded at it. “Somebody has to. It grows what it’s given, and if nobody tends it, it grows what it likes.” She looked at the orange lichen and made a small disapproving sound with her tongue. “Like that. I came to see to that. Somebody answered it last week with half a name and it’s been trying to grow the rest ever since. It’s very bad for it.”

“Can you stop it?”

“I can tend it.” The woman lifted the feather brush. “I can’t unwrite what’s been given. I can keep it from spreading.” She looked at them, one after another, Riarhin and Osselin and Vesket. “You didn’t answer.”

“No.”

“That was well done. Most people answer. They think it’s only manners.” She dipped the feather in one of the little pots and leaned forward and touched it, very delicately, to the edge of the orange marks, where they were softest and newest. “You’re going north.”

“Yes.”

“Following that.” She did not look at the gouges.

“Yes.”

The woman painted another careful stroke along the edge of the half-name. The orange lichen under her feather went slightly paler.

“You won’t get through the old seats,” she said. “Not this month. They’re full.”

“What old seats?”

“The amphitheatre. Up the way.” The woman nodded north and east, where the gouges went. “The boughs are mustering. They’ve put the green up. Anybody going through the seats without leave is trespassing, and they don’t like trespass when the green’s up. They don’t like anything when the green’s up.” She painted another stroke. “Whoever dragged that through went round. Through the bad ground on the east. Took her four days, I should think.”

“How do we get leave?”

The woman stopped painting. She looked at Riarhin with her very green eyes.

“You ask,” she said. “At the seats. Of whoever’s sitting.” She went back to the stone. “I’ll tell you which way to go. Don’t ask me anything else. I’ve a lot to do.”

Chapter 19 — The occupied seats

They heard the muster before they saw it.

It was a sound like water at first, a long low murmur coming through the trees from the north, and then as they came nearer it broke apart into voices: many voices, a crowd, talking all at once and none of them raised. There were other sounds under it. The knock of wood on wood. Animals, perhaps, a bleat and a lowing. A child crying and being hushed.

Then the trees opened, and Riarhin stopped.

The amphitheatre was a bowl in the earth, wide as a village, and its sides went down in tiers, and the tiers were roots. Great grey roots, thick as a horse’s body, curved round the bowl in rings one below another, worn smooth and flat on top and furred with moss, so that they made seats. The trees whose roots they were stood all round the rim, enormous, older than any she had seen, leaning slightly inward over the bowl so that their crowns met overhead and the light came down green and dim and broken. At the bottom of the bowl was a flat round floor of packed earth, and in the middle of the floor, a raised place, a platform of roots woven together, where somebody was speaking.

She had sat on those roots. At fifteen, with Ghere, on a winter evening, watching a woman tell a tale to a hundred people, every one of whom had heard it before. The seats had been half empty. The bowl had been quiet except for the speaker’s voice and the drip of water off the leaves.

It was not quiet now. Every seat was full.

Families, children, the old wrapped in blankets. Most were Lethai-al, tall and pale-haired, but not all: Riarhin saw others among them, smaller, broader, people she did not have names for. There were animals: goats on the lower tiers, tethered to roots, and on the far side a group of tall pale deer with braided cords on their antlers, lying folded in the moss like cattle. And over the seats, at intervals all round the bowl, there were banners. Green cloth, long and narrow, hung from poles thrust into the roots, one above each group of people, each a slightly different green, some embroidered, some plain, all of them hanging very still in the windless air.

“The green’s up,” said Osselin softly.

“Yes.”

“What does it mean?”

Riarhin watched the banners. She knew the word for them. She had not known that she knew it until she saw them. Boughs, it meant, or something like it, and the gathering under them was a word that meant both muster and mending, as though the two were the same thing.

“It means they’re all here,” she said. “Every holding from here to the river. They’ve come together to decide something.”

“Decide what?”

Riarhin listened to the voice from the platform. It was a man’s voice, a strong one, carrying easily across the bowl, and he was speaking in the forest language, the old one. She understood the words. She understood perhaps two words in three of what they meant.

“Land,” she said. “Ground that’s gone wrong. Somebody’s holding up the river has gone—” She did not have the word. It was a word that meant folded and forgotten at once. “It isn’t there any more. Since the spring. Fifty people need somewhere to go before the winter. They’re deciding who takes them.”

Vesket looked at her quickly.

“Since the spring,” he said.

“Yes.”

They stood at the rim of the bowl in the shadow of the trees. Nobody had noticed them yet. The way north lay across the bowl, down the tiers and over the floor and up the far side, where Riarhin could see a gap in the trees on the rim and a path going away into the forest beyond. The gouges of Farieth’s sledge did not go that way. Riarhin had seen where they turned off a quarter mile back, east, into ground where the trees grew close and the earth was broken and boggy.

“We need to cross,” said Vesket.

“We need leave.”

“From whom?”

Riarhin looked at the platform. The man speaking was tall and broad, with pale hair braided down his back and a green cloak over his shoulders, and he stood alone on the woven roots with his arms spread, and every face in the bowl was turned to him.

“From him,” she said.

She went down the tiers.

She went down by the steps at the end of the roots, where people went up and down, and she went slowly, with her hands open and away from her sides, and the sword on her back, and the others behind her. People turned to look as she passed. A child on the third tier pointed at her hair and was pulled down by its mother. An old man on the fifth tier said something she did not catch. She came down onto the packed-earth floor of the bowl and walked out across it towards the platform, where the tall man was speaking, and stopped at the foot of the woven roots, and waited for him to finish.

He did not finish. He saw her and went on speaking. His voice did not change. His eyes rested on her for a moment and moved on.

The man talked about the holding up the river. He named families. He named the number of their goats. Riarhin waited until he paused for breath, and then she spoke, in the forest language, clearly, the formal greeting she had been taught at fifteen for a stranger coming to a gathering:

“I come to ask leave to cross.”

The silence that followed was very large.

It went out from the platform across the floor of the bowl and up the tiers, row after row, until the whole of the great amphitheatre was quiet, hundreds of people, and the goats, and the pale deer lifting their heads in the moss. The tall man on the platform looked down at her. His face had not changed at all.

Then a voice behind her, from the lowest tier, said in the forest language, quite mildly, “She has spoken into the bough.”

Riarhin turned.

The person who had spoken was an old woman, very old, sitting on the lowest root of the bowl, close to the floor, with a blanket round her shoulders and a long flat board on her knees, like the moss-scribe’s tray, covered in marks. She had not been among those Riarhin had looked at. She was sitting low, in a plain place, under no banner at all. Her hair was white and very thin and her eyes were half closed.

Around her, Riarhin saw, people were looking past Riarhin, past the man on the platform, at the old woman. Waiting for her.

Riarhin felt her face go hot.

“Grandmother,” she said, the word one uses to the old. “I didn’t—”

“No,” said the old woman. “You didn’t.” She opened her eyes. They were pale grey, nearly white. “You spoke to the speaker while he held the bough. You asked him for something he can’t give. He doesn’t hold the seats. He holds the bough. You’ve asked the bough to stop and listen to you.” She looked at Riarhin for a long moment without expression. “It has. It will think about that for some time.”

On the platform, the tall man had folded his arms. He was looking at the old woman too.

“I’m sorry,” said Riarhin.

“Yes,” said the old woman. “I expect you are.” She looked past Riarhin at Osselin and Vesket, standing on the floor of the bowl behind her. “Those are not of the forest.”

“No.”

“And you are.”

“Partly.”

The old woman looked at her a while longer.

“Partly,” she repeated. She turned her board a little on her knees and ran a finger down the marks on it. “Partly of the forest, who knows the greeting and not when to give it, and who brings two from outside to the muster when the green is up, and asks to cross.” She looked up. “No.”

“Grandmother—”

“No. Not while the green is up. Not across the seats. You’ve spoken into the bough. You can go back the way you came, or round by the bad ground on the east, as the other one went.” She went back to her board. “Speaker. Go on.”

On the platform, the tall man unfolded his arms. He looked at Riarhin once more. Then he began to speak again, picking up the list of goats exactly where he had left it.

Riarhin stood on the floor of the bowl with her face burning.

Then she turned and walked back up the tiers, with Osselin and Vesket behind her, and nobody spoke to them, and the speaker’s voice went on behind them, naming families and their animals.

At the rim, in the shadow of the trees, she stopped.

“I’m sorry,” she said.

“What happened?” said Osselin.

Riarhin told her. Osselin listened. Vesket listened. When she had finished, Osselin said, “So you asked the wrong person.”

“Yes.”

“In front of everybody.”

“Yes.”

“While he was talking.”

“Yes.”

Osselin was quiet a moment. Then she said, “I did that once. At the Kelling moot. I was nineteen. I asked the reeve for the lantern rights and the reeve was in the middle of the harvest blessing. They didn’t let me hold a lamp for a year.”

Riarhin looked at her.

“You knew the greeting,” said Osselin. “You didn’t know who it was for. That isn’t the same as not knowing anything.”

“It cost us the crossing.”

“It cost us a crossing.” Osselin looked east, towards the bad ground. “There’s the other way.”

“Four days,” said Vesket. “If it took her four days with a counterweight, it might take us two without. But two days.” Two days of the thirty he had named at Thornholt, and ten of those already gone.

“You could go back,” said somebody. “And ask the right one properly.”

It was a young man. He had come up the side of the bowl behind them, on the outermost tier, and was standing on a root a little below them, so that his head was level with Riarhin’s chest. He was Lethai-al, very young, thin, with his pale hair cut short and a green cord round one wrist, and he had a basket on his hip full of something that looked like moss and was probably moss.

Riarhin looked at him.

“I asked,” she said.

“You asked wrong.” He said it without malice. “She’s the keeper of the seats. She says who sits and who crosses. She won’t let you cross now, not after you spoke into the bough. But she might let you go under.”

“Under?”

He nodded down at the bowl, at the woven platform where the speaker stood. “There’s a way under the stage. The old way. It goes under the floor and comes out on the north side. Nobody uses it. It’s blocked.” He shifted the basket on his hip. “The roots came down over it in the spring, when the ground went wrong up the river. Everything moved. The stage moved.”

“And?”

“And I tend the stage.” He said it with a certain pride. “The roots. I keep them from growing over the seats. I’ve been trying to open the way under since the spring and I can’t. It wants more than one. It wants somebody who can see where the roots were before they moved.” He looked at Riarhin. “You knew the greeting. You’ve been here before.”

“A long time ago.”

“Then you might remember.” He looked at Vesket and Osselin. “And they might help.”

“Why would the keeper let us?”

“Because it needs doing. The old way’s been shut since the spring, and some of the old ones can’t get up and down the seats.” He looked back down into the bowl, at the old woman on the lowest tier with her board on her knees. “And because it’s under. Not across. You wouldn’t be crossing the seats. You’d be mending the stage. She might let you go through when it’s done.”

“Might.”

“Might.” He looked at Riarhin. “I’m Teshan. I’ll ask her for you. Properly. If you want.”

Riarhin looked at Vesket. Vesket looked at the bowl, at the woven stage, at the speaker.

“Ask her,” he said.

Teshan went.

They waited at the rim. It was a long wait. Teshan went down the tiers by the steps at the end of the roots and across the floor of the bowl, round behind the stage, not in front of it, and came to the lowest tier where the old woman sat, and crouched beside her. He spoke. She listened, not looking at him. She said something. He spoke again. She ran her finger down her board.

Then she looked up, across the whole width of the bowl, at Riarhin standing at the rim.

Teshan came back.

“She says you can go under,” he said. He was out of breath. “If you open the old way. If you open only the old way and nothing else. If you don’t touch the stage. And if you put yourselves on the board.”

“The board?”

“Her board.” Teshan nodded down at the old woman. “The seat-roll. She keeps everybody who sits. Their name and their holding and who stands for them. She says if you’re going to work under the stage, you’re at the muster, and if you’re at the muster you go on the board.” He looked from one of them to the next. “Your names. And where you’re from. And who’d stand for you.”

Osselin said, “No.”

Teshan looked at her.

“No,” said Osselin again. Her voice was quite calm. “I’ll give my name. I’ll give my own name, if she wants it. I won’t give where I’m from.”

“She needs your holding.”

“My holding’s a village. Forty houses. A green with stones on it and a church with a bell that sometimes rings thirteen.” Osselin’s hands were folded in front of her. “I’m not putting forty houses on a board in a forest so that anybody who reads it knows where to find them.”

“Nobody reads it but her.”

“She reads it. And the next keeper. And whoever she tells.” Osselin looked down into the bowl, at the hundreds of people on the tiers, at the banners. “I don’t know what happens to a village when its name goes on a board like that. I don’t want to find out.”

Teshan looked at Riarhin, as if for help.

Riarhin looked at Osselin. Osselin did not look back. She was standing very straight at the rim of the bowl with her small hands folded.

Twenty days, perhaps. The bad ground on the east, two days, perhaps more.

“She’s right,” said Riarhin.

Teshan’s face fell.

“We’ll give our names,” Riarhin said. “Our own names. Not where we’re from. Not who’d stand for us. If the keeper won’t take that, we’ll go round by the east.”

“She won’t take it.”

“Ask her.”

Teshan looked at her for a long moment. Then he went down again.

Vesket said, very quietly, “Two days.”

“I know.”

“You heard what I said about the interval.”

“I heard.”

He did not say anything else. He stood beside her at the rim, looking down. After a moment Osselin unfolded her hands and put one of them, briefly, on Riarhin’s arm, and took it away.

Teshan was a long time with the old woman. Longer than before. They could see him talking, his hands moving. They could see the old woman not moving at all, her finger resting on her board. Once she looked up across the bowl at Osselin.

At last Teshan came back. He was frowning.

“She says your names,” he said. “And no holding.” He looked at Osselin. “She says she’s known keepers like you. She says she was one.” He shifted the basket on his hip. “And she says if the old way’s not open by the time the speaker’s done, you go round by the east anyway, and she’ll have your names on her board and nothing for them.”

“When’s the speaker done?”

Teshan looked down at the platform, where the tall man was still talking, naming families, naming goats.

“Tomorrow,” he said. “Noon. If nobody else speaks into the bough.”

Chapter 20 — The boards beneath the stage

The old way under the stage began behind it, at the foot of the lowest tier on the north side, where a dark opening went into the earth between two great roots.

Teshan showed them. He had a lamp, a little clay one with a wick floating in oil, and he held it up at the mouth of the opening so that they could see in. A passage went down, paved with the same pale stone as the road to the ruin, and then levelled, and ran away under the floor of the bowl. A few paces in, it stopped. The roots had come down.

They had come down like a falling wall. Great grey roots, thick as Riarhin’s waist, had pushed through the roof of the passage and the walls of it and lay across it in a tangle, wedged against each other and against the paving, so that the way ahead was a solid mass of root and earth and broken stone. Here and there, through the gaps between them, Riarhin could see the passage going on beyond, dark, and very far off, a grey glimmer of light.

“The north side,” said Teshan. “That’s where it comes out. Past the stage.”

Vesket was looking at the roots. He had set the copper case down at the mouth of the passage and was crouched in front of the tangle with Teshan’s lamp held close.

“They’re still alive,” he said.

“They’re the stage roots,” said Teshan. “Of course they’re alive. If you cut them, the stage comes down. And half the seats on this side.” He looked at Riarhin. “That’s why I couldn’t do it. I can’t cut them. I have to move them. And they won’t move unless they want to.”

“How do you make a root want to move?”

“You show it where it used to be.” Teshan looked at the tangle with a kind of exasperated affection. “They don’t know where they belong any more. If you can show them, they’ll go back. Slowly. If you push them the wrong way, they’ll come down further and the stage comes down with them.”

Riarhin went into the passage.

She went in as far as the tangle and stopped. She stood with the lamp in one hand and looked at the roots and at the walls and the roof of the passage, at where the roots had come through, and she let her memory give her what it had.

It gave her very little. She had walked this passage once, at fifteen, behind Ghere, in the dark, carrying a lamp exactly like Teshan’s. She remembered the paving under her feet. She remembered that it had been dry and smelled of earth. She remembered that she had been watching Ghere’s back and not the walls, because he had told her to watch his back, and so she had not seen the walls.

“Well?” said Teshan from the mouth of the passage.

“I don’t remember.”

“You said you’d been here.”

“I was watching somebody else.” She stood with the lamp and looked at the tangle, angry with Ghere, and with the fifteen-year-old who had been so careful to do exactly what she was told. “I remember the floor. I don’t remember the walls.”

“The floor’s no use. The floor’s still there.”

“I know.”

There was a silence. Then Vesket said, quietly, from behind her, “The floor’s some use.”

She turned.

He had come into the passage. He was crouched at the edge of the paving where the roots began, with the lamp close, looking at the stones.

“Look,” he said. “Look at the joins.”

She looked. The paving slabs were laid in a pattern, long and short, long and short, fitted close. In the joins between them a thin line of moss grew, as on the road to the ruin.

“They’re laid to a measure,” said Vesket. “Every slab. Look. Long, short, long, short. And the long ones are—” He took Vesper’s brass scale from his coat and laid it against a slab, and then against the next. “They’re in a proportion. The same proportion. All the way along.” He looked up at the roof, where the roots came through. “Whoever built this built it all to the same measure. The roof will be in the same measure as the floor. The walls. Where the roots came through the roof, they broke the measure. I can see where it’s broken.”

“Can you see where it was?”

“I can see where it should be.” He looked at her. “Which isn’t the same as where it was, if they built it badly. But they didn’t build it badly. Look at it.”

Teshan had come in behind them. He was looking at the paving with a frown.

“What does that do?”

“It tells you where the roots belong.” Vesket stood. He looked at the tangle of roots in front of him. “If I can show the passage what shape it was meant to be. Only for a little while. Only enough—”

“You can do that?”

“A little. For a little while.” He looked at Riarhin. “It’s a working. A small one. It makes a thing that’s gone out of shape want to go back to the shape it was made in. Only while I hold it. When I stop, it stops. If the roots haven’t moved by then, they won’t.”

“And if they move the wrong way?”

“Then I stop,” said Vesket, “and we run.”

Osselin, at the mouth of the passage, said, “That’s very reassuring.”

They worked through the night.

It was the hardest work Riarhin had done since the cliff path at the spring-house. Vesket sat on the paving in front of the tangle with the vellum unrolled across his knees and Vesper’s little compasses in his good hand and Tessel on her felt beside him, drawing, and he drew too, on a slate Teshan had brought, a figure that grew and grew: the measure of the floor, carried up the walls, carried across the roof. When it was done he sat for a long time with his hand open above it, not touching, and his lips moving. The lamp flame stood very straight. The air in the passage went very still, the way it had gone still in Brede’s lean-to.

Then the roots began to move.

It was so slow that at first Riarhin thought she had imagined it: a creak, deep in the tangle, a sound like a door opening a long way off. Then a shiver ran through the nearest root, the thick grey one lying across the passage at the height of her waist, and it shifted. A finger’s breadth. Upward. Towards the roof.

“There,” Vesket whispered. “There. That one.”

Riarhin put her hands on it. Teshan put his hands on it beside her. They did not push. Teshan had told them, a dozen times, not to push. They held their hands flat on the cool smooth bark and waited, and felt the root move under their palms, slowly, slowly, like something waking, and when it had moved a hand’s breadth it stopped, and Teshan said, “Now. Lift. Just a little. With it,” and they lifted, and the root went up another hand’s breadth and fitted itself into a groove in the roof of the passage that Riarhin had not seen until it was filled.

“There,” said Teshan, and his voice cracked. “That’s where it was.”

They did it again. And again. Root after root, through the night, Vesket on the paving with his face grey and wet in the lamplight, Riarhin and Teshan and Osselin with their hands on the roots, helping each one a little, the way you help a horse up a slope by walking beside it. Some roots went up into the roof. Some went sideways into the walls. One, very thick, went down, slowly, into a channel in the floor that ran along the side of the passage, and lay there like a sleeping animal, and Teshan sat back on his heels and laughed out loud.

“It was a gutter,” he said. “That one lived in the gutter. I’d never have known.”

Some time after midnight, somebody came down the passage behind them.

Riarhin heard him first. She turned with her hand going to her sword. A man was standing at the mouth of the passage with a torch, a big man, broad, with pale braids and a green cord on his wrist, and behind him, in the dark outside, she could hear the restless shifting of animals.

“Teshan,” the man said.

Teshan scrambled up. “Velhain.”

The man came in. He looked at the passage, at the roots lifted and fitted into the roof and walls, at the open stretch of paving where the tangle had been, and at Vesket sitting in front of what remained with his hand above his slate. His gaze travelled back along the cleared paving to Vesket’s hand.

“You’re opening it,” he said.

“The keeper said they could.”

“The keeper said they could open the old way.” Velhain looked at the roots. “It could be more than the old way.”

Teshan said nothing.

“I’ve forty head of the tall deer up on the north rim,” said Velhain. “And their calves. And the muster says we move them to the river holding before the frost, because the river people have lost their ground and they need the milk. And the only way down from the north rim to the river is across the seats.” He looked at Riarhin. “Or under.”

“This is the old way,” said Teshan. “It’s for people. It was always for people.”

“It could be for deer. If it were opened wider.” Velhain looked at Vesket. “You. Sumwright. Whatever you are. You’re putting the roots back where they were. Could you put them back a little higher? A little further into the walls?”

Vesket did not look up from his slate. His face was grey with effort.

“I can show them where they were,” he said. “I can’t show them somewhere else.”

“Could you try?”

“If I try,” said Vesket, “they won’t know where to go. And they’ll come down.”

Velhain looked at the roots. “You don’t know that.”

“No,” said Vesket. “I don’t know that. I know that I don’t know where they’d go. That’s enough.”

Velhain turned to Teshan. “You could ask the keeper. To let it be wider.”

“She said the old way. Only the old way.”

“She said that before she knew it could be done.”

“She said it.” Teshan’s voice shook. “And I gave her their names on the board, and if it’s more than the old way she’ll have their names for nothing and mine for worse.”

Velhain was silent. He stood in the passage with the torch held high and looked at the last of the tangle. Behind him in the dark the animals shifted and a calf bleated.

“Forty head,” he said. “And calves.”

“I know,” said Teshan.

“The river people need the milk.”

“I know.”

Osselin had been sitting against the wall of the passage with her hands in her lap. She got up.

“If the muster says the deer go to the river,” she said, “the muster can ask the keeper for the way. Tomorrow. In the seats. When the speaker’s done.” She looked up at Velhain. She came barely to his chest. “That’s how it’s done, isn’t it? You ask. In front of everybody. You don’t come down a hole in the middle of the night and ask the people mending it to make it bigger for you while nobody’s watching.”

Velhain looked down at her.

“You’re not of the forest,” he said.

“No. But I know what a muster’s for.” Osselin did not move. “It’s so that nobody does a thing like that in the dark.”

There was a long silence.

Riarhin’s hand was still on the hilt of the sword. She saw Velhain look at it, and at the small woman standing in front of him with her hands at her sides.

She took her hand off the hilt, slowly, so that he could see her do it. Something in his face changed.

“Tomorrow,” he said at last. “In the seats.”

“In the seats,” said Osselin.

Velhain turned and went back up the passage with his torch. They heard him outside, talking to someone, and the shifting of the animals, and then the sounds moving away along the rim.

Teshan let out a breath like a sob.

“He’ll ask,” he said. “He’ll ask tomorrow and she’ll say no, and he’ll know it was you.”

“Yes,” said Osselin.

“He won’t forget.”

“No.” Osselin sat down against the wall again. “People like that don’t. I’m sorry.”

Vesket opened the last of the roots an hour before dawn.

It was a small one, thin as a wrist, lying across the passage at the height of a step, and when it moved it moved quickly, almost eagerly, sliding up into a crack in the wall as though it had been waiting all night to be shown the way. Beyond it, the passage ran on, clear, towards the grey glimmer of light at its far end.

Vesket lowered his hand.

He did not get up. He sat on the paving with the slate in his lap and the vellum across his knees, and he put his face in his hands. His shoulders were shaking. Osselin went to him and knelt beside him and put her arm round him, and he leaned against her, and neither of them said anything.

Riarhin went down the passage.

She went alone, with Teshan’s lamp, to see that it was clear. It was clear. It ran under the floor of the bowl for perhaps two hundred paces, straight, and the walls on either side were faced with pale stone, and as she walked she saw that the stone was carved. She had not seen it at fifteen. She had been watching Ghere’s back.

The carvings were very worn. They were figures, in a long procession along both walls, tall people in long robes walking in a line towards the north end of the passage, carrying things. She could not tell what they carried. Bowls, perhaps, or lamps. At the head of the procession, on the north wall, where the passage began to rise towards the light, there was a larger figure, standing alone, with its arms raised, and over its head the stone had been carved with a circle, and inside the circle, lines, crossing, like the spokes of a wheel or the cracks in ice.

She stood in front of it with the lamp. She did not know what it was. Somebody had made it with great care.

She left it and went on.

The passage rose and came out under the roots of a great tree on the north rim of the bowl, among ferns, in the first grey light of the morning. Below her, behind her, the amphitheatre lay quiet, the people sleeping on the tiers under their banners, the deer folded in the moss. Ahead of her the forest went on north and east.

And east of her, perhaps half a mile away, where the land fell into a long boggy valley full of broken ground and drowned trees, she could see a line.

It was a line of broken saplings and crushed ferns, cutting straight across the valley from south to north, black against the grey-green of the bog. She could see where it had sunk, where somebody had laid branches under it, where it had been levered out and dragged on. She could see the place where it came up out of the bog on the far side and went on north, into the trees, towards whatever lay ahead.

Four days, the moss-woman had said.

Riarhin stood among the ferns at the mouth of the old way and looked at the line of wreckage across the valley, and thought of a tall woman with one good leg, alone in that bog, setting her foot and pulling, setting her foot and pulling, laying branches under the runners in the dark.

At fifteen she would have thought it the finest thing she had ever seen.

She turned and went back down the passage to tell the others it was open.

Chapter 21 — A missing case

The keeper let them through at noon, when the speaker had finished.

She did it without ceremony. Teshan went down to her and came back and said, “She says go,” and they went, down the tiers by the steps at the end of the roots, along the back of the stage where nobody would have to look at them, and into the old way under the floor of the bowl. Nobody stopped them. As they passed the lowest tier, the old woman lifted her head from her board and looked at Osselin, and Osselin looked back.

Velhain was on the north rim when they came out. He was standing among his deer, with a calf at his knee, and he watched them come up out of the old way into the ferns, and he did not say anything. Riarhin did not look at him longer than she had to.

They went on north and east.

The forest changed after the amphitheatre. The ground began to break. There were ridges now, and gullies between them, and outcrops of grey rock coming through the moss, and the streams ran faster and louder and cut deep channels with steep sides. The great trees grew further apart. Between them, sometimes, there were stones that had been shaped: a fallen lintel, a stretch of wall, the stump of a column in a bed of ferns. Once they passed a flight of steps going up a hillside to nowhere, broad and shallow and perfect, with a sapling growing out of the top step.

Farieth’s trail was easy to follow. Too easy, Riarhin thought, and kept them off it, parallel, as she had on the road to the ruin. The gouges of the sledge ran north and east along the bottoms of the valleys where the ground was soft, and every so often they came to a place where Farieth had had to drag the counterweight up a slope, and there would be a tree with rope marks round its trunk, scored deep into the bark, where she had used it to haul against.

On the second afternoon they came to a step.

It was a cliff, not high, perhaps three times Riarhin’s height, where the land dropped from one level of the forest to the next. A stream came over it in a white fall. The rock was grey and wet and broken into ledges, and at the bottom there was a wide shelf of flat stone and then the forest going on.

The gouges went along the top of the cliff and away east. Farieth had found a way round for the sledge.

“It’s two hours round,” Riarhin said. She had gone to look. “Down the ridge and back. It’s soft. She had to.”

“And down?” said Vesket.

Riarhin looked down the cliff. The ledges went down in steps. She could climb it. Osselin could climb it if she was careful and did not argue. Vesket could climb it.

The packs could not.

“We lower them,” she said.

They lowered them. Riarhin went down first, without her pack, to see the ledges and the shelf at the bottom, and then came back up and tied the rope round a tree at the top of the cliff and rigged it so that it ran down over a smooth place in the rock. They sent the food down first, in Osselin’s bag. Then the bedding. Then the charcoal, sack by sack, Riarhin paying out the rope at the top and Osselin at the bottom untying each load and sending the end back up.

Then the court in its oilcloth, which swung and knocked against the rock however slowly she let it down. Then Vesper’s box. Riarhin wrapped it in her own coat and lowered it so slowly that Osselin at the bottom called up to ask whether it had stuck.

Then the copper case.

Vesket would not let it go down alone. He wanted to go down beside it, on the ledges, keeping it level with one hand on the sling. Riarhin told him it would be safer if he went down first and waited at the bottom. He said it would not be safer for the case. She looked at him, at his burned hand still bound in Briar’s linen and his face set, and she let him.

So he went down the ledges beside the case, slowly, one ledge at a time, with his good hand on the sling and the case swinging gently between him and the cliff face, while Riarhin paid out the rope at the top, hand over hand, and Osselin waited at the bottom with her arms up.

They were halfway down when the rope jerked.

Riarhin felt it in her hands first, a tug and a twist, as though somebody had caught at the case from below. She looked down. The case was still there, hanging, a body’s length below Vesket on the cliff face. The rope above it was still there. And below the case, between the case and Osselin, the rope was not there.

It had been cut. Somebody had cut the tail of the rope where it hung below the case, the end Osselin would have caught. And the sling round the case, the sailcloth Riarhin had tied it with, was hanging open at one side.

The case tipped.

It tipped very slowly, like a thing in a dream. The sling opened. The case slid out of it, and turned in the air, and fell.

Vesket made a sound Riarhin had never heard a person make.

The case struck the ledge below him, and bounced, and struck the shelf at the bottom, and broke open. She saw the lid fly off. She saw a scatter of red coals and white wool and something bright, and then it was all lying on the shelf at the bottom, and Osselin was running towards it.

“Back,” Riarhin shouted. “Osselin, back—”

There was somebody on the shelf.

She was standing at the edge of it, where the forest began, under the trees, tall and still in a grey travelling robe, with a long pole in her hand. Not a pole. A spear. A slender ash shaft longer than she was tall, with a narrow leaf-shaped blade at the top that caught what little light came through the leaves. She was holding it in both hands, low, across her body. The blade was wet.

Osselin stopped dead.

Farieth looked at her. Then she looked up the cliff at Vesket, clinging to the ledge halfway down with his good hand, and then further up at Riarhin at the top.

She did not say anything.

She stepped forward, unhurried, to the broken case on the shelf, and put the point of her spear into the scatter of white wool and coals, and lifted something out on the blade. Riarhin saw it gleam. A rod. One of the copper rods. Farieth looked at it a moment on the end of her spear. Then she flicked it away, off the shelf, into the white fall of the stream, and it was gone.

“No,” said Vesket on the cliff.

Riarhin was already moving.

She did not go down the ledges. The ledges went down where Farieth wanted her to come down, onto the shelf, into the open, in front of the spear. She went sideways along the top of the cliff, fast, to where the stream came over, and went down beside the fall on the wet black rock, where the water roared and the spray hid her, and her boots slid and found purchase and slid again. Halfway down she felt something in the wet rock give under her hand and she let it go and caught the next hold with the other and swung, and came down the last of it in a scrambling rush into the plunge pool at the bottom, waist deep and so cold it knocked the breath out of her.

She came out of the pool with Remorse in her left hand.

Farieth had turned to face her. She had seen her coming down the fall and had moved, three paces, so that the broken case was between them, and Osselin was behind her, on her left, and the cliff with Vesket on it was behind her on her right.

“Osselin,” said Riarhin. “To the cliff.”

Osselin did not move.

“Osselin.”

“I’m not leaving him on a rock with that—”

“He’s coming down. Go to the cliff. Help him down. Stay by the rock.”

Osselin moved. She went sideways, along the foot of the cliff, never taking her eyes off Farieth, and Farieth let her go. She did not turn her head. She watched Riarhin.

“You came down the fall,” said Farieth.

Riarhin did not answer. She came round the plunge pool, slowly, keeping her distance, the sword low, its point towards the spear blade.

“That was well done,” said Farieth. “The ledges would have been easier. You’d have been in the open for six breaths.” She shifted her grip on the spear. Riarhin saw it. The right hand was bound with a strip of grey cloth round the palm and the wrist, and it held the shaft lower than the left, and less tightly. “I counted them.”

“You cut the rope.”

“I cut the rope.”

“Why not the man?”

Farieth looked at her with something like interest.

“Because you would have come down the ledges,” she said, “to him. And I would have had the six breaths.” She looked at the scattered wool and coals. “I wanted to see what you would choose.”

Riarhin came on. She was ten paces off. Eight. The spear was longer than her sword by the length of her arm, and Farieth held it the way a person holds a spear who has held one a long time, close and low, the blade moving very slightly, very slowly, side to side, so that it was never quite where the eye wanted to fix it.

On the cliff, Vesket was coming down. Riarhin heard him, the scrape of his boots, his breath. She did not look.

“Leave the case,” said Farieth. “It’s broken. The rods are gone. One of them is in the stream. The other—” She glanced down at the scatter. “The other is under my foot. It won’t sing again.”

“Step off it.”

“No.”

Riarhin went in.

She went in low and fast, to the left, to Farieth’s bad side, where the bound hand held the shaft. The spear came round to meet her. It came very fast, faster than she had believed a woman with a stiff leg and a damaged hand could move it, a sweep, the blade going across at the height of her chest. She went under it. She felt it pass over her. She came up inside the length of the shaft, where the blade could not reach her, and cut at the bound hand.

Farieth let go with that hand.

She swung the shaft one-handed with the left, short, the butt end coming round. Riarhin took it on the forearm. It was like being struck with a branch. She turned with the blow, and the butt slid off, and as she turned the blade came back from the other side, short, a backhanded draw, and she felt it go across the outside of her upper arm, the right arm, high, above the elbow.

It did not hurt. It felt like being touched with something cold.

She stepped back. Farieth stepped back. They stood four paces apart on the wet flat stone with the broken case between them.

Riarhin looked down at her arm. Her sleeve was cut open from the shoulder to the elbow, cleanly, as with a knife. Under it, a line of red was opening on the skin, a long shallow line, and as she watched, it filled and began to run.

“A graze,” said Farieth. “You moved well.”

Riarhin did not answer. She shifted the sword in her left hand. Her right arm had begun to burn.

From behind Farieth, at the foot of the cliff, Osselin said loudly, “He’s down.”

Farieth did not turn. But Riarhin saw her hear it.

“You’ve brought people,” said Farieth.

“Yes.”

“Into the forest. Where they don’t know the ground.”

“They know enough.”

“They know nothing. They’re standing exactly where I would put them if I wanted to use them against you.” Farieth did not move the spear. “I could take the small one now. You know I could. You’d come for her, and I’d have you in the open.”

Riarhin’s mouth was dry.

“Try,” she said.

Farieth looked at her for a long moment. Her face was quite calm. It was older than it had been at the walkway, Riarhin saw, thinner, the skin drawn tight over the bones, and there was grey in it that had not been there.

“Not today,” said Farieth.

She stepped back. Another step. She went back off the edge of the shelf into the trees, unhurried, with the spear low across her body and her eyes on Riarhin, and the ferns closed round her, and she was gone. Riarhin heard her for a few breaths, going away, one foot heavy and one foot light, and then she did not hear her any more.

She stood on the shelf with her sword up and her arm running blood down to her fingers and dripping onto the grey stone.

Then she turned.

Vesket was on his knees by the broken case.

He had come down the last of the cliff and gone straight to it, past Osselin, past Riarhin. The lid was off and lying a little way away, dented. The grate had broken out of its place and the coals were spread across the stone, most of them already grey. The wool was scattered and wet from the spray of the fall. The inks—Riarhin saw the pots, two of them broken, a stain of blue spreading across the wet stone like a bruise.

And the rod. The one Farieth had stood on. It lay in the wool, and it was bent, a long shallow curve in the middle where the weight had come down on it, and near one end, at the flaw where Vesket had let the die cool at seventeen, it had cracked.

Vesket picked it up. He held it in both hands, the bound one and the good one, and looked at it.

He did not say anything at all.

Osselin knelt beside him. She did not touch him. She began, very quietly, to gather the coals that were still alive into a heap on the stone, blowing on them, feeding them with a bit of dry moss from her pocket.

Riarhin stood over them. Her arm burned. Blood ran off her fingertips.

“I should have gone after her,” she said.

Nobody answered.

“I let her go. She was there and I let her go.”

“She’d have killed you,” said Osselin, not looking up. “Or me. Or him. She said so.”

“She said not today.”

“She said she could.” Osselin blew on the coals. A little flame came up. “You stood between her and us and she went away. I saw it.”

“And she took the rods.”

Osselin did not answer that.

Vesket put the bent rod down on the wool. He picked up the lid of the case and looked at it and put it down. He picked up one of the broken ink pots and turned it over and looked at the blue on his fingers.

Then he looked at Riarhin.

“You’re bleeding,” he said.

“It’s a graze.”

“It’s running off your hand.” He stood up. His face was white and very still. “Sit down. Ossie, the salve. And something to bind it.”

“It’s a graze.”

“Sit down,” said Vesket, quite loudly, and Riarhin sat down on the wet stone.

Osselin came with the salve. Vesket held Riarhin’s arm while Osselin cleaned the cut with water from the plunge pool and spread the mint-and-goose-fat over it and bound it with a strip of linen torn from the bottom of her own shirt. It was shallow, and it would heal. It hurt when Osselin pulled the binding tight. Riarhin sat still and let her.

Vesket did not say anything while they worked. When the binding was done, he went back to the case.

He picked up the wool and shook it out and laid it on a rock to dry. He gathered the unbroken ink pots and wrapped them in his handkerchief. He picked up the bent rod and looked at it for a long time, and then he wrapped it too, in a strip of wool, and put it in his coat. He did not go to the stream to look for the other one. Riarhin watched him not go.

“The coals,” said Osselin.

He looked. Osselin had a little fire going on the stone, a handful of coals glowing, fed with moss and twigs.

“Thank you.”

“They’ll want the grate.”

“The grate’s broken.” He picked it up. One of its little iron feet had snapped off. He turned it in his hands. “It’ll stand on three. It’ll stand crooked.” He set it in the case, crooked, and began to put the coals into it with a stick, one by one. “It doesn’t matter. There’s nothing to keep warm now.”

“The inks,” said Osselin.

“Two pots of the old inks.” He went on putting coals in the grate. “Vesper’s. The good blue’s gone. I’ll make it again if I can find apple vinegar in the middle of a forest.” He laughed, very short. “The rods are gone. The one I had since I was seventeen and the one I made beside it. The new ones are at Thornholt in a box. The others are in the stream and under her foot.” He put the last coal in. “I can’t measure the beat any more. I can’t measure anything. I’ve got a spider and two pots of ink.”

Osselin looked at him.

“And Vesper’s box,” said Riarhin.

Vesket stopped.

“It’s on the shelf,” said Riarhin. “It came down first. Before the case. In my coat.”

He turned. Vesper’s box sat at the foot of the cliff where Osselin had set it, wrapped in Riarhin’s coat, untouched, with the bedding and the food and the charcoal sacks beside it.

He looked at it for a long time.

“She didn’t take that,” he said.

“She didn’t know what it was.” Riarhin looked at the trees where Farieth had gone. “She knew what the rods were. She’s read your letters.”

“My letters.”

“To the hall. To Vesper. To Briar. You write everything. Briar said.” Riarhin held her bound arm against her body. “She’s been in Thornholt. Or she’s had someone there. She knew the rods were what told you what she was doing. She went for them.”

Vesket stared at her.

“She cut the rope below the case,” Riarhin said. “Not above it. She didn’t want it to fall on him. She wanted the rods.”

“And you.”

“And to see what I’d do.” Riarhin looked at the broken case with its crooked grate. “And whether you’d both stay where you were put.”

Vesket sat down slowly on the wet stone beside the case. He sat with his elbows on his knees and his hands hanging, the burned one and the good one, and looked at nothing.

Tessel came out of his coat.

She came out of the breast of it, where her basket must have been, and walked down his sleeve and onto the stone beside him, and began, very slowly, in the grey light by the white fall, to draw. A line. An angle. A line. She drew on the wet stone, between the case and Vesket’s knee, a small lattice of six-sided cells, and it leaned, very slightly, north and east.

Vesket watched her.

“Well,” he said at last. His voice was not steady. “There’s that.”

Chapter 22 — His wishes

The next day they came to the cutting.

It was a channel in the earth, deep and straight, with sides of dressed stone going down sheer to a stream at the bottom. It ran across their way from west to east as far as Riarhin could see in either direction, a long grey cut through the forest, perhaps thirty paces wide and three times her height deep. Old, very old. Ferns grew from the joints in the stone walls and small trees from the ledges, and the stream at the bottom ran fast and brown over a bed of fallen blocks.

There had been a bridge. Its two ends were still there, jutting out from either side of the cutting, broad stone abutments with the stumps of a span on them. The span itself was gone. It lay at the bottom of the cutting in the stream, in great broken pieces, half buried in silt.

Farieth was on the far side.

Riarhin stopped inside the trees and put out her good arm to stop the others. The sledge gouges ended at the near abutment and began again on the far side. Two ropes crossed between great trees, with a block and tackle hanging from them. On the near stones she could see where Farieth had turned the sledge and hauled it up.

The counterweight was not there. Farieth was alone on the far abutment with her spear, sitting on a block of stone, doing something to the stone in front of her.

She was setting iron.

Riarhin could see it from the trees. There were holes in the far abutment, square sockets cut in the stone in a ring, old sockets that had perhaps once held the posts of a railing. Farieth had a bundle open beside her, the waxed linen spread on the stone, the black rods lying in it. She was taking them one at a time and setting them in the sockets, upright, and bending their tops over towards the middle of the ring with her hands and her knee, so that they met. A cage of iron. The same as the one in the doorway of the ruin.

She did not look up.

“She knows we’re here,” said Osselin softly.

“Yes.”

“She’s letting us watch.”

“Yes.”

Vesket was looking at the cage of iron with his whole body leaning towards it.

“That’s a stay,” he said. “That abutment. That bridge was never a bridge. Or it was a bridge as well. It’s on the line. Look.” He pointed with his chin down the length of the cutting, west and east. “The cutting runs on the line. The bridge is on it. It’s one of the—” He searched for the word, and found it in his own language, and translated it. “One of the bindings. The things that hold the shape. She’s putting iron in it. She’s—”

He stopped.

“She’s doing what?” said Riarhin.

“I don’t know.” He was whispering. “I don’t know. I need the rods. I need to strike them on this side and then on that side and hear what the iron does to the beat. I need—” He stopped again. His hands had closed. “I can’t. I can’t, I haven’t got them.”

On the far abutment, Farieth set another rod in its socket and bent it over.

Riarhin came out of the trees.

She came out alone, onto the near abutment, and stood at its edge above the drop. She had left Remorse in its scabbard on her back. Her right arm, bound in Osselin’s linen, hung at her side. Thirty paces of empty air lay between her and her grandmother, and three times her height of drop to the stream.

Farieth looked up.

“Granddaughter,” she said.

Her voice carried easily across the cutting. It was the voice from the walkway, courteous and dry, interested.

“Grandmother.”

“Your arm.”

“It’s a graze. You said.”

“I did.” Farieth set down the rod she was holding. She did not stand up. She sat with the spear across her knees and looked across the cutting at Riarhin as though they were neighbours talking over a wall. “You came the quick way. Under the seats. I wondered whether you would. The old woman with the board is very particular.”

“You went round.”

“I went round. I had a heavy load.” She glanced at the ropes strung across the cutting. “Heavier than I’d planned. You did that.”

“Yes.”

“That was well done too. I hadn’t thought of the store. I should have. I’ve paid for storage often enough. I didn’t think you’d think of buying a man’s silence with the thing he already sells.” She paused. “Two marks and a month. It was a fair price.”

Riarhin did not answer.

“And now you’re here,” said Farieth, “with a Sumwright who has lost his rods and a lamp-girl from the downs, following me into the forest to stop me doing a thing you don’t understand.”

“Then explain it.”

Farieth tilted her head.

“You want me to.”

“I want to know what you think you’re doing.”

Farieth was silent for a while. She looked down at the half-made cage of iron beside her, and touched one of the bent rods with her bound hand, very lightly, the way Riarhin had seen Ailsa touch a seam to see if it would hold.

“You know what’s under the forest,” she said.

“Some of it.”

“You’ve read the papers. The ones the keeper copied. You know what I saw when my assistant stood in the room.” Farieth did not look up from the iron. “The seed answered. It answered a person. It knew a person was there, of a certain kind, and it moved towards them. Two boundaries went in less time than it takes to tell. I went into the vat because I had to. You’ve read that.”

“I’ve read that.”

“Then you know what I’ve been doing since.” Farieth lifted her head. “Your sister’s told you, and the papers. I went looking for the ones it would answer to. Your brothers and sisters. My son’s children. I meant to find them before it did.”

“And kill them.”

“And keep them from it.” Farieth’s voice did not change. “It comes to the same. I found some. You know which. And you know what happened at the shutter.” She moved her bound hand slightly. “I learned something at the shutter. Not what you think.”

“What, then?”

“That it can’t be done.” Farieth said it quite plainly. “Not that way. There are too many of you. You’re scattered across half the world. You have children. You’ll have more. The one who led me to the others has stopped leading me, and the rest have learned to keep their doors shut.” She almost smiled. “You taught them that. I could spend the rest of my life going from door to door and there would always be one more. I could spend it and fail.”

“So you’ve stopped.”

“I’ve stopped knocking on doors.” Farieth touched the iron again. “I’m going to the room.”

“I’m going to break it,” said Farieth. “The seed. The thing they caught and shut in the room and fed on for a thousand years. I’m going to break what holds it, and break it with it. When it’s gone, there will be nothing to answer. You can have as many children as you like. All of you. It won’t matter.” She looked across the cutting. “You see. I’m giving you what you wanted. You can stop running. So can they.”

“And everybody else?”

Farieth looked at her.

“The Sumwright told you,” said Riarhin. “He’s told the hall. He’s told everybody who would listen. When the holding breaks, the thing it holds—”

“Does what it does.” Farieth’s voice was very calm. “I know what he thinks. I’ve read his letters. He thinks that when the shape goes, what’s in it will spill out into the forest, and past it. He may be right.”

“And you don’t care.”

“I care. I’ve thought about it for longer than you’ve been alive.” Farieth set down the iron. “The ground up the river from the seats has already folded. The spring at the ferry has come up. A field in the downs is eating its own edges. I know. I’m not blind. Those are the first of it. There’ll be more, before the end, and after.” She looked at Riarhin with steady grey eyes. “And then it will be over. Once. And never again. Instead of waiting, for a hundred years, or a thousand, for one of you to walk into that room and open it by accident.”

“You don’t know that.”

“I saw it.”

“You saw it answer one person in a room you’d built so it would. You put her there. You shut the screen on your side.”

Farieth’s face did not change. But she did not answer at once.

“My father,” Riarhin said, and heard her own voice crack on the word and could not help it. “He came back to Bea. More than once. He came back to all of them, my sisters said. He wanted—”

“It doesn’t matter what he wanted.”

The words came across the cutting flat and quick, like something brushed off a table.

Riarhin stopped.

“He wanted a great many things,” said Farieth. “He always did. He wanted women and songs and to be left alone in the forest, and he wanted me to think well of him. I gave him what use he was good for and then he was no more use to me.” She picked up another rod. “He did what I needed when I needed it. After that, what he wanted was his own affair.”

She set the rod in its socket.

Riarhin stood on the edge of the abutment. She could not feel her hands. She could feel her heart, very fast and very loud, and the cut on her arm burning under the linen, and something rising in her chest that was more than anger, a great hot tide of it, so that her vision went narrow and bright at the edges and all she could see was the grey woman on the far side bending an iron rod over with her knee.

The ropes. The two ropes strung across the cutting, from the great tree behind her to the great tree behind Farieth. She could cross on them. Hand over hand, with her legs hooked over, the way Ghere had made her cross a gorge during her first years with him. Thirty paces. She could be on the far side in the time it took to say it.

She found she had taken a step towards the tree.

“Yes,” said Farieth, without looking up. “Come across. I’ve left the ropes for you.”

Riarhin stopped.

Farieth bent the rod over and looked up.

“Halfway,” she said, “you’ll be hanging by your hands over that drop with a cut arm. I’ll be standing at the end of the rope with a spear. I won’t need to cut it. I’ll only need to wait for you to reach the end.” She tilted her head. “You know that. You knew it before you moved. And you moved anyway.”

Riarhin stood very still.

“He did that too,” said Farieth. “Your father. Moved before he’d finished knowing.” She looked back at her iron. “It’s the forest in you. The other half. I never could teach it to wait.”

Behind Riarhin, in the trees, nobody made a sound. She could feel them there, Osselin and Vesket, watching her. She could feel Osselin not calling her back.

She did not cross.

She stood on the abutment until her heart had slowed a little, until the bright narrow edges of her sight had widened again, and the grey woman on the far side was only a grey woman, setting iron in stone. Then she said, as levelly as she could:

“I won’t let you.”

“I know,” said Farieth.

“I’ll stop you.”

“You’ll try. You’ll bring your Sumwright and your lamp-girl into the room, and they won’t know where to stand, and I will.” Farieth set the last rod in its socket and bent it over to meet the others, and the cage of iron was complete, a little black dome on the grey abutment. She looked at it a moment. Then she looked across at Riarhin. “Go home, granddaughter. Take them home. You’ll have what you wanted. You’ll have it whether you help or not. You don’t need to watch.”

“And if I don’t?”

Farieth stood up. She did it carefully, the bad leg first, using the spear. She stood on the far abutment and looked across the cutting, and for a moment her face held something Riarhin could not name, something that might in another woman have been tenderness, or only weariness.

“Then I’ll see you in the room,” she said.

She turned. She picked up the empty linen and folded it, in three, with the corner under, and put it in her robe. She took up her spear. And she went away from the cutting along the gouges of the sledge, north and east, into the trees, one foot heavy and one foot light.

Riarhin did not watch her go. She turned round and walked back into the trees, and Osselin was there, and Vesket, and she walked past them both and sat down on a root and put her face in her good hand.

Nobody touched her.

After a while, from somewhere north and east, through the trees, came a sound. Iron on stone. A long, slow, grinding scrape, and then silence, and then the scrape again.

Riarhin sat with her face in her hand. Between scrapes she listened for the next.

Chapter 23 — The dry channel

Every evening since the Gate, Osselin had lit her lantern.

She did it at the same moment each day, when the green light in the canopy went grey: she unwrapped one of the lanterns from its felt, opened its little door, and lit it from the other, the one she kept shuttered and burning in her bag all day, the flame she had carried from her own hearth in Wendle. Then she closed the door and held the lantern up at arm’s length and watched the flame for the time it took to count eight.

Riarhin had asked her about it on the second night.

“It’s what you do,” Osselin said. “At dusk. You light from your own light, and you watch it settle. If it settles straight, the lane’s quiet. If it leans, you look where it’s leaning.”

“And here?”

“It leans.” Osselin held the lantern up. The little flame inside the horn panes stood not quite upright, tipped, very slightly, towards the north. “It’s leaned every night.”

Vesket, feeding Tessel on the other side of the fire they did not have, had said, “Draughts.”

“There’s no wind.”

“There’s always a draught in a forest. Air goes up the trunks in the evening as it cools. It pulls.” He had not looked up from the spider. “It isn’t anything, Ossie.”

“It leans the same way every night.”

“The trunks are the same every night.”

Osselin had closed the lantern and put it away, and had not mentioned it again. But she had lit it every evening after, and Riarhin had seen the flame lean north every time, a little more, she thought, each night.

On the day after the cutting they found the channel.

The scraping sound had stopped in the night. In the morning they followed the gouges north and east from the bridge, keeping to the trees beside them, and the forest grew stranger. The great trees thinned further. Between them there were more shaped stones, more stretches of wall, the stumps of columns. The ground underfoot was paved in places, under the moss, so that Riarhin felt stone beneath her boots where she expected earth. Once they walked for a quarter mile along the top of a wall without knowing it was a wall until the trees on their left fell away and they saw the drop.

In the afternoon the gouges turned aside and went down into a channel.

It was a channel like the cutting at the bridge, but smaller and older, a long straight trench in the earth with sides of fitted stone, perhaps three times Riarhin’s height deep and twice as wide. Unlike the cutting, it was dry. There was no stream at the bottom, only a floor of pale stone, swept clean, with a thin line of moss growing down the middle where water had once run.

Farieth’s gouges went down into it by a ramp at the near end and ran away along its floor, north and east.

Vesket stopped at the edge.

“That’s it,” he said.

He had the wallet open before Riarhin could ask. He took out a sheet she had seen him look at a dozen times since the Gate, a copy he had made at Thornholt of one of Eshai’s papers, Farieth’s old hand, with a drawing on it: lines and circles, a great ring in the middle, and lines coming out of it like spokes, some solid and some dotted. He laid it on a stone and put his finger on one of the dotted lines.

“This,” he said. “This is a relief channel. She calls it that. It ran water away from the enclosure when the enclosure was working, to keep it—” He searched. “To keep it from filling. Overflowing. The old builders cut channels like this all round it, at the cardinal points and between. When the enclosure was sealed, after the fall, the channels were blocked. She marks this one dry. Dead. Sealed at the inner end.” He looked down into the channel. “This is the one. The eastern relief. It goes straight to the edge of the enclosure and stops.”

“And she’s gone down it.”

“Because it’s the easiest way.” Vesket looked at the gouges. “It’s level and paved and it goes where she wants to go. She can drag the counterweight right to the edge of the ring on the floor of it.” He looked at Riarhin. “That’s why I chose it.”

“Chose it?”

“For the court.” He put his hand on the oilcloth bundle Riarhin carried on her frame. “The papers say the binding is weakest where the dry channels meet the ring, because the seal there was put in afterwards, by people in a hurry. The seal’s the weak place. I meant to lay the court over the seal at the inner end of this channel and hold the shape there when the time came. Here, because it’s dead. Because if I hold the shape at a dead place nothing else is disturbed.” He looked down at the swept floor of the channel, at the gouges running along it. “And she’s using it as a road.”

Osselin had come to the edge beside them. She looked down into the dry channel.

“Is that bad?”

“I don’t know.” Vesket rolled the paper. “It’s a road for her. It’s still a dead channel. Water doesn’t run in it. Nothing runs in it. She’s only walking in it.”

“Then why do you look like that?”

He did not answer.

They went down the ramp into the channel. It ran straight, west to east, as far as Riarhin could see, the walls sheer with ferns in their joints. It was very quiet. The forest sounds came from above, muffled. The air was still and cool and smelled of stone.

“I want to test it,” said Vesket.

Osselin said, “With what?”

He took Vesper’s box from Riarhin’s frame. He opened it on the floor of the channel and took out the little resonance plate, a disc of brass the size of a palm, very thin, with a hole in the middle, and a little brass striker. He held the plate up by a loop of thread through its hole, so that it hung free.

“It isn’t the rods,” he said. “It doesn’t tell me the beat. It only tells me whether a place answers. If I strike it in a dead place, it rings and dies. If I strike it in a live one, it rings and something rings back.” He looked at Osselin. “Once. Only once. Only here.”

Osselin looked at him for a long time.

“Once,” she said.

He struck it.

The plate gave a single clear note, very pure, that went out along the dry channel in both directions and came back off the walls. Riarhin held her breath and listened. The note rang. It faded. It died.

Nothing rang back.

Vesket let out a long breath.

“There,” he said. “Dead.”

Osselin had her lantern out.

Riarhin had not seen her take it. She was standing a little way off on the floor of the channel with the lantern in her hand, lit, its little door closed, held up at arm’s length. She was looking at the flame.

“Vesket,” she said.

He turned.

The flame in the lantern was lying almost flat. Not flickering. Lying over, steady, as if it were being pulled by a hand, north and east, along the channel, so far over that it was licking at the horn pane on that side.

“It’s daylight,” said Osselin. Her voice was very quiet. “It only leans at dusk. It’s never leaned in daylight. It started when you struck that.”

Vesket stared at the flame.

“A draught—”

“There’s no draught. Look.” Osselin held up her other hand, the bare one. A strand of hair at her temple, loose from her crest, hung straight down. “There’s no air moving. Look at the ferns.” The ferns in the joints of the walls hung still. “It’s leaning the way it leans every night, and it’s never leaned this far.”

As they watched, the flame slowly lifted, wavered, and stood up straight again.

Nobody spoke.

“It went somewhere,” Riarhin said.

Vesket turned to her.

“The note.” She looked along the channel, east, where the gouges ran. “You struck it, and nothing came back here. But the flame leaned. Like the field. Like the spring-house. It didn’t answer here. It answered somewhere else.”

Vesket looked at the plate hanging from its thread in his hand. He looked at the flame, upright now, small and steady in its horn panes. He looked at Osselin.

“You told me,” he said. “Every night. You told me it leaned.”

“You said draughts.”

“I said draughts.” He closed his hand round the plate. “It’s been leaning towards this. Every night. Towards this channel, or what’s at the end of it. And I said draughts.”

Osselin did not say anything. She closed the lantern’s little door and stood holding it.

“It isn’t dead,” said Vesket. His voice had gone flat. “The channel. She marked it dead. I read her papers and I believed her because she was right about everything else, and because the papers are forty years old, and because I wanted it to be dead. It’s carrying something. Not water. Something.” He looked east along the floor. “And she’s walking along it with a load of iron.”

“What would happen if you laid the court here?” said Riarhin.

He did not answer at once.

“I need to know,” he said. “I need to know where it goes. What it’s carrying. Before I can say.”

“Then I’ll go and look.”

Osselin turned. “Alone?”

“Alone. Quiet.” Riarhin unslung the frame from her back and set it down against the wall of the channel, the court in its oilcloth and the charcoal and Vesper’s box. Her right arm ached where Farieth’s blade had opened it. She flexed her fingers and they moved. “She’s ahead of us. Somewhere. If she’s on the floor of this I’ll hear her before she hears me. If she isn’t, I’ll go as far as the end and come back.”

“And if she’s waiting?”

Riarhin looked at the high walls and the long straight floor running east.

“Then I’ll come back faster,” she said.

She went along the top.

Not the floor. Anybody walking the floor was in a trench with no way out but forward and back. She went up by the ramp and along the edge in the trees, keeping back from the lip. She went fast, and quietly, and every few hundred paces she stopped and crawled to the edge and looked down.

The gouges ran on along the floor of the channel. Twice she passed places where Farieth had stopped. There were iron cages set in the floor, the same as the one on the bridge abutment. They stood at intervals along the middle of the channel, where the line of moss ran.

The third cage she came to was different. The rods had been set in the floor, but they had been set across the channel, not along it, in a line from wall to wall, like the bars of a gate. And the stone of the floor round them was not pale. It was dark, as if wet, though it was not wet. When Riarhin lay on her stomach at the lip of the channel and looked down at it, she could feel, very faintly, through the stone under her chest, a slow vibration, like the hum of a hive.

She lay there a long time, listening.

The hum came, and swelled, and faded, and was gone. She counted. She counted to eight, and then on, carefully, with her lips shut, to the place where it came again. It came. Swelled. Faded. She counted again. The interval was the same.

It was a pulse. The channel was breathing.

She went on.

A mile further, the channel ended. It did not end in a wall. It ended in a great ring of stone, a circular wall of pale fitted blocks rising out of the forest floor, taller than the trees round it, smooth, without a door or a window, curving away on either side out of sight among the trunks. The channel ran straight up to the foot of the ring and stopped against it. There was a seal there, as the papers said: a great square slab set into the ring where the channel met it, of a different stone from the rest, darker, rougher, put in afterwards by people in a hurry.

Farieth’s gouges ran up to the seal and stopped.

The counterweight was not there. But the floor of the channel in front of the seal had been cleared. And set into the floor, in front of the slab, there was a ring of iron cages, eight of them, in a circle, and in the middle of the circle, a square socket cut deep into the stone, empty, the size of something very large and very heavy.

Riarhin lay at the lip of the channel and looked at the empty socket for a long time.

Then she looked at the seal.

The dark slab was cracked. A single crack, running from top to bottom. And from the crack, as she watched, at the interval she had counted, came a breath. She could not see it. She could see the ferns at the lip of the channel above the crack move, very slightly, all together, towards the crack and then away, as though something on the other side of the seal had drawn air in through it and let it out again.

Riarhin understood, lying there, what Vesket’s court would have done.

She went back faster than she had come.

Chapter 24 — The thing left unmade

“If I hold it here,” said Vesket, “I hold it open.”

He sat on the floor of the dry channel with Farieth’s drawing on his knees and his face the colour of the stone. Osselin sat against the wall with her lantern unlit in her lap. Riarhin stood. She had run the last half-mile and her arm was throbbing.

“Say it again,” she said.

“The court holds a shape. That’s all it does. It doesn’t change anything. It keeps it from changing.” He spread his hands on the drawing. “I meant to lay it at the inner end of this channel, over the seal, because I thought the channel was dead and the seal was holding. I thought if I held the seal in its shape when the alignment came, the enclosure couldn’t open there, whatever she did. I thought I’d be holding a door shut.”

“And instead.”

“The seal’s cracked. The channel’s breathing through it.” He looked down at the drawing. “My working would keep her road open when the alignment comes.” His voice cracked. “I’d be doing her work for her.”

Osselin said, very quietly, “You didn’t know.”

“I should have known.” He put his hand flat on the drawing, over the dotted line. “I read it. I read dry and sealed and I believed it because it was in her hand and her hand was right about everything else. I didn’t ask whether it was still dry. I didn’t ask whether forty years was long enough for a seal put in by people in a hurry to crack. I didn’t go and look.” He laughed, short. “Vesper said it. At Thornholt. It was sound when you looked at it eight years ago and you never looked at it again.”

“You looked today,” said Riarhin.

“Ossie looked. Every night. I said draughts.”

Nobody answered that.

Vesket sat for a while with his hand on the drawing. Then he took it away and rolled the paper up, very neatly, and put it in the wallet.

“The court,” he said.

Riarhin looked at the oilcloth bundle on her frame.

“It has to be unmade,” said Vesket. “Now. Here.”

“Unmade.”

“Taken apart. Every crossing cut. Every drop of ink scraped.” He did not look at the bundle. “I drew it from her drawing. Every square of it is measured to this channel and this crack and this ring. If I carry it on, it carries that measure with it. If anything happens to it, if she gets it, if it’s left somewhere, if I’m dead and somebody finds it and lays it out because it’s beautiful and they want to see what it does—” He stopped. “It’s a key to holding her road open. It can’t exist.”

Riarhin looked at him. Then she unslung the frame and laid the oilcloth bundle on the floor of the channel and unfolded it.

The court lay on the pale stone between them. Unfolded, it covered a space larger than the floor of Pellet’s room at Underarch: panel after hinged panel of fine copper wire in squares, each crossing knotted with a twist of wire and touched with a drop of blue ink, the whole of it catching what light came down into the channel in a soft greenish glow. It was very beautiful. Riarhin had carried it for twelve days and had never seen it laid out.

Vesket knelt at the edge of it.

He did not touch it for a long time.

Then he took the little knife from his coat, the one he had used to scrape a bad line off the vellum at Underarch, and he reached out and cut the first crossing.

It was a small thing. A twist of copper wire, snipped through. The two squares on either side of it sagged a little against each other. He moved to the next crossing and cut it, and the next.

Riarhin knelt on the other side.

“Faster with two,” she said. “Show me.”

He showed her. You cut the twist at each crossing. You did not pull the wire. You scraped the ink from the knot with the edge of the blade until the copper showed bright. She took out Toothpick and began.

It was slow. There were hundreds of crossings, and the wire was fine and springy and the knots were tight, and her right arm hurt every time she bent it to bring the knife in close. She worked her way in from one edge while Vesket worked in from the other, and they did not speak, and the copper squares came apart under their hands into a loose tangle of wire.

After the first panel, Riarhin began to gather the cut wire into a heap.

“What are you doing?”

“Saving it.” She coiled a length round her hand. “It’s good copper. Half of what’s in your case is copper. If the grate breaks again—”

“No.”

She looked up.

“No,” said Vesket. “It goes. All of it.”

“It’s wire. It’s only wire, once it’s cut.”

“It’s wire that was drawn and measured to this place. Every length of it is the length it is because of this channel.” His face was set. “If I keep a length of it and use it to mend the grate, the grate carries that length. If I use it to bind a pack, the pack carries it. I don’t know whether it means anything. I don’t know, and I won’t carry it on not knowing.”

“That’s—”

“That’s what?”

Riarhin heard herself about to say foolish, and stopped. She looked at the coil of wire round her hand, and at his face.

“We haven’t got time,” she said instead. “If we have to cut every crossing and scrape every knot and then go back over the wire and—”

“Then we take the time.”

“She’s ahead of us. She has a socket cut and eight cages set and a counterweight somewhere, and you said thirty days at Thornholt and we’ve used twelve of them, and we’re sitting in a ditch with knives—”

“And if we leave a piece of this and she finds it,” said Vesket, very loudly, “then we’ve given her the one thing she couldn’t make for herself.”
