His voice went along the channel and came back off the walls.

They stared at each other across the half-unmade court.

“I’m not mediating,” said Osselin.

They both turned. Osselin had got up from the wall while they argued and was standing a little way off with her lantern in one hand.

“I’m not getting in the middle of this,” she said. “Not till we’ve moved the spider and the water.”

“What?”

“The spider.” Osselin pointed. Tessel was on her felt beside the open copper case, at the very edge of the court, drawing. “And the water. They’re sitting right next to that thing, in a channel that breathes, and the two of you are shouting over it. I’m moving them. Then you can shout as much as you like.”

Vesket looked at the spider.

“Oh,” he said.

“And the case,” said Osselin. “And the inks. And the food. Everything we need to live on. Up. Out. Now.” She was already picking up the water-skins. “Then I’ll listen to you both. Not before.”

They moved them. Vesket carried Tessel up the ramp in her basket and the case after her, and Osselin carried the food and the water, and Riarhin carried Vesper’s box and the charcoal, and they set it all down in the trees at the top, back from the edge, and stood there among it, breathing hard.

Osselin looked at them.

“Now,” she said.

Neither of them said anything.

“You’re both right,” said Osselin. “It doesn’t help. Go back down and cut it all. All of it. And then we burn the wire. Then nobody carries it. Then nobody has to argue about whether it means anything.”

Vesket looked at her.

“Burn it,” he said.

“Copper melts. In a hot enough fire. You told me that at Kelling when you burned your boots.” Osselin almost smiled. “We’ve got charcoal. More than we need, now there’s nothing to keep warm.”

There was a long silence.

“Yes,” said Vesket. “Yes. That would do it.”

Riarhin looked at the coil of wire still wound round her hand. She unwound it, slowly, and held it out to him. He took it.

“I’m sorry,” she said.

“So am I.”

They went back down.

It took the rest of the afternoon. When the last panel came apart, Vesket knelt on the floor of the channel among the heap of loose wire and looked at it, and Riarhin saw him reach out once, towards one of the hinges, a little brass hinge he had made himself, with a flower stamped on it, and then draw his hand back.

He gathered it all up in his arms, the whole tangle, and carried it up the ramp.

Osselin had built a fire at the top, in a hollow among the roots, with a ring of stones round it. She had used half the charcoal. It burned white-hot. Vesket stood over it with the wire in his arms.

“Wait,” he said. “The ink. The ink first.”

He sat down by the fire and took the vellum out of its bone case. Not the roll he redrew every eight days, the Codex. Other sheets, that Riarhin had not seen, folded inside it. Drawings. The figure of the court, drawn out in blue ink, square by square, every crossing marked. He looked at them. Then he put them in the fire one by one. The vellum curled and blackened and the blue ink flared, briefly, and was gone.

Then he put the wire in.

It did not melt at once. It glowed. It went red and then orange and then a strange pale green at the edges, and the fire hissed and spat, and the smell was sharp and metallic and caught in Riarhin’s throat. Osselin fed the fire with charcoal. Slowly, the fine wire at the edges of the tangle began to soften and slump and run together, and the squares lost their shape, and the whole of it sank down into the coals in a glowing mass.

Vesket sat and watched it until there was nothing left that had been a square.

Then the ground moved.

It was a long slow lurch, like a boat rolling, that went through the earth under them from north to south. The trees creaked. Somewhere a branch came down with a crash. The fire shifted in its ring of stones and a spray of sparks went up.

Riarhin was on her feet with her sword out.

“What—”

It came again. Longer. Through the soles of her boots she felt the slow vibration she had felt lying at the lip of the channel, the hum of the hive, but stronger now, much stronger, and coming now in one long rising swell. Below them, in the dry channel, a sound began: a deep, hollow note, like wind across the mouth of a jar, rising and rising.

“The channel,” said Vesket. He had not moved from the fire. He was looking at it with his face very still. “It’s breathing in. Not out. In.”

“Why?”

“I don’t know.” He looked at the fire, at the glowing mass of copper sinking into the coals. “Maybe because something that was measured to it stopped existing. Maybe because she’s done something at the end of it. Maybe it does this every few days and we’ve never been here to hear it.” He stood up. “We need to be somewhere else.”

“The fire—”

“Leave it. It’ll burn out. There’s nothing in it now.”

They gathered what they had. Riarhin’s frame went back on her back, and it was lighter than it had been since Underarch.

The note from the channel rose and rose. The ground lurched again. Osselin lit her lantern and held it up, and the flame inside lay flat, pointing down into the channel, east, along the line of iron cages, towards the ring.

“That way,” she said. “It’s pulling that way.”

“Then we go the other,” said Riarhin.

They went. Up, away from the channel, north, through the thinning trees, over broken walls and the stumps of columns, while behind them the deep note rose out of the dry channel and filled the forest, and then, quite suddenly, stopped.

The silence afterwards was very large.

Riarhin stopped at the top of a rise and looked back. Through the trees she could see the place where they had been: the hollow among the roots, the ring of stones, the fire. The fire was out. Something had put it out. There was no smoke. And where the channel ran along below the rise, straight, east to west, she could see that its floor was no longer pale and clean. It was dark, the whole length of it, as though water had run along it and drained away.

Vesket stood beside her and looked at it.

“That’s where it was,” he said. “The court. That’s where I was going to lay it.”

Nobody said anything.

They turned and went on north, away from the channel, with the charcoal and the spider and the case with nothing in it to keep warm.

Chapter 25 — The route nobody kept

The ring went on for miles.

They followed it all the next day, north and then east, keeping well back from its foot among the thinning trees. It rose out of the forest floor like a cliff, pale and smooth and curving, the fitted blocks so close that Riarhin could not have slid a knife between them. Ferns did not grow in its joints. Moss did not grow on its face. Rain had run down it for a thousand years and left no stain. In places the trees had grown up close against it and their branches had pressed against the stone and turned aside, as though the wall were hot.

There was no door.

“There were doors,” said Vesket. He had the drawing out again. “Four of them, at the cardinal points. Here, and here.” He touched the circles. “They were sealed after the fall, like the channels. Better than the channels. The papers say the doors are solid now.”

“The papers said the channel was dead.”

He did not answer that.

At noon they came to the place where the northern door should have been. It was a recess in the face of the ring, very large, an arch three times Riarhin’s height, and the arch had been filled with the same pale stone as the ring itself, fitted as close, so that the arch was only a shallow shape on the wall, like the ghost of a door drawn on it.

Riarhin put her hand on it. It was cool and smooth and perfectly solid.

“There’s another way,” said Osselin.

She was not looking at the arch. She was looking at the ground beside it, to the left, where the ring wall met the forest floor.

Riarhin looked. At first she saw nothing. Then she saw that the face of the wall beside the arch was not quite smooth. It had a line in it, a very faint line, running up from the ground at an angle, and another beside it, and another, and they were not lines. They were edges. Steps. Small steps, very shallow, cut into the face of the ring wall itself, each no wider than a hand and no deeper than the first joint of a finger, climbing it at a long low slant and disappearing round its curve.

“They’re for feet,” said Osselin. “Look. They’re worn in the middle.”

They were. Each tiny step had a hollow in the middle of it, very faint, where something the width of a foot had stood on it many times.

“Maintenance,” said Vesket softly. “Somebody had to see to the top.”

“Somebody small,” said Riarhin.

Beside the steps, running up the wall at the same angle, there was something else. A ramp. It was a smooth broad band cut into the face of the wall, a little sunk, as wide as a cart, and it went up the wall beside the steps in a long perfect slope, without a break, without a hold, polished smooth as glass.

“That’s easier,” said Vesket.

Riarhin looked at it.

“For what?”

“For walking. It’s broad. It’s level. You could—”

“You couldn’t.” She went to the foot of the ramp and laid her hand on it. It was smoother than the rest of the wall. Cool, and very faintly slick, as though it had been oiled. “There’s nothing to hold. Nothing for a foot. Look how steep it gets.” She pointed. The ramp rose gently at first and then, further up, curved and steepened until it was nearly sheer. “It wasn’t made for people. It was made for something that could go up a wall like that.”

Vesket looked up the ramp. He was silent.

“What could go up a wall like that?” said Osselin.

Riarhin did not answer. She had seen ramps like that before, when she was young, in parts of the forest Ghere would not take her, and Ghere had not answered either.

“The steps,” she said. “The steps were for whoever came after. To see to the top. The ramp was for whatever was here before.” She looked at the little worn hollows. “People came this way. Not often. But people.”

“And nobody’s come since,” said Vesket.

“Somebody kept it.” Riarhin looked at the hollows in the steps. They were worn, but they were clean. No moss. No dust. “Or nothing grows here.”

They went up the steps.

It was the hardest climbing Riarhin had done since she was young. The steps were too small for her feet; she could put only the front of her boot on each one, and the wall was smooth on either side, with nothing for her hands but the step above, and the angle was low enough that she had to lean into the wall and go up crabwise, step and step and step. The others went behind her. Osselin, who was smaller, found the steps easier than Riarhin did, and went up them with her heel first and her body pressed to the wall, swearing under her breath, steadily. Vesket came last, with the copper case slung on his back now that there was nothing in it to keep level, and Tessel’s basket buttoned inside his coat.

Halfway up, the frame on Riarhin’s back caught.

The steps had come to a place where the wall bellied out a little, and the frame with Vesper’s box and the charcoal on it struck the bulge and pushed her outward, away from the wall. She stopped. She could not go on with it on her back.

She got it off. She did it slowly, one strap and then the other, with her chest pressed to the wall and the front of her boots on two tiny steps. Then she held it out behind her, down, for Osselin to take.

Osselin took it.

“Now you,” said Riarhin. “Up. Past me. Above the bulge. Then I’ll pass it.”

Osselin looked at the bulge, and at the steps going up over it, and at Riarhin clinging to the wall above her. Then she hooked the frame by a strap on a step and came up past Riarhin’s legs and hips and shoulder, very close, and went up over the bulge and stopped above it, and turned, and held down her hand.

“Pass it.”

Riarhin reached down for the frame where Osselin had left it hooked on a step. She took the weight with her right hand, the cut arm, and something in it tore, or felt as though it tore, a hot bright line from shoulder to elbow under Osselin’s linen, and her fingers opened.

The frame dropped a hand’s breadth. Riarhin caught it with her left. She hung on the wall with her right arm useless and her left hand gripping the frame by one strap and her feet on two tiny steps, and the whole weight of the frame swinging out over the drop below.

“I’ve got it,” said Vesket.

He was below her. He had come up close behind her on the steps while she was struggling, and now he reached up with his good hand and took the frame from her by its lower bar, and took its weight. Riarhin felt it go. She hung on the wall breathing.

“Up,” said Vesket. “Go on. I’ll pass it to Ossie.”

“Your hand—”

“My hand’s healed. Briar said. Go on.”

She went on. She went up over the bulge with her right arm held against her body and her left hand doing everything, and her heart going very fast. Above the bulge she stopped beside Osselin and looked down, and Vesket was coming up behind her, slowly, with the frame held against the wall beside him in his good hand and his burned one, still bound in Briar’s linen, braced flat on the stone.

He passed it up to Osselin. Osselin took it.

“I’ll carry it,” said Osselin. “From here.”

“It’s heavy.”

“I know it’s heavy. You can’t lift it.” Osselin was already getting it onto her back. It came down to the backs of her knees. “I’ll carry it till the top. Then we’ll see.”

Riarhin did not argue.

At the top of the steps the ring wall was broad, broader than a road, and flat, paved with the same pale stone. It curved away on either side, a great pale circle in the forest, with the treetops beside it on the outside and, on the inside—

Nothing. On the inside of the wall there was a drop, sheer, into grey mist.

They stood at the edge and looked down. The mist lay in the ring like water in a bowl, filling it, very still, a pale grey sea with the top of the wall for its shore. Riarhin could not see the bottom. It did not smell of anything. It was the same nothing she had smelled in the Hollow Field.

“It’ll lift,” said Vesket. His voice was very quiet. “The papers say it lifts. At certain hours. It breathes.”

“When?”

“I don’t know. Not now.”

They went along the top of the wall.

The paving ran on round the curve of the ring, and the maintenance steps had come up onto it at a particular place, and from that place a path went on along the wall, marked by the same small worn hollows in the paving where feet had gone. Riarhin followed them. They went east. After half a mile the path came to a gap.

The ring wall was broken. A section of it, perhaps twenty paces long, had fallen inward, long ago, into the mist, and left a gap in the top of the wall with ragged edges and a drop on either side. Across the gap, from one ragged edge to the other, lay a single long stone beam, a fallen lintel or a piece of the wall’s own coping, narrow, no wider than two hands side by side.

The worn footholds went across the beam.

Riarhin went to the edge and looked at it.

She could walk it. She had walked narrower. Ghere had made her walk a fallen trunk across a gorge in the rain carrying a basket of eggs, and she had dropped three and he had made her do it again. She could walk it with one good arm.

“Wait,” said Osselin.

Riarhin turned. Osselin had her lantern out. She had lit it, and was holding it up at arm’s length at the edge of the gap, and watching the flame.

The flame stood straight.

Then, as they watched, it leaned. Not far. A finger’s breadth, north and east, towards the gap, towards the mist on the inside of the wall. And as it leaned, the mist moved. Riarhin saw it. A slow swell went across the grey surface inside the ring, like a breath, and rose, and lapped up over the inner edge of the gap, and flowed across the stone beam, ankle-deep, grey and silent, and over the outer edge and down the outside of the wall in a long slow waterfall of mist that faded into the trees.

The flame stood straight.

The mist sank back. The beam lay bare.

“It does that,” said Osselin. “It’s done it four times since we came along the wall. I’ve been counting.” She did not take her eyes off the flame. “It goes over the beam. It comes and goes.”

“How long between?”

“I don’t know. I haven’t been counting long enough.” Osselin looked at the beam. “You don’t want to be on that when it goes over.”

“Why not?”

Osselin looked at her.

“Because it’s the same as the field,” she said. “Isn’t it. It’s the same nothing. You went into the field and you couldn’t eat for two days. That’s a field. That,” she nodded at the mist, “is a bowl full of it, and it comes over the beam to the ankle. I don’t want to be standing in it on a beam two hands wide over a drop. Do you?”

Riarhin did not.

“Count it,” she said.

Osselin counted.

She stood at the edge of the gap with the lantern held up, and counted under her breath, eight and a pause, eight and a pause, the way she counted in the lanes at Wendle, watching the flame. Riarhin watched with her. Vesket sat down on the paving behind them with his back to the outer edge and Tessel’s basket on his knees and did not watch. He had taken out the vellum. He was not drawing on it. He was holding it, the roll, unopened, in both hands.

The flame leaned. The mist came over the beam. Osselin counted. The mist went back. The flame stood straight.

“Forty-one,” said Osselin. “Eights. Forty-one eights and two.”

They waited. The flame leaned. The mist came.

“Forty-one,” said Osselin. “And three.”

“It isn’t even.”

“It’s nearly even. It’s close.” Osselin’s face was screwed up with concentration. “If I start counting when it goes back, I’ve got forty-one eights. Less, to be safe. Forty. That’s—” She worked it out. “That’s long enough to cross. If you don’t stop. If you don’t look down. If you go on my count and not your own.”

Vesket said from behind them, “I can help.”

They turned.

“With the count,” he said. He had unrolled the vellum on his knees. “A little. There’s a small working. It’s for keeping a beat true when you’re frightened and your heart wants to go faster.” He looked at Osselin. “Yours. If you’ll let me. So the count doesn’t drift.”

“Mine?”

“You’re counting. You keep the count. I only keep it steady.” He did not look at Riarhin. “It’s a very small working. It won’t cost much.”

Osselin looked at him a long time.

“All right,” she said.

He did it sitting on the paving with the vellum on his knees and Tessel on her felt beside him. He reached out with his good hand and touched the back of Osselin’s wrist, the hand that held the lantern, very lightly, two fingers, and his lips moved. Nothing changed that Riarhin could see. But Osselin drew a breath and let it out slowly, and her shoulders came down, and when she began to count again, under her breath, the count was very even, like a pendulum.

“Now,” said Osselin. “When it goes back. You first.”

The flame leaned. The mist came over the beam. It went back.

“Go,” said Osselin. “One.”

Riarhin went.

She stepped out onto the beam on Osselin’s count and walked. Not fast. Not slow. On the count, eight steps and a breath, eight steps and a breath, with her eyes on the far edge of the gap and her right arm held against her body and her left arm out from her side. The beam was firm under her boots. It was cold. On her left, the inside, the grey mist lay very still, a hand’s breadth below the edge of the beam, and she did not look at it. On her right, the outside, the forest floor was very far down and very green.

“Twenty-four,” said Osselin behind her. Steady. A pendulum.

She stepped off the beam onto the far edge of the gap.

She turned. Osselin was already on the beam, with the frame on her back and the lantern in her hand, coming across on her own count with her lips moving, heel first, her small body very upright and very still. She did not look down either. She came across in thirty-one eights and stepped off onto the far edge beside Riarhin and turned round at once and held up the lantern.

Vesket came last.

Osselin counted him across, and on the thirty-fifth count he stopped dead in the middle of the beam.

“Vesket,” said Osselin.

He did not move. He was standing on the beam with his arms out from his sides, looking down. Not at the forest. At the mist. At the inside of the ring.

“Vesket. Thirty-six. Walk.”

“Something moved,” he said.

“Walk.”

“Something moved, down there, in the—”

“Thirty-seven,” said Osselin, very loud, very steady. “Walk.”

He walked. He came the rest of the way across with his eyes on Osselin’s face and stepped off the beam on the fortieth count, and as he stepped off, the flame in the lantern leaned and the mist came up over the edge of the beam behind him and flowed across it, ankle-deep, grey, silent, where he had been standing.

He sat down on the paving.

Nobody said anything for a while. Osselin held the lantern. Riarhin sat down beside Vesket with her right arm in her lap. The mist went back. The flame stood up.

“What moved?” said Riarhin.

Vesket did not answer at once. He had his face in his hands.

“Down,” he said at last. “In the mist. When it went back. It went back further than before, and I could see, for a breath. There are walls down there. Buildings. Galleries. And something went along one of them.” He took his hands from his face. “A shape. Pale. Very large. It went along a gallery on the east side and through an arch and was gone.”

“The east side,” said Riarhin.

“The east side.” He took out the drawing and unfolded it on his knee with shaking hands. “The east side is sealed. The papers say so. It was sealed first. Everything on the east side was shut and walled and the galleries were filled. Nothing goes through the east side.” He put his finger on the drawing. “Nothing.”

Osselin said quietly, “The papers said the channel was dead.”

They waited at the edge of the gap until the light began to go. Riarhin sat with her back to the outer edge of the wall and her sword across her knees and watched the mist. The flame leaned and stood, leaned and stood. Each time it went back, it went back a little further, and the light was going, and the mist was thinning, and each time she could see a little more of what lay beneath it: a pale roof, the curve of a dome, a line of arches.

At dusk, when the mist went back, it went back a long way.

For the space of a breath Riarhin saw the whole of the inside of the ring. It was a bowl, very deep, terraced down in rings of pale buildings to a floor far below, and on the floor stood a dome, low and white and enormous, half sunk in the earth, with ribs running out from it in every direction like the spokes of a wheel. The galleries ran round the bowl on every terrace. On the east side, they were walled up, arch after arch filled with the same pale stone as the doors.

And something was moving along the lowest of them. A shape, pale, very large, going from left to right. It went through an arch that should have been walled and did not come out the other side.

The mist came back.

Riarhin sat very still. Beside her, Vesket had seen it too. Osselin had seen it.

“Again,” said Riarhin.

They waited. They watched. Osselin counted. Forty-one eights and two. The mist went back, further than before. The shape came along the lowest gallery on the east side, from left to right, pale and very large, and went through the arch.

“It isn’t moving,” said Vesket. His voice had gone strange. “It isn’t going anywhere. It’s doing the same thing. Again and again. On the breath.”

“Like a door swinging,” said Osselin.

“Like a door swinging,” said Vesket. “In a place where all the doors were shut.”

Chapter 26 — The edge of the working

They slept on the top of the ring wall, with the mist going and coming on their left and the forest dark on their right, and Riarhin did not sleep at all.

She sat with her back against a fallen block and watched. On every breath the pale shape went along the lowest gallery and through the walled arch. She stopped counting after forty. She did not stop watching.

In the night the mist thinned further. By the grey hour before dawn it lay only in the bottom of the bowl, a pale shallow pool round the foot of the great dome, and the terraces and galleries of the ring stood out of it in the starlight, row on row of pale arches going down into the dark.

She could see the seal.

It was on the east side, below her and to the right, where the dry channel came under the ring wall. She could see the dark slab of it from the inside now, set into the curve of the lowest terrace, rougher than the stone round it. And she could see that it was no longer whole. At its foot, beside the crack she had seen from the channel, somebody had cut a hole. A low ragged opening, the height of a crouching woman, dug through the base of the slab. Round it on the floor of the terrace lay chips of dark stone and the black stub of a broken chisel.

That was the scraping. Four days of it, in the bog, and three more here. A woman alone with a chisel and a hammer, chipping her way through a seal put in by people in a hurry.

Beyond the hole, inside the ring, a passage went down from the lowest terrace into the earth.

It was a broad sloping tunnel, open at the top like a throat, paved, running down from the terrace beside the seal at a steep angle towards the floor of the bowl and the foot of the great dome. Riarhin could see its mouth and the first part of its slope, and then it went under the terrace above and out of sight. And at its mouth, at the very top of the slope, there was something large and dark.

The counterweight.

She knew it at once, though she had never seen it. It was a block of iron the size of a cart, squared and rough, sitting on a sledge with iron runners at the lip of the tunnel. It was chocked. There were blocks of stone wedged under the front of the runners, three of them, holding it at the top of the slope. And from the back of the sledge a rope ran to a timber frame, a great tripod of logs lashed together at the top and set over the mouth of the tunnel, with a block hanging from it and the rope running through the block.

When the light came, she woke the others and showed them.

Vesket lay on his stomach at the inner edge of the wall with his chin on his hands and looked down at the counterweight on its sledge for a long time without saying anything. Osselin lay beside him with her lantern beside her, unlit.

“The socket,” said Riarhin. “At the end of the channel. Outside the seal. It wasn’t for the counterweight.”

“What was it for?”

“That.” Riarhin pointed at the timber tripod. “The foot of that. She stood a log in it and used it to haul the counterweight through the hole she cut. Then she took it down and put it up again inside, over the tunnel.” She looked at the sledge, at the three stone chocks under its runners. “She’s brought it to the top of the slope. She’s chocked it. When she takes the chocks out, it goes down.”

“Down to where?”

Vesket answered.

“The joint,” he said. He had the drawing out, spread on the paving under his hands. He put his finger on the drawing, at the centre of the great ring, where the lines like spokes came together under the dome. “The ribs hold the shape. They hold it from all sides at once, pulling, so it can’t open anywhere. And underneath the dome, where they all meet, there’s a place where they’re bound together. The joint.” He looked down at the tunnel. “That goes to the joint. It’s a service way. For the people who kept it. To go down and see to the binding.” He was silent a moment. “If something the weight of a cart came down that slope at the joint—”

“It would break the binding.”

“It would break some of it. It might break all of it. It depends on how it struck.” He looked at the iron cages along the dry channel, which they could see now from above, a dotted black line running away west from the seal into the forest. “And on what the iron along the channel does. I don’t know what it does. I think—” He stopped.

“Say it.”

“I think it’s to keep the channel from closing.” Vesket spoke slowly. “When the joint breaks, the shape goes. When the shape goes, the enclosure will try to close itself. Everything that’s open will try to shut. The doors. The channels. The old crossings, all of them. Like a hand closing.” He put his hand flat on the paving and curled the fingers in. “But the iron along the channel won’t let the channel close. It’s a road. One road out, held open, when everything else shuts.” He opened his hand. “Whatever is in there goes out by the one road it’s given.”

“To the west,” said Osselin. “Along the channel.”

“Along the channel. Into the forest. And then—” He looked west. “And then wherever it goes. The channel goes towards the Gate. Towards the coast.”

Nobody said anything for a while.

“And the alignment?” said Riarhin.

Vesket lay with his chin on the paving and his eyes closed. Riarhin watched him reckon. She did not know how he did it without the rods. He had Tessel, who had drawn three figures since the cliff, and he had Osselin’s count of the mist, forty-one eights and something, and he had his memory of every measurement he had ever made.

“Eleven days,” he said at last. “Ten. Twelve. Not more. The mist is breathing faster than it did. Ossie, was it forty-one at dusk?”

“Forty-one eights and two counts. From the start of one swelling to the start of the next.”

“And now?”

Osselin had been counting since she woke, under her breath. “Forty and five.”

“It’s shortening.” He opened his eyes. “When there are only eight eights between swellings, that’s the alignment. Ten days. Eleven.”

“And she takes the chocks out.”

“She takes the chocks out on the breath. When everything’s open at once. When the enclosure is most—” He searched. “Most itself. When it’s straining hardest against the ribs. That’s when a blow at the joint would do the most.”

“Then we stop her now,” said Riarhin.

They both looked at her.

“She’s down there,” Riarhin said. “Somewhere. By the counterweight, or near it. She’s alone. She’s tired. She’s spent seven days chipping through a seal with a bad hand. We go down, now, and we take the chocks out of her reach, or we break the tripod, or we—”

“And she sees us coming down the terraces,” said Osselin, “for an hour, in daylight, with nowhere to hide, and she waits at the top of the tunnel with her spear.”

“Then at night.”

“And the mist comes up at night,” said Vesket. “And you walk into it.”

Riarhin looked down at the counterweight on its sledge, chocked at the top of the slope. She could see the chocks. Three blocks of stone. She could see the rope running back to the tripod. It was so close. It was a quarter of a mile, perhaps, and three terraces down, and she could see every part of it in the clear morning light.

She could also see that there was nobody near it.

“Where is she?” said Osselin.

Riarhin had been asking herself that since dawn. The terraces round the counterweight were empty. The tunnel mouth was empty. Nothing moved on the inside of the ring but the mist in the bottom of the bowl, and, once on every breath, the pale shape on the east gallery.

“Somewhere she can see it,” said Riarhin. “Somewhere she can see whoever goes near it.”

She hated waiting. She lay on the pale stone at the top of the ring wall with her cut arm throbbing and looked at the counterweight. She thought of the spring-house cellar with the water singing at the fourth step, and the Brookside boy walking in smaller and smaller circles in the grey grass. She thought of Coryd’s letter. She asked whether my sister had been to visit.

Every hour they lay here, Farieth was down there working.

“There are three things,” said Vesket.

She turned her head.

He had sat up. He had the drawing on his knees and the vellum beside it and Tessel on her felt, and he was looking at the drawing, not at Riarhin, and his voice was very careful.

“Three things,” he said. “None of them is enough by itself. Listen.”

Riarhin listened.

“The first is holding the shape. Not the channel, the court was for the channel and the court’s gone. The joint. If, when the breath comes and the counterweight comes down, the joint can be made to keep its shape, only for as long as the breath lasts, then the blow strikes something that won’t give. It might crack. It might break. But it won’t open, not while it’s held.” He touched the vellum. “I think I can do that. Not with the court. There’s a place. On the drawing.” He put his finger on a small square near the top of the tunnel, on the terrace beside it, inside the ring. “A room. The papers call it the measured room. The floor is laid out true, the way the court was. The old keepers used it for exactly this, for holding the joint steady while they worked on it. If I can get to that room, I can hold the joint from there. For the length of the breath. Not longer.”

“And then?”

“And then I stop, because I can’t go on, and whatever happens happens.” He did not look up. “That’s the first thing.”

“The second.”

“The second is the road.” He moved his finger along the dotted line of the dry channel, west, away from the seal. “If the joint breaks anyway, if I can’t hold it, that road is where everything goes out. And it isn’t only that road. Everything that’s connected to this, all the old crossings, the spring-house and Coombe Barrow and the Hollow Field and the river holding where the ground folded, all of them have been pulled since the spring.” He looked up at last. “There’s a binding at the joint that ties them in. The old route. The papers draw it as a cord. A single thing, among all the ribs, that runs out from the joint to everything outside. If that’s cut—”

“Then nothing goes out by it.”

“Then nothing goes out by it. Then the crossings are cut off from the joint. Whatever happens at the joint stays at the joint.” He was silent a moment. “It would have to be cut by hand. And if it’s cut, it’s cut. It doesn’t mend. The old crossings that were tied to it don’t open again. Ever. Not on the right night or any night.” He did not look at Osselin. “The spring-house cellar would be a cellar.”

Osselin did not say anything.

“And the third thing,” said Vesket, “is getting out. Somebody has to keep a way out open. A way up, from the measured room, out of the ring, that isn’t the channel. Because when the joint is held and the cord is cut, the ring will try to close. The terraces. The galleries. The maintenance steps. Anything that’s part of it. And whoever’s inside will have to get out before it does. And somebody has to make sure that nobody,” he looked west along the channel, “goes out by the easy way, the open road, because whatever is waiting to go out will be going out by that road too.”

He folded the drawing.

“Three things,” he said. “All at once, at the breath. And the woman with the spear at the top of the tunnel, waiting for us to try.”

Riarhin looked down at the counterweight on its sledge.

“The cord,” she said. “Where is it?”

“At the joint. At the bottom of the tunnel.”

“Where she’ll be.”

“Where she’ll be. Or near it. She has to be at the top to take the chocks out. Then—” He stopped. “I don’t know where she’ll be then. I don’t know whether she means to go down after it.”

Riarhin looked at the tunnel mouth. At the counterweight. At the chocks.

She saw it then. She had been looking at it since dawn and not seen it, the way she had looked at the walls of the old way under the stage at fifteen and not seen them because she was watching Ghere’s back.

The tunnel was narrow. Not at the top, where the counterweight sat. Lower down, where it went under the terrace, it narrowed to the width of the sledge and no more. Anybody going down it to the joint would have to go down past the counterweight or in front of it, in a space no wider than the iron block itself. Anybody fighting in it would be fighting in a stone throat on a steep slope with a cart’s weight of iron chocked above them.

And the chocks were only stone.

“She wants me to come down the tunnel,” said Riarhin.

“She wants me to come and fight her. At the top. By the chocks. Or on the slope, below the counterweight.” Riarhin looked at the three small blocks of stone wedged under the runners. “If I go for her there, I go for her with the counterweight beside me, or above me. If I strike at her and miss, I strike the chocks. If she gives way and I follow, I follow her down the slope in front of a cart of iron. If I knock her down, she goes down against the chocks.” She heard her own voice, very level. “She doesn’t need to take the chocks out herself. She only needs me to fight her there.”

Vesket was staring at her.

“She told me,” said Riarhin. “At the cutting. I’ll see you in the room. She’s laid it out for me.”

“Then don’t fight her there.”

“Then she takes the chocks out on the breath, and nobody stops her.”

There was a long silence on the top of the ring wall.

“There’s another way down,” said Osselin.

She had picked up the drawing from beside Vesket and was holding it close to her face in the morning light, frowning at it, with her finger on the tunnel.

“Here,” she said. “Along the side. This line. What’s this?”

Vesket looked. Riarhin looked. There was a line on the drawing beside the line of the tunnel, a thin line, running parallel to it a little way off, and then joining it at the bottom, at the joint.

“A ledge,” said Vesket slowly. “A maintenance ledge. Along the wall of the throat, below the terrace. For seeing to the walls.” He took the drawing from her. “It runs along the side of the tunnel, higher up, offset. You could go along it from the measured room and come down to the joint without going down the tunnel.” He looked up. “Without going past the counterweight.”

“Is it still there?”

“I don’t know.”

Riarhin looked down at the terraces below her, at the tunnel mouth, at the place where the throat went under the terrace and out of sight. She could not see the ledge from here.

“Then that’s the next thing,” she said.

“What is?”

“Finding out.” She got up. Her arm hurt. She did not care. “And finding the measured room. And finding a way up out of this that isn’t the channel. And finding out where she sits to watch the chocks.” She looked at Vesket. “You said three things. We need to know where they are before we can do any of them.”

“That’s days.”

“We have ten.”

Vesket looked at her a long time. Then he looked at Osselin, who was sitting on the paving with her unlit lantern in her lap, very small and very still.

“The cord,” said Osselin quietly. “If it’s cut. The spring-house.”

“Yes.”

“For good.”

“For good.”

Osselin looked down at the lantern. She turned it in her hands.

“Briar said a day or two,” she said. “Fennel said she’d say it every day.” She did not look up. “We should go back down off this wall before the mist comes up. We shouldn’t be up here when she wakes, wherever she is. And we should eat something.”

She got up, and put the lantern away, and began to pack.

Part IV — Separate tasks

Chapter 27 — What the working can hold

The measured room was where the drawing said it would be, which surprised all three of them.

It took two days to reach it. They went down off the ring wall by the maintenance steps on the north side, and along the foot of the wall outside, and up again by another stair Osselin found a mile further on, a stair cut into the inside face of the ring that went down from the top in a long zigzag to the highest terrace. They went down it at dusk, between breaths of the mist, on Osselin’s count. They slept on the highest terrace in a gallery whose arches had not been walled, with the mist coming and going below them, and in the morning they went down two terraces more by a ramp that had been built for feet, and along a gallery on the north side, keeping always to the inside of the arches, out of sight of the tunnel mouth and the counterweight on its sledge.

They did not see Farieth.

Riarhin looked for her. Both nights, while the others slept, she went out along the galleries alone, quietly, with Remorse in her left hand and her right arm bound against her body. She found places Farieth had been. A gallery on the east side, above the seal, where the dust on the floor had been disturbed and there was a flat stone with the ash of a very small fire on it, cold. A ledge on the south side with a view down onto the chocks, where somebody had sat for a long time and left the shape of themselves in the dust, one leg drawn up and one stretched out. A strip of grey linen caught on a broken edge of stone, the kind she had seen bound round Farieth’s hand.

She never found Farieth in any of them. She did not expect to. She knew how Farieth waited. Farieth would not sleep twice where she had slept once, or watch twice from the same place. She would be somewhere in the galleries with a view of the chocks and a way out behind her, and she would know Riarhin was looking for her, and she would let her look.

On the second night Riarhin stood in the gallery above the seal, by the cold ash of the little fire, and looked down at the counterweight on its sledge in the starlight. It was three terraces and a slope. She could have gone down and done something to the chocks, or the rope, or the tripod. She stood there and thought of all the things she could do, and of what each of them would do, and of where Farieth would be watching from while she did it.

She went back to the others.

The measured room was on the second terrace on the east side, a little north of the tunnel mouth, so that its door looked along the terrace towards the top of the throat. It was square. Its walls were plain pale stone. Its floor was laid in squares of two colours, pale and grey, in a pattern that was not quite a chequerboard: the squares were of different sizes, large and small, and they fitted together in a way Riarhin’s eye kept trying to follow and losing.

Vesket stood in the doorway and looked at it.

“Oh,” he said.

He went in and knelt on the floor in the middle of it and put his good hand flat on a pale square and then on a grey one. He looked up at the walls, at the ceiling, which was low and plain, at the corners. He looked at the floor again.

“It’s the court,” he said. “It’s the same measure.” He laughed, and the laugh broke in the middle. “I drew it from her drawing. She drew it from this. I carried it for six months and I burned it in a ditch and it was here the whole time, laid in stone.”

“Can you use it?”

“I think so.” He sat back on his heels. “I think so. It isn’t measured to the channel. It’s measured to the joint. That’s what the keepers used it for.” He put both hands on the floor. “I think I can hold the joint from here. Through a whole cycle. I think.”

“You keep saying think.”

“Because I don’t know.” He looked at her. “I’ve never done this. Nobody living has done this. I’ve done it in a cellar to a stone and in a passage to some roots. This is—” He looked down at the floor. “This is the whole of it.”

At the back of the room there was a low door, and beyond it a ledge.

Riarhin found it while Vesket was on his knees. The door was half the height of a person, set into the back wall, and it opened onto a ledge of stone no wider than her shoulders that ran along the inside wall of the throat, high above its floor, and went down at a slant, steeply, out of sight into the dark. She lay on her stomach in the low doorway and looked along it. The ledge was whole as far as she could see. Below it, three times her height down, was the paved floor of the throat, sloping away towards the joint. Above it, a little way back up the slope, she could see the iron runners of the sledge and the front of the counterweight and the three stone chocks, very close, almost close enough to reach with a long spear.

A trip-line ran from the chocks down the throat.

She had not seen it from the ring wall. It was thin, a cord no thicker than her little finger, tied round each of the three chocks and joined into one, and the one cord ran away down the floor of the throat into the dark towards the joint, laid along the side of the slope where nobody walking down the middle would tread on it.

Riarhin traced the line down into the dark. Then she went back and told them.

“She means to pull them from the bottom,” said Vesket. “From the joint. At the breath.”

“So she can be there,” said Riarhin. “When it strikes.”

“Why would she want to be there?”

Riarhin did not answer at once. She thought of Farieth sitting on her block of stone at the cutting, bending iron over with her knee. I’ll see you in the room.

“Because she wants to see it,” she said. “And because that’s where I’ll go.”

At the side of the measured room there was an alcove.

It was small, a recess in the wall no deeper than a cupboard, and its floor was laid in the same pattern of pale and grey squares as the room, only smaller, as though somebody had made a little copy of the floor for practice. In its back wall there was a niche, a round hole the size of a plate. And across the niche, from one side to the other, ran a single pale strand, like a tendon, the thickness of a finger, stretched taut.

When the mist breathed, the niche changed.

Osselin saw it first. She was sitting against the wall by the alcove with her lantern, counting, and on the breath she stopped counting and said, “Look.”

Through the round niche, past the pale strand, they could see a pool. A small round pool of clear water in a ring of mossy stones, with ferns round it and green light on it, and a single leaf turning on its surface. It was somewhere else. It was there for the length of the breath and then the mist sank and the niche was a niche again, dark, with the strand across it.

“A crossing,” said Vesket softly. “A little one. A window. The keepers must have used it to see something. A spring. A well.” He looked at the strand. “And that’s what ties it in.”

“The way the cord ties the others in.”

“The same. Smaller.” He did not look away from it.

They tested it that afternoon.

It was Riarhin who proposed it, and she was surprised when the others agreed at once. They needed to know what Vesket could hold, and for how long, and what happened when he stopped. And what happened when a strand like that was cut. The alcove was small and the crossing in it was small and nobody they knew lived at the other end of it.

“We think,” said Osselin.

Nobody answered that.

Vesket knelt on the little floor of the alcove with the vellum unrolled across his knees and Tessel on her felt beside him and the brass compasses in his good hand, and on the breath, when the niche opened onto the pool, he did his working.

Riarhin watched. His lips moved. The lamp flame stood straight. The air went still. The swelling should have ebbed, closing the niche until the next began, forty eights after the first. It did not ebb. The pool stayed in the niche. The leaf on its surface stopped turning and hung there, still, as though the water had gone to glass. The mist in the alcove doorway, which had flowed in on the breath, stopped flowing and stood in the air, a grey curl, motionless, like something drawn.

“Now,” said Vesket through his teeth.

Riarhin cut the strand.

She set Toothpick to it. The strand resisted like horn. She sawed at it with the edge and it did not give, and she set the point against it and leaned on the hilt with her good hand and it did not give, and Vesket made a sound behind her, a thin sound, like a man holding something heavy at arm’s length, and she put her whole weight on the knife and felt the strand part.

It went with a soft sound, like a plucked string, very low.

The pool in the niche went dark.

It did not fade. It was there, still, glass-clear, with the leaf hanging on it, and then it was not there, and the niche was a round hole in a wall with nothing behind it but stone. The cut ends of the strand drew back into the sides of the niche, slowly, like the horns of a snail, and were gone.

Vesket held on.

He held on, trying to reach the end of the cycle. Riarhin counted from the start of his working, under her breath, eight and eight and eight, while the grey curl of mist stood motionless in the doorway and the alcove floor under her knees felt very solid, more solid than stone, as though it had been nailed to the world. At twenty-nine eights he began to shake. At thirty-four the lamp flame bent. At thirty-seven, outside on the terrace, Riarhin heard stone move: a long low grinding sound from somewhere along the gallery, and a patter of small pieces falling.

At thirty-nine Vesket let go.

The mist in the doorway flowed again. The lamp flame stood. The floor was a floor. Vesket fell forward onto his hands on the little grey and pale squares and stayed there with his head hanging, breathing in long ragged pulls.

Osselin had her arm round him before Riarhin could move.

“What was that outside?” Riarhin said.

Osselin went to look. She came back.

“A piece of the gallery roof,” she said. “Along there. Twenty paces. It came down. It wasn’t there before.”

Vesket lifted his head.

“That’s what it costs,” he said. He could hardly speak. “Holding it here. It pulls from somewhere else.” He sat back slowly against the wall of the alcove. “That was a cupboard. A little floor and a little crossing. And it took everything I had for forty eights and the roof came down twenty paces off.”

Nobody spoke.

Riarhin knelt in front of the niche and looked at it. A round hole in a stone wall. She put her hand into it. There was nothing. Stone, cool and smooth, at the back. The pool with the turning leaf was gone. It was somewhere, she supposed, a little round pool in a ring of mossy stones in some part of the forest, and nobody would ever see it through this hole again.

The spring-house cellar would be a cellar.

Riarhin looked towards Osselin and kept quiet.

She took her hand out of the niche.

That evening they went through it again. Not the plan. Everything that was not the plan. Vesket made them, sitting in the measured room with the vellum across his knees, his voice still hoarse.

They could go home. They could go back the way they had come, and be at the Gate in nine days, before the alignment, and tell the Tide Court what they had seen, and let Farieth take the chocks out on the breath. Nobody said what would happen then.

They could try to kill her before the breath. Riarhin could look for eight more nights, and Farieth would let her look.

They could do something to the counterweight. Break the tripod, cut the hold-back rope, take out the chocks themselves and let it go now, before the alignment, when the joint was not straining. Vesket thought about that one for a long time.

“I don’t know what it would do,” he said at last. “It might do nothing. It might crack the joint and she’d have a cracked joint to work on at the breath instead of a whole one. It might do what she wants, early.” He shook his head. “I don’t know. I can’t find out without doing it.”

They could cut the cord now, at the joint, without holding anything. Vesket shook his head at once.

“If the cord’s cut and the joint isn’t held, the whole ring tries to close at once, with all of us inside it, and her, and the counterweight at the top of the slope. And the joint’s straining already. I don’t know what it does. I know it isn’t good.”

They went round it twice. Osselin turned her lantern in her lap. Vesket unfolded the drawing again, and Riarhin had to make herself sit still.

“The breath,” she said at last. “When she pulls the line. You hold the joint. I cut the cord. She tells us the way out.” She looked at Osselin. “That’s what we said on the wall.”

“That’s what we said.”

“And you lose the spring-house.”

Osselin looked at her.

“I lost the spring-house on the wall,” she said. “When he said it. I’ve been losing it for two days. You don’t need to say it again.” She turned her lantern in her hands. “I want to send word. Before. To Briar. To Fennel. To the wardens. I want them told that it won’t open, so they stop waiting, and so the families stop coming up the river with handcarts. I want it to be from me.”

“How?”

Osselin looked at the alcove, at the round dark niche with nothing in it.

“The old way,” she said. “The way we always do. I’ll need help.”

“Mine?”

“Yours. And somebody outside.” Osselin almost smiled. “I think I know who.”

Vesket said, from where he sat against the wall, “The obligation.”

They looked at him.

“The cost. Of holding it.” He did not look up from the vellum. “It isn’t only the roof. It’s me. Every time I do it, more is asked. After the breath a great deal will be, and I don’t know what.” He touched the edge of the vellum. “That’s mine. I only wanted it said. I don’t want anybody thinking it’s free because I didn’t mention it.”

“Nobody thought it was free,” said Osselin.

“I know.” He rolled the vellum. “I wanted it said.”

Chapter 28 — The warning sent back

The moss-woman was where Osselin thought she would be.

There was a standing stone outside the ring, on the north side, a quarter mile from the foot of the maintenance steps they had come up by: a tall grey stone at the edge of the trees, crusted with lichen in many colours. Riarhin had seen it on the way and had not looked at it closely. Osselin had.

“It was growing,” she said. “The yellow. Fresh. Somebody’s been tending it.”

“The same one?”

“There must be more than one stone.” Osselin had something in her lap. “There’s probably more than one woman. But they’ll talk to each other. That’s what she’s for. She tends the stones and the stones tell the other stones. Or she walks between them. I don’t know which. If I leave word with whoever tends that stone, it’ll go.”

“Go where?”

“Out. To the next stone, and the next. To the edge. To the reeves at the Gate, I should think. Tern will know what to do with it. Tern sends to Thornholt and the wardens and Briar.” Osselin tied a knot. “It’s slow. It isn’t like a letter. But it goes.”

She had a length of red thread in her lap, and she was tying knots in it. Riarhin watched. They were not the knots of the way-cord. They were small, close, in groups, some single and some doubled, with spaces between, and once she tied three in a row and then untied the middle one and tied it again a little to one side.

“What does it say?”

“That a warden of Amedell has shut a crossing, and it won’t open, and the shutting is by her hand and nobody else’s, and people should go round.” Osselin held it up and looked at it. “Every warden from here to the coast can read that. My grandmother could have read it.”

“Does it say which crossing?”

“It says the spring-house. That’s this knot.” She touched a doubled knot near the end. “And this one says the field at Wendle stays shut too. And this,” she touched three single knots close together, “says don’t wait.”

Riarhin looked at the three knots.

“Fennel will read that?”

“Briar will. Briar will read it and tell Fennel.” Osselin did not look up. “And Fennel will say I told you and go and find work on somebody else’s boat.”

She finished the thread. She wound it round two fingers into a small hank and tied the ends. Then she took out a scrap of paper and a stub of pencil and wrote on it, slowly, in a round careful hand, and folded it twice.

“For the moss-woman,” she said. “In case she can’t read knots. She’ll be able to read that. Nobody’s name in it. Nothing for the stone to grow.”

Riarhin took out her own paper.

She had been carrying it since the measured room. She had written on it four times and scraped it four times with her knife. Now she wrote it again, sitting on the floor of the gallery with her back against an arch and the mist coming and going below, and she wrote it short, because everything longer had sounded like a woman saying goodbye.

C. and H.

I am past the Gate, in the forest, with the Sumwright and the warden. Our grandmother is here. She means to do something at a place under the trees that would hurt a great many people, not only us. We mean to stop her. The Sumwright says it will be some days yet.

Do not come. Do not send. Keep the cupboard shut. If word comes from the reeves at the Gate that the spring-house above the Slow Water will not open again, believe it. That is our doing. Tell anyone who asks to go round.

If you hear nothing, wait until the first frost and then write to the Sumwright’s hall at Thornholt. Ask for Vesper. She will know what the Tide Court knows.

It falls. He checked after rain.

She read it twice. She did not change anything. She folded it, not in three, and tied it with a length of Osselin’s red thread, a plain knot, and gave it to Osselin.

Osselin took it without looking at it and put it with her own.

“Who’s going?” said Vesket.

He was sitting by the door of the measured room, where they had come back to sleep, with Tessel in his lap. He had not done any working since the alcove. He had slept most of the day, and eaten what Osselin put in front of him, and his face had come back a little from the colour of stone.

“Me,” said Osselin.

“Alone?”

“With her watching.” Osselin nodded at Riarhin. “From the top of the wall. She can see the stone from there. She can see the steps and the trees and the whole of the ground between.” She looked at Riarhin. “And she can see if anybody comes.”

“And if somebody does?”

“Then she shouts,” said Osselin, “and I run.”

Vesket looked at her. Then he looked at Riarhin.

“She could take it,” he said. “She’s faster.”

“She’s faster. She’s also the one our friend with the spear wants.” Osselin tucked the hank of thread and the papers into her shirt. “If our friend sees her going down the steps alone, she’ll know something’s happening. If she sees me going down, she’ll think the lamp-girl’s gone to fetch water.” She stood up. “And it’s my warning. My knots. My crossing.” She looked at Vesket. “I’m carrying it. Don’t argue with me about carrying.”

He did not argue.

They went up the zigzag stair to the top of the ring wall in the grey before dawn, between breaths of the mist, on Osselin’s count. At the top, Riarhin lay down on her stomach at the outer edge of the wall above the maintenance steps, where she could see down the long slant of them to the forest floor and along the edge of the trees to the standing stone. Osselin stood beside her a moment with her lantern, unlit, and the hank of thread and the papers in her shirt.

“If I don’t come back,” said Osselin.

“You’ll come back.”

“If I don’t,” said Osselin, “don’t come down after me. You’ve got a job. So has he.” She looked down the steps. “I mean that.”

Riarhin looked at her.

“I know you mean it,” she said.

Osselin nodded. She went over the edge onto the first of the tiny steps, heel first, with her body pressed to the wall, and began to go down.

It took her a long time. Riarhin watched every step of it. She watched the small figure crab down the long slant of the wall, step and step and step, past the bulge where the frame had caught, down and down, until she was small enough to cover with a thumb. She watched her reach the bottom and stand a moment at the foot of the wall, and look round, and then walk out across the open ground towards the trees.

Nothing moved.

Riarhin did not watch only Osselin. She watched the trees. She watched the edge of the forest on either side of the open ground, every tree, every patch of fern, every shadow. She watched the line of the ring wall curving away on either side. She watched the standing stone at the edge of the trees and the ground round it. She lay on the cold pale stone with her chin on her good hand and her sword beside her and her eyes moving, moving, and she did not let them rest on anything for longer than a breath.

Osselin reached the stone.

She stood in front of it. Riarhin saw her lift her hand and touch the stone, very lightly, and take her hand away. Then she stood still, waiting.

Nothing happened for a long time.

Then something moved in the ferns behind the stone.

Riarhin’s hand closed on her sword. She half rose. Then she saw what it was: a small brown shape, wrapped in something green, sitting up out of the ferns on a log, with a tray across its knees. It had been there all along. She had looked at that patch of ferns twenty times and not seen it.

Osselin bowed.

Riarhin could not hear what they said. She saw Osselin take the hank of thread from her shirt and hold it out, and the moss-woman take it and run it through her fingers, knot by knot. She saw the moss-woman stop at one place and look up at Osselin, and Osselin nod. She saw Osselin give her the two folded papers, and the moss-woman unfold Osselin’s and read it, and not unfold Riarhin’s, and put them both in her lap on the tray.

Then the moss-woman said something, and pointed.

She pointed back at the foot of the maintenance steps, and then along the foot of the wall and away into the trees, north, past the standing stone and on into the forest.

Osselin turned and looked where she pointed.

They talked for a long time. Riarhin watched the trees. Twice she thought she saw something move at the edge of the forest to the east, and her whole body went still, and it was a bird both times, or the wind in the ferns, though there was no wind. She did not stop watching.

At last Osselin bowed again and turned and came back across the open ground towards the steps.

Halfway across, she stopped.

Riarhin saw her stop. She saw her turn her head, quickly, towards the east, towards the edge of the trees on that side, and stand very still.

Riarhin looked where she was looking.

There was somebody at the edge of the trees.

Tall, grey, very still, standing just inside the line of the forest a hundred paces east of Osselin, with a long pole held upright beside her. Not moving. Watching.

Riarhin was on her feet.

She did not shout. She did not need to. Osselin had seen. Osselin stood in the middle of the open ground, small and alone, with the steps a hundred paces in front of her and the tall grey figure a hundred paces to her right, and she did not run.

She lifted her lantern.

She had lit it at some point. Riarhin had not seen her do it. She held it up at arm’s length, towards the figure in the trees, and the little flame burned inside its horn panes, steady.

The figure in the trees did not move either.

Riarhin was already over the edge of the wall. She was on the steps, going down them fast, too fast, with her bound arm against her chest and her left hand on the step above and the front of her boots finding the tiny hollows and slipping and finding them again, and the wall was going past her face, and she could not see the open ground any more, only the pale stone, and she was counting the steps without meaning to and she would not get there in time, she knew she would not, it was a long way down and Farieth was a hundred paces from Osselin and had a spear.

She reached the bulge and went over it without stopping and nearly fell.

When she could see the ground again Osselin was walking towards the steps.

Walking. Not running. Holding the lantern up. And the tall grey figure was still standing at the edge of the trees to the east, exactly where it had been, with the pole upright beside it, watching her go.

Riarhin reached the bottom of the steps and stood at the foot of the wall with her sword drawn.

Osselin came up to her. Her face was white. Her hand holding the lantern was shaking so badly that the flame jumped in its panes.

“I told you not to come down,” she said.

Riarhin did not answer. She was looking past Osselin at the edge of the trees.

The figure was gone.

Riarhin watched the place where it had been. Nothing moved there. The ferns were still. She did not know when it had gone. She had been on the steps, looking at stone.

“She didn’t come,” said Osselin. “She stood there. I lifted the lamp. It’s what you do, in the lanes, if something’s standing in the dark where it shouldn’t be. You lift the lamp so it knows you’ve seen it.” She was breathing fast. “She stood there and watched me lift it. And then I walked. And she let me.”

“Why?”

“I don’t know.” Osselin lowered the lantern. “Because I’m not you. Because I wasn’t carrying anything she wanted. Because she wanted me to go back up and tell you she’d been standing there.” She looked at Riarhin. “You came down.”

“Yes.”

“I told you not to.”

“I know.”

Osselin looked at her for a long moment. Then she put the lantern down on the ground and put both her arms round Riarhin and held on, hard, with her face against Riarhin’s chest, and Riarhin stood at the foot of the ring wall with her sword still in her hand and her other arm bound against her body and let her.

“The moss-woman took it,” Osselin said into her shirt. “Both. She said it would go. She said it would be at the Gate in eight days if the stones were willing.”

“And the pointing?”

Osselin let go. She stepped back and wiped her face with the back of her wrist.

“She showed me a way,” she said. “From the bottom of these steps. Along the wall and then off into the trees, past her stone, north. She says it’s an old way. Keepers’ way. It goes out of the forest to the north. Not the way we came. Not the Gate.” She looked at the trees. “She says she’ll keep it clear. For us. If we need it. If we come down these steps in a hurry with the ring closing behind us, she says, go past her stone and keep going north and don’t stop.” She picked up the lantern. “I asked her why she’d do that for us.”

“What did she say?”

“That I’d asked properly.” Osselin almost laughed. “And that she didn’t want what was in the ring coming out past her stones either.”

They stood at the foot of the wall. The mist breathed on the other side of it; Riarhin could feel it through the stone, very faintly, if she put her hand on it. She put her hand on it.

“Thirty-nine eights,” said Osselin. “At dawn. I counted on the way down.”

“It’s getting shorter.”

“Yes.” Osselin looked up at the long slant of the maintenance steps. “Now we have to climb back up those.”

They climbed. Riarhin went behind this time, close, with her good hand ready, and at the bulge Osselin went over it without stopping and did not swear once.

Chapter 29 — The hour for sleep

On the last evening, nine eights separated the start of one breath from the start of the next.

Osselin had counted it every dawn and every dusk for six days, standing at the inner edge of the second terrace with her lantern held out over the drop, and every count had been shorter. Thirty-nine. Thirty-one. Twenty-two. Fourteen. Eleven. At dusk on the sixth day she counted three breaths before coming back into the measured room to sit down.

“Nine,” she said. “Nine and a bit. Nine and a hair.”

Vesket did not look up from the vellum. “Then tomorrow.”

“Tomorrow.”

“In the afternoon. Or towards evening. When it’s eight.” He drew a line. “She’ll know when it’s eight. She’ll pull the line when the interval reaches eight eights.”

Nobody said anything.

They had spent the six days learning the ground. Riarhin had walked the ledge from the low door at the back of the measured room down to the joint four times, twice by day and twice by night, until she could have done it blind. It went down along the north wall of the throat, steeply, with the wall on her left and the drop to the throat floor on her right. For the first part of it, she could look back up over her shoulder and see the low door, and the terrace beyond it, and the foot of the ramp where Osselin would be. Then the ledge went under the terrace above and into the dark, and she could see nothing behind her but stone. From there, it went on down, in the dark, for a long way, and came out on the north side of the joint.

She had stood in the joint each time for as long as she dared.

It was a round room under the dome, very large, its walls going up into darkness. Out of the walls, all round, came the ribs. They were great pale curving things, thicker than the trunks of the forest trees, smooth as bone, coming out of the walls at the height of a man’s head and curving down and inward to the middle of the room, where they all met. Where they met, they were bound together in a knot of the same pale stuff, a great twisted mass the height of Riarhin’s waist and twice as wide, like the knot of a tree’s roots, or the knuckle of a fist.

The binding. The joint.

Out of the binding, low down on the west side, ran a single strand. It was pale, like the ribs, but thin, as thick as Riarhin’s arm, and it ran away from the knot along the floor and into a low dark arch in the west wall, towards the seal and the channel. The cord. She had laid her hand on it once, the first night, and felt it hum under her palm, very faintly, at the pace of the breath.

Round the edge of the room, a little raised above the floor, ran a walk. A stone path the width of a person, a step up, going all the way round the wall under the place where the ribs came out of it. It joined the foot of the ledge on the north side, and on the east side it passed the bottom of the throat, where the paved slope came down into the room, and on the south side it went past a grating in the wall, a square of old pale metal bars as high as her knee with darkness behind it.

“A drain,” Vesket had said, when she drew it for him. He found it on Farieth’s drawing: a thin line from the joint going out under the ring, north and east, and stopping at the edge of the paper. “For seepage. For whatever came out of the binding when it was worked on. It goes out under the ring.”

“Where to?”

“The drawing doesn’t say. It runs off the edge.”

“Water went down it,” said Riarhin. “Water always goes somewhere.”

Vesket had looked at her and said nothing.

The trip-line came down the throat from the chocks and into the joint on the east side and ran across the floor to an iron ring set in the stone beside the binding, where it was tied. Riarhin had looked at it each time and not touched it. Each time she had wanted to cut it. Each time she had thought about what Farieth would do when she came down at dusk and found it cut, and where she would go next, and how long before the breath it would be, and she had left it.

Osselin had marked the way out.

She had done it carefully, over three days, going along the gallery and up the ramp and up the zigzag stair and along the top of the ring wall to the maintenance steps, and back, and along again. At every turn she had tied a short length of red thread round something, a broken column, an iron ring in a wall, the edge of a step, at the height of a hand, so that somebody running past with their eyes half shut could put out a hand and feel it. At the top of the ramp, where the gallery turned, she had chalked an arrow on the floor, and then rubbed it out and drawn it again larger, and then gone down to the bottom of the ramp and come up it running, with her eyes on the floor, to see whether she would see it.

“You won’t be looking at the floor,” Riarhin had said.

“Somebody might be.” Osselin had stood at the top of the ramp, breathing hard. “Somebody frightened. Somebody with something in their eyes. You don’t look where you’re going when you’re frightened. You look where you’ve been.” She had gone back down and drawn a second arrow, on the wall, at the height of a shoulder. “There. Now there’s two.”

Vesket had re-proved his Codex.

He did it on the fifth day, sitting on the floor of the measured room with the vellum unrolled across the grey and pale squares and the blue ink Osselin had made for him in a hollow stone from the last of the apple vinegar, which she had been carrying since Thornholt without telling anyone. He drew the whole great figure again, every line, the way he had at Underarch. It took him the whole of the afternoon. Tessel drew beside him. When he had finished he sat looking at it for a long time and then rolled it up and put it away and did not say anything.

Now, on the last evening, he was drawing again. Not the Codex. A small figure on the slate Teshan had given him at the amphitheatre, which he had carried since. Riarhin watched him.

“What’s that?”

“The floor.” He did not look up. “This floor. Where I’ll sit. Where my hands go. Which squares.” He drew a line. “I want to know it so well that I don’t have to look.”

“Do you know it?”

“I knew the cellar floor. I knew the passage under the stage. I knew the alcove.” He drew another line. “I didn’t know any of them well enough.”

Osselin got up and went to the food bag.

There was not much left. They had been eating carefully since the cliff. There was some hard bread, and a little cheese, and the last of Pellet’s dried fish, and at the bottom of the bag the little round loaf with the cross cut in the top that Osselin had packed in Wendle and had not let anybody eat.

She took it out.

“Now?” said Riarhin.

“Now.” Osselin broke it in three. “It’s a guest-loaf. You eat it when you’ve come somewhere and been let in.”

“We haven’t been let in.”

“We’ve been let in by the old woman with the board and the moss-woman with the tray and a door in the wall nobody’s opened for a thousand years.” Osselin held out a piece. “That’s three. Eat it.”

Riarhin took it. It had been baked a month ago in a red-doored house on a green in Amedell and carried across a river and up a cliff and through a sea-arch and down a waterfall and over a ring wall, and it was hard as a stone. She soaked it in water from the skin and ate it slowly. It tasted of rye and caraway and very faintly of smoke.

Vesket ate his without looking at it, drawing.

“He’s doing it again,” said Osselin.

“Doing what?”

“Eating without noticing. You put food in front of him and he eats it and afterwards he couldn’t tell you what it was.” Osselin looked at him. “At Kelling I gave him a whole pie and he ate it while he was working out the angle of a bridge and afterwards he asked when supper was.”

Vesket said, without looking up, “It was a very good bridge.”

“It fell down.”

“It fell down because they didn’t build it the way I drew it.”

“They built it the way you drew it. You drew it wrong because you were eating a pie.”

Vesket put down his pencil.

“That isn’t true,” he said.

“It’s completely true. Ask anybody at Kelling.”

“Nobody at Kelling remembers.”

“Everybody at Kelling remembers. They call it Vesket’s Bridge. They put a sign up.” Osselin was trying not to laugh and failing. “It says Vesket’s Bridge and under it somebody’s drawn a pie.”

Vesket stared at her. Then he put his face in his hands, and his shoulders shook, and Riarhin realised he was laughing, helplessly, silently, the way people laugh when they are very tired. Osselin laughed too. Riarhin sat with the hard bread in her hand and watched them and found that she was laughing as well, and could not stop, and did not try.

When it was over Vesket wiped his eyes.

“I never saw the sign,” he said.

“I’ll take you,” said Osselin. “When we get back. We’ll go to Kelling and you can look at it.”

“I don’t want to look at it.”

“You’ll look at it,” said Osselin, “and I’ll stand behind you, and that’ll be the end of it.”

He looked at her. Riarhin looked down at her bread.

She bound her arm.

The cut had closed well. It was a long red line from shoulder to elbow, healing at the edges, and it no longer bled, but it was stiff and it pulled when she lifted her arm above her shoulder. She unwound Osselin’s old linen and looked at it in the lamplight and then bound it again with a clean strip, tighter, so that the arm would not try to move in ways that would tear it. She flexed the fingers of her right hand. They closed. They would hold a wall. They would not hold a sword. She had not fought with her right hand in years. It did not matter.

She checked Remorse. She drew it and looked at the edge in the lamplight, and wiped it, and put a little oil on a rag and ran the rag along both edges, and looked at the guard and the grip and the binding of the grip, and put it back in the scabbard. She honed Toothpick, slowly. It had taken all her weight to cut a finger-thick strand in the alcove. The Last Blade lay beside it, its shortened point already dressed and oiled.

She would need something else for the cord.

For six days she had tried to think of a better edge. Neither duelling knife would do. Osselin had a bread knife and shears; Vesket had nothing heavier. The cord in the joint was as thick as her arm and hard as horn, and she had stood with her hand on it and known that she could saw at it with Toothpick for an hour and not get through it.

She had seen what would get through it. A broken chisel lying in a heap of dark chips at the foot of a seal. Farieth would have another chisel. She would have it with her, at the joint, for the iron.

Riarhin put the knife away.

“What will you use?” Vesket asked.

“Her chisel. And her hammer.”

His pencil stopped.

“You’ve seen them?”

“The broken one by the seal. She’ll need another for the iron.” Riarhin left her hand on Toothpick’s hilt. “I haven’t seen it yet.”

“So you have to take it from her,” said Osselin. “Before you can cut anything.”

“Yes.”

Vesket looked at the slate, then back at her.

“If she hasn’t brought it?”

“I come back.”

“And if you can’t get it?”

Riarhin took her hand off the knife.

“The same.”

Vesket fell asleep in the middle of a sentence.

He had been explaining something to Osselin about the slate, about which squares on the floor were which, and his voice slowed and slowed, and stopped, and when Riarhin looked up he was asleep, sitting against the wall with the slate in his lap and his head tipped sideways and his mouth a little open.

Osselin looked at him.

She got up, and went to him, and took the slate out of his lap and put it on the floor beside him. She took the blanket from her own bedding and put it over him. She moved the lamp so that it would not shine in his eyes. Then she sat down beside him, against the wall, close enough to touch, and did not touch him.

“He meant to stay awake,” she said quietly. “He meant to go over it all again.”

“He knows it.”

“I know he knows it.” Osselin looked at him. “He doesn’t know he knows it. That’s different.”

Riarhin did not answer.

After a while Osselin lay down on the floor beside him with her head on her arm. She did not sleep at once. Riarhin watched her lie there with her eyes open, looking at the ceiling. Then her eyes closed. Then her breathing changed.

Riarhin sat awake.

She sat with her back against the wall by the low door at the back of the room, the door that went out onto the ledge, with Remorse across her knees, and listened to the two of them breathing, and to the mist breathing on the other side of the wall, nine eights and a hair.

She could go now.

She could get up, quietly, and go out through the low door onto the ledge, and go down it in the dark, the way she had gone four times, and be in the joint before midnight. Farieth would not be there yet. Farieth would come down at some hour of the night or the next day to be ready for the breath. Riarhin could be waiting for her. She could be at the bottom of the throat in the dark when Farieth came down it, and Farieth would not know she was there, and it would be over before the others woke.

They would not have to hold anything. They would not have to cut anything. Osselin would not have to stand at the foot of the ramp with her lantern. Vesket would not have to sit on a floor of grey and pale squares and hold the whole of the joint in his hands until the roof came down somewhere. The spring-house would stay a crossing.

She would do it alone, in the dark, the way she had done the things she was best at. The way she had gone along the steppe alone killing Tulgoth’s sons.

She sat with her hand on the sword and thought it all the way through.
