Farieth would not come down the throat at night. Farieth would know Riarhin had walked the ledge. She had stood at the edge of the trees and watched Osselin go to the stone; she had sat on a ledge on the south side with a view of the chocks. She would know which way Riarhin would come and where she would wait. She would come by some way Riarhin had not walked, at some hour Riarhin had not guessed. And Riarhin would be in the joint alone, with nobody holding anything, and nobody at the top of the ramp with a lantern, and the breath at nine and a hair and shortening.

And if it went wrong, it would go wrong with Vesket and Osselin asleep in the measured room above the throat, not knowing.

She sat a long time.

Then she took her hand off the sword.

She did not get up. She sat with her back against the wall by the low door and watched the lamp burn down. Once, Vesket said something in his sleep, a word in his own language, and Osselin, without waking, put out a hand and found his sleeve and held it, and he was quiet.

Riarhin did not sleep. She had not expected to.

Towards dawn she got up and went to the door of the measured room and looked out along the terrace. The mist lay in the bottom of the bowl, very low, very still. Twenty paces south along the terrace the counterweight sat on its sledge at the lip of the throat, black in the grey light, with its three chocks under its runners and the trip-line running away down the slope. The tripod stood over it. Nothing moved.

She went back in and woke them.

“It’s morning,” she said.

Osselin sat up at once. Vesket woke slowly, and looked at the blanket over him, and at Osselin, and at the slate on the floor beside him, and then at Riarhin.

“I fell asleep,” he said.

“Yes.”

“I meant to—”

“I know.”

He looked at the slate. Then he picked it up and looked at the figure on it for a long moment, and put it down.

“I know it,” he said, sounding surprised.

Osselin was already at the food bag.

“There’s bread,” she said. “And cheese. Eat it. Both of you. All of it. We’re not carrying it out.”

Chapter 30 — The places agreed

They went over it once more after they had eaten, sitting on the floor of the measured room. Riarhin drew it for them on Vesket’s slate, with his pencil, the whole of it, the way she had drawn it four times already.

The measured room, here, on the second terrace. Vesket in the middle of the floor, on the squares he had drawn on the slate.

The throat, here, twenty paces south along the terrace. The counterweight at its lip. The trip-line going down.

The ledge, here, from the low door, down the north wall of the throat, under the terrace, to the joint.

The joint, here, at the bottom, under the dome. The binding in the middle. The cord going out west under the low arch. The walk round the edge. The throat coming in on the east side. The grating on the south side.

The way out, here: from the door of the measured room north along the gallery to the foot of the ramp. Up the ramp to the third terrace. Along it to the zigzag stair. Up to the top of the ring wall. Along the wall to the maintenance steps. Down the steps. Along the foot of the wall to the moss-woman’s stone, and past it, north, and on.

Osselin at the foot of the ramp. From there she could see the door of the measured room, and the lip of the throat, and the top of the ledge where it came out of the low door. From the upper part of the ledge, Riarhin could see her.

“Until here,” said Riarhin, and put the pencil on the place where the ledge went under the terrace. “After that I can’t see you. Or the terrace. Or anything but the ledge.”

“And I can’t see you,” said Osselin.

“No.”

“Then the lamp’s no good after that.”

“The lamp’s for the top. For when I’m coming back. If I come back up the ledge and the lamp’s lit and steady, the way out’s clear.” Riarhin looked at her. “If it goes dark and light and dark and light, twice, the way out isn’t clear, and you’ve gone, and I find another.”

Osselin nodded.

“And the rest?” said Vesket.

“The rest is you.”

He took out the bent rod. It was wrapped in its strip of wool, and he unwrapped it and held it up. The crack near the end, at the flaw, showed as a dark line in the copper. He took the resonance plate out of Vesper’s box and hung it by its thread from his finger, and struck the plate with the bent rod, lightly.

The sound was ugly. The plate gave none of the clear note it gave to its own brass striker. It was harsh and split, a cracked sound with something wrong in the middle of it, and it went out of the room and along the terrace and down, Riarhin was sure, into the throat.

“Nobody else in the world makes a sound like that,” said Vesket.

“Good.”

“The throat will carry it. Stone carries. You’ll hear it at the bottom.” He struck it again, once. The cracked note went out and faded. “Once every eight counts, while I’m holding. Keep that count steady, whatever the mist does. That means it’s holding.”

“And withdrawal.”

He struck the plate and then the rod against the edge of the floor, quickly, one and then the other: the cracked note and then a dull lower one, falling.

“Like that,” he said. “Three times. A falling pair, three times. That means I can’t hold any more and I’m letting go and we’re going.” He looked at Riarhin. “Not come back. Not hurry. Going. That’s what it means.”

“I know what it means.”

“Say it.”

Riarhin looked at him.

“When I hear the falling pair three times,” she said, “you’ve let go and you’re going up the ramp with Osselin and you don’t wait. If I’m at the top of the ledge I come with you. If I’m not, I find another way.”

“And we don’t come back down for you.”

“And you don’t come back down for me.”

Osselin said, “We agreed that.”

“I know we agreed it. I want her to say it.” Vesket put the rod down. His face was very still. “She has to know exactly what we’ll do. I want her to remember that she said it here, sitting on this floor, eating cheese.”

Riarhin looked at him for a long moment.

“I said it,” she said. “Sitting on this floor. Eating cheese.”

“Thank you.”

“What if you can’t strike it?” said Osselin. “What if you’re too tired?”

Vesket did not answer at once.

“Then I’ll have stopped holding,” he said, “and she’ll know it some other way.”

Nobody said anything.

Riarhin looked at the slate. She put the pencil on the grating on the south side of the joint.

“If the ledge goes,” she said.

“The drain?” said Vesket.

“The drain.”

“It goes off the edge of the drawing.”

“It goes somewhere.” She looked at him. “I’m not going to need it. I want you to know it’s there.”

“So we know where to tell people to look,” said Osselin.

Riarhin did not answer that.

They saw Farieth at noon.

Riarhin was at the door of the measured room, looking out along the terrace towards the throat. Osselin was beside her with the lantern. Vesket was on the floor behind them, sitting in the middle of the squares he had drawn on the slate, not working yet, only sitting there with his hands on his knees and his eyes closed, getting the floor into his body.

Farieth came down the terraces on the south side.

She came openly. She came down a ramp on the far side of the throat, a broad ramp built for feet, in the full grey noon light, with nobody hiding and nothing to hide behind, and she walked along the second terrace on the south side towards the lip of the throat, slowly, one foot heavy and one foot light. She had the spear in her right hand, its butt on the paving, using it as a staff. At her belt there was a hammer, short-handled and heavy, and a chisel thrust through the belt beside it, long, its edge bright. Over her shoulder there was a coil of rope.

She came to the counterweight and stopped.

She stood beside it with her hand on the cold iron and looked across the throat, twenty paces of air and a long drop, at the door of the measured room where Riarhin stood.

She did not raise her hand. She did not speak. She stood there with her hand on the counterweight and looked at Riarhin, and Riarhin looked back.

Then Farieth looked down at the chocks. She bent, carefully, the bad leg first, using the spear, and put her hand on each of them in turn, and on the trip-line where it was tied round them. She straightened. She looked at the tripod, and the hold-back rope through its block, and the knot where it was made fast. She looked down the throat, along the trip-line, into the dark.

Then she went down.

Not down the throat. She went along the lip of it to a place on the south side, beside the tripod, where Riarhin had seen nothing, and stepped over the edge, and was gone. Riarhin saw the top of her head, for a moment, going down, and then not.

“There’s another ledge,” said Osselin, very quietly. “On her side.”

“Yes.”

“You didn’t see it.”

“No.”

“She had a ledge of her own all along.”

“Yes,” said Riarhin. “She would.”

They stood in the doorway. Behind them, Vesket had opened his eyes.

“She’s gone down,” he said.

“To the joint.”

“The breath?”

Osselin held up her lantern over the edge of the terrace and watched the flame. She counted under her breath. Riarhin watched the mist in the bottom of the bowl rise and sink. She did not count. She did not want to know the number in her body.

“Nine,” said Osselin. “Just. Nine, and not a hair more.” She lowered the lantern. “Towards evening. Like he said.”

“Then we go now,” said Riarhin.

Nobody moved.

Then Osselin put the lantern down on the floor and came to Riarhin and stood in front of her. She did not say anything. She reached up, because she had to reach a long way, and took the collar of Riarhin’s shirt in both hands and straightened it, as though Riarhin were a child going to market, and let go.

“There,” she said.

She picked up the lantern and went out of the door and along the terrace north, towards the foot of the ramp, without looking back.

Riarhin watched her go. She watched the small upright figure walk along the gallery, past the arches, in the grey light. Halfway to the ramp, the gallery bent a little round the curve of the ring, and a column stood at the bend, and Osselin went past the column.

Riarhin waited for her to come out the other side.

She did not come out. The column was between them, and the bend, and the arches beyond it, and Osselin was somewhere behind them, walking to the foot of the ramp, and Riarhin could not see her.

She had meant to watch her all the way. She had meant to see her reach the foot of the ramp and turn and lift the lantern. She stood in the doorway and looked at the column for longer than she should have, and the gallery stayed empty, and then, very small, at the far end of it, beyond the arches, at the foot of the ramp, a light came up. A little steady flame, behind horn, held up at arm’s length.

Riarhin let out her breath.

Behind her, Vesket said, “Go on.”

She turned. He was sitting in the middle of the floor of grey and pale squares with the vellum unrolled across his knees and the brass compasses in his good hand and Tessel on her felt beside him, drawing. The bent rod lay beside him, and the plate, hanging from its thread over his left knee.

“Go on,” he said again. “I’ve got the floor. She’s got the ramp. You’ve got the ledge.”

“Vesket.”

“Don’t.” He did not look up. “Don’t say anything. We’ve said it all. We said it eating cheese.”

She stood in the doorway a moment longer. Then she went to the low door at the back of the room, and crouched, and went out onto the ledge.

Chapter 31 — The first interruption

On the upper part of the ledge she looked back twice.

The first time, she could see the low door behind her and the edge of the terrace and, far along the gallery beyond the measured room, at the foot of the ramp, Osselin’s light. Small, steady, held up. The second time, a little further down, she could see only the light. The terrace had gone behind the curve of the throat wall.

The third time she looked back, she could see nothing but stone. The ledge had gone under the terrace above, and the light was gone.

She did not look back again.

She went down in the dark with her left hand on the wall and her right arm bound against her body and Remorse on her back. She had walked it four times. She knew where it narrowed, where a block had sunk and tilted, where a crack ran across it that she had to step over without looking. Below her and to the right, the throat fell away into darkness, and somewhere above and behind her, at its lip, the counterweight sat on its chocks.

Halfway down she heard the hammer.

It came up the throat from the joint, faint and regular, iron on iron. A blow. A pause. A blow. Somebody was driving something in.

She went on.

The ledge came out of the dark onto the north side of the joint, high on the wall, where the walk round the edge of the room began. She stopped at the end of it, crouched, and looked down.

The room was lit. There was a lamp on the floor beside the binding, a small clay one, and its light went up the pale ribs where they curved down from the walls and lay on the great twisted knot of the binding in the middle, and on the woman kneeling beside it.

Farieth had her back half turned. Her spear lay on the floor beside her, within reach. In her left hand she had a rod of black iron, and she was holding its end against the binding, in a crack in the pale stuff where two of the ribs met, and with the hammer in her right hand, her bound hand, she was driving it in. The rod went into the crack a finger’s breadth at each blow, and round it the pale knot gave a faint high sound, like a stone being split, and closed on it.

There were others. Six rods, driven into the binding between the ribs, each sunk a hand’s depth into the pale knot and standing out from it at an angle. Seven, with the one Farieth was driving.

The trip-line came across the floor from the bottom of the throat on the east side and was tied to an iron ring set in the stone beside her knee.

The breath came.

Riarhin felt it in the stone under her feet before she saw it. A long slow swell. The ribs creaked. All of them, together, a deep groaning sound from every side of the room, as though the whole of the great pale cage were being pulled outward and straining back. The mist came in round the edges of the room, from the dark mouths of the arches, grey and low, and flowed across the floor to the binding and round it and round Farieth’s knees, and Farieth stopped hammering and knelt very still until it ebbed.

Riarhin counted it. She did not want to, and she did.

Nine eights between swellings. Not a hair more.

One more breath, perhaps two. Then eight.

Farieth set the hammer to the rod again.

Riarhin went down off the end of the ledge onto the walk.

She lowered herself with her good hand and dropped the last of the height with her knees bent. It made very little sound. She went along the walk to the right, east, fast and low, keeping close under the ribs so that they were between her and the lamp.

Farieth stopped hammering.

Her head lifted. She did not turn round. She knelt by the binding and listened, and then, very slowly, she set the hammer down on the floor beside her spear.

Riarhin reached the east side of the room, where the throat came in.

The paved slope of the throat came down out of the dark into the room through a broad arch, and at the foot of it the trip-line turned off the side of the slope and ran across the floor towards the binding. Riarhin dropped off the walk beside it, took out Toothpick, and cut it.

It was ordinary cord. It parted at once.

The cut end on the binding side went slack and lay on the floor. The other end, the end that ran up the throat to the chocks, she took in her hand and drew up the slope a little way, out of reach of anybody standing at the bottom, and let it go. It lay on the slope in the dark.

Farieth had stood up.

She stood by the binding with the spear in her right hand and looked across the room at Riarhin, at the foot of the throat, with the knife in her hand and the cut line on the floor between them.

“Granddaughter,” she said.

Riarhin put the knife away. She drew Remorse.

“You came by the north ledge,” said Farieth. “I thought you would. You walked it four times. I heard you.” She did not seem troubled. She looked at the cut line on the floor. “That was quick. I hadn’t thought you’d go for the line first. I thought you’d come for me.”

“I thought about it.”

“I know.” Farieth almost smiled. “I watched you think about it on the terrace above the seal, by my fire. Twice.”

Riarhin stood at the foot of the throat with the sword in her left hand, its point low.

“You can’t pull it from here now,” she said.

“No.”

“You have to go up.”

“I have to go up.” Farieth looked at her, and then at the arch of the throat behind her, the paved slope going up into the dark. “And you are standing at the foot of it.”

The breath came.

The ribs groaned. The mist came in over the walk and across the floor, grey, low, round Riarhin’s boots, round Farieth’s, round the binding with its seven black rods. Riarhin did not count it. She felt it in the stone, long, longer than the last, and she felt it end, and she knew.

Eight.

Far up the throat, so faint she might have imagined it, came a sound.

It was harsh and buzzing, a cracked note with something wrong in the middle of it. It came down the throat and went round the walls and faded.

Farieth turned her head.

And the room changed.

All at once, in the middle of the breath, the ribs stopped groaning, as though a hand had been laid on them. The mist on the floor, which had been flowing in round the binding, stopped flowing. It lay there on the stone, grey, motionless, every curl of it fixed, like something drawn. The lamp flame by the binding stood up straight and did not flicker. Riarhin felt the floor under her feet go very solid, more solid than stone, the way the alcove floor had felt under her knees. Nailed to the world.

The cracked note came again, eight later. Faint, from far up.

Farieth looked at the mist lying motionless round her feet. She looked at the ribs. Then she looked up the throat, into the dark, towards the measured room.

“The Sumwright,” said Farieth. “In the keepers’ room.”

“Yes.”

“Holding it.”

“Yes.”

Farieth looked at the binding, and then at Riarhin.

“He can’t hold it long,” she said. “It took the old keepers twelve of them to hold it, in that room, in turns, and they did it for an hour at a time and no longer, and they had the whole of the ring behind them.” She lifted the spear. “He’s one man with a spider.”

She came across the floor.

She came straight, not fast, across the open floor, with the spear low in both hands and the bad leg swinging a little stiffly. The spear was longer than the sword by the length of Riarhin’s arm. Riarhin waited for it.

At four paces Farieth stopped and thrust.

It was a short thrust, low, at the knee, and Riarhin moved her foot and let it go past, and Remorse came round and down onto the shaft behind the blade and pushed it aside, and she stepped in along the line of the shaft to close.

Farieth had already gone back.

She had thrust and withdrawn in the same movement. She was a pace to the side and a pace back, and the spear blade was coming round again, wide, at the height of Riarhin’s ribs, and Riarhin had to stop her step and turn it and give back the ground she had taken.

They stood at four paces again.

She had learned. At the shutter she had come in close with the sabre and taken a cut in the forearm. Now she stood off at the spear’s length and thrust and withdrew, thrust and withdrew, and every thrust was at the place Riarhin would have to move to answer the last, and Riarhin could not get inside it.

The cracked note came down the throat.

Farieth thrust high, at the face. Riarhin turned her head and let the blade go past her ear and went in under it, low, and Farieth let go of the shaft with her bound hand and swung the butt round one-handed, the way she had on the shelf by the waterfall, and Riarhin was ready for it. She took it on the flat of Remorse, close to the guard, and turned the sword and let the butt slide away down the blade, and cut at the left hand on the shaft.

Farieth drew the hand back along the shaft. The edge took a strip of the grey sleeve.

Riarhin went on in.

And Farieth stepped back onto the slope.

It was a single step, up and back, onto the paved foot of the throat. Riarhin had been standing there. She had been pushed off it, a pace at a time, without seeing it happen, and now Farieth was on it, a pace up the slope, with the throat behind her going up into the dark.

Riarhin stopped.

She had the position. Farieth would have to keep her footing on the steep slope with a bad leg while Riarhin came at her from below. If Riarhin went in now, hard, Farieth would have to go back up the slope, and back, up the narrow part of the throat where it went under the terrace, until they both came to the counterweight.

Farieth watched her eyes go up the slope.

“Come up,” said Farieth.

Riarhin did not move.

“You have me on the slope,” said Farieth. “You’ll never have better. Come up after me. Push me back. I can’t hold you on a slope with this leg. Push me all the way.”

“To the chocks.”

“To the chocks.”

“And when you’re there you let me knock you down against them.”

Farieth did not answer.

“Or you fall against them yourself,” said Riarhin. “We both go down under the iron. So does the joint.”

The cracked note came down the throat.

Farieth looked down at her for a long moment.

“You were always quick,” she said at last. “Your father was quick. He saw everything and did the wrong thing anyway. You see everything and stand still.” She shifted her grip. “Very well. I’ll come down.”

She came down.

She came off the slope with the spear thrusting, hard and fast, three thrusts one after another, low, high, low, and Riarhin gave ground to all three, backwards, away from the foot of the throat, out into the room, towards the binding. If she stood, the spear would find her. If she went sideways she went onto the walk, and the walk was narrow and raised and the spear could sweep her off it.

On the third thrust the blade came in low, under her guard, and she turned it, and it went past her, and on the way back Farieth turned the shaft in her hands so that the edge of the blade drew across the outside of Riarhin’s left thigh as it withdrew.

It went through the cloth and into the flesh. A cold line and then heat.

She went back another step and felt something hard against the back of her leg.

The binding.

She was against it. Her right calf was against the pale knot, and behind her, a hand’s breadth from her shoulder, one of the black iron rods stood out from it at an angle.

Farieth stood at three paces with the spear levelled at her chest.

“There,” said Farieth softly.

Riarhin did not move. The binding was faintly warm against her leg, like skin, and under the warmth she could feel it straining, even now, even held, like a muscle held still with effort.

“Strike at me,” said Farieth. “Go on. You’re against it. You can’t go back. If you go left, you go round it, and I’m there. If you go right, the iron’s there. You’ll have to come forward.” The spear blade was very steady. “Come forward and strike. Or turn my spear. Turn it hard. Turn it the way you turned it at the shutter.”

Riarhin looked at the spear blade, and at the iron rod by her shoulder.

If she turned the spear hard, it would go to her right, into the binding, where the iron rod stood out of the knot. If she struck at Farieth and Farieth turned her stroke, Remorse would go the same way.

The cracked note came down the throat. It was fainter than before. Or she imagined that.

“The Sumwright’s tiring,” said Farieth. “I can hear it. He’s a little late on the eight. Strike, granddaughter. While he’s still holding. While it’s still whole enough to feel it.”

Riarhin went down.

She dropped onto her side at the foot of the binding, so that the spear thrust that came at once went over her into empty air above the knot, and she rolled, under the line of it, round the foot of the knot, through the grey motionless mist that lay on the stone like poured glass and did not stir as she went through it, and came up on her knees on the far side of the binding with the knot between them.

Her thigh was bleeding into her boot, and Farieth was on the other side of the knot, between her and the throat, with the whole of the slope behind her and nobody at its foot.

The cracked note came down. Late.

Farieth looked at her across the binding. Then she looked over her shoulder, up the throat.

Chapter 32 — The narrow ground

Farieth did not go up.

She looked up the slope into the dark, towards the chocks, and Riarhin saw her measure it: the steep paved slope, the narrow part under the terrace, the long climb to the lip with a bad leg; and behind her, a woman with a sword who would be at the foot of the slope before she was a third of the way up.

Farieth turned back.

“You would follow me,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Up the slope. With me above you. I’d have the height.”

“You’d have your back to me,” said Riarhin, “on a slope you can’t run on.”

Farieth did not answer that. She came round the binding.

She came round it to the left, towards Riarhin, with the spear low. Riarhin went round it to the left too, away, keeping the knot between them. Farieth stopped. Riarhin stopped. They stood with the binding between them, and the lamp on the floor beside it throwing their shadows up the ribs, long and tangled.

The cracked note came down the throat.

Farieth went round to the right. Riarhin went round to the right. Farieth stopped.

“This is foolish,” said Farieth.

“Yes.”

“We can go round and round it until he stops holding.”

“Yes.”

“And then it moves again, and we’re both standing next to it with iron in it, and the breath comes, and it’s eight.” Farieth’s eyes went to the binding and back. “You know what happens then.”

“I know what you think happens.”

Farieth’s mouth tightened.

She thrust across the binding.

It was a long thrust, over the top of the knot, with the whole length of the spear behind it, at Riarhin’s face, and Riarhin leaned back from it and Farieth drew it back across the knot. As it came back, the edge of the blade scraped across one of the black iron rods, and the rod rang, a high flat ugly sound, and the pale stuff round it gave a faint cracking noise, like ice.

Farieth did not look at it. She was watching Riarhin.

She thrust again, across the knot, low this time, and Riarhin stepped back off the line of it, and the blade came back and struck another rod. The same ringing. The same crack.

Riarhin saw it on the third thrust. Every time the spear came back, the blade struck one of the iron rods in the knot, on purpose, hard, with the weight of the withdrawal behind it.

Under her boots, through the motionless mist, Riarhin felt the floor shiver.

The cracked note came down the throat. Late. Very late.

Riarhin went to the walk.

She went away from the binding, backwards, three long steps to the edge of the room, and up onto the walk. Farieth checked. She looked at Riarhin on the walk, a few paces off, and then at the binding beside her, and the rods.

She thrust at a rod, not at Riarhin at all. The blade struck it full on and the rod rang and the crack round it widened, a dark line in the pale knot, running.

Riarhin went along the walk, fast, to the right.

She knew where a slab was tilted, and where a rib came out of the wall so low that she had to duck.

Farieth turned.

She looked at Riarhin going along the walk towards the throat, and Riarhin saw her face change.

Farieth left the binding.

She came across the floor towards the throat, fast, faster than Riarhin had believed she could move with the leg, the spear held across her body. She was cutting across the middle of the room, the short way, and Riarhin was going round the edge, the long way, and Farieth was going to get there first.

Riarhin dropped off the walk.

She came down onto the floor in Farieth’s path, with Remorse up and its point at Farieth’s face, and Farieth stopped, short, at the length of the spear.

They stood in the open floor of the room, between the binding and the throat, three paces apart.

The cracked note came down. So late Riarhin had begun to think it would not come.

Farieth was breathing hard. There was sweat on her face, running in the lines of it, and the hand on the spear shaft, the bound one, was trembling very slightly. She had been hammering rods into the binding for hours.

“You want the throat,” said Riarhin.

“I want it done.”

“The rods haven’t done it.” Riarhin’s own breath was loud. Her thigh was wet. “You still need the weight.”

Farieth looked at her.

“You’ll go for the throat,” said Riarhin. “Whatever you pretend.”

“And you’ll stand in front of it.”

“Every time.”

Farieth thrust.

They fought in the open middle of the room.

Riarhin did not try to close. She kept the length of Remorse between them and the point at Farieth’s face, and she moved all the time, a step in, a step back, never where the spear expected her to be. The spear came at her and she turned it, not hard, never hard, a touch on the side of the blade or the shaft, enough to let it go past, and she did not follow it, and she gave ground when she had to and took it back when she could. Her left wrist ached with the small corrections.

And every time Farieth moved towards the throat, Riarhin was there.

Farieth tried it six ways. She feinted at the binding, and Riarhin did not go to the binding. She thrust at Riarhin’s bound arm, the right, to drive her left, away from the throat, and Riarhin went left and came back. She went for the walk, to go round the long way as Riarhin had, and Riarhin cut across the middle and was at the foot of the slope before her. Each time, Farieth stopped, and turned, and came back, and the spear came again.

Riarhin’s thigh was bleeding down into her boot. She could feel it squelch when she put her weight on that foot. Her right arm was a long hot ache from shoulder to elbow. Her breath was coming short and loud and her mouth was dry, and the room had begun, at the edges, to go dark and narrow.

The cracked note came down the throat.

And then another sound.

The cracked note, and then, after it, at once, a dull lower note. Falling.

Riarhin’s whole body heard it. A falling pair. Once.

Farieth heard it too. She stopped and listened, with her head a little on one side.

The room shivered.

The mist on the floor, which had lain fixed and motionless round their feet for so long that Riarhin had stopped seeing it, moved. A single curl of it, near the binding, lifted, and turned over, and lay down again. The ribs creaked. A single small creak, from one of them, high on the north wall, and then silence.

The lamp flame by the binding bent, and stood up again.

“He’s letting go,” said Farieth.

Riarhin did not answer.

“That was a signal. I heard it. It wasn’t the holding note.” Farieth’s eyes were very bright. “He’s letting go. Or he’s telling you he will.”

The cracked note came down the throat. Holding. Still holding.

Riarhin stood between Farieth and the foot of the throat with her sword up and her thigh bleeding and her breath sawing in her chest, and did not move.

Chapter 33 — Farieth

The second falling pair came down the throat while they stood there.

The cracked note, and the dull note under it, falling. Riarhin heard it in her teeth. Two. One more, and he would let go, and he and Osselin would be going up the ramp, and they would not come back down for her, because she had said so.

The ledge was behind her and to the left, twenty paces along the walk, thirty. She could be at the foot of it before Farieth could cross the floor, and up it in the dark the way she had come. She could be at the top when the third pair came. She could be with them on the ramp.

And Farieth would turn and go up the throat to the chocks, and nobody would be at the foot of it.

The holding note came down. Late. Faint.

The mist round their feet stirred. All of it, this time, the whole grey floor of it, lifting a little and turning and lying down again, like a sleeper turning over. The ribs creaked, three or four of them, from different sides of the room. The lamp flame bent and stood.

Farieth heard the ribs. She looked up at them. Then she looked at Riarhin.

“Go,” said Farieth.

Riarhin did not move.

“That was the second. I heard you count it. There’ll be a third.” Farieth’s voice was almost gentle. “Go up your ledge, granddaughter. Go to your Sumwright and your lamp-girl. I won’t follow you. I won’t need to.”

“No.”

“You’ll die here.”

“So will you.”

“I was always going to.” Farieth shifted the spear in her hands. “I didn’t think you would. I didn’t want you to. I came to the grave and saw you there and thought, that one I’ll leave till last.”

She shifted her weight towards the throat. “Go.”

Riarhin stood between her and the throat.

She thought of the drain, the grating low in the south wall that ran off the edge of the drawing. She thought of Osselin straightening her collar. The loose trip-line lay a little way up the slope, where she had pulled it. To climb with her bad leg, Farieth would have to plant her good foot beside that wall.

She stepped aside.

She did it slowly, so that Farieth would see. She took one step to her right, off the line, away from the foot of the throat, and lowered the point of her sword a little, and stood there with the slope open beside her.

Farieth did not move.

She looked at the open slope. She looked at Riarhin, and Riarhin saw her look for the reason, and not find it, and look again.

The ribs creaked. A long low groan, from all round, the beginning of a breath. Faint and held, but beginning.

The holding note did not come.

Farieth went for the throat.

She went fast across the three paces of floor and onto the foot of the slope, with the spear in her right hand and her left hand going out to the wall of the throat to pull herself up. She went up the first pace of the slope, and the second, with her bad leg swinging stiffly and her good leg driving. She did not look back. She was looking up the throat into the dark, towards the chocks.

On the third pace her foot came down on the cord.

A small dry sound, like a stick rolling. The cut end of the trip-line lay loose along the side of the steep paving where Riarhin had pulled it, and it rolled under Farieth’s good foot on the smooth stone, and the foot went out from under her.

She did not fall. She was too good to fall. She caught herself on the wall with her left hand and threw her weight onto the other leg, the bad one, to hold, and the bad leg could not hold. Riarhin saw the knee go. She saw Farieth go down onto it on the slope, hard, and twist, and bring the spear round one-handed as she turned, because she had heard Riarhin coming.

Riarhin was already on the slope.

She came up it behind Farieth, and the spear came round, low and fast, and she did not turn it. She went in under it, close, closer than she had been all night, inside the length of the shaft where the blade could not reach her, and the shaft struck her across the ribs and she felt something give, and she did not stop.

She put Remorse into her grandmother under the arm, on the left side, and drove it in.

It went in the way a blade goes in when it has found the place, without any resistance at all for a moment, and then the guard came up against cloth, and Riarhin stood on the slope with her hand on the hilt and her grandmother’s weight coming down against her.

Farieth made no sound.

The spear clattered on the paving and slid away down the slope and onto the floor of the room. Farieth’s left hand came off the wall and found Riarhin’s shoulder and gripped it. Her face was very close. Riarhin could see the lines in it, the grey in the skin, the sweat. She could see the grey eyes looking at her from a hand’s breadth away, not angry, not surprised, only looking.

Riarhin drew the sword out.

Farieth slid down. Riarhin held her. She held her with her left arm round her and the sword still in her left hand, and they came down the slope together, slowly, Riarhin’s boots sliding on the smooth paving, until they were at the foot of it, on the floor of the room, and Riarhin knelt and let her down.

Farieth lay on her side on the stone at the foot of the throat. Blood was coming from under her arm, a great deal of it, dark in the lamplight, spreading on the pale paving.

The ribs groaned. The breath was coming. The mist lifted round them and began, slowly, to flow.

Farieth’s eyes moved. They went past Riarhin’s face, up, to something behind her, over her shoulder.

“Left,” said Farieth.

Riarhin did not understand.

“Move left.” It was very faint. “That one.”

Riarhin moved left.

Something fell where she had been kneeling. A flake of pale stuff the size of a door, from the underside of the nearest rib where it came down out of the wall above the foot of the throat, broke away with a dry crack and fell and struck the floor and broke into pieces, and the pieces slid across the stone and lay still.

Riarhin looked at them. Then she looked at her grandmother.

Farieth was looking at the pieces too.

“I told you,” said Farieth. “At the walkway. Not everything you recognise.”

“Grandmother.”

Farieth’s eyes came back to her face.

There was something Riarhin wanted to ask. She did not know what it was. She had wanted to ask it since the grave, since Ailsa’s daughter took the comb from her and worked the first tangle out and passed it back, and now she knelt beside her grandmother on the bloody stone and still did not know what it was.

“Did you—” she began, and stopped.

Farieth looked at her.

Her lips moved. Riarhin bent close.

“Granddaughter,” said Farieth.

That was all.

Riarhin knelt and watched her. She watched the chest under the grey robe rise, and fall, and rise, a little, and fall. She watched the grey eyes, which were still on her face, and saw them stop seeing her. She watched the chest not rise.

She waited.

She put two fingers against the side of her grandmother’s throat, under the jaw, where the pulse would be. She held them there for the count of eight. Then for another eight. The skin was warm. Nothing moved under it.

She took her fingers away.

The breath came. The ribs groaned all round her, and the mist flowed in across the floor and round her knees and round the body and away towards the binding, and nothing held it. The holding note did not come down the throat.

Riarhin looked up the throat.

Then she looked at the binding, at the dark crack running through it. The crack was wider. As the breath came, it opened, a little, and the ribs strained outward, and the knot creaked.

And low down on the west side of the knot, the cord ran away from it across the floor into the low dark arch. Pale. As thick as her arm. Humming.

Riarhin got up.

She got up badly. Her thigh had stiffened while she knelt and the leg would not straighten at first, and something in her side, where the spear shaft had struck her, stabbed when she moved. She stood. She looked down at Farieth.

At Farieth’s belt, beside the empty loop where the hammer had hung, there was the chisel.

Riarhin bent and took it. It was long and heavy, a mason’s chisel, its edge bright and new-ground. Farieth had sharpened it for the iron. Riarhin slid Remorse into its scabbard, one-handed, and took the chisel in her left hand and went to the binding, and picked up the hammer from the floor beside the lamp.

She went to the low arch on the west side, where the cord ran out, and knelt beside it, as far from the binding as she could get, and laid the chisel on it.

She could not hold the chisel and swing the hammer. She tried. She put the chisel edge on the cord and held it with her left hand and lifted the hammer in her right, and her right arm, bound against her body, would not lift. The hammer came up a hand’s breadth and her fingers opened and it fell.

She sat back on her heels.

The ribs groaned. The crack in the binding creaked open. The mist was flowing faster now, in from every arch, round her, across the cord.

From up the throat, very faint, very far, came a sound.

The cracked note.

Riarhin lifted her head. It came again. Not eight later. Sooner. Ragged. And again. As though somebody up there had caught hold again.

The mist on the floor round her slowed. It did not stop. It slowed.

Riarhin took the chisel in her right hand.

She took it with the fingers, which would close, and set the edge of it on the cord, and pressed her right hand down on it with her forearm against her thigh to hold it there, the arm bound against her body and the cut in it screaming. Then she took the hammer in her left hand. She lifted it and brought it down.

The chisel bit. The cord gave a low sound under it, like a plucked string, very deep.

She lifted the hammer and brought it down.

The cord was harder than the strand in the alcove, harder than horn, and the chisel went into it a finger’s breadth and stopped, and the cord hummed under the blade, and the hum came up the chisel into her right hand and her arm and her teeth.

She lifted the hammer and brought it down.

The cracked note came down the throat, ragged.

Down. The chisel went in.

Down. The hum rose. The mist round her stopped moving altogether, and lay on the floor like glass, and the ribs went silent, and for the space of a breath the room was very still, nailed to the world.

Down.

The cord parted.

It went with a sound she felt rather than heard, very low, below hearing, like the deepest string of an instrument plucked once and let go. The two cut ends sprang apart. The one on the binding side drew back across the floor towards the knot, slowly, like the horn of a snail, and into the knot, and was gone. The one on the arch side drew back into the dark of the arch, and was gone.

The binding went quiet.

It did not stop creaking. It went quiet in another way: the faint hum Riarhin had felt through her boots since she came into the room stopped. The ribs stood round the room, straining, groaning, held. The mist lay still.

And somewhere above her, far above, the ring began to close.

Chapter 34 — The outward signal

It began as a sound.

A long low grinding, very far off, from somewhere above and to the east, like a millstone turning. Then another, closer, from the south. Then a deep heavy thud that she felt in her knees through the floor, and a patter of small stones and dust falling from the dark above the ribs onto the floor round her.

Riarhin stood up.

The cracked note came down the throat. Ragged, and then not ragged. A falling pair.

Three.

He had let go.

The ribs groaned. But they groaned standing. The binding sat in the middle of the room with its iron rods and its dark crack, and the crack did not open further. The mist lay on the floor. Riarhin stood and waited for the breath, and it did not come.

The ground lurched.

It lurched hard, all at once, the whole room, so that she staggered and caught herself on a rib, and from the throat behind her came a roar of stone moving. She turned. Dust was pouring down the throat out of the dark, a grey river of it rolling down the slope and out across the floor of the room. Through it, far up, she heard the grinding again, closer, and a long cracking sound, and then a great heavy crash that went on and on.

She went to the foot of the throat.

She pulled her collar over her mouth and stood at the bottom of the slope beside her grandmother’s body and looked up into the grey rolling dark, and then the dust thinned a little, and she could see.

The throat was closing.

The walls of it, the sheer sides of fitted stone, were coming together. Slowly. Far up, where it went under the terrace, the narrow part had already gone: the two sides had leaned in and met, and there was a wall of jumbled stone across it where the slope had been. Below that, the walls were leaning. She could see the fitted blocks shifting against each other, the joints opening and closing, dust pouring out of them.

The ledge.

She ran for the walk.

She ran along it to the north side of the room with her thigh tearing at every step and her side stabbing, and reached the foot of the ledge, and went up it, in the dark, fast.

The ledge shook under her. Dust came down on her head. Somewhere below and to the right, in the throat, stone was grinding on stone. She went up past the place where a block had sunk and tilted, and stepped over the crack that ran across it, and went on up through the dark under the terrace, and ahead of her, at last, grey, there was light.

The ledge came out from under the terrace.

It came out onto the open face of the throat wall, high up, where she had been able to look back and see the low door and the terrace. She came out of the dark into the grey light and stopped.

The ledge in front of her was gone.

It had broken away. Ten paces ahead of her it stopped, a ragged edge of stone, and beyond it there was nothing for a long stretch, a raw scar on the wall where the shelf had been, and then, far beyond, the ledge again, going on up to the low door, which stood open, dark, in the face of the wall above the throat.

Between, there was nothing to stand on. The wall was sheer. Below, the throat was closing, its walls leaning, dust pouring up out of it.

Riarhin stood at the ragged end of the ledge and looked across the gap.

She could not do it. She knew it at once and looked anyway, the whole length of the scar, for a hold, for anything. There was nothing for a left hand alone. Ghere could not have done it with two good arms.

She looked past it.

From here she could see the terrace. She could see the door of the measured room, open, empty. And she could see, far along the gallery beyond it, past the arches, past the column at the bend, at the foot of the ramp, a light.

Osselin’s lantern.

It was held up. It was steady.

As she watched, it went dark.

Then light. Then dark. Then light.

Twice.

The way out isn’t clear. We’ve gone. Find another.

It stayed lit, the second time. Somebody at the foot of the ramp was holding it up, still, at arm’s length, towards the throat.

And then, beside it, she saw another figure. Small, bent under something on its back, a square shape, a case. It came along the gallery towards the light from the direction of the measured room, slowly, stumbling, one hand on the wall of the gallery. It reached the light. The two figures stood together at the foot of the ramp.

The light lifted.

It lifted higher, the lantern held up at the full length of an arm, towards the throat, towards the ledge. Not dark and light. Lifted. Held there.

You lift the lamp so it knows you’ve seen it.

Riarhin did not know whether they could see her. She was a small figure at the end of a broken ledge on the face of a wall, in dust, in grey light, a long way off. She did not know whether Osselin was lifting the lamp to her, or to the throat, or to the dark.

She lifted her hand.

She lifted her left hand, the one that held a sword, empty now, high over her head, and held it there.

The light stayed lifted for a long moment.

Then it came down. And the two figures turned, together, and went up the ramp, the one with the light ahead and the one with the case behind, slowly, and at the top of the ramp the light went round the corner of the third terrace and was gone.

Riarhin stood with her hand up.

She put it down.

The ledge shook under her. A crack ran across it at her feet, opening. She stepped back from the ragged end, and back, and the stone she had been standing on tilted and slid away from the wall and fell into the throat, turning over, and she heard it strike far below.

She went back down the ledge into the dark under the terrace, with her hand on the wall, and the ledge shook and cracked behind her. She went down by the sunken block and the crack, faster than she had come up, and came out onto the end of the walk and dropped off it onto the floor, and fell, and lay there.

Behind her, with a long roar, the ledge came down.

She lay on the floor of the joint with her face on the stone and dust coming down on her and listened to it come down, all the length of it, from the low door to the foot, rolling and grinding in the dark, and then the great final crash of something very heavy far up the throat. Iron on stone. It went on and on, a long tearing scraping roar coming down the throat towards her, and then a jolt, and silence.

She lifted her head.

The foot of the throat was full. Where the paved slope had come down into the room, there was a wall of tumbled stone and dust, and in the middle of it, wedged, a corner of something black and squared and rough, stuck fast in the jaws of the throat a little way up the slope, where the walls had closed on it. The counterweight.

Farieth’s body lay at the foot of the rubble, half covered in dust.

Riarhin lay on the floor and looked at it.

The room was very quiet. The ribs stood round it, pale in the light of the lamp, which was still burning on the floor beside the binding, its flame standing straight. The mist lay on the floor, still, thin, settling. There was no breath. Far off, above, the grinding had stopped. Once, from somewhere to the east, very distant, came a last long rumble of stone, and then nothing.

She was on the wrong side.

The ledge was gone. The throat was full of stone and iron. The way up to the measured room and the ramp and the zigzag stair and the maintenance steps and the moss-woman’s stone and north, out of the forest, was gone.

They had gone up the ramp. Both of them. She had seen them go.

She lay on the stone and breathed.

After a while she got up. She did it in stages, onto her good knee, then her hand, then the bad leg, slowly, with her teeth together. She stood. The room tilted and settled. She went to the binding and picked up the lamp. It was nearly out of oil.

She looked round the room.

The grating on the south side.

She went to it along the walk, slowly, with the lamp. It was set low in the wall, a square of old pale metal bars as high as her knee, with darkness behind. She held the lamp to it. Behind the bars a passage went back into the stone, small, round, sloping very gently downward, its floor stained with old dark marks where water had run. Cool air came out of it against her face. It smelled of earth, and stone, and, very faintly, so faintly that she was not sure she had not imagined it, of something else.

Salt.

The bars were old. They were set in the stone at top and bottom, and at the bottom, where water had run past them for a thousand years, the stone round two of them had worn away. She set the lamp down. She took the chisel from her belt, where she had put it after the cord, and set its edge against the worn stone at the foot of the nearest bar, and struck it with the heel of her hand.

It took a long time. The lamp burned down. Twice she had to stop and sit with her back against the wall and her head on her knees until the room stopped turning. But the stone was soft where the water had worn it, and the bar was loose, and in the end she got the chisel behind it and leaned on it and the bar came out of its socket with a grinding shriek, and then the next one, more easily, and there was a gap.

She sat back on her heels and looked at it.

Then she looked back across the room, at the rubble at the foot of the throat, and the grey shape lying half buried in dust at the bottom of it.

She went back. It was a long way across the room. She knelt beside her grandmother and brushed the dust from her face with her good hand. The eyes were still open, grey, looking at nothing. Riarhin closed them. She did it carefully, the way she had seen women do it, one and then the other, with the side of her thumb.

She did not know what else to do. She knelt there a moment longer. She thought of Coryd folding a dead woman’s waistcoat along its old creases. She thought of the potter, who had said there was room for the horse where the rain would not reach it.

She took the folded linen from inside Farieth’s robe, the strip she had seen her fold at the cutting, in three, with the corner tucked under, and looked at it. Then she put it back where it had been.

She got up and went back to the grating with the lamp, and lay down on her stomach and went into the drain.

Chapter 35 — The wall at her back

The drain went back into the stone for forty lengths of her body and then opened into a chamber.

It was small and round, a cistern or a sump, where several smaller drains came together from different directions and ran out by one larger one on the far side. Its floor was a shallow bowl, dry, with a crust of pale old silt in the bottom of it, and its walls rose round her into darkness. When she held up the lamp she could see the mouths of the other drains, five of them, dark round holes at different heights in the wall.

She crawled out of the drain into the bowl and sat down with her back against the wall.

The lamp flame was very small. She set it on the floor beside her where she could see it, and sat, and for a while did nothing at all.

Then she began.

The thigh first. She knew it was the worst because it was the one she could not feel properly any more. She pulled her trouser leg away from it where the blood had dried and stuck the cloth to the skin, and it came away with a sound and a long bright pain, and she sat with her teeth together until the pain went down to something she could think around. It was a long cut on the outside of the left thigh, a hand above the knee, across the muscle, deeper than the arm, not as deep as she had feared. It had bled a great deal. It was still bleeding, slowly, from one end. It would not stop by itself.

She had no linen. Osselin had all the linen, in her bag, at the foot of the ramp.

She cut a strip from the bottom of her shirt with Toothpick. It took a long time, one-handed, holding the cloth down with her knee. Then she put her hand in her coat for something to pad the cut, and found, in the inner pocket, something soft and greasy.

A pad of felt.

She looked at it in the lamplight. It was one of the pads she had cut on the shingle outside Pellet’s net loft at Underarch, from the penny roll of old greasy felt, for the inks in the copper case. She had cut one too many. It had been the wrong shape for any hollow in the case, and she had put it in her pocket to throw away and forgotten it.

She laid it over the cut. It was greasy and stiff with old fish oil and it would keep the wet out. She bound it on with the strip of shirt, as tight as she could pull one-handed, and knotted it with her teeth.

Then her side. She felt along it with her fingers, under the shirt, where the spear shaft had struck her on the slope, and found the place, and pressed, and the pain went through her so hard that she could not breathe for a moment and the lamp flame doubled and swam. A rib. Cracked, or broken. She could breathe if she did not breathe deeply. There was nothing to be done for it.

Then the arm. She unwound Osselin’s old binding and looked at it. The long cut from shoulder to elbow had opened again along half its length. It was bleeding a little. She bound it again with the same linen, tighter.

She sat back against the wall.

She went through what she had. She did it slowly, one thing at a time, putting her hand on each.

Remorse, in its scabbard, on her back. She drew it, a hand’s breadth, and looked at the blade in the lamplight. It was dark along one edge near the point. She had not cleaned it. She did not clean it now. She slid it home.

Toothpick, in its sheath at her belt. The Last Blade beside it; her thumb found its worn hilt.

Farieth’s chisel, thrust through her belt beside it, long and heavy, its edge dulled now where it had bitten the cord and the stone round the grating.

The water skin, at her hip. She shook it. Half. Perhaps a little less than half. She did not drink.

The lamp. Nearly out of oil.

Nothing else. Her pack was in the measured room, with the food and her blanket and the sailcloth from the Gate and the whetstone. She had come out onto the ledge with a sword and a knife and a skin of water, because that was all she would need in the joint, and she had been right.

She sat with her back against the wall of the sump and listened.

Above her, through the stone, she could hear nothing. The ring had closed, or finished closing, or stopped trying to close. She put her palm flat on the wall beside her and waited, and the stone was cool and dry and still. For days she had felt the breath in every stone she touched. It was like putting her hand on a chest and finding no heartbeat, and then remembering that it was a wall.

The binding had held. The ring had closed round it. Whatever was in it was in it still.

She did not know what else had closed: the terrace, the gallery, the ramp. She had seen the light go round the corner of the third terrace. She had not seen the zigzag stair, or the gap with the beam across it, or the maintenance steps.

She sat with her hand on the wall and thought: they went up the ramp. Both of them. On the signal. The way we said.

And under it, rising, there was something else. It came up through her like the breath had come up through the stone, slow and strong. She had stood on the end of a broken ledge with her hand in the air, and they had turned and gone. She had told them to. Vesket had made her say it, sitting on the floor, eating cheese.

She remembered. It did not help.

She was so angry that she put her fist against the wall, not hard, and held it there, and pressed, until the knuckles hurt.

She was sitting in a hole in the stone on the wrong side of a closed ring with a hole in her leg and a broken rib and half a skin of water, and they had done exactly what she told them, and she wanted to shout at them for it.

She did not shout. She sat with her fist against the wall until the anger went down a little, not much, and then she took her fist away and looked at the lamp.

The flame was very small and blue at the base.

She could stay here. The sump was dry. Nothing would come down the drains. She could sit with her back against the wall and wait for her leg to stop bleeding and her rib to knit and her arm to close, and drink her half skin of water a mouthful at a time.

She thought about it properly. She thought about how long half a skin of water would last a woman who had bled as much as she had. She thought about the lamp going out, and then the dark, and no way to tell day from night, and the cold, which was already in her, coming up from the stone through the seat of her trousers. She thought about her leg stiffening as she sat, so that every hour she stayed it would be harder to get up.

She thought about the drain she had come in by, which had run downhill, gently, all the way.

Water always goes somewhere.

She looked at the larger drain on the far side of the sump, the one that went out. It was the lowest. She held the lamp up to it. It was round, larger than the one she had crawled in by, large enough to go along on her hands and knees rather than her belly, and it sloped downward, gently, and cool air came out of it against her face.

She smelled the air.

Earth. Stone. And, very faint, under them, the thing she had smelled at the grating. She was surer now. Salt.

North and east, Vesket had said, looking at the drawing. It goes out under the ring, north and east, and runs off the edge of the paper.

She knew what lay north and east of the Valewood. She had stood on it as a girl, on the edge of the trees, with Ghere, and looked at the grey sea. The Thin Shore. Where the forest came down to the water, and the Linn dragged their boats across the portage, and the ships went north before the gales.

She drank one mouthful of water. One. She held it in her mouth a long time before she swallowed.

Then she picked up the lamp and got onto her hands and knees, the bad leg dragging, and went into the drain on the far side, downhill, towards the salt.

Part V — The coastal strip

Chapter 36 — Downstream without water

The lamp went out in the first hour.

It did not gutter. It burned smaller and smaller, blue, and then it was a spark, and then it was nothing, and she was on her hands and knees in a stone pipe in the complete dark, and she stopped, and stayed very still, and listened to herself breathe.

Then she went on.

She went on with her left hand on the floor of the drain in front of her, feeling for it, and her right arm against her body, and her knees and her bad leg dragging behind. The floor was smooth and dry and sloped very gently down. The walls were close on either side; she could feel them with her shoulders. Every so often, her hand found the edge of a joint between the stones, a thin line, regular, and then another, the same distance on. She began to count them. Eight joints, and a pause. Eight joints. It gave her something to do with the dark.

At some point, she did not know when, the drain began to be wet.

First a dampness on the floor under her palm. Then a thin film of water, running with her, downhill. Then a trickle, the width of her hand, cold, running in the bottom of the pipe. She cupped her hand and drank, a little, and it tasted of stone and iron, clean. She drank again. She filled the skin, slowly, holding its mouth in the trickle, and sat in the dark with it full against her side and laughed, once, silently, because it hurt her rib to laugh aloud.

She went on.

The drain came out of the stone into the forest in the grey of dawn.

She did not know it was dawn until she saw it. The dark ahead of her went from black to a very dark grey, and then to grey, and then she was looking at a round of grey light at the end of the pipe, and green beyond it, and she crawled towards it, and put her head out of the end of the drain into air.

It came out in the side of a bank, low, among ferns, below a slope of earth and roots. Above her, through the trees, she could see something pale and very large, curving away: the ring wall, from the outside, from the north-east. It looked the same as it had looked from the forest on the first day. Smooth. Pale. No door.

The water from the drain ran out of the mouth of the pipe and down through the ferns into a stone channel.

The channel was like the dry channel, but smaller, and old, very old, its stones tumbled and moss-grown, and the water ran in the bottom of it in a thin bright line.

She sat in the mouth of the drain and looked out.

It was a made thing. It ran straight, the way the dry channel ran, the way everything made by the people who made the ring ran straight. It went north and east, towards the salt.

She thought of the clear avenue on the hump. She thought of Ghere in the trees, letting her get halfway.

But that was a road. A road was made for walking. This was a channel. It was made for water. And water did not care who walked beside it. Water went down.

She got up out of the drain mouth, slowly, using the bank, and went along beside the channel.

It was easier than she had feared. The channel ran along the bottom of a shallow valley, and beside it on the right there was a strip of level ground, stony, where nothing much grew, where the channel had perhaps once had a path beside it for whoever kept it. She walked on that. She walked badly. The thigh had stiffened in the drain and every step on the left leg sent a long bright pull up it, and she had to stop every hundred paces and lean on a tree and wait for the forest to stop tilting. But she walked.

At noon, or what she thought was noon, she came to a place where the forest opened.

There was a clearing ahead, wide and green and level, full of soft grass and flowers, with the light lying in it like water. A deer was grazing at the edge. The channel ran straight across the clearing in its stone bed and out the far side. And on the left, away from the channel, a broad clear way went out of the clearing between the trees, level, open, going north.

Riarhin stopped at the edge of the clearing.

She looked at the clear way for a long time. It went north. The sea was north. It was level and easy and it went exactly where she wanted to go, and every part of her body that hurt wanted to walk along it.

She looked at the channel. On the far side of the clearing it went into the trees where they grew thick, and the ground beyond looked rough.

She looked at the deer. The deer had lifted its head and was looking at her, and it did not run. It stood at the edge of the clearing by the channel and looked at her, and went back to grazing. There was good grass on the clear way, long and green. The deer stayed by the channel.

She followed the channel.

It was rough beyond the clearing, as she had seen. She went on beside it through thick trees and over fallen trunks, slowly, and it was worse than the level strip, and every fallen trunk was a long business with one arm and one good leg. But an hour later, looking back from the top of a rise, she could see the clear way going north from the clearing between the trees, and where it went, half a mile on, it went down into a long hollow, and the hollow was full of mist. Grey, still, low. The same nothing.

She looked at it a long time. Then she went on beside the channel.

In the afternoon the channel failed her.

It came to a place where the ground dropped in a step, a little cliff of grey rock, and the channel went over the edge of it in a small fall, and at the bottom, instead of going on, it went down. There was a hole at the foot of the rock, a black round mouth in the ground, and the water went into it, and was gone. Beyond it, the forest went on, rough and broken, and there was no channel. There was nothing made at all.

She stood at the top of the step and looked down at the hole.

She could not follow water into the ground. Not again. Not without a lamp, with a leg that would not bend.

She went down the step. She had to. There was no way round that she could see, and the step was not high, three times her height, broken into ledges like the cliff where Farieth had cut the rope. She went down it with her left hand and her good leg, slowly, ledge by ledge. On the third ledge she put her weight on the bad leg to reach the next one, and the leg folded.

She went down the rest of it in a rush and landed on her side at the bottom beside the black hole with the water going into it, and her rib went off like a firework, and she lay there with her face in wet moss and could not breathe at all.

She lay there a long time.

When she could breathe she lay there longer. The water ran past her into the hole, very close, with a soft continuous sound. The moss was cold against her face. She could smell it, green and wet, and under it, much stronger now, salt.

She opened her eyes.

There was a fern in front of her face, a hand’s breadth away. Its fronds were wet. Not with the spray of the fall. With something else: drops of water hanging from every tip, clear, catching what light came down through the trees. She looked past it. The whole of the bank at the foot of the step was ferns, and moss, thick, very green, and the rock face above them was dark and shining, and water was coming out of it. Not the channel. A seep. Running down the face of the rock in a thin sheet behind the ferns and dripping off the fern tips into the moss.

She had filled her skin in the drain. She drank from it anyway, a long drink, lying on her side in the moss. Then she held the mouth of the skin under the drips from the ferns and filled it to the top again, a drop at a time, and it took a long time, and she did not mind.

Then she lay with her back against the wet rock under the ferns, where the seep ran down behind her shoulders, cold, and could not have got up if she had wanted to.

It was a bad place, wet and cold, with the black hole beside her. But it was a place she could see out of, and nothing could come at her from behind, and there was water.

She took out the pad of felt from her thigh binding, looked at the cut under it in the failing light, saw that the bleeding had stopped, and put the felt back.

She sat with Remorse across her knees and her back against the wet rock and watched the light go.

Chapter 37 — A second night

She woke because she was cold.

She had not meant to sleep. Now it was full night and she was shaking so hard her teeth knocked together.

The seep had soaked her. Her coat and her shirt and her trousers were wet through behind, where she had leaned against the rock, and the cold had gone into her through the wet and down into her bones. She could not feel her feet. Her hands were stiff. When she tried to close her left hand on the hilt of the sword it closed slowly, like a hand belonging to somebody else.

She could not stay there.

She got up. It took a long time. She used the rock and the sword, the point in the moss like a stick, and got her good leg under her and then the bad one, and stood. The forest swung round her and settled.

The floor was alight.

It had come out while she slept: the pale green glow, out of the moss and the fallen leaves, spread across the forest floor between the trees in every direction as far as she could see, very soft, breathing. Thousands of small cold lights. The golden ones drifted in the dark above them.

She stood at the foot of the step and looked at it, shaking.

She had to walk. If she did not walk she would not get warm, and if she did not get warm, she knew where it ended. She had seen men end that way on the steppe in winter, sitting down in the snow because they were tired, and smiling. She had to walk, and the only way to walk was across the floor, through the lights.

Don’t go down and walk in them. You won’t want to stop.

She had said that to Osselin, in the crotch of the tree, on the first night past the Gate. She remembered Osselin’s hand finding her ankle in the dark and holding it.

She began to walk.

It was beautiful. She had not been among them since her first year with Ghere. She had looked down on them from trees and from the walls of ruins, but she had not walked in them, because Ghere would not let her, and now she understood why he would not, and was not sorry. The lights were all round her feet, soft, cold, green, and they breathed, very slowly, brighter and dimmer, and when she put her foot down among them they did not go out. They glowed round her boot. The moss was soft. The cold was less, among them. Or she thought it was less.

She found that she was walking more easily. Her leg hurt less. Her rib hurt less. She walked between the trees across the glowing floor and the golden lights drifted round her head, and she was not shaking any more, and she felt, for the first time since the joint, something like peace.

She thought: I could walk here a long time.

She stopped.

She stood among the lights with her heart going suddenly very fast. She did not know how far she had come. She looked back, and the step with the seep and the black hole was not there. There were trees, and the glowing floor between them, going away in every direction, the same, breathing.

She did not know which way she had come.

She stood very still. She made herself feel her leg. It hurt. It hurt badly, the long bright pull from the knee to the hip, and her rib was a knife in her side, and she was cold, deep in, and shaking again, now that she noticed. She had not stopped shaking. She had stopped feeling it.

She did the only thing she could think of.

She counted. Out loud, in the dark, in a hoarse cracked whisper, the way Osselin counted in the lanes at Wendle. One. Two. She took a step on each. Three. Four. She did not know which way she was going. She went the way she was facing. Five. Six. Seven. Eight.

She stopped.

She did not say nine. She stood with the ninth number in her mouth and did not say it, and breathed.

Then she began again. One.

On the third eight she smelled it.

Salt. Strong. Cold. On her face, from in front of her, from the way she was going. A wind. The first wind she had felt since the Gate. It came through the trees smelling of salt and weed and wet stone, and it moved the ferns, and the golden lights drifting above her bent in it and went streaming away behind her.

She walked into it, counting.

It was the cold that brought Ailsa back.

She was walking into the wind with her face stiff and her hands numb and her wet coat flapping against her legs, counting, and the cold came into the hollow under her collarbone where her shirt was open, and she was standing in the yard behind the workroom on the coast, on a winter morning, with the water jar too heavy and the wind coming off the harbour, and Ailsa was behind her.

Ailsa had put both hands under Riarhin’s collar, at the back of her neck, from behind. Her hands had been warm from the oven. She had held them there against Riarhin’s skin and said, There. Now you can carry it.

Riarhin had carried it.

She stopped walking. She stood in the wind on the glowing floor with the numbers gone out of her head and her eyes shut and felt Ailsa’s hands on the back of her neck, warm, so clearly that she put her own hand up to touch them, and there was only her own cold collar, and the wind.

She stood there and wept. She had not wept since the grave. She had not wept when Farieth came to it, or when the siblings came, or in the shed at the baths, or on the walkway, or on the floor of the joint with her grandmother’s weight coming down on her.

Nothing came to comfort her.

When it had gone down, she found that she wanted, very much, to be warm. Not warm as she had been among the lights. Warm in a room. With a fire. With a bowl of something too hot to eat and somebody telling her to wait for it. With a bed with one short leg and a folded scrap of cloth under it. She wanted it so much that she could taste it.

She found the numbers again. One. She walked.

The lights thinned. The trees thinned. The ground began to fall, gently and then steeply, and the wind grew stronger and colder and wetter in her face, and she could hear something now, under the wind, a long slow sound that came and went and came. Not a breath. Something larger and further off.

The sea.

She stopped at the top of a slope where the trees ended and the ground fell away in front of her, very steeply, into darkness. Below, far below, she could hear the sea moving: a long roar and then a hiss, a long roar and then a hiss, over and over. She could see nothing. The lights had stopped behind her among the last trees.

She could not go down. Not in the dark. She could not see where her feet would go, and the slope went down very steeply, and her leg would not bend.

She sat down at the top of it, under the last tree, with her back against its trunk, out of the worst of the wind, and pulled her wet coat round her, and put Remorse across her knees, and sat in the dark listening to the sea, close enough to smell it, close enough to hear every wave, unable to go any further.

She did not sleep. She sat and shook and counted waves, eight and a pause, and waited for the light.

Chapter 38 — Where the trees stop

The light came grey and slow, and showed her the sea.

It was below her, a long way down, at the foot of a slope so steep it was nearly a cliff, all loose earth and rock and roots with small twisted trees clinging to it. At the bottom of the slope there was a strip of shingle, narrow, grey, running away to left and right as far as she could see, between the foot of the slope and the water. And beyond the shingle, the sea: grey, heaving, enormous, going out to a horizon lost in cloud, with long lines of white breaking on the shingle and drawing back, and breaking.

Above the strip of shingle, all along the top of it, the forest stopped.

It stopped as though somebody had drawn a line. The trees came down the slope to the edge of the shingle and no further. Their roots reached out over the stones and curled back. Their branches leaned out over the strip, over the grey stones, and did not touch them. Between the last of the roots and the first of the waves there was a band of bare grey shingle perhaps thirty paces wide, and nothing grew on it at all.

Riarhin sat under her tree at the top of the slope and looked at it.

She had seen it before. As a girl, with Ghere, further south. He had brought her to the edge and stood with her among the last trees and told her the forest did not go onto the shingle. She had asked why. He had said it did not, and that was all, and that people who came along the shore in boats could land on the stones, and would not come past the roots, and that was all too.

She had not understood. She did not understand now.

She began to go down.

It took most of the morning. She went down sitting, mostly, on her backside, the way Coryd had come down the roof of the fourth house at Drevan, with her good leg braced and her bad leg out in front and her left hand on the roots and the earth, and Remorse on her back, and the water skin against her side. Twice the earth went from under her and she slid, a body’s length, two, in a rattle of stones, and caught a root and stopped. Once she sat for a long time on a ledge halfway down with her face against her knee, because her rib had caught and would not let go, and the sea roared below and the wind came up the slope cold and wet and smelling of weed.

She came down at last onto the shingle.

She stood at the foot of the slope, among the last roots, and looked at the grey stones in front of her. Then she stepped out onto them.

Nothing happened. They were stones. They shifted under her boots and rattled. The wind came off the sea, cold, straight into her face, and found every wet place in her clothes, and she stood on the shingle in the open with the forest behind her and the sea in front, and for the first time since the Gate, there was sky over her head. Grey, low, moving fast. Sky.

She stood and looked at it.

Then she looked along the shore.

There was nothing. The strip of shingle ran away north and south, grey, empty, between the forest and the sea. No boat. No smoke. No sign that anybody had ever come along it. The waves broke and drew back, broke and drew back.

She did not know how long she could stand.

She looked at the shingle under her feet. She looked at the tide. The line of weed and wet stones where the last high water had reached was far up the strip, almost to the roots, a few paces from where she stood. When the tide came in, the strip would be narrow. A few paces of stones between the water and the trees. She looked at the sky in the south-west, where the wind came from. It was darker there. Rain. Perhaps more than rain.

There was a hollow in the slope behind her, under the roots, where the earth had fallen away and left an overhang. She could get in there, out of the wind. She could sit there out of the rain with her back to the earth.

From in there, nobody on the sea would see her.

She stood on the shingle in the wind and looked at the hollow and then at the sea.

She went down to the water instead.

Not to the edge. To the line of weed at the top of the beach, where the shingle was wet and dark and the stones were larger. She stood there in the open, in the wind, where anybody on the water would see a figure against the grey stones and the dark trees, and she unbuckled her sword belt one-handed and drew Remorse out of its scabbard, and held it up.

There was no sun. The blade did not flash. It was a long dark line against the grey sky, held up at the end of a tired arm.

She lowered it.
