She looked at it in her hand. Then she put it back in the scabbard, and took off her coat.

It was hard, with one arm. She did it slowly, shrugging the wet heavy thing off the bound arm and then the good one, and the wind caught it and nearly took it from her and she held on. Under it her shirt was wet too, and torn, and the wind went through it as though she were naked. She did not care. She found a long piece of driftwood in the weed, grey and bleached and light, as long as she was tall, and she knelt on the stones and tied the sleeve of the coat round the end of it with her teeth and her good hand, and stood, and held it up.

The coat flapped on the end of the pole, a dark shape against the grey, and her arm shook holding it, and she held it.

Nothing came.

She held it until her arm would not hold it, and then she drove the end of the pole into the shingle as deep as she could and piled stones round its foot, and it stood, leaning, with the coat flapping on it. Then she sat down on the stones beside it with her back to the wind and her knees drawn up and waited.

The rain came in the afternoon.

It came out of the south-west in a grey wall across the sea, and reached the shore, and she was in it. It was cold and hard and it came sideways on the wind, and in a moment she was wetter than she had been in the seep, and the shingle round her ran with water, and she could not see the sea at all, only grey. The coat on its pole flapped and streamed.

She did not go to the hollow.

She sat by the pole in the rain with her arms round her knees and her face down and shook. She did not think about anything. She thought about being warm. She thought about Ailsa’s hands. She thought about Osselin, very small, lifting a lamp at the foot of a ramp, and Vesket stumbling along a gallery with an empty case on his back.

The rain passed.

It went on north along the shore, a grey wall, and the sea came back, heaving, white-capped, enormous, and the light came back, a strange yellow light under the edge of the cloud.

And there was a ship.

It was far out, to the south, coming up the coast. A dark shape on the grey water with a single square brown sail, high at the bow and high at the stern, leaning a little in the wind. Riarhin saw it and did not move. She thought she had imagined it. She watched it come on, slowly, rising and falling, and it did not go away.

The old man with the goat on Fennel’s ferry. They go up the Thin Shore past the forest and on up to wherever they come from.

Fennel, leaning on the sweep. They stop for water. They have to. There’s places they know.

Riarhin got up.

She got up and went to the pole and pulled it out of the shingle and held it up with the coat on it, high, and waved it. Slowly, from side to side, the way you wave a flag across a valley. Her arm shook and her rib stabbed at every swing and she waved it.

The ship came on up the coast. It was going past, well out, a long way out, along the line of the shore. She could see men on it now, small dark shapes at the stern and in the waist. Nobody was looking at the shore. Why would they look at the shore. It was the forest side. Nobody landed on the forest side.

She waved the coat.

The ship came level with her, far out, and went on past.

She did not stop waving. She waved until the coat was too heavy, and then she let the pole down, and stood holding it, and watched the brown sail and the high stern go on north, getting smaller.

A mile up the coast, perhaps more, where the slope came down to the shore at a little point, the ship turned.

She saw the sail swing. She saw the high bow come round, slowly, towards the land, towards the point. She saw something drop from the side of the ship into the water. A boat. A small boat, dark, with men in it, and oars. It pulled away from the ship towards the point, towards the shore.

A watering. A stream mouth under the point, where they knew there was fresh water.

Riarhin began to walk.

She walked up the shingle towards the point with the pole in her good hand, using it like a staff, dragging her bad leg. The shingle shifted under her. The tide was coming in now and the strip was narrowing, and she walked along the top of it, close under the roots of the trees, where the stones were dry. It was a mile. It was more than a mile. She walked it.

Halfway, she saw the boat reach the shore.

It came in through the white water at the point and men jumped out of it into the surf and dragged it up onto the stones. Five of them, six. They began to carry things from the boat up the shore towards the trees at the point, where the stream must come out. Barrels. They did not look her way.

She stopped and lifted the pole and waved the coat.

Nobody saw.

She went on. She was close enough now to see them clearly: men in rough wool and leather, bareheaded, broad, one with a red beard, one very young, a boy. She could hear them. Their voices came to her on the wind, broken, shouting to each other over the noise of the sea, and she knew the language. It was the speech of the north. She had heard it in the Black Banner camps, from Linn who sold their swords, and she had learned enough of it to swear in and buy beer.

She stopped again, two hundred paces off, and lifted the coat, and tried to call.

Nothing came out. Her throat was dry and raw and her voice was gone, and what came out of her mouth was a cracked whisper that the wind took away at once.

The boy saw her.

He was coming back down the shore from the stream with an empty barrel on his shoulder, and he looked up, along the shingle, and saw her: a figure under the trees with a pole and a dark flag, standing on the strip between the forest and the sea. He stopped dead. The barrel slid off his shoulder and rolled on the stones.

He shouted something. The others turned.

Nobody moved for a long moment. Riarhin stood with the pole and the coat, swaying, and the six men stood by their boat at the point two hundred paces off and looked at her, and the sea roared between.

Then one of them, the one with the red beard, cupped his hands round his mouth and shouted.

It came to her on the wind in pieces. The speech of the north, slow and loud, the way you shout to a stranger. She understood it. Who are you? Are you of the forest?

She tried to answer. She had the words in her head, in their tongue, no, I’m not, I need help, I’m hurt, and her mouth would not shape them. She stood with her mouth open and nothing came.

She lifted the pole. She let it fall. She sat down on the shingle, because her leg would not hold her up any more.

On the point, the men were talking. She could see them, gathered by the boat, arguing. The red-bearded one was pointing at her. Another one, a big man, broad as a door, was shaking his head and pointing at the trees behind her and then at the sea and the ship. The boy was standing a little apart with the barrel at his feet, looking at her.

The big man walked away and began to load the boat.

The red-bearded one shouted at him. The big man shouted back. The red-bearded one stood still a moment. Then he turned and looked along the shore at Riarhin, sitting on the stones, and he called out something to the others, loudly, one short sentence, and Riarhin heard it.

I’m going for her. I’ve said it.

He began to walk along the shore towards her.

The boy went with him.

Chapter 39 — The boat alongside

The red-bearded man stopped a few paces off and looked at her.

Close to, he was older than she had thought, his beard going grey at the chin, his face weathered brown and deeply lined round the eyes. He had a knife at his belt and he kept his hand away from it. The boy stood behind him. The boy was perhaps fourteen, thin, with fair hair cut short and very blue eyes, and he was staring at Riarhin as though she were a seal that had come up the beach and sat down.

“You’re hurt,” said the man, in the northern speech, slowly.

Riarhin nodded.

“Can you talk?”

She opened her mouth. A sound came out, very small, cracked. She closed it. She lifted the water skin from her side and drank, and the water was cold and tasted of stone, and she held it in her mouth and swallowed and tried again.

“Yes,” she said. It was a whisper. “A little.”

He looked at her for a long moment.

“Are you of the forest?” he said.

“No.” She thought about it. “Half.”

He looked at her ears, at her hair, at the trees behind her. The boy said something to him, very fast, that Riarhin did not catch.

“He says you came out of the trees,” said the man. “He says he saw you on the slope this morning when we came round the south point. He says he told Hallbjorn and Hallbjorn said it was a deer.”

Riarhin looked at the boy. The boy went red.

“I wasn’t sure,” said the boy, to her, in the northern speech, very quickly. “I said I saw something. He said nobody comes out of the trees. Then you were on the stones with the coat.” He stopped. “I didn’t know if it was a person.”

“It’s a person,” said the man. “Eyvind. Get her other side.”

They got her up between them. She could not walk on her own any more. She put her good arm over the boy’s shoulders and the man took her round the waist on the other side, carefully, keeping away from the bound arm, and his hand found her rib and she made a sound and he moved it lower. They walked her along the shingle towards the point. It was a long way. The boy was strong for his size and he did not say anything, only breathed hard and kept his feet. The man talked. He talked steadily, in the northern speech, not to her exactly, about the tide and the weather and the stream at the point, where they had watered for twenty years, and the ship, which was called Staðfast and was bound north, and the skipper, who was called Sigrun and would have something to say about this, and Riarhin understood perhaps half of it and did not need to understand any of it. It was a voice. It went on. She leaned on it.

At the boat, the big man was waiting.

He stood in front of it with his arms folded. He had a black beard and a scar through one eyebrow, and he looked at Riarhin hanging between the red-bearded man and the boy, and then at the sword on her back, and then at the trees.

“No,” he said.

“Hallbjorn,” said the red-bearded man.

“No. Orm. Look at her.” Hallbjorn did not unfold his arms. “She came out of the forest. Nobody comes out of the forest. She’s bleeding, she’s got a sword, she can’t stand. She’s got fever, look at her face. We’ve forty days to the Hvalgard coast and the gales coming and twelve men in the hold and no room. If she dies on board, who’s she belong to? Who do we tell? What do we tell them?” He looked at the trees, and his voice changed. “And if she brings something out with her. Out of there.”

Orm did not answer at once.

“She might,” said Hallbjorn. “You know she might. You know what they say about this shore.”

“I know what they say.”

“Then leave her. Leave her water. Leave her a blanket. We’ll tell the skipper she was here.” He looked at Riarhin. “Somebody else will come.”

“Nobody else will come,” said Orm. “We’re the last boat north. You know that. That’s why Sigrun pushed us so hard at the river.”

Hallbjorn’s jaw set.

Riarhin hung between them and listened. She did not say anything. She did not think there was anything she could say that would help. She thought Hallbjorn was right about most of it. She did not know what she might have brought out of the forest.

“I said it,” said Orm. “On the shore. I said I was going for her. You heard me. They all heard me.”

Hallbjorn looked at him for a long moment.

“You said it fast,” he said.

“I said it fast so I couldn’t take it back.” Orm’s voice was quite calm. “I saw her. We all saw her. She was on the stones in the open with a coat on a stick, waving, and we saw her. If we row away now, we row away having seen her. I’m not doing that. I won’t sit by a fire this winter knowing she sat down on the stones and we watched her do it and went back to the ship.” He shifted Riarhin’s weight a little. “I’ll answer for her. To Sigrun. To anybody. If she dies, she’s mine to tell. If she brings something, it’s mine.”

The other men had stopped loading. They stood by the barrels and looked at Orm. Nobody said anything.

Hallbjorn unfolded his arms.

“The sword goes in the bottom of the boat,” he said. “Not on her. And somebody else rows. I’m not sitting next to her.”

“Fair,” said Orm.

They lifted her into the boat.

It was a bad business. The boat was afloat in the shallows, rising and falling on the waves that came in at the point, and she could not climb, and she could not help, and they had to lift her, three of them, over the side, while the boat moved under them. Orm took her shoulders and the boy took her legs and Hallbjorn, with his jaw set, took her waist, and they swung her up and over the gunwale, and her bad leg struck the side of the boat and her rib caught and the world went white and roaring.

When it came back she was lying in the bottom of the boat in a finger’s depth of seawater with her head on something soft, a coil of rope, and the boy was kneeling beside her.

“Your sword,” he said, in the northern speech. “He says you have to give it.”

She looked at him.

“He says it goes under the thwart,” said the boy. “Where you can see it. He says nobody touches it.”

She reached up with her left hand and unbuckled the belt. The boy took it, with the scabbard, very carefully, holding it in both hands, and laid it under the thwart by her head, where she could turn her eyes and see the hilt.

“And the other,” she whispered.

He looked at her.

She drew Toothpick from her belt and gave it to him hilt first. She gave him the Last Blade as well. Then she drew the chisel, Farieth’s chisel, and looked at it a moment, and gave him that too. He looked at it with surprise. He put the knives and the chisel under the thwart beside the sword.

“That’s all,” she said.

The boy nodded. He stayed kneeling beside her.

They rowed out through the white water at the point. Riarhin lay in the bottom of the boat and felt it lift and drop and lift, and heard the oars knock, and the men grunt, and the sea roar, and saw the sky over her, grey, moving, with gulls in it. She turned her head once and looked back at the shore: the strip of grey shingle, and the slope, and the forest along the top of it, dark, endless, with its branches leaning out over the stones and not touching them.

She did not look again.

The ship was bigger than she had thought. It rose over the boat, a high dark wall of overlapping planks, tarred, with the brown sail furled on its yard above and faces looking down over the side. Somebody threw a rope. Somebody shouted. A sling came down, a loop of rope with a board in it, and they got her into it somehow, Orm and the boy, sitting her on the board with the rope round her back, and she held on with her good hand, and they hauled her up the side of the ship with her feet scraping on the tarred planks and her bad leg dragging, and Hallbjorn caught her under the arms at the top and lifted her in over the rail.

She was on a deck. Wet planks under her. People round her. A great many people, it seemed, all talking.

A woman pushed through them.

She was old, short, heavy, with white hair braided round her head and sleeves rolled to the elbow, and she knelt on the wet deck beside Riarhin and put her hand flat on Riarhin’s forehead and held it there.

“Fever,” she said. “Get her below. Out of the wind. Get the brazier lit.” She looked up. “And get her out of these. Everything. They’re soaked through.”

“Asta—” somebody said.

“Below,” said the old woman. “Now. I’ll look at her when she’s dry. Not before.” She looked down at Riarhin. “Can you hear me?”

Riarhin nodded.

“Good. Where are you hurt?”

Riarhin told her. Leg. Side. Arm. Asta nodded at each as though she had expected it.

“Anything else?”

Riarhin thought.

“Cold,” she said.

“I can see that.” Asta stood up. “Orm. Carry her.”

Orm carried her. He carried her down a ladder into the dark of the hold, with the boy going ahead and Asta behind, and laid her on a pile of sacks in a corner near the stern, where there was a little iron brazier on a stone slab, and somebody was already kneeling at it with flint and steel. The hold smelled of tar and wet wool and fish and salt and men. It was very dim. It moved, all of it, slowly, rising and falling, and the timbers creaked.

Asta knelt beside her.

“Clothes,” she said. “All of them. Can you?”

Riarhin could not.

Asta did it. She did it without any fuss at all, cutting where she had to with a short sharp knife, peeling the wet cloth away where it had stuck, and Riarhin lay on the sacks and let her. The boots. The trousers, cut away from the bound thigh. The shirt, cut up the back. Asta looked at the felt pad on the thigh and lifted an eyebrow and took it off and looked at the cut under it, and grunted. She looked at the arm under Osselin’s old linen. She laid her hand on the rib, very lightly, and Riarhin made a sound and Asta took her hand away and nodded.

“Broken,” she said. “Not out of place. You’ll live with it.” She looked at the thigh. “This wants stitching. Not now. When you’re warm.” She looked at the arm. “Who did this binding?”

“A friend.”

“Your friend did well. Somebody undid it.”

“Me.”

“Then you did badly.” Asta took a blanket from somebody behind her, thick, rough, smelling of sheep, and put it over Riarhin, and then another. “Lie still. Eyvind. Water. Not cold. From the pot.”

The boy brought water in a horn cup, warm, tasting of something sharp and green. Asta held Riarhin’s head and she drank. It went down into her, warm, all the way down. She drank all of it. The boy brought more.

“Slowly,” said Asta. “Not all at once. You’ll bring it up.”

Riarhin drank slowly.

The brazier had caught. She could feel it, the warmth coming off it, on her face and her good arm where it lay on the blanket. She was shaking. She was shaking worse than on the shingle, worse than in the seep, great hard shudders that went through her whole body and made her teeth knock and her rib scream, and Asta put another blanket on her and sat beside her and held her good wrist, not tightly, and waited.

“That’s the cold coming out,” Asta said. “It’s good. Let it.”

Riarhin let it.

Above, on the deck, she could hear voices. Feet. Orders being called. A long creak, and a shudder through the whole ship, and then a new motion, longer, steadier, a lean to one side and a lift. The sail had gone up. They were moving.

“Where—” she said through her teeth.

Asta looked at her.

“North,” she said. “We’re bound north. Hvalgard. Before the gales.” She tucked the blanket round Riarhin’s shoulder. “We don’t turn back. Not for anything. Not for you, either, I’m sorry. Sigrun won’t, and she’s right. If we turn back now we lose the season and we winter on the wrong coast.” She looked at Riarhin’s face. “You’ll come north with us. There’s nowhere else for you to go.”

Riarhin lay under the blankets and shook.

North. Away from the forest, and the Gate, and the hawthorn country, and Amedell, and Drevan, and the bridge with the eleven houses. Away from wherever Osselin and Vesket had come out of the forest, if they had come out. Away from all of it.

She did not mind, lying on the sacks in the dim warm hold with the blankets over her and the old woman holding her wrist and the brazier hissing. Later she might.

“Thank you,” she said.

Asta looked at her for a moment.

“Thank Orm,” she said. “And the boy. The boy saw you.”

“I know.”

“And Hallbjorn.”

Riarhin looked at her.

“Hallbjorn said no,” said Asta. “Somebody has to. If nobody ever said no, we’d have every half-drowned thing on the coast aboard and nowhere to sit.” She almost smiled. “He said no and then he helped lift you over the side. I saw him. Thank him too.”

Riarhin closed her eyes.

Chapter 40 — The watch beside her

Asta stitched the thigh in the evening, by the light of a lamp hung from a beam, while the ship rose and fell and the boy held Riarhin’s good hand and looked at the wall.

It hurt. Asta did not pretend it would not. She cleaned the cut with something that burned worse than the cut itself and then she stitched it, eleven stitches, small and close, with a curved needle and gut, and her hands were steady even when the ship lurched. Riarhin lay with her teeth together and watched the lamp swing on its beam.

“There,” said Asta, and tied off the last one. “That’ll hold if you don’t walk on it. You won’t walk on it. There’s nowhere to walk.”

She bound the arm again with clean linen, firmly. She bound the rib, a broad band of cloth round Riarhin’s chest, tight, so that she could not breathe deeply and did not want to. Then she sat back on her heels and looked at her work.

“Who were you fighting?” she said.

Riarhin did not answer at once.

“My grandmother,” she said.

Asta looked at her for a while. Then she nodded, slowly, as though she had heard that answer before.

“Did you win?”

Riarhin looked at the lamp swinging on its beam.

“She’s dead,” she said.

Asta put her hand on Riarhin’s good shoulder, briefly, and took it away, and got up, and went to see to somebody else.

The fever came in the night and stayed for two days.

She kept only pieces of it, which surprised her, because she kept everything. The lamp swinging. The boy’s face, close, with a cup. The ship’s timbers groaning in a long rhythm that her body kept trying to count, eight and a pause, and could not, because it was not eight. Asta’s hand on her forehead, cool. Voices above, on the deck, shouting in the wind. Once, rain coming down through a hatch somebody had left open, and a man swearing and the hatch slamming. Once, very clearly, the sound of somebody singing on the deck above her, a rough cheerful line repeated over and over, and she knew it. She had heard it from the top of the cliff path above the Slow Water, across the water, from the deck of a ship at the stone quay, the evening the spring came up.

She did not know if it was the same ship. She did not ask.

There was always somebody there.

Whenever she woke, in the dim moving hold, there was somebody sitting on the sacks beside her. Sometimes it was Asta. Sometimes it was the boy, Eyvind, with his knees drawn up and his chin on them, half asleep. Once, it was Orm, sitting with his back against a beam and a piece of rope across his knees that he was splicing, slowly, his big hands moving without his looking at them. He saw her eyes open and nodded and went on splicing. Once, in the grey of what might have been morning, it was Hallbjorn.

He was sitting on an upturned bucket a little way off, not close, with his arms folded, looking at her.

She looked back at him.

“You’re awake,” he said.

“Yes.”

“Asta said to sit.” He did not unfold his arms. “Till the boy comes. He’s on the morning watch.”

“You don’t have to.”

“Asta said.” He looked at her. “You don’t argue with Asta on this ship. Not even Sigrun argues with Asta.”

Riarhin lay on the sacks and looked at him. He was so big that the bucket was hidden under him. He had a thick wool cap pulled down over his ears and his black beard was wet; he had come down from the deck. There was a piece of sail on the floor beside him, and a needle stuck in it, and a palm-guard of leather. He had been mending it on the deck and had brought it down when Asta sent him, and had not touched it since.

“Thank you,” said Riarhin.

Hallbjorn looked surprised. Then he looked suspicious.

“For what?”

“Asta said you lifted me over the side.”

“Orm said he’d answer for you. Somebody had to lift.” He shifted on the bucket. “I still think you shouldn’t be here.”

“I know.”

“If you bring something.”

“I know.”

He looked at her for a long moment. Then he looked away, at the dim hold, at the sacks, at the lamp swinging on its beam.

“My brother went into those trees,” he said. “Twelve years ago. Off a boat. Just to look. Just past the roots.” He did not look at her. “He came back out. He came back out and he was fine and he sat by the fire that night and laughed about it and told us there was nothing in there, nothing at all, only trees. And in the spring he walked into the sea.” He was quiet a moment. “Nobody saw him go. We found his boots on the stones.”

Riarhin did not say anything.

“So,” said Hallbjorn. “I said no.”

“You were right to.”

He looked at her quickly.

“I don’t know what I brought out,” said Riarhin. “I don’t think I brought anything. I don’t know.” She looked at the beam above her. “If I were you, I’d have said no.”

Hallbjorn looked at her a long time. Then he unfolded his arms, and picked up the piece of sail from the floor, and the needle, and the palm-guard, and put the guard on, and began to stitch. He stayed on the bucket, stitching, until the boy came down the ladder rubbing his eyes, and then he got up and took his sail and went up the ladder without a word.

The boy sat down on the bucket.

“He sat with you,” said Eyvind, amazed.

On the third day the fever broke, and she was hungry.

Asta brought her porridge, thin, with salt in it and a little fat, in a wooden bowl, and sat beside her while she ate it, and told her not to eat it too fast, and Riarhin ate it too fast and was not sick. Asta brought her more. It was the best thing she had ever eaten. She ate it sitting up against a sack with the bowl wedged in her lap and the spoon in her good hand, and the ship rose and fell under her, and there was grey light coming down through the open hatch, and she could hear the sea.

“Where are we?” she asked.

“North of the forest. Two days. Past the portage.” Asta took the empty bowl. “Another eleven to the first harbour, if the wind holds. It won’t hold. Fifteen.”

“What harbour?”

Asta told her a name. Riarhin had heard of it. A Linn town on the coast, a trading place, where the ships put in before the long run north.

“Do ships go south from there?”

Asta looked at her.

“Some. Before the ice. Not many.” She considered. “There’s a man there sends letters south with the traders. For a price. If you have something to send.”

Riarhin looked at the hatch, at the grey light.

“I have something,” she said.

“Then you’ll send it from there.” Asta stood. “If you’re well enough to hold a pen. Which you won’t be, yet.”

“I can hold a pen.”

“You can hold a spoon.” Asta looked down at her. “You’re not going anywhere with it. Not south. You know that.”

“I know.”

“As long as you know.”

She went up the ladder with the bowl.

Riarhin lay against the sack.

She would write to Coryd. At Sivelle’s house in Valora, with the stair that did not creak and the newel post carved with acorns. She would write that she was alive and on a ship going north and would come back when she could, and she would not say when, because she did not know. She would put the stupid lines at the bottom. Coryd would read it on the stair. Coryd would write to Harissa.

She would write to Thornholt, to Vesper. She would ask whether a Sumwright and a lantern-warden had come out of the forest. She would ask Vesper to tell them, if they had, that she had come out too. She did not know how to say the rest. She did not know whether there was a rest. She thought of the way-cord in Vesket’s coat with its forty knots, and an old woman under a sea-arch with a box of cords that had never been closed.

She would write to Briar. No. Osselin would have written to Briar. Osselin would have gone to Briar herself, along the cliff path to the sister’s house full of cats, and sat with her and told her.

She lay against the sack and thought about Osselin sitting in Briar’s sister’s house with a cat on her knee. It was the first thing she had let herself think about Osselin since the ledge. She let it stay.

The boy came down the ladder.

He had a slate in his hand, and a stub of chalk, and he looked embarrassed.

“Sigrun says,” he said. “For the ship. For the—” He did not have the word. “For saying who’s aboard. In case. She says everybody aboard has to have a name said, so if anything happens somebody can say it.” He held out the slate. “She says I have to ask you.”

Riarhin looked at the slate. There were names on it already, in a careful round hand, a column of them. Sigrun. Orm. Asta. Hallbjorn. Twelve. Thirteen. At the bottom, Eyvind, smaller than the others, as though the boy had written it himself.

“What do they call you?” said Eyvind.

Riarhin looked at the names on the slate for a long time.

She thought of the half-name in orange lichen on a scribe’s stone, soft and bright and new, growing. She thought of the old woman in the amphitheatre with her board on her knees, saying your names, and no holding. She thought of Tern under the arch, saying name it.

“Riarhin,” she said.

The boy wrote it. He wrote it slowly, sounding out each part under his breath, and when he had written it he held the slate up and showed her, and he had spelled it wrong. She did not correct him.

“Riarhin,” he said, carefully, trying it. “Of where?”

She looked at him.

“Just that,” she said.

He nodded, and did not ask again, and went back up the ladder with the slate.

In the evening she slept, properly, for the first time since the measured room, and when she woke it was dark in the hold except for the lamp swinging on its beam, and somebody was sitting beside her.

It was Asta. She had a basket of something in her lap, wool, and a pair of needles, and she was knitting without looking at it, her hands moving in the lamplight. She saw Riarhin’s eyes open and did not stop.

“You should be asleep,” said Riarhin.

“I’ve the next watch. I’ll sleep after.” Asta knitted. “Orm’s on deck. He’ll come down when he’s done.”

“You don’t have to stay.”

“I know I don’t.” Asta did not look up. “Go back to sleep.”

Riarhin lay and watched her knit.

Above them, on the deck, people were talking. It came down through the hatch, muffled by the wind and the sea, in the northern speech, and she could follow most of it. It was an argument. It had been going on for some time, she gathered. Somebody had eaten the last of the dried apples, and nobody would admit to it, and the cook was threatening to stop the beer until somebody did.

“It was the boy,” said a voice she thought was Orm’s.

“It was not,” said the boy, outraged.

“You had apple on your breath at the watch change.”

“That was from the porridge.”

“There’s no apple in the porridge.”

“There’s apple in Asta’s porridge,” said the boy. “She puts it in for the woman.”

There was a silence on the deck. Then a great shout of laughter, several voices at once, and somebody saying Asta’s been giving our apples to the forest woman, and somebody else saying then the forest woman owes us beer, and the cook saying something Riarhin did not catch that made them laugh harder.

Beside her, in the lamplight, Asta’s needles did not stop.

“That’s true,” Asta said. “About the apples.”

Riarhin looked at her.

“You needed them more than they did.” Asta turned the knitting and started a new row. “Don’t tell them I said so.”

Riarhin lay on the sacks under the sheep-smelling blankets and listened to them laugh on the deck above her, and to the needles clicking, and to the long slow groan of the timbers as the ship leaned into the wind, and the sea going past outside the planks, a hand’s breadth from her head, north.

She did not count anything. She lay and listened, and after a while she slept again, and Asta stayed beside her until Orm came down the ladder at the change of the watch, and then Asta got up and gave him the bucket and went to sleep herself, and Orm sat down with his rope across his knees, splicing, and the ship went on.
